The House of Borrowed Voices

The House of Borrowed Voices

A novel — first draft

Part One What comes back

Chapter 1 The empty lighter

The man on the lighter had finished unloading three hours before anybody noticed his hand.

He stood beside the after cleat with his other arm out, waiting for a weight that was no longer there. The Brindle’s master held a lamp above him. Whenever the boat rose, the light showed the same four fingers disappearing beneath a plate of bent iron.

“Don’t bring her any closer,” Lyra said.

Daven looked toward the man at the bow line. “You heard.”

The boat settled a little away from the steps. Lyra sat on the lowest dry tread, lowered her feet to the gunwale, and stepped aboard. Her right arm objected when she put weight through it. She took the lamp with her left hand.

A season of mending had given her back nearly everything except the ability to forget the arm. She could dress herself, haul a small load, sleep on that side if she was tired enough. A sudden reach still found the place where the gate had pinned it.

The Worn man’s sleeve had been rolled above an elbow gray with salt. His hand entered the gap palm upward. Blood and black grease made the rest difficult to see.

“How did this happen?”

“Last barrel went over. He caught the running strap. I think the plate lifted, then came down on him.” Daven held the lamp steady while she knelt. “He hasn’t said a word. I thought he was waiting for his people.”

“Did you call them?”

“There wasn’t anyone there to call by then. They told me to bring the empties here.”

There were no empties. Lyra looked at him.

“Straps,” he said. “They wanted the straps back. That was the word they used.”

The trapped man moved his free hand. His fingers closed around the air above Lyra’s shoulder, carried the imagined burden inward, and opened.

“What’s his name?”

Daven shook his head.

Lyra set the lamp on the deck. The water was still black beneath the quay, but the last stars had gone. Above them an early delivery cart knocked over the joints in the paving. Its driver called down to ask whether they needed a rope. Daven said no, then changed his mind and asked for a wooden wedge. The driver began searching among his loads.

“I’m going to touch your wrist,” Lyra told the man. “Keep still if you can.”

He watched the place where the cargo had stood.

She put two fingers above the trap. There was movement beneath the skin, a weak recurring pressure. The arm was cold in a way she knew from other Worn and did not mistake for her own. Fine grains rasped against her fingertips. She could feel where the tendon had been pulled tight.

“Can you hear me?”

His free arm reached again. This time his forearm struck her temple.

Daven caught it. The man leaned against him with slow, considerable force.

“Don’t pull him away. You’ll open the hand.”

“What should I do?”

“Give him something.”

Daven looked around the empty boat. He found a rolled strap, put it across the waiting palm, and nearly lost his footing when the man moved it toward the missing cargo. Lyra caught the strap’s other end before it went overboard. Together they brought it down onto the deck. The man released it and reached again.

“Again,” she said.

For several minutes Daven supplied the strap and took it back. Lyra worked a thin piece of wood into the gap beside the fingers. The plate had torn one fixing out of the timber. The other remained fast. Each time the Brindle moved against her lines, the bent iron pressed a little farther down.

The cart driver came to the steps with a wedge and a short hammer. He watched the loading motion, then looked at Lyra.

“Shall I fetch somebody?”

“Fetch my clerk at the Opera House. Tesset. Tell her we need a sheltered room, clean cloths, and someone to stay with an injured man. He mustn’t be left beside working gear.”

“The Opera?”

“Yes.”

He recognized her then. She saw it pass over his face, followed by the uncertainty about what he ought to call her.

“Go,” she said, before he had to decide.

Daven was sweating despite the cold. The Worn man had begun taking the strap in both the free hand and the ruined effort of the other. Each attempt sent a little fresh blood along the underside of the plate.

Lyra examined the remaining fixing. It had been driven through a timber thick enough to take the boat’s towing load. The bolt below was inaccessible without lifting a deck board beneath the man’s feet.

“We can try levering it,” Daven said.

“It will come down on him if the timber splits.”

“Then take the bolt.”

“Can you lift that board with him here?”

Daven looked at the deck. He had an answer before he spoke, and she could see how much he disliked it.

“Not whole.”

“Bring the saw.”

“The board runs under the side bench.”

“Then we take part of the bench.”

He stopped passing the strap. The trapped man thrust his free hand at Lyra’s face, searching for it. She ducked and gave it back to Daven.

“I use that bench,” he said.

“I’ll pay.”

“It isn’t only the bench. I have a load at second water.”

“I’ll pay for the missed load. Get the saw.”

Daven’s jaw worked. Then he pushed the strap into her left hand and went forward to the tool locker.

The man carried their strap through its small journey. Lyra followed him as well as she could from her knees. Up, inward, down. The motion had a hesitation halfway through. She wondered whether he had once been accustomed to a second person receiving the weight there. When she tried meeting his hand at that point, he held on until she took the strap fully. Only then did his fingers open.

“All right,” she said. “I have it.”

He reached again.

Daven returned with a narrow saw and a chisel. He measured the cut with his thumb, muttered something about whoever had laid the deck, and set to work. Lyra took the lamp between her knees. The saw’s first movement brought the man’s gaze down. He stared at it with an attention that made her speak to him again.

“We’re opening the wood. The wood, not your hand.”

He said something. It scraped in his throat, too low to make out.

She leaned closer.

“Load.”

“We’ve finished the load.”

He reached. She gave him the strap.

The saw caught twice on old nail ends. Daven changed his angle and worked more carefully. A woman stopped on the quay with a basket against her hip. Then a porter, then two men in clean aprons on their way to an early kitchen. Lyra told them to leave room on the steps. One asked whether it was safe to have him out in the street.

“He’s standing on a boat,” she said. “Keep the steps clear.”

The woman remained. When Daven needed another wedge, she fetched one from his locker without asking him to stop the saw and describe it a second time.

At last he lifted a short piece of the bench and split the board beneath. Lyra slipped her right hand through the opening to find the bolt. Pain ran up to her shoulder as she turned her wrist.

“Give that to me,” Daven said.

“I can reach it.”

“So can I.”

She moved. He lay full length in the sawdust, one arm beneath the deck, and swore with his face against the boards. The nut turned a fraction. The iron above shifted, and the trapped man made a sound unlike the word he had spoken.

“Stop.”

Daven stopped.

Lyra seated the wedge farther in. The plate no longer bore on the hand, but its bent edge would tear the skin if they lifted it straight up. She followed the angle with her finger, finding the way it had entered.

“When I say, turn the plate toward the stern. Slowly. It has to come off him along the bend.”

The woman on the steps held the lamp. Daven backed the nut free. Lyra took the weight of the plate with her left hand and guided with her right, feeling the injured arm tremble. The man’s free hand pressed across her shoulder as though she were a sack that had been laid badly.

“Now,” she said.

Daven turned. The iron came away with a wet sound. Lyra caught the man’s wrist before he could put it back into the gap.

He tried once. When she held him, he bent at the waist and reached for the place with his other hand.

“Cover it.”

Daven set the cut bench board across the hole. The man brushed it aside.

“Something he can’t move.”

They laid a long plank over the opening and wedged its ends beneath the side seats. The man felt along it. His ruined fingers left dark streaks on the new surface.

Lyra wrapped the hand in clean cloth from her bag. When she turned him toward the steps, he resisted without looking at her. Daven still held the strap. He raised it, and the man followed its lift with his whole body.

“Bring that,” she told him.

They took nearly as long to get him ashore as they had taken to free him. He could walk. He could climb, when he found a burden to follow. At the top he stopped to stare at the empty quay. The woman put down her basket, took the strap from Daven, and walked backward until he began to move again.

Tesset met them under the receiving arch with a barrow padded in sailcloth.

“He won’t sit,” Lyra said.

“We’ll find that out when he’s tried.”

She moved the barrow beside the wall, away from the open yard. Daven lowered the strap over its handles. The man’s knees bent. He sat abruptly, staring at his hands.

Tesset covered the injured one with another cloth. Lyra had seen her face when she first looked at it. Now she was telling the woman with the basket where she could wash the blood off her fingers.

“What’s his name?” Tesset asked.

“We don’t know yet.”

The room Tesset had found was a disused wardrobe store near the kitchens. It had no machinery, no large window, and a low bench along the cooler wall. Someone had removed a row of hooks before they arrived. Their pale rectangles remained on the boards.

The man would not lie down. He sat with the strap across his knees and his good hand closed around its edge. Lyra put a stool in front of him and began unwrapping the injured hand.

Two fingers were badly crushed. She could ease the swelling around the others. She could close the worst of a torn vessel if the tissues answered her. She could not make the bone whole by wanting it.

“Can you hold this basin?” she asked Tesset.

“Where?”

“Under the wrist. Lower. Thank you.”

The first water ran almost black. Lyra washed again, working dirt out of the folds with a piece of softened linen. The Worn man’s eyes moved to her mouth when she spoke, then returned to the strap.

She had treated people who could not answer before. Gyllan used to make her tell them what she was doing anyway. Once, when she had complained that a fevered man heard none of it, he had asked how she proposed to establish that fact. She had been annoyed with him for the whole afternoon and had told the man about every cloth afterward.

“This will pull,” she said. “I need the iron dirt out.”

The man leaned forward. Tesset steadied the basin against her knees.

Lyra settled her fingertips around the open wound. The pulse was difficult to follow. She waited until it distinguished itself from the small movements in her own hands, then worked with what remained. A little heat gathered beneath the chilled skin. The running blood slowed. She felt exhaustion arrive in a familiar place behind her eyes.

Outside, breakfast pots were being set down. Someone laughed, and someone else told him to bring a cloth if he intended to laugh while spilling it.

The edge of the wound drew together. She stopped before the pressure of her own desire made her press harder.

“That’s enough for now.”

Tesset watched her tie the dressing. “Will he keep them?”

“I don’t know.”

“Who ought to see him?”

“A surgeon who will look at the hand before he looks at who sent him.”

“I’ll ask Marda.”

Lyra raised her head too quickly. Tesset held her gaze for a moment, then went back to winding the remaining cloth.

Marda knew people who had cared for the changed. She also knew who had allowed them inside and then charged for the cleaning of an entire house. It was a sensible choice. Lyra nodded.

The man looked at the basin as Tesset stood. His good hand opened.

“No,” Lyra said gently. “You don’t have to carry that.”

He reached for it. Tesset set it down outside the door and came back empty-handed.

They tried water in a cup. He did not take it. When Lyra put the edge against his lips, he swallowed a little and pushed it away. She moved the cup within reach and left it there.

Daven arrived with his dispatch sheet. It had spent the night tucked behind a board in the little cabin. He had smoothed it with wet palms, making the handwriting less legible.

“There. That may be him.”

Tarn. Beside the name was a stroke and a quantity of straps. A different hand had written the destination: east stores, Salt Ward navigation canal.

“Is that the place you loaded?” Lyra asked.

“Loaded at their lower landing. Barrels for the oil press. They sent two men. The other went ashore with the last barrel. This one stayed.”

“Who dispatched them?”

“Clerk with a broken red pen tucked in his cap. I don’t know his name.”

Lyra knew the seal beneath the instructions. A little salt had caught in its folds, though Daven had kept it out of the rain.

“Did you know whose business it was?” she asked.

“I knew where I was loading. You hire boats there too.”

“I hire boats everywhere.”

“Then you’ll know how often a master gets to ask who owns what goes aboard.”

She looked up. He was tired and had reached the limit of being grateful that someone else could deal with the injured man. His bench was cut apart. Second water would come whether she was pleased with his answers or not.

Lyra took out her purse.

“Tell Tesset the repair cost when you know it. This is for the missed load.”

He looked at the money in her palm. “I haven’t told you what it paid.”

“If it paid more, she’ll settle the rest.”

He took it carefully, counting only after it was in his own hand. Then he put two coins back on the stool beside her.

“That covers it.”

She gave him the dispatch sheet. “Let her copy that before you go.”

“And him?”

“He stays here until we have somewhere better.”

Daven glanced at the man. “They’ll ask.”

“Give them my name.”

For the first time that morning he smiled, though it did not last. “That I can do.”

By midday the surgeon had come, the wounded man had drunk half a cup of water, and a boy from the east stores was waiting in the receiving court with an order for the return of a laborer.

Lyra met him there. He was old enough to dislike being called a boy and young enough to keep glancing at the guards. She read his paper in the sunlight. It called Tarn fit for assigned work and required his prompt return with all issued equipment.

“The equipment is on Daven’s boat,” she said. “The laborer is injured.”

“He was sent sound.”

“Did you see him?”

“No.”

“Then don’t tell me that.”

He swallowed. “That’s what they told me to say.”

“Tell them the surgeon has him under care. If they want to dispute it, send somebody who can explain which hand they expect him to use.”

“Can I have that in writing?”

She had been about to send him away. His request made her look at him properly. A dark bruise ran out from beneath his cuff.

“Yes,” she said. “Wait here.”

At her desk she wrote a short description of the injury and the location where it had occurred. She did not promise a date of return. She signed her own name, folded the paper, and gave it to him with a piece of bread from the plate Tesset had left.

He stared at the bread.

“You can eat it on the way,” she said.

After he had gone, she went through the west court to look at the main receiving hoist. It had been roped off since the previous afternoon. One of the men waiting for it held out a bronze sleeve with a split running nearly its full length.

“Esten says this was old before we got it.”

“Esten says that about everything.”

“He says we need another.”

Lyra turned the sleeve in her good hand. She could see where someone had filed the inside to make it fit a shaft it had never been cast for. The bright uneven wear had cut through the last of the metal.

“How many loads are waiting?”

“Five carts. Seven when the dyers arrive.”

She looked across the court at sacks already stacked under canvas. Rain would spoil the lower ones. Hiring more backs to carry them down would eat what remained of the day’s earnings.

“Get Esten here tomorrow morning. I’ll find the fitting.”

In her room that evening she lowered her sore arm into a bowl of cool brine. The small pressure of the water around it was pleasant. Then it seemed to close in separate rings, too many for the width of the bowl. She moved her fingers. Five pale shapes moved in the water; something behind them appeared to keep curling after she stopped. A thread from the cloth, perhaps. She waited before looking away. Her arm had ceased trembling.

Below, somebody was learning a tune on a pipe and going wrong at the same turn. She knew the tune. Maeron had sung it while checking rope, and once she had taken the end from him just to make him use both hands and stop conducting with it. She remembered his laughter before she remembered the gate.

Lyra lifted her arm out of the bowl. Water fell from her elbow onto the floor. She wiped it up, set a folded cloth beneath the bowl, and laid the split bronze fitting where she would see it in the morning.

Chapter 2 A second pay day

Tesset’s sister had put the money under the bread.

She had also cut the bread, covered it, and left a knife in the wrong drawer, so that Tesset spent the first part of the morning believing the little bag had been stolen. When Nella came down in her night shift, Tesset was on her knees looking beneath the chest.

“I moved it away from the window.”

“Into the loaf?”

“Under the loaf. Anyone coming by could have seen it.”

“The shutters were closed.”

“The shutters have holes.”

Tesset sat back on her heels. Above them, one of the girls began calling for the other to stop putting cold feet on her. Nella closed her eyes.

“It belongs to work,” Tesset said. “Please don’t move it again.”

“Then don’t leave it where we eat.”

It was Tesset’s table, in Tesset’s room. Nella had a table upstairs. Tesset knew exactly how the rest of the argument would go if she said so, and she needed her sister to take a parcel to the bindery later. She stood and lifted the loaf.

Nothing was missing. She counted twice while Nella watched, offended by the second count more than the first.

“I brought Leda’s scarf down too.”

“Where is it?”

“On your bed.”

The yellow scarf lay folded on the pillow Tesset had slept beside. Leda had left before dawn to help finish a run of printed calendars. Tesset had meant to wake and give her breakfast. She remembered a hand against her hair and saying something that had apparently satisfied them both at the time.

“Will you take it to her?”

“I will if you stop looking at the bread as though I’ve put an extra charge on it.”

Tesset laughed despite herself. Nella broke off the heel and handed it over. They ate standing, one listening to the children overhead, the other thinking of the two envelopes she had to keep separate all day.

The larger held Opera House wages. The smaller had Sera’s mark across the flap. Tesset put that one inside her bodice, where neither a helpful sister nor a careless porter would rearrange it.

Senn was waiting beside the office door when she arrived.

He had washed and changed his shirt, which generally meant he was going to ask for something. He was only a little younger than Dorel, broad through the back and so quiet in his movements that Tesset had once locked him in a store while he was taking an inventory. Since then he made a deliberate noise when she entered a room.

“Have you seen the correction?” he asked.

“Which correction?”

“My pay.”

She opened the office. He remained in the doorway while she set her bag down, took out the ordinary wage list, and found the change in Lyra’s handwriting.

One evening duty removed. Dorel’s extra hours added. The two amounts did not match, because Dorel was paid more.

“You missed the relief on fifth day.”

“Dorel said he could stay. I told Seldo before I left.”

“And who told Lyra?”

Senn looked toward the guard’s empty stool. “I thought I had told the person who would.”

Tesset looked through the notes clipped beneath the list. Seldo had recorded Senn’s departure. He had written nothing about an exchange.

“Dorel was on all night,” she said.

“I know. I took his morning yesterday.”

“That isn’t here.”

“I was there.”

He said it without emphasis. Tesset disliked the sensation of being asked to doubt a man whom she had watched eat his supper beside a doorway so he could keep an ear on the crystal.

“What happened on fifth day?”

“My father fell getting out of the bath. The woman who looks in on him couldn’t lift him. She sent here.”

“Did you tell Seldo that?”

“He asked where I was going. I told him.”

Tesset sat down. There were eleven payments to make before noon, and she had hoped the arithmetic would be the difficult part.

“I’ll get it corrected.”

“When?”

“When Lyra comes up.”

Senn pressed his lips together. Then he nodded and stepped aside for a porter bringing in a cracked jar.

“It should be today,” he said. “I need the money today.”

After he had gone, Tesset took the smaller envelope out and laid it beside the wage book. She had not yet opened it. The agreement inside began on the next first day, and its sums could not cover an earlier shortfall by being written in a different hand.

Lyra came in smelling faintly of the room where the injured Worn man had spent the night. She was carrying a cup she had evidently forgotten to drink.

“The surgeon wants someone with him until the fever settles,” she said. “Marda has found a woman who can do afternoons. Put her under care expenses, not boat repairs.”

Tesset pointed to Senn’s entry.

Lyra read it. “He left.”

“He arranged cover.”

“He told a guard he was leaving. Those aren’t the same thing.”

“Dorel agreed to stay, and Senn worked his morning afterward.”

“Then somebody ought to have told me.”

“I’m telling you.”

Lyra drank from the cup. It had gone cold; Tesset saw her realize it and drink again anyway.

“Put his morning on the list.”

“That still leaves him short. The exchange should be entered as an exchange.”

“Dorel worked longer than he should.”

“Dorel agreed.”

“Dorel would agree if you asked him to hold the ceiling up while everyone else had dinner.”

Tesset considered that. It was close enough to true to irritate her. “Then we need somebody else trained to take a turn.”

“We have two attendants.”

“We have a day and a night every day. They sometimes need to leave.”

Lyra set the cup down beside a sheet Tesset had just ruled. A circle of brown water spread into the corner. Tesset moved the sheet without speaking.

“All right,” Lyra said. “Restore it. But tell him that next time he comes to me.”

“What if you’re away?”

“Then you.”

Tesset took the pencil. “I’ll write that down.”

“Must you sound like Sera when you do it?”

She had meant it as a small joke. Tesset could tell by the shape of her mouth, and almost answered in kind. Then she thought of Senn waiting outside to learn whether his father’s fall had cost him a day’s food.

“I sound like somebody who will be asked what you said.”

Lyra looked at her for longer than the remark required. At last she took up the cold cup.

“Make it clear, then.”

The descent to the measuring room took Tesset past the damaged hoist and the salt stores, then down the broad north freight stair. The rails used for the crystal’s earlier movement were stacked along one wall. Someone had wrapped the ends so they would not scrape the passersby. On the middle landing an empty basket had been left precisely where a person carrying another basket would put a foot.

She moved it and left it upside down beneath a bench.

At the bottom, daylight entered from the raised canal landing. It reached the measuring room’s outer threshold but failed before the crystal, whose own light lay against the far wall. Seldo sat beside the door mending a strap on his glove.

“Wages?” he asked.

“Some of them.”

“Have mine come?”

“Upstairs after second bell.”

He held out the glove. “Tell me if that’s going to hold.”

Tesset inspected his large stitches. “For carrying a cup.”

“It only has to carry my hand.”

“Your hand keeps doing things.”

He grinned and went back to work. She liked him. It had become awkward to like people whom she needed to ask about the order still nailed above their heads.

The paper had curled at one corner. Three council signatures remained visible under a protective strip of thin horn. Seldo had watched the strip being fitted. He had refused to step aside for the men named in the order and then helped preserve the writing from damp.

Inside, Dorel was showing Senn how to distinguish a shift in padding from a shift in the supported frame. The intact crystal rose between them, fractured and held. Aria’s lifted hand faced the door. Tesset never quite stopped expecting it to finish its movement.

The old harvesting valve remained beneath its covering plate. Senn checked the edge against the previous day’s drawing, leaving the dated seal untouched. Tesset could read two of the witnesses’ names from where she stood. The place beneath it, where the collecting bottle had once hung, was empty.

She waited until Dorel had put the measuring slips away.

“Senn, the deduction’s been removed.”

His shoulders dropped. “Thank you.”

“You need to tell Lyra or me about an exchange. It’s written now.”

“Can I tell you even if she’s here?”

“Yes.”

Dorel gave her a look of quiet gratitude that she did not feel she had earned. She took out the second envelope.

“This begins next first day.”

Senn did not reach for it. Dorel glanced toward the door.

“Have they settled it?” he asked.

“The independent fund has money for your wages and a relief attendant. It pays for your work caring for Aria. You report those hours to Sera’s appointed clerk.”

“Who is that?”

“For now, me.”

Senn looked from the envelope to her face. “Does Lyra know?”

“She knows the fund exists.”

“That wasn’t the question.”

Tesset felt herself grow hot. She put the envelope on the little worktable rather than hold it out like a temptation.

“No. I haven’t told her I’m carrying this today. Sera will give notice of the change in payment.”

Dorel pulled out a stool. “Sit down.”

“I have other people waiting.”

“They can wait while we understand it.”

She sat. Senn closed the inner screen against a draft from the canal. Beyond it, Seldo coughed and scraped his chair nearer the wall.

They asked about food, sick days, the cost of reaching the cutting house, and whether the fund would require them to live there. Tesset answered what she knew and marked the questions she could not answer. The room outside the city had space for an attendant to sleep, but that did not mean every attendant must give up a home in Silkstrand. A relief person would need training before either man could rely on the arrangement. No one would be paid twice for the same hours. The first interval would be paid in advance.

Dorel’s finger remained against one phrase.

“Instructions concerning movement,” he read.

“Those come from the council under the charter, with Aria’s answers and the handling safeguards recorded.”

“And if Lyra gives another instruction?”

Tesset heard a boat pole touch the landing outside. She had imagined this conversation on her walk to work, but in that version Dorel had understood everything as soon as she passed him the envelope.

“Then you will have somewhere else to take it,” she said.

“An office?” Senn asked.

“An office that pays you. A person who has to answer. You won’t have to stand here deciding which order means you’ll eat next week.”

Dorel looked toward Aria. The faint continuing tone in the crystal had not altered. They had prepared no contact, asked no questions. Tesset made herself remember that silence told them nothing about the conversation they were having beside her.

“When will they want to move her?” he asked.

“I don’t know. There will be a date. You’ll have notice.”

“And if the supports don’t pass inspection that morning?”

“Then she doesn’t move that morning.”

Dorel nodded. Senn drew the envelope toward him and read the first page again, slowly.

“I want my father’s address put somewhere,” he said. “If they’re sending me outside the city, I need somebody to know where he is.”

Tesset found a clean sheet.

Sera’s temporary office occupied the back room of a counting house whose owner had made his displeasure clear by removing all the comfortable chairs. Tesset sat on a stool with a hard ridge beneath one thigh and reported the unanswered questions.

Sera listened without interrupting. On the table between them lay a copy of the Coin charter. The original was kept elsewhere. Tesset knew only that Lyra could not reach it by sending Seldo to unlock a drawer.

“The father can be included in the contact instructions,” Sera said. “Travel is an employment cost. The relief attendant needs a separate agreement.”

“And food?”

“We can pay for food.”

“They eat in the Opera kitchens now.”

“Then we pay the kitchen if it agrees to provide meals.”

“If Lyra refuses?”

“We buy meals elsewhere.”

Tesset rubbed her forehead. “I sound as though I want it to fail.”

“You sound as though you have watched a promise arrive without breakfast.”

Sera moved the charter copy aside and set out a smaller sheet with figures Tesset could follow. The independent transfer money remained intact. Renewed boat hire and rent at the cutting house were already allowed for. The new wage arrangement would spend more before the departure, but it would leave enough to maintain care afterward. There was no large reserve to hide mistakes in.

“One of the merchants won’t advance another coin against Lyra’s quay,” Sera said. “He has kept his undertaking to Aria. I would prefer not to make him regret that distinction.”

“Will the guards be paid from this?”

“Replacement guards. For the measuring room and transfer only. We cannot take over every watch she has hired.”

“She says they’re protecting the whole place.”

“Some are.”

Tesset looked up. Sera had not bothered to deny it. The plain agreement was more unsettling than another list of Lyra’s breaches would have been.

“What happened when the first order came?” Sera asked.

“You were there.”

“I saw Seldo refuse. What happened before that?”

“He spoke to the other guards. Then the porters stood beside him.”

“Why?”

Tesset thought of the wages paid when no merchant would advance them, of Lyra carrying a man out with blood running into her sleeve, of the weeks in which a hot meal appeared because she had made somebody bring it. She also thought of men who disliked the Court enough to stand against anyone carrying fine paper.

“They thought you would turn the place over to people who hadn’t done the work.”

“I don’t need them to like me,” Sera said. “I need them to know what they can do when she tells them to stop the attendants.”

“You make it sound as though all they’ll need is somewhere to be paid.”

“No. I think they need that before the harder part begins.”

For a time they worked through the names of people who could serve a limited watch. Tesset crossed out a man who had once fallen asleep while minding a brazier and then apologized for crossing him out so vigorously. Sera put a mark beside two women employed in the counting house’s night rooms. Neither had worked for Lyra. Neither would be asked to fight her way down a stair.

“Will you carry the attendants’ pay?” Sera asked when they had finished.

Tesset looked at the envelope returned to her for the next first day.

“Lyra employs me.”

“I know.”

“If she asks whether I’m helping you?”

“What will you tell her?”

Tesset had been hoping Sera would supply a phrase that made the two things sit comfortably together. There was no such phrase on the table.

“That I am.”

Sera nodded. She did not thank her yet. They went back to the cost of meals.

At home Tesset found the girls making a boat from the broken lid of a flour box. The younger wanted to put it in the wash basin. The elder wanted to put it in the street gutter and follow it all the way to the harbor. Perron, home from the dyehouse with blue sunk into the creases of his hands, was explaining that the harbor proposal required at least one additional wheel.

“Boats haven’t got wheels,” the younger girl said.

“This one needs them between here and the water.”

Tesset let them use two scraps from her waste bundle as sails. The elder turned one over and began sounding out the beginning of a rejected letter.

“Not that one.” Tesset took it back.

“It’s a ship’s paper.”

“It’s a complaint about lamp oil.”

“That can be its ship’s paper.”

Nella laughed from the stair. Tesset found a blank scrap and surrendered it. For half an hour the boat occupied her table, and the household occupied every remaining inch of the room. She was hungry, tired, and unexpectedly glad of the noise.

Leda came after supper with the yellow scarf around her throat and a brown paper parcel under one arm. Inside were sheets that had been printed upside down and could not be sold. She and the children made a second sail before Nella called them upstairs.

When they were alone, Leda loosened the scarf and came to stand between Tesset’s knees.

“You look as though you spent the day persuading a door to open.”

“Several doors. One was a man.”

“Did he open?”

“Not yet.”

Leda put her hands against Tesset’s face and kissed her. Tesset caught the back of her skirt, keeping her there for another kiss. The room still smelled of supper and the paste Leda used at work. A little of it had dried beneath one thumbnail. Tesset pressed her mouth to the fingertip and made Leda laugh.

“I’ve been thinking about that room by the rope market,” Tesset said.

“The one with the narrow bed?”

“A bed can be changed.”

Leda sat beside her. “You’ve been thinking a great deal, then.”

“It has a door the girls wouldn’t mistake for their own.”

“And a window into somebody’s kitchen.”

“Only if you stand on the bed.”

Leda leaned her head on Tesset’s shoulder. They sat for a while without settling anything. Upstairs, Perron dropped a shoe, and one child announced that it had struck the boat.

“Come and look again on rest day,” Tesset said.

“All right. I’ll look.”

Tesset reached for her hand. The small wage envelope lay in the locked chest beneath the window. She had counted its contents before supper. For the first time since bringing it home, she stopped thinking about it.

Chapter 3 The fitting

The seventh day without the hoist began with a sack of meal bursting over Lyra’s boots.

She had warned the men that the stair turn was too tight to take two sacks abreast. They had tried it anyway, reasonably enough, since the first six pairs had gone through. The seventh caught on a broken nail. A pale stream ran from the seam, widening as the porter shifted his grip.

“Down,” she said. “Set it down.”

He tried to lift it clear instead. The sack opened from the nail to its bottom corner.

For a moment everybody watched the meal fall. Then the other porter lowered his own load carefully out of reach of it.

“I told you,” Lyra began, and stopped. The man with the torn sack was red to his ears. He had heard her the first time. Hearing it again would not put the meal back.

“Get a clean sheet. We can save what’s on the upper steps.”

“What about that?” He pointed to the little heap beside her boot.

She looked down. Water from a leaking roof channel had made a dark crescent around it.

“Keep it separate.”

She helped lift the unbroken sack. Her shoulder pulled, but held. At the salt-level landing, Esten was waiting with the rejected replacement fitting in both hands.

“No,” he said before she asked.

“You haven’t tried it.”

“I’ve measured it.”

“They said it could be bored.”

“It can. Then there won’t be enough left to carry the shaft.”

The pumpwright had the habit of pushing his front teeth against his lower lip when he was angry. Lyra had learned that he could continue doing useful work in that state for several hours. She hoped to direct some of it at a supplier.

“What will fit?”

He gave her a folded drawing. There were two versions: one showing the dimensions he needed, one showing the marks a fool should look for if the dimensions frightened him.

“Revet cast three last season. Same shaft size. I saw them on his rack.”

“He says they’re gone.”

“Then ask where.”

Lyra took the drawing. Behind them the porters began spreading a sheet beneath the spilled meal.

“What will it cost to keep carrying by hand?”

Esten glanced at the stair. “How long do you want to do it?”

She went upstairs without answering.

Revet’s shop stood behind the bronze founders’ yard, where the furnace heat had made the wall too warm for the damp to keep its grip. A line of customers waited beneath an awning. Lyra stood behind a woman holding two broken hinges, listening to the blows inside.

The woman looked at Lyra’s boots.

“Mill?”

“Bad sack.”

“I’ve got a good one at home with a bad husband inside it, if you’d like an exchange.”

Lyra smiled. The woman showed her the hinges. Her husband had hung a door before fitting its lower support, then blamed the hinges when it fell. She intended to make him watch while somebody explained which part of the operation required a wall.

By the time Revet called Lyra in, the smile had survived longer than her patience.

He saw her and looked toward a side door. It was a small movement. She would not have noticed it if she had not already decided to pay attention.

“I sent word,” he said.

“You sent a refusal. I have a measurement.”

“I don’t need the measurement. I know the piece.”

She put Esten’s drawing on his bench. Revet was a heavy man with a narrow face that seemed to belong to someone else. Heat had reddened his forearms to the elbow. Behind him, half-finished bronze sleeves stood on a rack beside several wrapped parts ready for dispatch.

“Esten says you have three.”

“Esten saw three. They are sold.”

“To whom?”

“I don’t discuss other customers’ work.”

“Then offer to buy one back.”

“I’ve offered you a new casting.”

“In six weeks.”

“Five, if the metal comes.”

Lyra took a wrapped piece off the bench. Revet’s hand closed on it before hers had finished moving.

“That isn’t yours.”

“I can see the mark.”

The wrapping had shifted enough to reveal the little dispatch seal. Salt Ward navigation works. She had been looking at it all week on the paper Daven brought.

“Are they rebuilding the whole canal?” she asked.

“You would need to ask them.”

“Do they pay you well enough to leave everyone else waiting?”

“They pay on delivery.”

“So do I.”

He moved the piece behind him. “Then you can pay when I deliver. Five weeks.”

Outside, someone called for help lifting a mold. Revet looked toward the sound with visible relief, but Lyra remained between him and the door.

“Come to the Opera House this afternoon,” she said. “There may be something you want to discuss privately.”

“I’ve said what I have.”

“Your brother hasn’t.”

He became very still.

The furnace continued its steady rush beyond the wall. Lyra could hear the woman with the hinges telling somebody that she intended to bring the entire door next time.

“Which brother?” Revet asked.

“Ansel.”

He let go of the wrapped fitting. She saw the mark of his fingers stay in the soft paper.

“After third bell,” she said. “Ask Tesset to show you up.”

She had kept the Gallery papers in two locked chests and a shallow iron-bound case that fitted beneath her own bed. The case held the files she might need quickly. Revet’s was in the second chest.

Lyra took it out before lunch. The room had grown warm in the sun, and she opened the window a hand’s width despite the noise from the receiving court.

The file was not long. A servant’s description, two letters, a report from a Court house, and a record of the payment made to a man who had found Ansel after he attempted to leave. The letters were in Ansel’s hand. Revet had supplied the money and a borrowed name for the passage. Both details had been underlined by someone in the Gallery.

She had read worse things in those chests. She had learned to put them down between meals and finish what was on her plate. This file had no pictures. Its useful part lay in the address of a room Revet continued to pay for, and in the servant’s mark beside a declaration that he had returned willingly.

The mark was cramped. The letters above it were not.

Lyra closed the file when Tesset brought her lunch.

“Revet’s coming,” she said.

“He refused again?”

“He may have reconsidered.”

Tesset set the plate on the clear side of the desk. “Sera sent the notice about the attendants’ pay.”

“I saw it.”

“Dorel wants to know where to send the next care account.”

“Here, as before.”

“And to Sera?”

“If she wants to pay him for copying it, I won’t object.”

Tesset remained by the desk. Lyra opened the lid of the little bowl beside her bread. Stewed onions, still warm, with a piece of fish beneath. She had forgotten how hungry the morning had made her.

“Anything else?” she asked.

“The new pay begins tomorrow.”

“Good. We can stop finding it from the cargo money.”

Tesset’s expression altered, almost imperceptibly. Lyra disliked the feeling that some part of the conversation had happened beyond her reach.

“Send Revet straight up,” she said.

He arrived early. There was soot along one cheek, as though he had wiped a hand across his face and forgotten it. He refused a chair, then sat when Lyra set the file where he could see the name.

“I can make the piece,” he said. “I told you that.”

“I need one this week.”

“You can’t ask me to break another undertaking.”

“You have three. Take back one.”

“They’re paid for.”

“I’ll pay what it costs to return the money.”

“You think that is all they lose?”

“No. I think it’s the objection you’ve given me.”

He looked at the file. Lyra opened it to the first letter and let him read the opening line. His face changed with recognition before he put his hand over the words.

“Where did you get that?”

“The Gallery.”

“They said the records were being given over.”

“Some were.”

Revet’s hand remained on the page. The heat had gone from his face.

“What do you want?”

“The bearing sleeve, delivered tomorrow. Esten will inspect it before payment.”

“And this?”

“It stays here.”

“Give it to me.”

“No.”

He pressed harder, wrinkling the paper. Lyra moved her hand onto its lower edge.

“Don’t damage it.”

He withdrew his fingers as though the page had become hot.

“My brother works for me now,” he said. “He doesn’t belong to that house.”

“Then you won’t want this sent back with a question about the address.”

Revet stood. The chair scraped against the floor so loudly that someone paused in the passage outside.

Lyra waited. Her pulse had quickened, though nothing in the room had moved toward her. She could see him considering the desk, the window, the distance to the door. Then his attention fell to her right arm, held a little carefully beside the file.

For a moment she wanted him to try.

He put both hands flat on the chair back instead.

“Tomorrow,” he said.

“Before noon.”

“And you will pay.”

“On acceptance. Full price, including the returned advance.”

He left without touching the paper again. Lyra waited until his steps had reached the stair, then smoothed the crease he had made. The file went back into the chest. She locked it and returned to the lunch that had cooled beside her elbow.

The fitting came in a handcart with Revet’s stamp on the wrapped end. He did not come with it. Two shop men waited while Esten measured it, checked for a casting flaw, and took it over to the shaft.

Lyra had expected to spend the morning being pleased. Instead Esten found that the old support had worn unevenly and required packing before the new sleeve would sit true. The carts outside multiplied. Rain began while they were taking the shaft’s weight.

“Hold there,” Esten said.

Lyra leaned on the long timber with three porters. The shaft lifted a finger’s breadth. Another man slid the packing beneath the seat.

“More at your end.”

Her shoulder burned. She shifted her grip lower, using her weight instead of pulling. The new endurance in her body made the labor possible long after it should have driven her off the timber. It did nothing to spare the old injury. She felt both things distinctly, the depth of strength and the narrow place where it failed.

“Now?” she asked.

“Hold.”

Rain struck the roof channel. The sleeve slid home with an abrupt soft click that she felt through the timber.

Esten lifted his hand. They lowered together.

The first turn was empty. Lyra watched the shaft, expecting a jump at the worn seat. It revolved cleanly. They tried a load of broken iron, then a pallet of stone, then the first sacks waiting under canvas. At each trial Esten stopped the hoist to feel the warmed metal and look beneath the seat.

At last he stepped away.

“It will work.”

The porters cheered him with a degree of mockery that made him wipe his face and threaten to charge them for applause. Lyra laughed. She had been standing in rain for half an hour and had meal paste drying around the seams of her boots. The first proper load descending toward the salt stores was a pleasure so large and plain that she let herself watch the whole of it.

“Get the men something hot,” she told Tesset. “All of them. The shop men too.”

Tesset had brought a cloth to keep the payment papers dry. She laid it over the wheelbarrow’s handles and counted out Revet’s money there. One shop man signed. The other stood watching the shaft.

“Fine piece,” he said.

“It is,” Lyra agreed.

He looked at her, then back at the machine. Whatever he had intended to add stayed unsaid.

Ada found Lyra at the kitchen table with her boots under a bench and both hands wrapped around a bowl. Marda had sent enough stew for the repair crew and included a second pot for the people who would certainly arrive when they smelled it.

“Have some,” Lyra said. “We have bread somewhere.”

“I’ve eaten.”

“Then sit.”

Ada sat opposite her, moving a child’s abandoned cup out of the way. She had washed her face, but the skin around her nails was reddened from lye. A damp mark crossed her skirt where she had carried a bundle against it.

Lyra put the bowl down.

“What is it?”

“A woman has seen him.”

The kitchen noise seemed to move farther away. Lyra looked toward the passage before she could stop herself. Ada saw it.

“No. She hasn’t brought him with her.”

“I didn’t mean—”

“I know.”

Ada put a folded scrap on the table. It held a rough drawing of a sleeve: three narrow repairs across an elbow, a patch at the cuff.

“Isen washes at the Shingle Steps. On the navigation canal, beyond the busy landings. She came to the boiling yard because her copper split. We were waiting for space. She saw the sleeve I was mending and asked where I’d learned to patch along the old stitches.”

Lyra looked at the drawing. “Jorin’s?”

“Mine. I did that to his work shirts too. You can mend the same tear more than once if you don’t cut the old cloth away.”

She touched the lowest mark with a reddened finger.

“He tore this one carrying timber. Then again on a pin. I put cream cloth over it because that was what I had. Three strips by the time he went missing. The cuff was dark. A triangle. I made it from the corner of an apron.”

“Did she see his face?”

“She said a man who fits what I told her. But she saw the sleeve properly. She took it off him to wash blood out. He would give her one arm and not the other.”

Lyra picked up the drawing. “When?”

“Last month. She doesn’t remember the day. She takes washing when their loads come in. Sometimes they bring the men ashore to change, sometimes only bundles.”

“Which boat?”

“She doesn’t know. She doesn’t go down to the water.”

“Which crew?”

“She doesn’t know that either.”

Ada’s voice had sharpened. Lyra set the paper down.

“I’m trying to learn what she can tell us.”

“So was I.”

“I know.”

Ada looked at the stew. Lyra pushed the bread toward her without speaking. After a while Ada took a piece and broke it in two.

“I want you to see her,” she said.

“I will.”

“Not to send a man who asks for a name she hasn’t got and comes back saying it was useless.”

“I’ll go myself.”

“She won’t be there until her copper is mended. She comes back on washing day next week.”

“Then next week.”

Ada ate the bread. Lyra felt the day’s satisfaction still present in her body, awkwardly beside the tightness that had followed the words seen him. A working hoist. A man alive somewhere. Neither made the other smaller, but she could not find a way to speak from both at once.

“Do you need somebody to take a shift?” she asked.

“I’ve arranged it.”

“Passage money?”

“I can reach the steps.”

Lyra picked up her spoon and laid it down again. “I was going to come tomorrow.”

“You still can. The latch is sticking.”

“I can look at it.”

“I meant you may need to push.”

They both laughed, briefly and with some effort. Ada took another piece of bread.

When she had gone, Lyra made a copy of the sleeve on a clean sheet. She sent a porter to learn whether the Shingle Steps remained open to ordinary foot traffic, with instructions to ask nothing about Jorin. Then she put the drawing in her coat, where it would travel with her instead of waiting among the other papers.

That night the sound of the repaired hoist continued beyond supper. Each descending load brought a rattle and a pause, then voices from the salt level telling the men above that they could send the next. Lyra lay with her sore shoulder supported by a folded blanket and listened until the last load had gone.

Chapter 4 The brother

Revet had charged twice for the cart.

Tesset found the second charge while checking the week’s payments. She had accepted it in the rain, with Lyra telling everyone to eat and the shop men waiting for their money. The original delivery had included a cart. The revised sum included another.

She hoped it was a mistake. She hoped, less kindly, that Revet would be embarrassed enough not to make her explain it at length.

The foundry yard was empty when she arrived. Heat still came through the shop wall, but the furnace doors were closed. A man sat at the bench sorting little bronze offcuts into wooden trays.

“Revet?” Tesset asked.

“He’s at the casting house.”

“When will he be back?”

The man looked at the strip of daylight beneath the outer door. “Before they close. Did you come from the Opera?”

“Yes.”

His hands stopped moving.

Tesset held up the folded account. “I need a correction.”

He stood too quickly and caught the tray with his sleeve. Several pieces fell onto the floor. He bent to collect them, then stood again without picking up the last one.

“Is it the fitting?”

“No, the delivery charge.”

“I can get him.”

“If he’ll be back soon, I can wait.”

“No. I can get him.”

He crossed to the door, remembered she was still there, and came back. Tesset watched his confusion with growing discomfort. He was a grown man, older than she was, with a patch of prematurely white hair above one ear. Nothing in the account justified this alarm.

“I don’t need him fetched,” she said. “It can wait until tomorrow.”

“I’ll write it down.”

He took a scrap from beside the scales. When he reached for the pen, Tesset saw that his fingers had settled into a cramped curl. He opened them against the bench before taking it.

“What’s your name?” she asked.

He looked up at her.

“So I can say who took the message.”

“Ansel.”

“Thank you. I’m Tesset.”

“I know.”

She put the account down and showed him both charges. His eyes moved readily through the figures. Whatever else frightened him, he knew the business.

“One was for the first journey,” he said. “To the canal store.”

“We didn’t order a journey to the canal store.”

“The part was already there. Revet took it back. He had to hire a cart to bring it from their shed before he could send it to you.”

“Oh.”

She sat on the nearest stool to look again. Her error was clear enough once somebody supplied the missing journey. Revet had written recovery, and she had read delivery through a water stain.

“That’s my mistake. I’ll correct our copy.”

Ansel remained standing. He had not put the pen down.

“You aren’t taking anything else?” he asked.

“No.”

“Because the other two have gone. They came for them yesterday. There aren’t any more.”

“I came about a cart.”

He nodded several times. Tesset wished she had not sounded irritated. She folded the account and rose.

At the door he said, “Does she know where I sleep?”

Tesset turned back.

“Lyra?”

He nodded.

“I don’t know.”

“There’s a room in the papers. I don’t sleep there now.”

She waited. He looked at the floor, where the bronze offcut still lay.

“I haven’t for a long time.”

“Do you want me to tell her that?”

“No.”

The answer came so sharply that she put the account back into her bag just to give her hands something to do.

“All right,” she said.

“Don’t tell her I asked.”

Tesset looked at the little trays, the tidy stacks of wrapped orders, the door through which Revet would return. She could leave. Ansel seemed to want her to leave and want her to stay long enough to say something he could believe.

“I won’t tell her where you sleep,” she said.

“I haven’t told you.”

“No. You haven’t.”

He sat. She picked up the last offcut and put it in the nearest tray. He moved it to another after she had turned away.

Lyra had moved a table into the main office so that the men waiting for payment could sit without occupying the stairs. The new table was too wide for the room. People had to pass sideways behind Tesset’s chair, and every passage disturbed her elbow.

When Tesset returned, Lyra was trying to fit a spare latch to its drawer.

“Did Revet give it back?”

“There was nothing to give back. I read the charge wrong.”

Lyra looked pleased. “Then he was honest about something.”

“What did you use?”

The latch remained in Lyra’s hand. “For what?”

“To get the fitting.”

“An inducement.”

“Ansel was in the shop.”

Lyra put down the latch.

Tesset had spent the walk home trying to find a way to ask without telling Lyra what Ansel had asked her to keep. Now the latch was still, and Lyra was waiting.

“What did he say?” Lyra asked.

“Enough to make me ask you.”

“The Court kept something it should not have kept. His brother prefers it to remain here.”

“Did you give him that choice?”

Lyra leaned against the table. “We have the part. We paid for it. Revet still has his business.”

“And Ansel?”

“Still works in it, apparently.”

Someone entered with a bucket for the roof leak. Tesset moved aside. He put it near the window, tried one position, then another, watching for the drip. By the time he left, Lyra had resumed turning the latch in her hand.

“Are you finished?” she asked.

Tesset felt a sudden desire to be cruel. She could tell Lyra how the man had stood, how readily he had expected further harm. She could make the room uncomfortable for both of them and carry that discomfort home as evidence that she had tried.

“No,” she said. “But I’ve nothing more I can tell you.”

Lyra looked at her. Tesset sat down and corrected the cart entry.

For the rest of the afternoon they spoke about work. A damp shipment had to be turned out. Two men wanted advances for a new roof. The woman caring for Tarn needed someone to take the next evening. Lyra agreed to the advances, then went down herself when the injured man began pulling at his bandage.

Tesset could hear her voice through the open stairwell, patient in a way it had not been upstairs.

Near dusk, Lyra returned to ask whether Tesset wanted the small jar of pickled plums somebody had sent. She had remembered that Tesset liked them.

“Yes,” Tesset said.

Lyra gave her the jar. Neither mentioned the foundry.

On rest day, Tesset went to collect Leda from the bindery and found her standing on a table.

“Don’t look so frightened,” Leda said. “I’ve been up here before.”

She was reaching for a roll of cloth on the highest shelf. The table had been cleared except for a stack of boards at one end. Beneath it, a young apprentice watched with his hands raised in case catching her became necessary.

“Move the boards,” Tesset told him.

“She said leave them.”

“Leave them,” Leda repeated. “I’m coming down.”

She lowered the roll into Tesset’s arms, sat on the table’s edge, and slid off. Dust had whitened the knees of her skirt.

“You said you’d be finished,” Tesset said.

“I am. This was the last thing.”

The apprentice held out a folded sheet.

“Except that,” Leda admitted.

Tesset leaned against the door while Leda showed him how the inner fold had crept ahead of the outer one. It was a small difference, almost invisible until she set the edges together. The apprentice tried to pull them straight without opening the sheet, and Leda stopped him.

“You’ll tear it. Here. Begin again.”

He smoothed the paper out with a sigh large enough to fill the workshop. Leda smiled at Tesset over his head. Tesset had seen her finish mending a shirt, wash her hands, and then inspect the seam one last time before bed. She had not often watched her teach.

The apprentice folded again. This time the edges met, though a second crease remained beside the first. He looked at Leda, waiting for it to be rejected.

“That’s better,” she said. “Keep that one beside you while you do the next.”

He took it to the window. Leda went to wash her hands, rubbing at the paste around her nails with a stiff little brush. Tesset picked up one of the spoiled sheets from a basket beside the door. A bird had been printed over the beginning of a poem, so that its tail divided the first word.

“Can I take this?”

“For the girls?”

“For me. I like the bird.”

“Then take it. They’re kindling now.”

Tesset folded it carefully with the bird outside. When Leda came back, she touched the crooked edge and did not correct it.

“There used to be a woman here who could fold while talking about something else,” Leda said. “I’d watch her tell a whole quarrel, with all the voices, and the sheets would keep coming out square.”

“Where did she go?”

“Her brother’s shop. More pay, and she wanted to help him. I miss her.”

“Can you do it?”

“Sometimes. Until I notice myself doing it.”

Leda took off the apron and hung it from a peg. Beneath it she wore the blue dress Tesset liked, with new ties at the throat. Tesset had brought her the ribbon, but Leda had chosen what to do with it. Seeing it there made Tesset’s expectations for the afternoon rise foolishly and pleasantly.

They bought a shared pear on the way to the rope market. It was ripe enough that Leda had to hold Tesset’s wrist while she bit, keeping the juice from running down her sleeve. Tesset laughed and offered her the other side. For several streets they walked with their shoulders touching, moving apart only to let people pass.

The room at the rope market had already been let.

A woman in a loose blue dress met Tesset and Leda at the stair and told them so with the satisfaction of someone who had warned them not to wait. She had another room, however. Larger. Only a little more. If they wished to see it, they must decide before she went out.

They followed her up another flight. Tesset had brought enough money for a deposit. It made her bag feel heavier than its contents justified.

The second room had a good ceiling and a fireplace too narrow for anything except a careful fire. The floor sloped toward the door. Its one window overlooked the back of a tannery.

Leda opened the shutter, waited, and closed it again.

“The smell isn’t always like that,” the woman said.

“When is it different?” Leda asked.

“When the wind changes.”

“Does it change often?”

“I’m not in charge of the wind.”

Tesset walked to the fireplace. Someone had painted little red flowers along the shelf. She liked them despite the uneven stems. There would be room for her chest beside the bed, a table by the shutter, a second chair that no child would need for a harbor voyage.

“The lower room was cheaper,” she said.

“The lower room is let.”

Leda touched her elbow. “Let’s go.”

“I could ask whether she’d take less.”

“Don’t.”

The woman crossed her arms. Tesset felt foolish in front of her, then angry with Leda for letting it show. They came down the stairs in silence.

At the corner Leda stopped beside a stall selling hot chickpeas. She bought a paper twist and held it out. Tesset shook her head.

“I should have taken the first room,” Tesset said.

“Perhaps. But I wouldn’t have moved into it.”

Tesset looked at her.

Leda lowered the chickpeas. “I meant to tell you before we went in.”

“You said you’d look.”

“I did look.”

“You knew what I was asking.”

“Yes.”

A cart passed between them and the stall, forcing them against the wall. Tesset stood with her deposit money in the bag and watched two chicks of escaped steam rise from Leda’s paper.

“Is it me?” she asked.

Leda took a breath. “Some of it.”

That hurt more than the kindly answer Tesset had expected. She looked away.

“I want to see you,” Leda said. “I like staying. I want to wake beside you without thinking about the walk to work. But I don’t want every evening to be in the place you bring all this home.”

“All what?”

“The papers. The worries. People knocking because you know where somebody can be found. Your sister coming down to ask whether you can take the girls. You answer before you’ve decided whether you want to.”

“I thought a room elsewhere would change that.”

“It would change where we kept the bed.”

Tesset pulled the strap of her bag higher. She wanted to defend her sister, though she had complained about Nella’s intrusions often enough to make that defense unfair. She wanted to say that work would grow easier when the present trouble had passed. Leda had heard that before too.

“What do you want?” she asked.

“A room with light. My own table. Somewhere I can leave a sheet half-folded and find it there afterward.”

“You could have that with me.”

“Perhaps. I don’t want to promise it now.”

Tesset stared at the ground. Between two paving stones a little plant had escaped the sweepers. Somebody had crushed it under a wheel, and its smell came up as they stood there.

“Will you eat?” Leda asked at last. “They’re going cold.”

“No.”

“All right.”

They walked as far as the market fountain. Leda did not try to take her hand. Tesset had been dreading that she would, and found herself wounded that she did not.

At the fountain a man was selling cheap combs laid out on a cloth. Leda stopped to replace the one she had broken at the bindery. Tesset watched her choose, testing the teeth against her thumb. This ordinary delay, when Tesset’s own evening had altered so completely, made her want to walk away.

Instead she sat on the fountain edge. When Leda returned, she moved enough to make room.

“I thought you’d be pleased,” she said.

“I was pleased you wanted it.”

“That’s a very small consolation.”

“I know.”

Leda sat beside her. They finished the cold chickpeas together, dropping the skins into the paper. Tesset did not feel better quickly. Leda stayed while it took time.

Nella had left soup on the stove and gone upstairs. Tesset heated it while Leda mended the torn tie of her work apron. They ate at the small table. No papers lay there. Tesset had cleared them before going out, in the optimistic belief that they would return with something to celebrate.

“I could take a room alone,” she said.

“You could.”

“You needn’t sound so encouraging.”

Leda put her spoon down. “Would you like me to be miserable?”

“For a little while.”

Leda laughed, then stopped when she saw Tesset’s face. “I’m sorry.”

“No. I was trying to make a joke.”

“I know. It was almost one.”

Tesset laughed too, painfully at first. Then the laughter loosened something, and she leaned across the table until Leda took her hand.

“I don’t want you to stop coming,” Tesset said.

“I don’t want to stop.”

They sat with their hands between the bowls. Upstairs, the girls were arguing about which one had hidden a ribbon. The accusation changed sides twice before Perron entered and asked whether it was the ribbon tied around his thumb.

After supper Leda washed the bowls. Tesset dried them and put them away. Leda kissed the back of her neck while she was reaching for the shelf. Tesset turned into the kiss with more urgency than she intended. Leda held her against the table, one wet hand cold through the back of her dress.

“Stay tonight,” Tesset said.

“Yes.”

The answer did not make the room at the rope market theirs. She knew that. For a while she was able to stop asking it to.

Later, Leda lay asleep with one arm above her head. Tesset could see the dark crease left by ink at her wrist. She had meant to tell her about Ansel, then found that she did not want the man from the foundry in the bed with them. There would be other evenings. She turned onto her side and listened to Leda’s breathing until the sounds upstairs ceased.

The next first day, Sera counted the transfer pay into three separate packets while Tesset watched.

“The attendants,” she said. “The relief period. The handling preparation.”

“We haven’t hired all the handlers.”

“No. This covers the people who come to inspect and rehearse. They should not have to wait until the crystal moves to be paid for the time.”

Tesset checked each sum. There was enough for an ordinary morning’s work at the proposed rate, enough for meals, and very little for a prolonged argument at a locked door.

“Who gets paid if the movement is stopped?” she asked.

“Anyone who has done the work.”

“If Lyra sends them away?”

“The same.”

“And afterward?”

“We can offer some continuing work at the cutting house. We cannot promise everyone a new livelihood.”

Tesset nodded. Sera waited for her to finish the count, then took out a sheet authorizing her to administer the payments and receive reports. The wording gave Tesset no right to change Aria’s care or overrule the handlers about a dangerous load. It did give people an address at which to find her if Lyra refused them entry to the Opera House.

Tesset read that address twice.

“A room here?”

“Two afternoons a week. More if necessary. The owner has agreed.”

“Does he know what he has agreed to?”

“He has asked me that himself.”

Through the wall, a clerk was teaching somebody how to cut a pen. The pupil kept testing the point before the cut was finished. Tesset could hear the clerk’s patience diminish one instruction at a time.

“What does Seldo say?” Sera asked.

“I haven’t asked him the right question yet.”

“Which is?”

“What he’ll do if Dorel walks past him with the carriage.”

“Ask it soon.”

Tesset held the pen over the authority. She had handled independent wages already. She had answered questions, carried messages, corrected Lyra. This sheet would make those things easier for other people to rely on and harder for her to call incidental errands.

“Will you tell Lyra before I do?” she asked.

“I will send notice this afternoon. You may tell her yourself if you prefer.”

“No. Send it.”

She signed before she could decide that a more convenient day would come. Sera sanded the line, waited, then gave her one copy and kept the other.

Outside, Tesset went to a baker and bought a small cake with poppy seeds on top. She had intended to take it to Leda. At the corner she stopped, unwrapped it, and ate half herself. The rest she carried back to the Opera House with crumbs on her skirt and the three pay packets tied separately inside her bag.

Chapter 5 White spoil

Ada had brought the wrong sleeve on purpose.

She showed it to Lyra when they stopped beneath the arch before the Shingle Steps. It was folded inside a clean cloth, the elbow repaired with broad dark patches that bore no resemblance to the drawing.

“I want to hear what she says before I show her.”

“Before you show her what?”

“The other one.”

Ada took out a second piece of cloth. This sleeve was worn through at the shoulder, cut away from a shirt whose body had gone to other uses. Three narrow cream strips crossed the elbow. The cuff had been reinforced in the same dark material as the first sleeve, but its patch made a triangle.

“He had two shirts,” Ada said. “I mended them with what was left from the same things.”

Lyra touched the pale repair. The old cloth had been left beneath it, just as Ada had described. There were several generations of stitches.

“Will she mind being tested?”

“She might. I don’t mind finding out.”

They had walked from the Opera House in a fine rain that had become a wetness in the air rather than anything that fell. Ada carried the bundle beneath her shawl. Lyra’s coat had darkened along the shoulder seams. Farther inland the city would already be warming, but here the wind followed the navigation canal between high walls and found every opening in their clothes.

A cart passed with its wheels wrapped in old rope to quiet them. Beyond it the canal narrowed through a row of service landings. There were sheds, two workshops with their doors closed, and a drying yard where the empty lines sagged beneath drops of water.

Lyra had expected more traffic. The man she sent had reported that the footway was open and the Shingle Steps still used. He had also reported three guards at a landing with no goods on it.

Today there were two. They looked at Lyra without challenging her. One followed her progress with a hand resting against his belt.

Ada saw him too. She did not slow.

Isen’s wash yard lay above the steps, between a shed for charcoal and a house whose back door had been boarded over. Two tubs stood under a patched roof. A copper boiler had returned from repair with a bright new seam across its lower belly.

Isen was trying to set it onto its stand. She had put a brick beneath one foot and discovered too late that the brick tipped when she lifted the opposite side.

“Put your bundle there,” she told Ada. “Take that handle.”

Ada did. Lyra moved the brick and fetched a flatter one from beside the wall. Between the three of them they set the copper squarely enough to stop its rocking.

Isen put a little water in and watched the new seam.

“I paid for it not to leak,” she said. “Now I get to discover whether I paid enough.”

She was a broad woman with a low voice and a white line through one eyebrow. When she bent to inspect the repair, a row of wooden pegs fell out of her apron pocket. She picked them up one at a time, refusing Ada’s help with a motion of her hand.

“You came back,” she said.

“You told me to.”

“I tell a great many people things.”

Ada introduced Lyra. Isen looked at her face, then at the careful way she held the injured arm.

“The one at the Opera.”

“Yes,” Lyra said.

Isen waited for a title or an explanation. When neither came, she drew a bench away from the wall.

“Sit if you’re staying.”

They sat. Ada laid the broad-patched sleeve across her knees.

“You said it was like this.”

Isen leaned forward, then shook her head.

“No. It was narrow strips. You’ve shown me somebody else’s.”

“Another of his.”

“Then he mends them differently.”

“I mend them.”

Isen’s face changed. She looked from Ada to Lyra and back.

“I told you what I saw.”

“I needed to be certain,” Ada said.

“You could have asked again.”

“I am asking again.”

The copper began to tick as the small fire took beneath it. Isen got up to adjust the fuel. She stayed crouched longer than the task required. Lyra watched Ada fold the wrong sleeve, each movement precise with embarrassment and determination.

At last Isen returned.

“Show me the other.”

Ada did. Isen held it near the repaired copper, using the brighter light.

“Like this. The cuff more worn. There was another bit folded over here.”

She pinched the narrow point of the dark triangle.

Ada’s mouth opened. No sound came for a moment.

“He caught it,” she said. “The last week. I turned the point under because there wasn’t enough to sew.”

Isen handed the sleeve back. Ada held it against her lap with both hands.

“What did he look like?” Lyra asked.

“Gray. They all look gray by the time they’ve been out in it.”

“His hair?”

“Dark. Thin here.” Isen touched her temple. “Face longer than hers, if they’re related.”

“He’s my husband,” Ada said.

“Oh.”

Isen sat heavily. She had understood the inquiry before; now Lyra watched her understand the woman making it.

“He came ashore with two others,” she said. “One had blood down the front. This one had it only on his sleeve. They brought clean shirts in a bundle. The man with them told me to take the dirty ones, but yours wouldn’t raise his right arm. I took the left out and pulled the neck across.”

“Was he hurt?” Ada asked.

“I didn’t see a wound.”

“Did he speak?”

“Only when the man told him to lift.”

“What did he say?”

Isen looked at Lyra as though hoping somebody else could answer. “I don’t remember. Something about the work.”

Ada’s fingers tightened on the sleeve. Lyra could hear two taps in her own memory, Jorin’s old response in a passage she had left without calling to him. She kept her hands flat on her knees.

“Was there a name on the washing bundle?” she asked.

“A number. I don’t keep those after they pay.”

“Do the same boats come?”

“Some of them. Sometimes a different man brings the washing. Sometimes they don’t bring it at all.”

“Could you find out which boat brought that bundle?”

Isen frowned. “I can ask who else washed for them that week. People remember being paid late. I can’t promise they’ll remember a shirt.”

“Please ask,” Ada said.

“I will.”

Lyra reached for her purse. Isen saw the movement and shook her head.

“Pay me when there’s washing.”

“For your time.”

“I’ve been watching the seam. You haven’t stopped me.”

A bead of water appeared at the new join in the copper. All three women watched it swell. Then it ran down the side and hissed on the hot stand.

Isen swore. Ada rose, folded both sleeves into their cloth, and went to help her empty the boiler before the water warmed too far.

They left the wash yard together, but Ada stopped at the next turning.

“I want to go back.”

“You can.”

“Without you.”

Lyra had not expected the small hurt. She stepped out of the way of a man carrying poles.

“All right.”

“I frightened her. She thinks she’ll be promising me something if she speaks.”

“Would you like me to wait?”

“No. I’ll come by when I’ve finished.”

Ada went back with the bundle beneath her shawl. Lyra watched until she had entered the yard, then continued along the footway.

A pump sounded beyond the wall. She had heard it while Isen spoke and assumed it belonged to an ordinary repair. Now she could see the water running from its outlet into the navigation canal, a strong even stream that scarcely altered when the machine took its next stroke.

Lyra stopped.

She had paid Esten to nurse three worn pumps through a month of bad weather. She knew the cough of a leaking valve, the pause when a handler failed to keep the stroke even, the dribble of water that meant somebody had spent the morning working mostly for exercise. This machine drew and discharged with an expensive regularity.

Beyond the outlet, a second pump stood covered under a roof. It was not running. Its polished shaft and fresh bronze seat were visible from the footway. Someone had provided a spare good enough to replace a machine that did not appear to need replacing.

A man in an oilskin apron came out to inspect the discharge. Lyra asked what they were draining.

“Maintenance reach.”

“Which one?”

He pointed vaguely behind the wall.

“Must be a great deal of water.”

“Enough to keep us busy.”

“Are you widening the cut?”

“No.”

He went back through the gate. She heard a bolt settle on the other side.

The canal above the pumps carried a single empty scow. Two ducks had settled beside a mooring post, undisturbed by anything with a keel. Lyra walked on until she reached the loading apron where spoil was being tipped into a barge.

Mud struck the boards with a heavy wet slap. It carried broken brick, shells, dark timber, and pale stone in sharp pieces unlike the rounded rubble she was used to seeing dredged from a working harbor. One block turned as it fell. A clean cut face flashed through the dirt, white enough to catch the poor light.

The barge master stood beneath a short awning, trying to keep his meal dry. He watched Lyra looking at the stone.

“Ballast?” he asked.

“Perhaps.”

“You’ll have to take mud with it. I don’t sort.”

“Where’s it going?”

“Where I’m paid to put it.”

“An enviable arrangement.”

He grinned. Half his meal was lodged in one cheek. He swallowed before answering.

“Fill beyond the lower walls. Unless you’ve a better use.”

Lyra stepped onto the end of the loading plank. Nobody stopped her. A small chip lay where a barrow had spilled its edge. She picked it up and rubbed the wet dirt away with her thumb.

It was pale through the broken surface, fine-grained and heavy for its size. She knew nothing useful about stone beyond what happened when she asked it to hold a fastening. This did not look like the common dark foundation blocks along the canal.

“Is all of it like this?” she asked.

“Some loads. Some aren’t.”

“Could I have a look at the larger pieces?”

Before he answered, a clerk came down the apron carrying a leather case beneath his coat. He wore a broken red pen in the band of his cap.

Lyra put the chip in her palm and closed her fingers.

“This load is taken,” the clerk told the master.

“It was taken when I came. I’m moving it for them.”

“The waste. It has been purchased.”

“By whom?”

“The maintenance office.”

The master looked at the mud pouring into his barge, then at the gate from which the clerk had come.

“They’re buying their own mud?”

“They’re retaining the material for another use.”

“Then they can pay for moving it twice.”

The clerk’s mouth tightened. He took out a paper. Lyra saw his eyes pass over her hand.

“If you want stone,” he said, “there are proper yards.”

“I found a man who was willing to sell some.”

“He had no right.”

The master put down his meal. “I have the right written on my hire sheet. Waste at master’s disposal after accepted delivery. Ask whoever wrote it.”

The clerk looked at him with open dislike. He opened his case and counted out money.

Lyra watched the sum. It was more than the load could have earned as rough fill. The master counted too, then asked where the material was to go. The clerk gave him a new place inside the same works, beyond a barred cross-channel. The trip would take most of the afternoon and bring him almost back to where he had begun.

“Second hire,” the master said.

The clerk added money.

Lyra enjoyed the man’s persistence. She would have enjoyed it more if the clerk had not been watching her as carefully as he watched the coins.

“Will you need a paper for that?” he asked, nodding toward her closed hand.

She opened it. The chip was no larger than the end of her thumb.

“For this?”

“It belongs to the works.”

“Then I can pay.”

“The material isn’t for sale.”

“You’ve just bought it.”

The master barked a laugh and bent over his hire sheet. The clerk stepped closer.

Lyra let the silence stretch. Behind her, the footway remained open. Men were passing with ordinary loads. The clerk could insist on searching her coat for a piece of broken stone, and she could make him do it in front of them.

At last he said, “Keep your curiosity. It seems to cost us less.”

He turned away.
