Lyra put the chip in her coat, beside Ada’s drawing. As she left, another barrow came down the apron. The man pushing it had salt gathered thickly along the collar of his shirt. His face turned toward the sound of the pump, and for a moment his feet continued walking after the barrow had stopped.

The clerk struck its side with a stick. The man began moving again.

Ada was already at home when Lyra arrived that evening. She had brought in a small bundle of Isen’s washing to finish while the copper was repaired again.

“She knew another woman who takes their sheets,” Ada said. “She’ll ask her.”

“Did she remember anything else?”

“No.”

Lyra accepted the answer. Ada gave her a shirt to hold while she pulled the damp seam straight. They worked together beside the window until the cloth lay evenly across the line.

The sticking latch still caught when the door moved in a draft. Lyra examined it, found the swollen edge, and asked for a knife.

“You don’t have to mend everything in the room,” Ada said.

“Only this.”

“Last week it was the stool.”

“The stool still stands.”

Ada smiled. “It does.”

Lyra shaved a little wood from the door’s edge. She had learned enough from Berris to know how readily a small improvement could become a permanent gap. She stopped twice to test it. Ada stood at the wash line, watching with her hands beneath her apron.

“Do you think it was him?” she asked.

Lyra kept the knife still.

“Yes. I think it may have been.”

“That’s a careful answer.”

“It’s the one I have.”

Ada nodded. She took a shirt off the line and folded it though it was not dry.

“Sometimes I think I remember him better when I’m angry,” she said. “The little things. How he’d leave the basin with a skin on it. How he’d pretend he hadn’t heard me if somebody came to the door while he was eating.”

Lyra set the knife on the stool.

“When I’m trying to remember him kindly, I get the same few things. I can’t make the rest come.”

“Perhaps they’ll come when you aren’t trying.”

“Perhaps.”

Ada unfolded the shirt and hung it up again. Lyra returned to the latch. The next time she tried it, the door closed cleanly.

They ate bread and soft cheese while the evening light thinned. Ada told her about a customer who marked every sheet with such an elaborate knot that washing it took less time than untying the bundle. Lyra described the barge master’s insistence on a second hire. She left out the red pen and the Worn man at the barrow.

At the door Ada touched her good arm.

“Come next week even if there’s nothing.”

“I will.”

“And don’t bring a carpenter.”

Lyra glanced at the repaired latch. “You are very difficult to please.”

“I’ve been told.”

Tarn had been moved to a cool back room at Marda’s shore house. The surgeon had removed the worst of two damaged fingers. The remaining ones had begun to heal, though the hand was unlikely to close properly again.

Lyra went there the following afternoon with the care money and a packet of clean cloth. Marda met her in the yard, sleeves rolled above the changed skin at her wrists.

“He’s eaten,” she said. “Before you ask.”

“Did I look about to ask?”

“You’ve brought enough cloth to wrap the house. It generally means you’re worried about something else.”

Lyra followed her in. Tarn sat on a low bench beneath the shutter. A loose length of rope rested across his knees. It had no knot, hook, or weight on its end.

“He likes to have it,” Marda said. “We tried taking it away while the surgeon worked. He pulled at the bed instead.”

“Does he sleep?”

“Sometimes. Not when we tell him.”

Lyra sat beside him, leaving space between their shoulders. “Tarn.”

He turned his head a little. The movement might have followed the voice without attaching to the name. She showed him the clean cloth before touching the dressing.

Marda stayed in the doorway while she checked the hand. There was no new heat, no spreading redness. Lyra eased a stiff fold of bandage away from the skin and rewound it more loosely. Tarn watched the rope.

“Better?” she asked.

He lifted his arm, lowered it, and went on waiting.

They could not keep him in a room without anyone watching. They could not put him among working loads and expect him to protect the hand. Marda had arranged two women and a man who took turns, with a neighbor covering the gaps. Lyra looked through the hours and found one long interval on the following evening.

“I can come then.”

“You have a receiving boat.”

“Tesset knows how to receive a boat.”

Marda took the money. “Then come.”

In the kitchen, Jorad was cutting bread for people expected at an observance. The little carved cups stood drying near the window. Lyra recognized one with a shallow nick beside the rim. Maeron had dropped it and laughed at himself for being unable to carry a cup after raising half a wreck.

Jorad saw her looking.

“We still use it.”

“I can see.”

“You needn’t stay.”

“I hadn’t said I would.”

He nodded and continued cutting. His altered hands had thickened around the joints; the knife handle had been wrapped to make it easier to hold. Marda took a slice from the pile and ate it standing beside Lyra.

For a while none of them spoke. People arrived in the yard, greeting one another softly. One woman laughed as she complained about a leaking shoe. Another voice answered with Maeron’s name in it, affection plain enough to reach the kitchen.

Lyra wanted to go. She wanted to hear the rest. Both desires remained while Jorad set a cup beside the empty place at the table.

“I’ll be here tomorrow evening,” she told Marda.

“I heard you the first time.”

Outside, Lyra took the stone chip from her pocket. It had dried almost white. She wrapped it in a scrap of paper so it would not wear through the coat, then went back to the Opera House to put it in her desk.

Chapter 6 Relief

By the third independent pay day, Dorel had stopped asking whether the money would arrive.

He still counted it. Tesset would have been alarmed if he had not. He laid each coin on the little table, moved two aside for meals, then put the rest into a purse with a repaired drawstring.

Dorel set an eating-house slip beside the coins. “Senn paid for supper twice yesterday. The boy gave his bowl to the guard.”

“Which guard?”

“I didn’t ask. He was eating it.”

They had arranged those meals so that the Opera kitchens could no longer leave the attendants hungry. Four days without a mistake. Tesset turned the slip over and found the second payment, in Senn’s careful hand.

“I’ll speak to them,” Tesset said.

“Don’t make it hard for the boy. He carried the bowls all the way down.”

“I’ll speak to the woman who sends him.”

Dorel nodded. He returned to the sheet on which he recorded the condition of the supports. Tesset watched his pencil move, slow and certain. Through the inner screen she could see the crystal’s light. Avenna’s visit was not until the next day. There had been no prepared contact that morning.

Senn came in carrying a parcel for his father. The independent travel allowance had let him pay a neighbor to look in twice on days when he could not get away. He had also used some of his own wages to buy a chair with arms, and was looking for somebody to shorten one leg so it would stand square on an uneven floor.

“Ask Berris,” Tesset said.

“She’s working.”

“So are most people who can shorten a chair.”

“I don’t want to ask her for free.”

“Then tell her what you can pay.”

Senn considered this as though it were a more daring solution than she intended. He had grown accustomed to asking permission for favors and seemed less certain how to purchase one.

At the outer door, Seldo was turning away a woman in a plain leather coat. She stood with her hands visible, a folded paper between two fingers. Tesset recognized one of the counting house’s night guards.

“She’s with me,” Tesset said.

Seldo looked relieved, then wary of his relief. “You didn’t say you’d bring anyone.”

“She came to see the post.”

“What for?”

“The relief watch.”

His gaze went to the paper above his head. The old replacement order still stood there, its three signatures protected from the damp.

“There’s no relief here.”

“There is an order for it.”

“You know what I mean.”

Tesset knew exactly what he meant. She wished she could become angry enough to find it easy.

The woman in the leather coat lowered her paper. “I don’t need to go into the care room. I’d like to know where the guard stands and where the next watch reports.”

“Here,” Seldo said.

“And if the door is locked against us?”

“Then there won’t be a post to take.”

Tesset saw the woman study him. She had worked night watch in a counting house for years. She knew what a locked door did to instructions better than any of them.

“Where do we report if that happens?” she asked Tesset.

“The counting house. To me or Sera. You are paid for attending, whether he admits you or not.”

Seldo’s face changed at the word he. Tesset had made him the obstruction in a sentence spoken while standing close enough to touch his glove.

“That’s not fair,” he said.

“No,” Tesset said. “It isn’t a fair place to stand. I’m asking what will happen.”

He looked past her toward Dorel. The attendant was waiting with his purse in one hand.

“I’ll speak to Lyra,” Seldo said.

The guard nodded once and returned her paper to her coat. She had learned what she came to learn. Tesset went with her to the foot of the freight stair, where there was enough room for other people to pass.

“If there are handlers,” the woman said, “keep their route clear of anyone who expects us to push through with a sword. My partner and I can hold a watch. We aren’t a company.”

“We know.”

“Make sure the handlers know.”

Tesset took out her little book. “Say that again.”

The woman did, waiting while Tesset found space beside the handlers’ names.

Berris was repairing a rolling frame in the upper workshop. A boy stood beside her holding a candle in daylight, trying to put the light into a crack that vanished whenever he moved.

“Not there,” Berris said. “The other side. No, your other side.”

Tesset waited until she set the tool down.

“Can you look at the north route this afternoon?”

“I’ve looked at it.”

“With the proposed handlers.”

“Have they been paid to propose?”

“They will be paid to attend.”

“That’s better.”

She sent the boy to fetch a straightedge and showed Tesset the wheel she had taken off the frame. One side of the wooden rim had swollen, making it turn unevenly.

“This is why I want the empty carriage pieces checked again. Things sit. Wood takes water. Then someone remembers that it was sound when they last looked.”

“The crystal’s carriage has been watched every day.”

“And will be watched on the day it moves. I’m talking about the temporary gear.”

Tesset nodded. “The broad stair?”

“For access and a spare way if we need to shift gear. Aria is already down in the measuring room. We’re not hauling her up three flights to prove we can haul her down again.”

“I know.”

“Tell anyone who wants to rehearse with the weight.”

They agreed to inspect the stacked rails and the landing clearances with an empty frame. The actual route from the measuring room to the raised canal landing would be checked separately, then the loading onto the hired vessel. The old quarry crane remained excluded. Berris had written that in large enough letters that Tesset could read it while the sheet lay upside down.

“Who says when the preparation is good enough?” she asked.

“The people doing it. Dorel for the supports. The boatmaster for the boat. Me for what I’ve built. None of us for all of it.”

“And if Lyra says stop?”

Berris set the wheel on the bench. The boy had come back with the straightedge and was waiting to be told where to put it. Berris took it from him, thanked him, and sent him to the yard for two dry wedges.

“Then she’ll have to say why.”

“She has said why.”

“Then she’ll have to say it while we’re there.”

“Will you stop?”

She looked at the repaired frame. “If someone draws a blade in front of that crystal, I will. I won’t have them break her while we argue about whose turn it is to give orders.”

“That wasn’t what I meant.”

“It’s part of what you asked.”

Tesset had hoped for a firmer answer. She was learning how many people could agree on the thing that should happen and still need different things before they would do it.

Berris picked up the wheel and set it into place. “This afternoon. Send them after the meal.”

The handlers came in a group of four and brought their own lifting slings.

Tesset had told them to bring only themselves. They had chosen to bring something that made the visit look like work. She understood the impulse after the first guard at the receiving arch asked who had hired them.

“The transfer fund,” she said.

“Whose fund?”

“Sera Vesper’s administration. Under the council order.”

He scratched the side of his face. “Lyra said no additional men for the salt level without her knowing.”

“They’re inspecting a route. They aren’t joining the salt crews.”

“Does she know?”

“Notice was sent.”

“That’s different.”

Behind Tesset, one handler shifted the sling on his shoulder. She heard the first little movement of doubt in the group. A man had left paid work elsewhere for the afternoon. Another would have to be home before dark. None had come to spend hours explaining who Sera was.

“Fetch Berris,” she said.

The guard looked unhappy, but Berris was an ordinary enough request. He sent a boy. While they waited, Tesset paid the first half of the agreed attendance money in the archway. The handlers counted it openly.

Berris arrived carrying a length of rail on one shoulder.

“Those are mine,” she told the guard, nodding at the men. “Come and carry something.”

They went through.

Tesset followed, angry that Berris’s familiar face had accomplished what the written order could not. She was also grateful. By the time they reached the upper landing, both feelings had become useful enough to set aside while she wrote down the time lost at the gate.

They took the empty rolling frame down the broad north freight stair. Its three short flights needed a change of grip at each turn. Berris made them stop at the middle landing and describe what they would do if the lower pair lost their footing. They tried the answer with no load on the frame. The upper men held, but a sling slipped over a projecting bolt.

“Again,” Berris said.

Tesset stood out of the way and watched the difference between a good answer and a piece of rope behaving badly. The second attempt worked. The third was slower because the men were beginning to tire, and Berris made them stop before she explained the point she wanted them to keep.

“On the day, nobody proves he’s strong. If you need the next man, ask for him.”

At the raised canal landing, Dorel came out to look at the frame. He did not bring Aria. She remained in the measuring room on her supported carriage, with Senn inside and the guard at the outer door.

The distance from that door to the loading place was short enough to look easy until somebody set an actual frame across it. A raised sill caught a wheel. Berris marked where the temporary bridge would sit. One of the handlers found a loose stone where the aft line would be led. He knelt and pulled it out with both hands.

“Somebody could have told us that,” he said.

“You’ve told us now,” Berris answered. “Put it where no one steps on it.”

The man set it against the wall. Tesset wrote down its position, then crossed the note out when Berris moved it again.

No one had asked Aria a question during the inspection. Tesset could hear the low sound from inside whenever the door opened. She had the records of earlier contacts in her bag: dates, safeguards, one held note for the journey, two for remaining. Another contact would come before a day was fixed. The papers had not grown less clear while they waited for people to become able to act on them.

When the inspection ended, the handlers stayed to talk about the return journey and the hours they would lose coming back from the cutting house. Tesset paid them for the full time. One asked whether there would be food aboard. She said yes and put a mark beside it large enough to catch her eye later.

Seldo watched them leave.

“You’ve put a great deal into this,” he said.

“Other people have too.”

He rubbed his thumb along the repaired glove. The stitches had begun to pull apart.

“If she tells me to shut the door, what do you expect?”

Tesset turned to him. “That’s what I’ve been trying to ask you.”

They went to the little open space beside the stair so the next attendant could pass without hearing every word. Seldo stood against the wall. Tesset had worked with him long enough to recognize the posture he used when he intended to look immovable while making room for someone carrying a tray.

“She kept this place,” he said. “You were here.”

“I was.”

“They’d have put people out. There were men ready to do it.”

“I know.”

“And now three people sign a paper.”

“They signed the charter before she took the post. The replacement rule hasn’t changed.”

He shook his head. “I’m not a clerk.”

“You don’t need to be. Dorel will be there. Senn. Berris. People you’ve eaten with. If Lyra tells you to pull the carriage back, will you put your hands on them?”

“I won’t have anyone hurt.”

“Then what will you do?”

“I’ll talk to her.”

“If she’s standing there telling you to do it?”

He looked toward the measuring room. Senn had come out to fetch a fresh cloth and was waiting for a porter to finish at the water jar. The porter said something that made him smile. Seldo watched them with an expression Tesset could not read.

“Does Aria know who these new guards are?” he asked.

“She’ll be told before the move. The contact will be witnessed.”

“Does she know Lyra’s afraid of what happens afterward?”

“She’s heard Lyra.”

Seldo leaned his head against the wall and closed his eyes. He looked tired. Tesset remembered how eagerly he had taken the watch when the Opera House first became theirs to keep, how pleased he had been by a proper coat and the authority to send trouble away from a door.

“I can’t promise you what a day will be like before it comes,” he said.

“No. But don’t make Dorel discover it when he’s got both hands on the carriage.”

His eyes opened. For a moment she thought he would answer. Instead he asked whether the independent watch would keep his place for him if Lyra dismissed him.

“There may be work,” Tesset said. “I can’t promise the same post.”

“Then you see.”

“Yes. I see.”

He nodded, relieved by her agreement, and she had to continue before he mistook it.

“I’ve signed to carry the pay. If she dismisses me, I’ll keep doing that until someone else takes over. Sera has a room where people can find me.”

“You’ve already signed?”

“Yes.”

“She won’t like it.”

“She knows.”

That was not entirely the answer to what he meant. Lyra had received the notice. She had said nothing beyond a question about whether Tesset intended to charge twice for her afternoon. Tesset had answered no. The rest waited between them.

Seldo looked at her hand around the strap of her bag.

“I’ll talk to the men,” he said.

“Ask them the question.”

“I heard it.”

She left him there. At the upper landing, she stopped long enough to let her own hands cease shaking before she went into the office.

Lyra was away when Tesset finished work. She had kept her promise to sit with Tarn at the shore house and left instructions about a boat arriving near dusk. Tesset received it, argued over a missing sack, found the sack beneath an awning, and sent the relieved master away with his copy of the weight accepted.

It would have been easy to stay another hour. There were always things that became easier if somebody remained. Tonight she put the unfinished work in the drawer and locked it.

At home, Perron had taken the girls out to buy winter shoes. Nella sat alone upstairs with a sewing basket and the exhausted expression of a woman whose quiet hour had arrived too late to know what to do with it.

Tesset climbed the last few steps and sat beside her.

“No work?” Nella asked.

“Plenty.”

“Did they send you away?”

“Not yet.”

Nella looked up sharply. Tesset told her enough to explain why there might be a change in wages, and stopped before turning the whole evening into a meeting.

“Can you pay for your room?” Nella asked.

“Yes.”

“For how long?”

“A while. I’ve kept the deposit I was going to use.”

Nella put down the shirt she was mending. She had heard about the room by the rope market and been more offended by Tesset’s wish to leave than Tesset had expected.

“You can stay,” she said.

“I pay you to stay.”

“I meant if it goes badly.”

Tesset looked at her sister. Nella’s face was flushed from sitting near the stove; a loose strand of hair had caught in the button at her throat. Tesset reached over and freed it.

“Thank you.”

Nella resumed sewing. “I would still want something toward the food.”

“I know.”

They sat until the children returned, each wearing one new shoe and carrying the other. Perron said the system had emerged from negotiations and he did not recommend reopening them.

After supper, Tesset went downstairs and copied the day’s practical changes for Sera. A bridge over the sill. The loose stone removed. Food for the return journey. Names and hours of the handlers who had come. She left a space for Seldo’s answer and did not invent one to fill it.

At the bottom she wrote the place where the replacement watch would report if barred from the Opera House. Then she added her own name as the person who would meet them.

She folded the paper for morning. Leda had not come; she was finishing a binding order and had said she would sleep in her own room. Tesset washed, put out the lamp, and lay down in the bed with room on both sides of her.

She was nearly asleep when she remembered that she had not set the little clock’s striker. Tomorrow there would be another contact, another payment, and people expecting her at the counting house. She got up, set it, and put it where she would have to rise to silence it. Then she climbed back beneath the blanket and drew its cold edge under her feet.

Part Two The departure

Chapter 7 The same answer

The red thread had been tied too tightly around the packet of gloves. Lyra worked at the knot with her left thumbnail, then put the packet on Dorel’s table and cut it.

“Those are for the spare box,” he said.

“The thumb has gone in mine.”

“I could mend it.”

“Then there would be a mend against the crystal.”

He took the old gloves from her without answering. Beyond his shoulder, Senn was warming a covered cup in a bowl of water. The room smelled faintly of thyme. They had begun keeping their food there since the kitchen sent down a meal so late that both attendants had gone through the afternoon without eating. Lyra had spoken to the cook. The bowls and the little kettle had stayed.

She opened the new gloves, checked the seams, and laid them on the table. There was fresh chalk beside the carriage wheels. Two parallel lines ran toward the outer doors, stopped short of the sill, and began again on the canal landing.

“Who marked those?”

“Berris.” Dorel laid a clean cloth over the gloves. “She wanted to check the width.”

“She checked it before.”

“The wheel shoes have been altered since.”

“By whom?”

“By Berris.”

Dorel had learned to answer a question exactly. Lyra remembered a time when he would have given her the maker of the iron, the hour of the alteration, and three reasons it could not be blamed on him. She did not like the new restraint. She liked less that he looked healthier with it.

Avenna arrived carrying her writing case and a small green apple. She set the apple on the windowsill, where its shadow fell across the list of temperatures Senn kept. Sera followed her. The Coin scholar had come directly from the street; a narrow line of wet ran around the hem of her coat, although the morning had been dry when Lyra descended.

“Rain?” Lyra asked.

“Someone washing a balcony.”

Sera looked at the chalk and then at Dorel. It was a glance Lyra might have missed if she had still been fighting with the thread.

“Where is Katerina?”

“With Callista,” Avenna said. “She won’t come to this contact.”

“She arranged it.”

“I arranged it. We changed the day because Aria was tired yesterday.”

Senn moved the warmed cup out of the way. He had already put the board beside the carriage, within Avenna’s reach. The day’s questions were written in a large plain hand with a space beneath each. Lyra read them. Identity, visible object, a reversed question, a description of the cutting house, the journey, the people who would attend it. Nothing she had not heard before.

At the bottom, below a line drawn to separate it from the questions, stood an instruction: stop on a failed check, an unanswered question, or Dorel’s judgment of strain.

“You have added a guard,” Lyra said.

“A prospective escort,” Sera answered. “His name won’t be asked today. Aria first needs to agree to hearing the proposal.”

“Corven.”

“Yes.”

She looked at Sera. “He said he was leaving this work.”

“He left your employment.”

Avenna shifted the board half a hand’s breadth away from Lyra, enough to put it in line with the light. “We ought to begin while she’s rested.”

Lyra sat on the stool Dorel had placed for her. The right arm was troublesome that morning, not sharply painful, but heavy from shoulder to wrist. She had helped carry a replacement door the previous afternoon and continued holding it after Seldo told her that he had the weight. She could still feel the pressure of its edge across her palm. She set that hand in her lap before anyone offered to arrange it for her.

Dorel stood beside the supports, with his gloved fingers on the prepared contact plate. Senn watched the fine fracture near Aria’s foot. Avenna told Aria who was present, including herself, although she had been speaking in the room for several minutes. She waited between names. Beneath the room’s small sounds, the low tone continued.

The tests were familiar. Aria gave one held note when asked whether Avenna had brought an apple and two when asked whether the apple was red. Sera wrote the sounds before looking at Avenna’s page. Dorel inspected the support nearest the fracture, then nodded.

Avenna read the description of the cutting house slowly. Two windows, one facing the water; a room prepared away from the cutting floor; a stove in an adjoining room rather than beside the crystal; attendants paid through the separate fund. The old quarry crane would not be used. The carriage would move by rails and a temporary trestle. Katerina had agreed to stay through the first settling period. Avenna would continue visiting. Aria would be asked before any additional visitor entered.

Lyra watched the hand raised inside the crystal. It had acquired a pale green tint from the apple’s reflected light. She had examined that hand so often that a change in daylight could seem a change in the woman herself. Once, foolishly, she had thought a finger had moved. She had told no one.

“Do you wish to leave this room for the cutting house as described?”

The note held until Senn lifted his eyes from the fracture. It was clear enough to make Lyra’s teeth ache.

Avenna waited. Her pencil stayed still.

“Do you wish to stay in the measuring room instead?”

Two notes, with a breath of quiet between them.

There was no need to ask which room Aria meant. Lyra had corrected that ambiguity herself, months before. She knew the order of every question, and resented the care with which they made the answer impossible to mistake.

Avenna recorded it. “Do you wish to hear the proposed arrangements for an escort on the journey?”

One note.

“We will ask on another day, if you are rested. Would you like me to read now?”

No answer came.

The faint song remained. Somewhere beyond the outer doors a boatman shouted a number, and another voice disputed it.

Avenna put the board down.

“Perhaps she didn’t hear,” Lyra said.

Dorel took his fingers from the plate. “We’ll stop.”

“I didn’t ask you to continue.”

“No.” He began to undo the fastening of his glove. “You didn’t.”

Avenna wiped her pen before closing the case. Sera compared the pages and initialed the unanswered question. Lyra opened her mouth, then rose from the stool instead.

She went to the carriage. Avenna stepped aside for her. The crystal’s fine fracture was unchanged; the support beneath it held a pad of undyed felt, cut so precisely that no loose corner could work against the surface. Beneath the casing, the harvesting valve remained covered by its plate. Lyra read the date and the witnesses’ marks. Dorel had rubbed the plate clean. Nothing had gathered around the sealed seam.

“Have you checked the wheel pins under load?” she asked.

“The replacement pins?”

“All of them.”

“Yes,” Dorel said. “Berris checked. I watched.”

“The lower right shoe used to bind.”

“She eased it.”

She bent, supporting herself on her left knee, and looked. The shoe had been dressed back. A thin curl of bright metal showed along the old edge, beneath fresh grease. It was good work. Berris had not taken off too much.

“Tell her not to make further alterations without bringing them to me.”

Dorel took a moment before answering. “I’ll tell her what you said.”

Lyra straightened. “You could say you will.”

“I said I would tell her.”

Sera had put away the contact record. She stood by the table with both hands resting on her case, waiting to be spoken to. Lyra turned toward her.

“Who is paying for this?”

“The transfer fund. It has continued to earn its rent, and the subscribers renewed the boat hire. The revised sum is on the paper I sent you.”

“I didn’t agree to a revision.”

“We did not charge it to the Opera House.”

“This is the Opera House.” Lyra put her foot over one chalk line. “So is this floor.”

“And the three signatories have renewed their instruction for access.”

“They have a great enthusiasm for instructions.”

Sera did not smile. “A copy will be delivered to your office this afternoon.”

Lyra looked past her toward the door. Seldo was standing in the passage. He had either been there the entire time or arrived quietly enough that she had not heard him. Usually the sight of him settled her. Today he was studying a knot in the doorframe.

“Then I shall read it this afternoon,” she said.

The receiving hoist lifted four barrels without a tremor. Lyra watched the new bronze bearing turn, caught herself feeling pleased, and let herself feel it. Revet’s delivery had been worth having. The old assembly had heated under a smaller load, producing a smell like a wet coin held too long in the mouth. Now there was only grease and timber and the ordinary complaint of rope.

Berris stood beside the lower landing with a pencil tucked through her hair. She had made a mark on each barrel before it went up, to test whether the platform leaned under unequal weight. One barrel had its mark upside down.

“You let the kitchen have the stencils?” Lyra asked.

“They made a cake board out of one.”

“Will it come off?”

“The cake?”

“The paint.”

“Not from the cake.”

She laughed before remembering she had come to be angry with Berris. The carpenter grinned, then put her fingers against the bearing housing and withdrew them. Warm, not hot. She nodded to the winchman.

Lyra waited until the empty platform returned. “I have seen your lines in the measuring room.”

“I thought you would.”

“No further work on the carriage.”

Berris set down the pencil she had taken from her hair. “The current work is finished.”

“Then no work on the rails.”

“They’ve hired me for two more days.”

“I employ you here.”

“For the upper floor and the hoist this week. The theatre repairs were paid through last rest day.”

“Do you intend to explain my own arrangements to me?”

Berris rubbed a black mark off the heel of her hand. It spread instead of disappearing. “I need to know whether you’re ending them.”

Above them, a porter called down to ask if the platform could come up again. Berris raised one finger for a minute’s delay. The man went away muttering.

Lyra could hear the hoist rope settling in its groove. She had expected protest about the work, perhaps a plea for Aria. Instead Berris wanted to know whether to take her tools home. Lyra could dismiss her. She could dismiss every handler who took their money. The upper floor would remain open to the weather, and the new bearing would have nobody she trusted to watch it through the next heavy deliveries.

“You will finish what I’ve paid you to do.”

“Yes.”

“And you will give the Opera work precedence.”

“Tomorrow’s rail work is before the hoist shift. If something here breaks, you can send for me.”

“I have not agreed that the rails should be laid.”

“I know.”

Berris looked unhappy now. It gave Lyra no satisfaction. The carpenter had once spent a whole night fitting a door so that women leaving the refuge could close it from either side. She remembered Berris’s hand shaking when the carpenter finally drank the broth Marda brought. Berris would not have thought of herself as opposing Lyra that night. Neither had Lyra.

“Where are they storing the timber?”

“On the canal landing. Under the blue sheet.”

“Keep it clear of my doors.”

She nodded. Lyra moved aside, and Berris signaled the platform up.

At the foot of the broad freight stair, two men were lowering a spare rail by hand. They stopped when they saw her. The stair rose behind them through three short flights to the salt level, wide enough for a carriage on prepared runners, with room for the hauling lines to be worked at the turns. It had carried Aria down on the night of the Festival. There was no reason to take her up it now; the measuring room and the raised landing were already below. Lyra saw the new stops at each turn and understood that the rehearsal would give the handlers a route to bring gear from above, or recover the load if the landing became unusable before embarkation. Careful people. They had accounted for her building without asking her.

“Who told you to use this stair?”

“Berris,” one said.

“Leave the rail where it is.”

The man at the lower end glanced upward. “Can’t leave it on the steps.”

“Then take it down.”

They did so. Lyra watched them carry it onto the landing and put it beneath the sheet. Her command had accomplished exactly what they had intended to do.

She found Seldo eating beside the guardroom window. He had a piece of fried fish on a flat cake, folded so the oil would not run onto his coat. When she came in, he stood, and the oil ran onto his coat.

“Sit down.”

He sat. She remained standing.

“No one is to move the crystal.”

Seldo put the cake on its paper. “I’ve heard.”

“From whom?”

“Dorel. He said you’d spoken to the carpenter.”

“I am speaking to you.”

He took a cloth from his pocket and blotted the spreading stain. “Yes, my lady.”

“There is another order coming. Three signatures. The same people, I expect. I don’t want to find the measuring room open because someone has mistaken a signature for my permission.”

“The room’s opened for the attendants.”

“For the attendants. For Avenna on her visits. Not for moving a carriage.”

Seldo folded the cloth around the oily place. He had left a strip of fish exposed; it cooled while she looked at it.

“If the attendants move it?” he asked.

“Then they will stop when you tell them.”

“And if they don’t?”

“You can close a door.”

“There’ll be people at the door.”

“You have dealt with people before.”

He raised his eyes. There was no reproach in them that she could identify. He seemed tired, and more frightened than she thought the situation warranted.

“Would you like the fish brought somewhere else?” she said sharply.

“No.”

“Then eat it. Keep the door. Tell me at once if they try to move anything.”

“At once,” he repeated.

She went upstairs with the discomfort of a conversation left unfinished. At the second landing she nearly turned back. She could make him say the words she wanted. She knew how long to stand in silence and where to put the next question. There were six men on his watch; two had followed her from the partnership, and the others had been paid by her when no one else could find the money. All six knew what stood outside.

She continued up.

The renewed order lay on her desk, weighted with the stone Tesset used when the window was open. Lyra read the signatures, the dates, and the clause that made the replacement guards answerable to the three signatories. Her assent was nowhere required. It had not been required the previous time. She folded the paper, unfolded it, and wrote a direction for Seldo on a separate sheet. No movement pending further instruction from her. He would have it in writing. Whatever happened, he could not later say he had misunderstood.

Tesset came in while Lyra was sanding the ink. She carried the day’s letters and a bowl with a cloth over it.

“Marda sent this up.”

“What is it?”

“I didn’t look.”

“Then how do you know it isn’t a severed hand?”

“It’s warm.”

Lyra almost laughed again. She disliked Tesset for bringing her so close to it. “A fresh one, then.”

Tesset set down the bowl. Under the cloth were beans with a little meat, and a heel of bread tucked against the rim. Lyra had forgotten she was hungry.

“Eat with me.”

“I have to take the answers to the counting house.”

“They can wait ten minutes.”

Tesset looked at the written direction beside the sand box. “I’ve eaten.”

That was probably true. Lyra did not ask where.

“Send this to Seldo.”

Tesset read it before taking it. Her lips moved faintly at one phrase. Then she put it on top of the letters she was carrying out.

“You know what it says?” Lyra asked.

“Yes.”

“Good.”

After Tesset left, Lyra ate standing at the window. Rain had begun in earnest. Water struck the blue sheet on the landing, gathered in a shallow pocket, and spilled over the edge. A handler came out and lifted one corner so it would drain. He worked without hurry, keeping his hands clear of the rails beneath. When he finished, he went into the measuring room.

Lyra ate the last of the bread and stayed at the window until the rain made it impossible to distinguish one man’s coat from another.

Chapter 8 The rail rehearsal

Tesset had paid for six handlers. Seven put out their hands.

The seventh was a broad woman with a chipped front tooth, who said she had been told to come by her cousin. Her cousin stood behind her, suddenly interested in the roof. Tesset searched the names again, although she had written them and knew the woman was not there.

“Which place were you told to take?”

“Landing brake.”

“That is Dannet’s.”

“He said Dannet would be at the head rope.”

Dannet was sitting on a coil, dividing an onion with a knife. He held up the knife. “I said if I was at the head rope, we’d need someone on the brake. I’m at the brake.”

The cousin began to explain. Tesset let him finish, partly because she needed the time to decide what to do with the woman. Sending her away unpaid would save money that had already been promised elsewhere. Paying her for the day would leave Tesset short for the return cart. She had budgeted a return cart because everyone had told her not to assume they could carry the rails back when tired.

“We can use another pair at the stair,” Berris said.

“We can use twenty,” Tesset answered. “We have six places.”

The woman put her hands in her coat. “I missed a washing turn for this.”

“I understand.”

“Do you?”

Tesset wished she had not said it. She had never had to tell Lyra there was no money for a seventh handler; Lyra would take it from a different purse and make someone unhappy later. Tesset had enjoyed that certainty while she had it.

She paid the woman half a day for presenting herself and gave her the name of Sera’s clerk, who kept the list for later work. The woman accepted the coins without thanking her. Tesset struck through the cart allowance on her own page. She would have to speak to Sera before noon. The cousin asked whether he might have the same arrangement if there were too many people tomorrow.

“No,” Tesset said. “Tomorrow you will tell the person hiring you exactly how many people you intend to bring.”

Berris waited until the woman was gone. “She could have held the stair line.”

“And what would you have given her?”

“Some of my pay.”

“You could have said so while she was here.”

Berris looked after the woman, then picked up her tools. Tesset wanted to apologize and could not decide which part of the exchange she regretted.

The rehearsal load was a low trolley carrying stones in closed boxes. Its wheels had been set at the same spacing as the carriage; Berris had added two projecting battens to show where the crystal’s frame would overhang. The weight was divided to imitate the uneven load, and the boxes were strapped so that nothing could shift on a turn. There would be no crystal on the freight stair today or on the day of departure. The stair was the handlers’ access from the salt level, and a possible retreat before the boat was committed. Berris nevertheless wanted every man to know what to do if the landing had to be cleared in a hurry. Tesset had once thought that rehearsing an unlikely retreat would make them more afraid. Watching the unloaded lines laid where hands could reach them, she changed her mind.

Dannet put away his onion and stood at the brake. He was shorter than his assistant, with a scar across the tip of one ear and eyebrows that appeared to have been cut with the same impatience as his hair. Esten had sent him with a note saying that he knew the portable winch and was not to be diverted to repairing anything merely because he stood near it.

“Palm out means stop,” he told them. “Anybody can give it. Two fingers toward me means take the strain. Not one finger. If you point at your own boot, I’ll think you have something in your boot.”

One of the men laughed. Dannet waited for him to finish.

“You laugh now because there’s nothing across it. Have we all got that?”

They had. Tesset stood beside the first turn with the board and watched them raise the load one short flight. The winch clicked. Berris crouched to look along the inner rail. A porter took up slack by walking backward, his eyes fixed on Dannet’s hand. At the landing, a wooden stop settled beneath a wheel with a dry crack that made Tesset lift her own hand before she knew she had done it.

Everything stopped.

Dannet came up, examined the block, and tapped it with his knuckle. The crack had been in a little tongue left on the end, beyond the bearing face. Berris cut it off. They checked both faces, then replaced the block anyway.

“Write that one down,” Dannet told her.

“The spare?”

“The noise. We need people who stop at noises. You did right.”

She wrote, pleased in a way she did not want the others to see. Her shoes had never cost so much and were never so poorly suited to any work as they were today. They slipped on stone and pinched when she tried to crouch. She had bought them to look competent at meetings. No meeting had yet compensated her for their use.

At the second turn, the inside hauling line rubbed against the battens. Berris shifted the temporary eye. At the third, the handoff left one man too close to the wheel. They did it again, and then once more without Tesset reading the instructions. By the time the trolley came down to the canal level, the men had begun speaking to each other instead of waiting for Berris to tell them everything. Dannet returned to his onion. It had acquired a skin of dust.

“You’re going to eat that?” Tesset asked.

“Underneath’s clean.”

“You could cut the outside off.”

“Then there’d be hardly any onion.”

He held out the smaller half. She declined. He appeared neither offended nor relieved.

The rails from the measuring room to the landing were simpler, almost level, but the sill required a removable bridge and the outer turn needed more space than the chalk had suggested. Avenna came out while they were setting the second piece. She waited until the hammering stopped before telling them Aria was resting and asking whether the last trial could be made without hammering near the room.

Berris took off her cap, rubbed her head, and put the cap back on.

“We can cut the wedges outside.”

“Thank you.”

“No one is in danger because she hears a hammer,” one of the handlers said after she left.

“No,” Berris said. “She’s tired.”

They carried the trestle pieces beyond the corner before cutting them. Tesset helped steady a plank and got a splinter under the nail of her smallest finger. Dannet removed it with a needle he carried in the lining of his cap. He told her not to watch if she was likely to snatch her hand away. She watched anyway and did snatch it, though not until the splinter was out.

At noon the trolley rolled over the sill without catching. Tesset made the men repeat it with a folded cloth covering part of the inner rail, the nearest thing she could contrive to a piece of clothing left in the way. This time the nearest handler stopped them. Dannet looked at her, then at the cloth.

“Don’t lay traps on the actual day.”

“I won’t.”

“You’ll think of something useful just as we’re moving. Tell me before you do it.”

She put the cloth in her pocket.

Neris’s answer reached her at Sera’s hired room, wrapped around a little drawing of a rope eye.

Tesset had written to ask whether the new hauling rope, which would lie damp between rehearsal and departure, should be dried in a heated store. Neris replied that they should hang it loosely where air could pass, away from the stove, and inspect the inside of the eye where the cover might hide rubbing. She had drawn the place she meant because she did not trust Tesset to know which part was the throat.

Tesset smiled at the sentence. Neris knew her accurately.

The second side of the page described a receiving shed with a door that swelled whenever the rain came inland. Her aunt blamed Neris for leaving it open; Neris blamed the man who had fitted the hinges. She was learning to distinguish rope sold for fishing from rope sold to people who would only discover the difference offshore. Her employer had let her mark rejected lengths in red. She liked making the mark. There was a place beside the shed where she could sit with her feet in seawater without anyone asking if she had fallen ill.

At the bottom she had written that she would not come back for the transfer. She hoped it went well. She wanted to hear where Aria’s window faced when she was settled. If Lyra asked, Tesset could tell her that the aunt had laughed at the pickled onions and eaten three.

Sera read the drawing with more attention than the personal page. “Will you send this to the carpenter?”

“I’ll show her. I’d like the letter back.”

“Of course.”

Tesset reported the extra handler, the lost cart money, and Berris’s offer made too late. She spoke too fast, as if speed might turn the mistake into weather. Sera asked how much she had paid. Tesset told her.

“Use the contingency purse.”

“That was for a delay at the basin.”

“It is for things we didn’t correctly allow for.”

“I allowed for six men.”

“And a seventh came.”

Sera opened the small chest herself. Tesset did not have its key. The fund had two holders, and the other held the original subscriptions elsewhere; its separation from Lyra did not make it freely available to Tesset. Sera counted out the replacement amount and asked her to record why it had been used. There was no reprimand, which somehow made writing the explanation more difficult.

“Have the guards been paid?” Sera asked.

“The advance. They collect the rest after the relief.”

“Whether it succeeds or not?”

“Yes. I wrote that into their terms.”

“Good.”

Tesset smoothed Neris’s letter. “Seldo has Lyra’s instruction in writing.”

“So do we.”

“That doesn’t make the doorway wider.”

Sera sat back. She had ink on the inside of one wrist, where a cuff would hide it from everyone except the person handing her a coin. “No. It doesn’t. Have you spoken to him again?”

“He says he’ll send for her if anyone starts.”

“Then she’ll be there.”

Tesset had known this. She had also been allowing herself, in the spaces between tasks, to imagine Lyra occupied elsewhere at the necessary hour. The imagination had made it easier to sleep. Sera’s flat statement took it away without malice.

“There isn’t a way to do it quickly enough,” Tesset said.

“No.”

“What if she orders him to strike someone?”

“Has he told you he will?”

“He hasn’t told me he won’t.”

Sera closed the chest. “The replacement watch will have its own report and its own wages. Dorel knows where to bring an objection. Berris knows I will pay the days she loses here if Lyra dismisses her for the transfer. We have done what we can to give people something besides obedience.”

“And if it isn’t enough?”

“Then we stop before someone is crushed between two orders. We don’t pretend the charter can lift a carriage by itself.”

Tesset folded the letter and put it in her sleeve. She wanted to hear that someone else would make that decision. Instead Sera asked her to walk through the stopping places one more time.

Corven came to the measuring room two days later. Tesset met him at the upper gate, where he was arguing with a guard about the jar under his coat.

“It needs water,” he said.

“Everything needs water.”

“Then you understand why I brought it.”

Tesset told the guard who he was. The guard knew, and became less happy when she said the familiar had been included in the agreed equipment. Corven waited while the jar was inspected. The eyeless eel moved slowly against its glass, a pale turn of body beneath cloudy brine. Tesset tried not to look at the place where its eyes should have been. Corven did not help by rotating the jar to show the guard that nothing else was in it.

“You can leave it in the outer room,” Tesset told him.

“I intend to.”

He had a different coat from the one she remembered, shorter and less grand than the long wet garment he had worn during Lyra’s early lessons. One cuff was patched with leather. The marked chain hung in a cloth bag across his back, making less noise than she expected. Without the sound of it, he seemed almost like a man arriving to repair a roof.

Aria had already agreed to hear his proposal. Avenna made the checks, including asking whether Tesset was present. Tesset felt oddly exposed when the answering note came. Until then she had thought of herself as a person at the edge of Aria’s attention, holding boards and counting days. Someone inside the crystal knew who was standing there.

Corven remained beyond the prepared contact, his hands open at his sides.

“I would travel beside the carriage from this landing to the cutting house,” he said. “I would help keep the passage clear, and oppose anyone trying to take you away from the agreed route. I would not give instructions to your attendants. I would leave when the carriage was secured in the receiving room and the first watch accepted it.”

He paused for Dorel to check the light along the fracture.

“I have hurt people in this work. You have seen me do it. If you do not want me on the journey, there are other escorts.”

Tesset had not expected him to say that. His proposal lay on her board in much drier words. She glanced at Avenna, but Avenna was watching Aria.

“Do you agree to Corven accompanying you under those terms?”

The note came after a long enough silence that Tesset had begun to prepare herself for none.

One held note.

Avenna asked the reversed question after a rest. Two notes. Corven bowed his head without moving closer.

In the passage afterward, he read the written terms again and added a line about the boat master’s command on the water. He would not fight the vessel’s handling in order to make a better place to stand. He also asked for half his fee that day and half when the agreed escort ended, whether the journey reached the quarry or was stopped at a safe point.

“If Lyra refuses?” Tesset asked.

“I expect she will.”

“Will you stop her?”

He rested his hand on the bag containing the chain. “I will not start a fight over a carriage with a fractured crystal on it. If she strikes an attendant, I will stop her if I can. If your guards decide to stand aside, I can’t make the entire journey happen alone.”

It was a disappointing answer. It was also more useful than the one Tesset had wanted.

“Does she know you’ve taken the work?”

“She knows she doesn’t employ me.”

“That isn’t what I asked.”

“I sent her a note this morning.”

He signed beneath the new line. His hand was firm; the script was poorer than she remembered. She noticed a stiffness in two fingers when he set down the pen and wondered whether she had mistaken silence for recovery.

Outside, Dannet was trying to persuade Berris that pickling an onion in a sealed jar inside a warm room produced a better result than leaving it in the shed. Berris said she had once found an onion under a stage that had survived three seasons and no longer belonged to any ordinary kind. Corven stopped to listen. By the time Tesset came out with the copies, all three were disagreeing about what should go into the jar.

She had expected the day to remain solemn after Aria’s answer. She stood with her papers while Dannet explained that he intended to grow his own onions when he could afford a room with a yard. Beans first, because a wall could serve them. Then something that did not have to climb. Corven suggested that he might begin by finding a room whose roof did not leak.

“Watering saved,” Dannet said, and accepted his copy without reading it until he had wiped his fingers.

Leda had brought a broken book home. Most of it had been cut wrong, with the inner edge of a page taking half the beginning of every line. She spread the folded sheets across Tesset’s bed and asked whether the missing words could be guessed.

“It starts halfway through a wedding,” Tesset said.

“It starts before the wedding. The cutting takes out the part where they meet.”

“That would save trouble.”

“Read it.”

Tesset sat with her back against the wall and her feet beneath Leda’s thigh. Leda was warm from the walk. She had taken off the yellow scarf and looped it over the bedpost, where the fringe caught on a splinter. As Tesset read, Leda eased it free one thread at a time.

The lovers had met at a fountain, or perhaps quarreled there; the cut made either possible. The bride’s father owned something extensive that began with the surviving letters “orch.” Tesset offered an orchard. Leda preferred an orchestra, which would account for the bride’s haste to leave. They supplied whole speeches where the paper had failed them. By the time the bridegroom fell into a river, Tesset was laughing hard enough that Nella stamped on the floor above them.

“Your sister has children,” Leda said. “She shouldn’t object to noise.”

“She objects to other people’s.”

Leda put the sheets aside and rubbed Tesset’s cold toes through the stocking. “You’ll go tomorrow?”

“The day after, if the weather holds.”

“Are you frightened?”

“Yes.”

“Of Lyra?”

Tesset drew one knee up. “Of making a mistake in front of her.”

Leda’s hand stopped. “That sounds worse.”

“It isn’t all of it.”

She tried to explain the carriage without turning the evening into another rehearsal. The room at the quarry, the three names on the order, the men whose wages would continue if Lyra sent them away. Leda listened, then asked whether Tesset’s own wages would continue. Tesset had considered that question so carefully for everyone else that she resented it when it was put to her.

“I have some saved.”

“Enough?”

“For a while.”

Leda reached for her hand. Tesset expected reassurance and instead received a careful inspection of the finger from which Dannet had removed the splinter.

“You should wash that again.”

“I did.”

“With soap?”

“With water.”

Leda got up. There was no grandness in the way she carried the bowl over, settled it between them, and held Tesset’s wrist while she washed the little red place. Tesset watched the bent dark head and wanted to say that she would have liked to stay like this for several days, eating upstairs when Nella called and inventing the missing parts of other people’s weddings. She did not say it. When Leda finished, Tesset drew her down onto the bed and kissed her until the cooling water was in danger of spilling.

Later Nella brought them a plate of sliced pears and demanded the bowl back. Perron had come home with dye on both hands and somehow on one knee. He claimed the knee had acted without instructions. The girls wanted Leda to make a paper bird that would fly from the stair. Leda made one that flew neatly into the bowl, and the room became too crowded for Tesset’s fears to occupy their usual place.

She woke before dawn with Leda’s arm across her middle. For a little while she lay still. The folded instructions waited beneath her clean coat. She knew every word on them. She let herself remain where she was until the first cart passed in the lane.

Chapter 9 The freight stair

Lyra was cleaning the cut in a porter’s palm when Seldo’s runner found her.

The boy stopped in the doorway, saw the blood in the basin, and forgot the beginning of his message. Lyra had to ask him twice. The porter watched her face instead of his hand.

“They’ve laid the bridge at the measuring room,” the runner said. “Seldo says they’ve brought the order.”

“Who has?”

“The new watch. And the others.”

“Is the carriage moving?”

“Not when I left.”

She set down the little bone probe. The porter had caught his palm on the broken handle of a crate. Two slivers were out, and a third remained beneath a fold of skin. If she left it, the wound might close over it. She could feel him preparing to tell her he could wait.

“Hold still.”

She took his hand again. Her right arm trembled with the small effort of maintaining the angle. Gyllan had once made her sit through an entire lesson picking seed husks from a child’s scraped knee because she had wanted to begin the healing while one still remained. She remembered the bright specks on his dark cuff. He had kept talking to the child about a cat that would not permit anybody to sit in its chair. Lyra could no longer remember what had become of the cat.

The third sliver emerged. She laid it beside the others, washed the wound, and rested her fingers along its edges. Her own hand was cool enough that the porter shivered. She waited for the familiar work to settle, for the torn flesh to answer in the little ways it could. No light announced it. Beneath her fingers the bleeding eased. There would still be stiffness, and he would have to keep the wrapping clean.

“You’re to carry with the other hand today,” she said.

“There’s a whole stack to go out.”

“Then someone else can carry your share.”

She bound the dressing and tied it herself. Only when he had gone did she tell the runner to take her bag to the office.

At the stairhead she found Corven’s note folded in her pocket. She had brought it down intending to show Seldo the narrow wording of his undertaking. Escort. Prevention of diversion. An end when the new attendants accepted their watch. There was no promise of service to the Opera House, and no suggestion that he needed her approval. She had written a reply, then burned it because she could hear Maeron in the sentence asking him to remember who had taught him to endure.

The smoke had troubled her more than the words. She had opened the window and gone to see the injured porter.

Now she put the note away and descended.

The first flight of the broad freight stair was clear. At the turn, a porter held a bundle of folded sacks against his chest. He pressed back to let her pass. Lyra recognized one of the men who had stood beside Seldo when the earlier replacement watch came. She had given his wife work sorting recovered cloth after the man’s leg was crushed. He could climb without a stick now.

“Put those down,” she said. “I may need you.”

He looked toward the next flight. “They’re pads for the carriage.”

“Put them down.”

He laid them on the landing. Lyra went past. Before she reached the second turn, she heard him lift the bundle again. She stopped, and he stopped behind her.

“Did you not hear?”

“I heard.”

“Then leave them.”

“Dorel asked for them. Someone will fetch them if I don’t take them. I might as well finish.”

For a moment she could not find a response that would not sound absurd on the empty stair. She could seize the sacks. She could drag them back up to the salt level. The carriage had other pads, and the man would be standing behind her with empty hands.

“Go, then.”

He did not thank her. He moved carefully around her and continued down.

The measuring room’s inner door stood open. Seldo waited beside it, sword sheathed, his hands bare. The two women sent by the counting house were farther along the passage. Their coats had no device of hers. One held a folded order; the other had a short staff laid against her thigh rather than raised across the way. Tesset stood near the board of questions with a pencil behind her ear, and Sera was beside the outer doors. Dorel had both hands resting on the carriage frame.

Lyra looked first for movement. The wheels remained chocked. The bridge over the outer sill was in place, and through the open doors she could see the blue sheet folded neatly along the landing wall. Berris crouched beside the nearest rail. Dannet stood farther out with the brake line, looking into the room without expression. Corven was beside the canal steps, beyond the working space. He had kept himself out of her doorway.

“Nothing moves,” Lyra said.

The new guard opened the order.

“I’ve read it,” she told her.

“I am required to present it.”

“Then present it.”

The guard held it out. Lyra did not take it. Sera came nearer, but Lyra raised her left hand before the woman spoke.

“Seldo. Close the outer doors.”

He looked at the bridge across the sill. “The rails are there.”

“Have them removed.”

Berris stood, wiping her hands on her apron. “The carriage is secured. If the order is to stop for safety, we’ll stop. It isn’t in danger where it is.”

“Move the rails.”

“No.”

She said it without raising her voice, and then seemed surprised by the sound. Lyra looked at her until Berris found somewhere else to put her eyes.

“Those doors are in my charge,” she said.

Sera answered. “The measuring room watch has been replaced under the charter. These two women answer to the three signatories. The attendants answer to the care agreement.”

“Seldo has not handed over his watch.”

Seldo’s mouth moved. Lyra turned toward him.

“Have you?”

“No.”

“Then keep it. Take them out of the room.”

The porter with the sacks was behind her. He set them down beside the carriage and stayed there. The new guard folded the order again, carefully following its existing creases. Tesset took the pencil from behind her ear.

Seldo stepped toward the nearer guard. Neither drew a weapon. They were much the same height, though Seldo was broader in the shoulder. Lyra remembered him holding the lower landing through a rush of men while she went for the gate. He had obeyed then without asking whether the men coming at him were properly employed.

“You’ll have to move,” he told the guard.

“I have a valid relief.”

“I know what you have.”

“Then you know where I report if you refuse it.”

The exchange was so quiet that Lyra almost interrupted to make them speak plainly. Seldo turned back to her. He had not touched the guard.

“Outside,” she said. “Both of them.”

He looked toward Dorel. The attendant remained by the carriage, his palms against its frame. Senn had his gloves on and was watching the pale fracture. He did not look like a man who expected guards to settle his day.

“If I put them outside,” Seldo said, “the attendants still have their order.”

“Then the attendants will wait.”

Dorel raised his head. “We’ve waited through the renewed hire. We won’t wait on another instruction from you.”

“You may find you no longer work here.”

“I know where to report.”

Lyra’s eyes went to Tesset. The clerk held the board across her middle, as if it were something solid to put between them. There was a small red place on the side of her finger.

“You have been busy,” Lyra said.

“Yes.”

“How long have you been paying people to refuse me?”

“The attendants’ independent pay began before the rail work. You have the dates.”

“Don’t answer a different question.”

Tesset swallowed. “I made sure they would still be paid if they followed the relief. I made sure Berris knew where to collect her wages. Sera approved it.”

“And you did not think to discuss that with your employer.”

“I brought you the papers.”

“You brought papers. You ate in my room.”

Tesset’s face changed then. Lyra saw the injury land and was pleased, briefly and unmistakably pleased. She wanted the room to know that the easy evenings had been real enough to be betrayed.

“I did,” Tesset said.

A wheel clicked as someone eased a chock. Dorel immediately put his hand out. The handler stopped. Lyra turned so sharply that the ache in her right shoulder became a bright line down the arm.

“I said nothing moves.”

“No one is moving her,” Dorel said. “We need to reset the chock if we wait.”

He knelt and set it with his own hands. The work made the room quiet. Lyra could hear water knocking against the landing piles. It was not yet slack, and she knew from the sound that they had enough time for this dispute. They had deliberately allowed enough. Tesset had always been good at allowing for the part of a task that other people wanted to pretend would not happen.

“Aria was asked this morning?” Lyra said.

Avenna came forward from the far side of the crystal. Lyra had scarcely registered her presence. “She was.”

“And agreed?”

“Yes. The checks and questions are here.”

She held out the page. Lyra took it. The answer had been witnessed by Avenna, Senn, and the woman appointed by the second signatory, who stood beside the stove with her cloak still on. No signature from Katerina. No room for Lyra to say the person who had abducted Aria was arranging the answers to suit herself.

“I want to ask.”

Dorel rose slowly. “We have asked everything needed for the journey. More contact takes strength.”

“One question.”

Avenna looked at the crystal. “The answer won’t become different because you ask it.”

“I said one question.”

“And a check,” Dorel said. “If we’re making contact, we make it properly.”

He fetched the prepared gloves again. Lyra stood beside the carriage, within sight of the hand raised inside. From here she could see the apple’s place on the sill, empty today. All the little pieces of the room had been packed. The kettle, the cleaning cloths, the cup with its chipped lip. They had not left the practical objects for her to return later as gifts.

Dorel made contact. Avenna checked a visible fact, waited, reversed it. The answers came more faintly than Lyra remembered, although perhaps she had always remembered them louder after a refusal.

Lyra stepped nearer.

“Do you wish to leave today with these people?”

One held note sounded.

She had no second question ready. If she asked whether Aria would remain, the two notes would come. She could hear them before they were made. She could ask something complicated enough that the answer failed, or something for which neither yes nor no was adequate. Dorel watched her, his fingers steady on the plate.

“Stop the contact,” she said.

He did so. Avenna wrote the question in Lyra’s exact words. She had not needed to ask permission to record it.

Lyra turned toward the outer doors. There was still enough room between the carriage and the sill for her to stand. She walked into it. Berris stepped back from the rail to give her space, an old courtesy that made Lyra want to strike her.

“Put the chocks in,” she told the nearest handler.

“They are in.”

“Then leave them.”

He looked at Dorel. Not at her. Dorel said nothing, because no one had given the movement call. The men could wait until the tide changed and lose the boat hire. They could try again another day. She could station four guards in this space, pay them directly, replace Seldo with someone who had not eaten with Dorel, and make the next refusal more difficult. She could make every door in the Opera House answer to one key.

“Seldo,” she said. “Bring the other watch down.”

He did not move.

“How many?” he asked.

“All of them.”

“To do what?”

She stared at him. “To hold this room.”

“Against Dorel?”

“Against anyone who moves without my permission.”

He rubbed his thumb across the side of his hand. It was a gesture she knew; he did it before taking a difficult stair or lifting a man who might struggle. “I won’t strike the attendants.”

“I did not tell you to strike them.”

“They won’t go when I ask. I won’t strike Berris either. Or her.” His eyes flicked toward Tesset and away. “I’ll stand here if someone comes to hurt you. I’m standing here now.”

The new guards stayed still. Neither tried to take advantage of the admission. Sera’s hand rested on the back of a chair; she had stopped holding herself quite so upright. Lyra could see the effort in everyone now, the fear beneath the prepared phrases. No one in the room believed she was harmless. Corven remained outside, one foot on a dry patch of landing, his bag at his side. He could reach her before she reached Dorel. Perhaps he could not. Her altered body was stronger than he knew, and she had learned things during the months when he thought she was only listening.

She imagined the carriage jolting when someone fell against it. Her own hand on Tesset’s throat. Seldo trying to separate them. The thought was clear, without the comforting confusion of a fight already begun.

“Tesset,” she said. “Come here.”

The clerk hesitated, then came. She stopped beyond Lyra’s reach, and Lyra noticed that too.

“Tell them to put away the rails.”

“No.”

“You still work for me.”

“Yes.”

“And you understand what this costs?”

“I understand some of it.”

Lyra wanted to make her look at the gates above them, the salt stores, the men whose injuries had been dressed in her room. She wanted to demand which of these Tesset would hold when the Prince stopped being cautious. But Tesset was already looking at her. The people beyond her had been there during the Festival, and the woman inside the crystal had paid more dearly for the Prince’s caution than any of them.

Lyra stepped off the rail.

For a moment nothing happened. She had moved only far enough for the carriage to pass, and everyone seemed to wait for the movement to become an order.

“I am not starting a fight on this sill,” she said. “You have heard what I said. Do your work.”

Sera opened her mouth. Lyra looked at her, and she closed it.

Dorel went to the front support. Senn checked the fracture. Dannet called from the landing for the strain to be taken. The handlers moved into their places. Berris crouched beside the wheel that had once bound and ran two fingers along its dressed edge. Tesset returned to the board, her pencil shaking so much that she put it down and gripped the edge with both hands.

“Ready at the rear?” Dannet called.

The answer came. Chocks lifted. The carriage moved toward Lyra, slower than a walking person, bringing the crystal’s faint sound with it. She could see Aria’s face through a new angle of light. The raised hand passed close enough that Lyra could have put her own against the glasslike surface between them.

She kept both hands at her sides.

At the sill the wheels paused. Berris checked the bridge, gave the sign, and the carriage crossed. Nothing cracked. No support slipped. The plan she had examined and refused worked in front of her.

The rear wheel left the measuring room. Dorel walked beside it onto the landing, and the porter who had carried the sacks followed with the spare pads under one arm. Avenna gathered the contact papers. The visiting witness took the chair out of Sera’s way. Objects that had occupied the room for months disappeared in the order in which somebody would need them next.

Tesset turned back. “I will return after the receiving watch is settled.”

Lyra looked past her. “Bring the Opera keys.”

“I have them.”

“Bring all of them.”

Tesset nodded and went out.

Seldo remained beside the inner door. Lyra waited until the empty carriage space was clear of people before looking at him.

“The west stores are still under my watch.”

“Yes.”

“Put two men there. No cargo leaves without my mark until I have reviewed the dispatches. Send the receiving lists upstairs. Anyone whose work ends with this movement can collect what they are owed from Sera.”

He accepted these instructions with visible relief. She hated him for the relief and was grateful that he went quickly.

On the broad freight stair, a loose wedge lay beside the wall. Lyra picked it up with her left hand and climbed to the salt level. She did not turn toward the canal when the first hauling call sounded behind her.

Chapter 10 Slack water

On the landing, Tesset discovered that she had been holding the board upside down.

Nobody had noticed. There was nothing on it she needed immediately; the movement instructions were nailed beside the brake, and Dannet knew them well enough to complain about the order of two words. She turned the board over and found the place where she was meant to record the condition of the supports before embarkation. The pencil made a line through the wrong space.

Avenna put her hand over Tesset’s.

“Take a breath.”

“I am breathing.”

“Then take another.”

Tesset did. The air hurt high in her chest. Avenna stayed beside her until she could write the hour without digging the pencil through the paper, then went back to Dorel. From the inner room came the scrape of a chair being moved. Lyra’s footsteps had already gone up the freight stair. Tesset had thought the sound would release something in her. Instead she wanted to go after it and make sure they had not mistaken a pause for permission to continue.

Dannet caught her eye and held up his palm.

She stopped walking.

“That means you too,” he said. “The line’s across your feet.”

She looked down. The slack brake line ran over the toe of her shoe. She stepped out of it and moved to the wall.

The hired flat lay against the landing with her deck lower than the sill. Her master stood at the forward cleat, a heavy woman with a narrow plait tucked inside her collar. She had refused to bring the vessel close enough until the falling current eased. Now she was watching a wisp of straw beside the stern. It moved slowly past the piling, then caught and turned.

“A little longer,” she said.

They had already paid for the waiting time. Tesset repeated that fact to herself when a handler shifted impatiently. No one would lose wages because the boat master preferred to wait. No one had to make a hurried decision to spare Lyra a charge she might dispute afterward. The thought helped, though it did not stop Tesset hearing Lyra say that she had eaten in her room.

Dorel tested each strap by hand. Senn knelt where he could see the fracture through the lower opening in the frame. The crystal remained covered above shoulder height by the light travel cloth, leaving Aria’s face clear of folds and the necessary surfaces visible. Tesset had wondered whether covering any part of her would feel like closing a door. Avenna had asked, on a separate rested day, and Aria had agreed to the cloth described. The written answer was in the oilskin case beneath Tesset’s coat.

Corven had put his jar in a padded box on the boat. He came back along the gangway with no weapon in his hands. One of the new guards asked whether he wanted someone at the canal steps. He said yes, until the carriage was aboard, and showed her a place from which she could see both approaches without standing in the path of a rope.

Tesset knew the guard’s name but could not summon it. She was frightened that she would forget something of greater use next.

“Tell me the next part,” she said to Berris.

Berris looked at her. “You wrote it.”

“Tell me anyway.”

She took Tesset to the edge, away from the loaded lines. The bridge would be fixed to the landing and rest on a broad shoe on the flat’s deck. The boat would settle as the weight moved aboard. The shoe could travel a little without pulling the bridge out of place; the shore end would remain pinned. They would stop with the front wheels at the midpoint, then with all wheels aboard, checking the boat’s level before shifting to the deck rails. The frame would be secured to the vessel’s prepared bearers. No one would stand in front of it to stop it with a shoulder.

“I know,” Tesset said at the last part.

“So does everyone. They still do it.”

Berris was tired already. There was a grayish smear at one temple where she had rubbed her face with her wrist. Tesset wanted to thank her for refusing Lyra, but the moment seemed too close to ask her to feel anything about it.

“The seventh woman,” she said instead. “We should have kept her.”

“We have enough.”

“That’s not what you said.”

“I said we could use her. We could have. We have enough.”

The boat master raised her hand. “Now.”

The word made everybody quieter. The bridge went down. Berris checked the pins, the shoe, the rail ends. Dannet took the strain, and the rear chocks were lifted.

Tesset watched the carriage move. She had seen the ballast trolley cross twice, but it had not contained a woman’s lifted hand. The crystal’s faint tone seemed to change when the front wheels touched the bridge. Dorel leaned nearer, listening, and Senn kept his eyes on the fracture. Neither called a stop.

At the midpoint, the deck sank a little beneath the advancing weight. The master eased the shore line. The broad shoe moved as it had been designed to move, with a scrape that went through Tesset’s bones. Dannet held his hand steady. Everybody stopped. Berris crouched at the shore end, then crossed beside the carriage, where there was just enough room for her to pass without touching the frame.

“Rail ends true,” she said.

The master looked along the vessel. “Another hand’s breadth.”

They moved it that far. She adjusted the aft line and waited for the boat to settle. Tesset could see the little strip of water between hull and landing open, close, and open again. It was ordinary motion. It had been happening beneath every vessel she had ever boarded. Today it seemed to require an explanation.

“Ready,” said the master.

The rear wheels crossed. The flat took the full weight with a slow, even lowering. Tesset had expected something abrupt. There was no lurch, only a changed view of the deck and Berris reaching up from farther below to guide the end of a brace.

The carriage was aboard.

It took much longer to secure it than to move it. Each fastening had to hold the frame without drawing the supports out of their established positions. Dorel stopped them once to shift a leather pad. Dannet replaced a rope that had taken an awkward turn around its eye. Neris’s drawing had been copied onto the edge of the inspection sheet; Tesset ran her finger under the cover and found no cut strands. She told Dorel that Neris wanted to know which way the window faced.

“Toward the water,” he said.

“One of them.”

“Then tell her there are two.”

He smiled, and Tesset found she could smile back.

The master would take four handlers to the quarry, not six. The remaining two would dismantle the shore bridge and keep the landing clear. Their return cart had been paid for.
