Tesset handed over the agreed money and made herself watch each man count it. This was a thing she knew how to do even while her stomach felt as though it had been left in the measuring room.

Corven came aboard last. The new guard returned to her post under Sera’s authority. Tesset stood near the stern, clear of the master’s work, and watched the boat slide away from the raised landing.

No one waved from the upper windows. She had not expected anyone to. She looked until the angle of the wall concealed them.

In the inner basin the water held them almost still. The master had warned Tesset that slack did not mean absence of every movement; the wind could lay a vessel against a wall while the water beneath it seemed asleep. They used the short poles first, then the towline from the bank. Tesset had never traveled so slowly through a place she usually crossed on foot.

The Opera House receded behind a row of sheds. A boy carrying a basket watched the flat pass, walked ahead, and watched again from the next opening. His basket contained eels. Tesset could see their bodies moving beneath the loose lid. At the third opening he was joined by an older woman who took the basket from him and sent him away. Neither called out. Most people had work that did not become less urgent because a strange covered carriage passed their door.

Avenna sat beside the crystal where Aria could see her. She did not sing. The master had asked for quiet enough to hear the bankside calls, and Avenna had agreed. Sometimes she said where they were: the last stone bridge, the end of the houses, the tow path widening. Tesset could not tell how much of the sky Aria could see through the clear part of the crystal. She had asked Dorel whether the surface altered the view from within, and he had said he did not know.

Corven stood forward, watching the path. His chain remained in its bag. Once he loosened the fastening and inspected a marked link where it lay against a folded cloth. He did not take it out. Tesset was grateful for that, although she knew gratitude had no bearing on whether it would be needed.

She went to him when the channel widened enough for the master to let two people change places.

“Would you have done it?” she asked quietly.

“What?”

“Stopped her.”

He looked toward the bank. “She stepped aside.”

“That isn’t an answer.”

“It’s what happened.”

Tesset folded her arms. “You used to give longer answers.”

“I used to be more certain which part people wanted.”

For a while they watched the towline slide through the grass at the bank’s edge. The man hauling it was strong enough for the pace but kept looking behind him, unable to trust that the boat would follow. The second towman told him to watch his footing. It sounded like a conversation that had been going on between them for years.

“I didn’t know whether I could make her move,” Corven said at last. “I knew I would try if she hurt Dorel. That isn’t the same thing.”

Tesset nodded. The answer had come after she had stopped preparing a response, which made it easier to hear.

“Do you have work after this?”

“A wall repair. A merchant wants to recover a chest from a flooded cellar. I haven’t agreed to the cellar.”

“Because of the water?”

“Because he says it is only a small chest.”

She laughed. Corven’s mouth moved a little. The familiar was quiet in its padded box at his feet.

After the houses thinned, the channel opened into a broader reach with a low ridge on one side. The wind came across the water instead of along the walls. The master stopped the boat against a prepared mooring while the towmen changed their harness. Avenna took the opportunity to offer Dorel food. He said he would eat after the next check. She put bread in his hand and waited until he bit it.

Tesset sat on a folded sail and ate a boiled egg that had been in her pocket since morning. She had forgotten the salt. Dannet, who had come with the winch, produced a little paper twist of it and then an onion wrapped in cloth. She accepted a piece this time. It was sharp enough to bring tears to her eyes.

“Better warm,” he said.

“How can an onion be better warm?”

“Nearly everything is better when you sit down to it before somebody calls you back.”

He ate slowly, braced against the boat’s side. The scar on his ear was pink in the sun. Tesset asked whether he had found the room with the yard. He said a room had been offered, but the yard belonged to a dog, and the dog had declined to discuss sharing it. He had been bitten through his stocking during the inspection. The landlady had said it showed the place was well guarded.

Berris laughed so hard that she lost a piece of bread over the side. Dannet offered her onion in compensation. For several minutes the boat held a meal, with people reaching past one another for a cup and arguing about who had brought the knife. Tesset took a piece of onion and asked for the bread before Berris fed any more of it to the harbor.

The cutting house appeared first as a row of square openings in a long stone wall. The quarry basin lay beneath it, quiet under the shelter of the ridge. An old crane stood at the far end with its arm tied back and a board fixed across its controls. No part of the transfer led toward it.

Their trestle was nearer the water, broad and low, with a level track running through double doors into the prepared room. Katerina waited beside it in a work coat too large for her shoulders. Two receiving handlers stood with her. One was holding a kettle. Tesset saw the kettle and felt a sudden, unreasonable affection for whoever had thought to make water hot before they arrived.

The flat came alongside without difficulty. The master held them off while Berris compared the deck height with the trestle. A removable ramp made up the difference. The boat had to be secured against movement while the load passed, and the bearing faces checked for grit. Tesset had been here during the inspection. She knew the stones under the first trestle legs were sound. She went to look anyway.

Katerina came down to the water’s edge. “How is she?”

“Dorel says unchanged.”

“And the departure?”

Tesset looked at her.

“She’s here,” she said.

Katerina accepted that. She gave Tesset the kettle and helped lay the spare cloths where Dorel wanted them. There was none of the glitter Tesset remembered from the Court about her, but Tesset distrusted the ease with which a work coat could change a person’s appearance. Katerina had helped put Aria into the place they were bringing her out of. Her hands could be useful now without making the earlier hands disappear.

The ramp met the deck true. Berris checked it once more, then transferred the hauling line to the receiving winch. Dannet gave the signals. This time Tesset recognized the little motion of weight passing from one support to the next. The load would come off the flat and the boat would rise; the master had to let her move without taking the ramp with her. Tesset watched the master, who watched the wheel nearest the water. Neither spoke until the front pair reached the trestle.

At the pause, Senn called for Dorel. Tesset’s whole body went tight.

A strip of felt had crept outward beneath one of the secondary pads. It was not the pad supporting the fracture. Dorel checked the adjacent strap, asked them to hold, and eased the strip back with a flat tool. Berris put a thin keeper beside it so it could not travel again. The task took less than two minutes. Tesset counted almost every breath.

“Ready,” Dorel said.

The wheels moved. The master let out the shore line in her hands. The flat rose a little behind the departing carriage.

Inside the cutting house, the air was cooler than Tesset expected. The stove’s warmth came through an open inner door, enough for the people sitting their watch without heating the crystal directly. The walls had been washed. The old grooves in the floor remained visible beyond the new timber track, and one patch of plaster had been left rough because smoothing it would have delayed the work for no benefit.

The carriage came to its marked position with its long side toward the windows. Dorel checked the light, then asked for a small turn so that the nearest window would not reflect directly across the fracture. They had allowed for that adjustment. The bearers were broad enough, the rails extended far enough, and nothing had to be improvised under a hanging weight.

When the final chocks were in, Dannet let the line slacken. Berris watched the frame settle onto its supports. Senn stayed on his knees for a long time, looking from one end of the fracture to the other. At last he sat back on his heels.

“The same,” he said.

Dorel repeated the inspection, then wrote it. The harvesting valve’s dated plate was checked and found unbroken. The cloth was lifted away. Light from the water moved across Aria’s face.

Tesset put down the board and discovered she could not stop crying.

She went outside before anyone spoke to her. There was a pile of waste wood beside the door. She sat on it, pressed her sleeve to her eyes, and tried to make her breathing quiet. Everything she had feared seemed to be arriving after the event, with no useful task to attach itself to. Lyra in the doorway. The rope across her foot. The two minutes on the trestle. Her own bed and Leda’s hand over hers. She cried until her face hurt.

Katerina came out with a cup, set it on the wood beside her, and went away again. Tesset was grateful that she did not stay.

Corven’s job ended before supper. The receiving watch had inspected the room, accepted the conditions, and put its names beside Dorel’s. The outer doors could be closed from within and opened by the attendants; the guards had been told where visitors were to wait. The new arrangement had a place for an objection that did not lead to Lyra’s desk.

Tesset counted out Corven’s remaining fee. He counted it again without apology, put it away, and signed. Then he took the jar from its padded box and held it up to the light. The eel moved against the glass.

“Are you going back tonight?” she asked.

“I have a place along the road.”

“Will you come if they need you?”

“They can ask.”

She smiled despite herself. “Yes. I suppose they can.”

He touched two fingers to the doorframe before leaving. Tesset did not know whether it was farewell to Aria, a habit, or a way of testing the timber. She did not ask him to make it one thing for her.

The promised stew had burned while everyone watched the landing. Katerina scraped what she could into a clean pot and sent a boy from the neighboring yard for bread. The boy brought bread and a claim that the baker would not extend credit merely because they had taken a large house. Tesset paid him from the food purse and made a note to introduce the house’s keeper to the baker before another meal was attempted.

They ate at a narrow table in the adjoining room. Dorel went first, then Senn, so Aria was never left unwatched. Dannet wanted to know why the onions had been put in whole. Katerina said he was welcome to cut them. He did, passing the pieces around as though he had supplied the meal himself.

Afterward Avenna read quietly by the carriage. There was no contact; Dorel wanted a night’s rest before questions. Aria’s low tone remained. Tesset sat where she could see the windows darken, and for once had nothing to write.

Callista arrived the next morning on a cart padded with sacks. The journey had tired her, and she was angry that everyone could see it. Katerina helped her down without offering the arm she had already refused, keeping close enough to catch her if her knees failed. They did not. Callista sat in the chair brought to the doorway and looked at the room before asking to enter.

Aria had rested. The morning checks were brief, and the answers distinct. Avenna named the visitors and asked about Callista first.

One held note.

Katerina came no farther than the outer wall until the question was put about her as well. The answer was the same. Tesset saw Katerina close her eyes, then open them and remain where Aria could see her.

Callista had brought a piece of blue cloth to put over the back of a chair. It was an impractical gift for someone who could not sit, and an ordinary gift for a room that had been made ready too recently to belong to anybody. She explained that she had been tired of looking at it at home. Her voice cracked on the last word. Avenna laid the cloth where it would be visible from the crystal.

They stayed a little while. Callista spoke about the woman who brought her medicines and insisted that swallowing them with honey made them pleasant. Dorel watched for strain. When Avenna asked whether Aria wanted the visit to continue, two separated notes sounded.

Callista stopped in the middle of a sentence. Then she nodded.

“All right.”

Katerina helped her outside. No one asked the question again. The chair stayed, with its blue cloth over the back.

Tesset left after the midday watch changed. She took the completed journey record, copies of the handover, and the keys Lyra had demanded. The room would remain occupied after she went. On the road she turned once to look back at the water-facing window. Sunlight made it difficult to see through the glass, but she could make out Avenna moving the chair so it would not obstruct Dorel’s path.

Chapter 11 The measuring room

The measuring room had become larger.

Lyra stood inside the door and looked at the pale rectangles where the carriage supports had rested. Dust lay around them, darkened by old damp. One felt shaving remained beside the wall. There was no crystal to make the shaving seem dangerously close to a fracture, no attendant to sweep it away before she could mention it.

The window had always been that size. She knew it had. Without the carriage beneath it, the opening looked too small for the room.

She had waited until the morning after the departure to come down. During the afternoon she had been busy. During the evening she had found more work. She had gone through the receiving lists twice, moved a chest of small tools from the exposed store to the inner court, and instructed the night watch to count the lamps before lighting them. Seldo had done all of it. He had not asked whether she wanted company at supper.

Now the lamp hooks were empty. The little table had been left because it belonged to the Opera House. Its top showed a pale ring where Senn’s bowl had stood. Lyra pressed her thumb into a rough place in the wood, then took her hand away.

She had brought the room key. It no longer opened the outer lock; the replacement watch had changed it after the departure, with a copy of the order pinned where she could see. The inner door had been left open for an inventory. Someone would eventually ask her to return the useless key, and until then it would remain on the cord beneath her collar.

On the canal landing, a guard she did not know was shaking water from a coat. He looked toward her and straightened. He did not call her my lady.

“Who has the room now?” she asked.

“The council’s appointed keeper.”

“Which keeper?”

“I’ve been told to keep it clear until the survey.”

“You weren’t told a name?”

“No.”

“Then find one. A room can’t report its own leaking roof.”

He nodded. It was a reasonable instruction, and she resented that reasonableness more than she would have resented defiance.

A shout came from the freight stair. She turned before the second one. The voices were angry, familiar, and concerned with something she could still put right.

At the salt level, Seldo had two carriers stopped beside a stack of wrapped meal. The cook stood behind them with a paper in her hand. She had tied a cloth over her hair and looked capable of biting through it.

“These are for the shore house,” she told Lyra. “They’ve been promised since last week.”

“Nothing leaves without my mark.”

“So I was told. Yesterday. And before breakfast. Marda’s people have come twice.”

Lyra took the paper. The meal was part of a joint purchase, with a separate contribution for the shore house. The writing was Tesset’s. At the bottom, in Lyra’s own hand, was the approval she had given before the departure.

“Then it has my mark.”

“The guard says all marks were withdrawn.”

Lyra looked at Seldo.

“You said no cargo until you’d reviewed the dispatches,” he said.

“I said that yesterday.”

“There wasn’t an end to it.”

The carriers waited with their hands tucked under their arms. One had an empty basket strapped to his back. Lyra could see the ridges it had made in his coat. He had come expecting to take food and was spending the morning beside food he could not touch.

“Release it,” she said.

The cook held out her hand for the paper. Lyra did not give it back immediately.

“How much remains?”

“For our kitchen? Enough through the next delivery.”

“When is it due?”

“Today.”

“And where is it?”

“At the outer store, waiting for a towboat.”

“Why?”

The cook’s anger became uncertainty. “You’d know better than me.”

Lyra gave her the paper. “Take the shore house’s share. Leave ours where I can see it.”

“That was the intention.”

The carriers lifted the first load. Seldo stepped out of their way. Lyra went to the office for the incoming dispatches, then stopped at the foot of the last flight because she did not know where Tesset had put the messages that arrived after dusk. They had always been waiting when Lyra wanted them. There were trays, a shelf, a string across the window. Each apparently served a purpose she had failed to learn.

She found the towboat notice under the stone weight. The service had been suspended during a review of maintenance priority. Other vessels would be assigned according to their existing harbor agreements. The notice bore the mark of a company whose men worked for the Prince.

Under it lay two earlier notices, delivered before Aria left. One concerned pump hire. The other asked every user of the eastern navigation canal to confirm the men permitted to load there. Tesset had placed a note beside them asking for a joint answer before the end of the week. Lyra remembered telling her that the matter could wait until they had settled the crystal.

She read the dates again.

Tesset returned in the afternoon with mud dried white along the edge of her shoes. She stood in the office doorway until Lyra told her to come in.

“Is she settled?” Lyra asked.

“Yes.”

“The fracture?”

“Unchanged at the receiving inspection and this morning. Dorel and Senn both signed.”

“The supports?”

Tesset put the journey record on the desk. “The secondary pad needed easing during the landing. They stopped and did it. There was no movement at the fracture. Berris added a keeper.”

“Show me.”

Tesset showed her. The drawing was small and exact, with the original pad marked in a different hand from the keeper. Lyra read both inspections, the times, the description of the vessel’s movement. She could find nothing to accuse them of omitting. The receiving room had the two windows they had promised. The guards answered to the independent keeper. The money was there for the next interval. Corven’s escort had ended.

“Callista went?” she asked.

“This morning. Not for long.”

“Was that wise?”

“She chose to come. She rested before going back.”

Lyra turned the page. At the bottom was the record of Aria ending the visit. Two notes. The visitors had left. No discussion of whether she had meant them all or only one. They had asked a clear question and acted on the answer.

She put the report down.

“The keys.”

Tesset laid them beside it. Three iron keys, a small brass one, and the flat warded key to the inner writing chest. She separated the brass key from the others.

“That opens the drawer with the lamp money. The drawer sticks if it’s pulled from the left.”

“I know.”

“The lists for next week’s wages are finished. The disputed days are marked, but I haven’t resolved them.”

“Sit down.”

Tesset sat. She placed her hands together in her lap. Lyra had seen her do that on the day she first came to discuss work, before she learned she could interrupt Lyra to correct a figure or ask what an instruction meant. The return of the gesture made the room feel unfamiliar.

“You no longer work for me,” Lyra said.

Tesset looked at the keys. “From today?”

“From now.”

“Then I’ll need what is owed through today.”

“You’ll have it.”

“And my papers from the side drawer.”

“Which papers?”

“My own letters. The pattern for Nella’s shelf. Two pages copied from the tide book.”

Lyra had expected argument. She had prepared for Tesset to tell her that the transfer was lawful, that Aria had wanted it, that wages did not purchase every act of a person’s life. She had answers to some of those things, and anger enough for the rest. She had not prepared to discuss a shelf.

“You could have told me what you meant to do.”

“I did.”

“Don’t.”

Tesset looked up. “I brought the order. I brought the payment arrangement. I said I was handling the transfer pay. You told me not to confuse those things with your permission.”

“You knew I would stop it.”

“I knew you would try.”

The words were quiet. Lyra felt them in her face as if the window had been opened on a cold day.

“You let me go on speaking to you as though we were working together.”

“We were working together. On the hoist, the stores, Ada’s inquiry. I didn’t change any of that.”

“You arranged for my own people to refuse me.”

“Yes.”

Tesset’s hands were no longer folded. She held the edges of the chair. Lyra could see the strength it took her to remain in it.

“Then you should have expected this.”

“I did expect it.”

That hurt more than Lyra had intended the conversation to hurt her. Tesset had measured the loss of the office, the wages, the meals, and continued. Lyra wanted to ask whether there had been anything she would not exchange for the satisfaction of being right. The question was so close to one Maeron might have asked that she could not bear to put it into the room.

She opened the drawer with the lamp money, remembered it was the wrong one, and shut it. Tesset did not correct her. Lyra took her own purse instead.

“The current week, through today,” she said.

Tesset named the sum. Lyra counted it onto the desk.

“There is also the runner’s charge from the second day,” Tesset said. “I paid it. The note is under the green pin.”

Lyra added the charge without looking for the note. Tesset looked at the coins and then at her.

“I can show you.”

“I believe you.”

Tesset’s face moved. For an instant Lyra thought she would cry. She did not want that, and wanted it, and hated the whole room for giving her no place to put either wish.

“Will you write the dates I worked?” Tesset asked.

“You want a recommendation?”

“I want a statement of the work and the dates. You can say you dismissed me.”

Lyra took a fresh sheet. She wrote the work Tesset had done: correspondence, payments, dispatches, handling of accounts, arrangement of stores. When she came to the last day, she left a blot because the pen had paused too long. She did not state a cause for the dismissal. Neither did she praise the clerk’s judgment. She sanded the page and pushed it across.

Tesset read it before folding it. Lyra respected that and would have preferred not to notice.

“Thank you.”

“Your papers.”

Tesset went to the side drawer. There was more in it than she had named: a broken comb, a clean handkerchief, a packet of dried leaves Marda had given her for a cough. She gathered everything into the handkerchief and tied the corners. The shelf pattern was on the back of an old dispatch, with careful measurements along its edges. Lyra remembered being asked to check them. She had suggested a stronger bracket and Tesset had laughed because Nella intended the shelf to hold three cups, not a harbor chain.

Tesset left the drawer open for Lyra to inspect. Lyra closed it herself.

“At the cutting house,” Tesset said, “the kettle is in the adjoining room. If you send anything for the attendants, it goes through the keeper. Aria’s visits are arranged through Avenna.”

“I can read the agreement.”

“Yes.”

She waited in the doorway. Lyra looked down at the keys.

“Is there anything else?”

“No.”

Tesset went. Her footsteps continued down the passage, stopped once by the outer room, and then went on. Someone said her name. Lyra could not hear the answer.

She moved the keys into a straight line, then scattered them because the straight line made her feel ridiculous.

Ada was cutting a shirt into strips when Lyra arrived. She had put a basin on the floor to catch the dust and a second chair beside the window for herself. Lyra had brought bread and cheese. Ada took them, cut the bread, and gave her the smaller knife.

“You can do the sleeves.”

“What are these for?”

“Bandages. The woman next door takes them to the place keeping the man from the lighter.”

“Tarn.”

“Is that his name?”

“It’s what was on the dispatch.”

“Then perhaps.”

Lyra sat. The cloth had been washed so often that it tore easily once the edge was cut. Ada showed her the width and asked her not to make the strips too long. Long strips tangled in the boiling pot.

They worked for a while without speaking. Lyra had expected Ada to ask about the departure. When she did not, Lyra found she wanted to tell her.

“Aria is at the cutting house.”

“I heard.”

“The transfer went well.”

“Good.”

Ada folded a strip twice and put it in the clean basket. The simplicity of her answer annoyed Lyra. It also made it impossible to quarrel with her without inventing a grievance.

“Tesset has left my employment.”

Ada looked up then. “She left?”

“I dismissed her.”

“Oh.”

“Have you something to say?”

“I was thinking she won’t be in the office if the washer comes with news.”

“She can come to me.”

“I told her she could come to either of you.”

Lyra pulled the cloth too hard. The strip narrowed halfway along and ended in a point. Ada took it from her and put it in a separate pile without comment.

“There is no new sighting?” Lyra asked.

“No. The woman who saw the sleeve has gone to work farther along the landings. I’m asking through the people who send washing there.”

“I can have someone look.”

“You have.”

“I can have someone look again.”

Ada rested her hands on the cloth. “All right. But don’t tell them to ask the same man the same question every morning. He’ll give an answer just to end the asking.”

Lyra knew that. She had used it. She nodded.

Outside the window, two women were lifting a bed frame through a neighboring doorway. The frame would not turn. One woman insisted it had come in that way. The other said that did not oblige it to come out. Ada got up, went to the window, and suggested taking off the side rail. They tried it. There was a silence, then a shout of thanks.

“Was that your idea?” Lyra asked when she sat down.

“Jorin’s. When we moved in.”

Ada picked up the knife. She did not smile, but neither did she begin crying. The recollection had given her something useful for the women across the lane. Lyra stayed until the shirt was finished and they had eaten the bread. When she left, Ada sent a bundle of strips with her for the injured man, and asked whether someone at the Opera House could spare another clean pot.

“I’ll find one,” Lyra said.

That evening, Lyra took the small practice frame from the cupboard. One hinge was loose. She tightened it before setting the weight beyond the moving face. There was no use making a working hold what a screw ought to hold.

She mixed brine in a shallow bowl and laid her left palm against the fixed wood. Her right hand touched the little door. The old ache in the arm grew while she checked the direction of the cord and the pull of the weight. Maeron had once tapped her knuckles with a stick for reaching beyond the place she could sustain. She could hear the tap more clearly than she could hear his voice.

She moved her hand nearer the hinge.

The first hold failed when her fingers shifted. The weight drew the door open, and the cord slapped the frame. She reset it. On the second attempt she kept contact and felt the pressure settle into the prepared lines. The door held for the short interval she intended. Then she released it and let the weight fall onto its pad.

She was smiling. The realization embarrassed her, alone in the room. She did the working once more, because she liked the exact moment when force became something she could feel along the bones of her hand.

Afterward she dried the frame and put it away. The arm would need rest. She bound it lightly, heated water for a drink, and went through the towboat notices again. This time she set the dates in order. The first had arrived while Aria was still in the measuring room. The next had been prepared before the renewed transfer hire. Somebody had been choosing the ground of a later argument while she kept returning to the same door.

At the bottom of the last notice was an invitation to discuss continued access. It named the Salt Prince’s landing and an hour on the following day.

Lyra put it beside the lamp, where she would see it when she woke.

Chapter 12 Terms of access

The towboats were not under repair.

Two lay at the Prince’s landing with their lines coiled and their crews eating in the shade. A third was drawing a loaded lighter into the navigation canal. Lyra watched its stern settle under the pull, then looked at the notice she had brought. The company had not claimed that all its boats were damaged. It had said their use was subject to maintenance priority. She could admire the wording without forgiving the man who chose it.

Seldo stood a little behind her. He had asked whether she wanted a second guard, and she had said no. He had come in a clean coat. The stain from the fish remained at one cuff, darker where somebody had tried to scrub it out.

“Wait here,” she told him.

“Inside would be better.”

“For whom?”

“For me, if something happens to you.”

She looked at him. He met her eyes this time.

“At the door,” she said.

The Prince received her in an open-sided store facing the water. The space had been swept so thoroughly that the pale dust stood in a line beyond the threshold. Crates occupied one end. At the other, a table had been set between two chairs, with no food on it and a jug that might have held water for a less exact host.

A foreman stood beside the table with his right hand wrapped in cloth. The wrapping was wet. The Prince held the man’s wrist while he inspected the fingers protruding from it.

“You can still close it,” he said.

The foreman closed the hand. His face whitened.

“And open it.”

He opened it.

“There. A broad range of useful motion.”

The Prince released him. There was a rasp beneath his breath that continued after he had finished speaking. Lyra could not place its rhythm against the ordinary movement of his chest. He wore the preserved skin of his father loose across his shoulders. One edge rested against the table, with a little crease at the place where living skin would have yielded to an elbow. Salt had gathered at his collar in grains too large to be sweat.

The foreman stepped back. The Prince lifted the lid of a little box beside the jug, and the man’s eyes followed it with such naked hope that Lyra looked too. From her angle she could not see inside.

“When the crane is clear,” the Prince said, closing it.

The foreman tucked the injured hand beneath his arm. Lyra pulled the spare stool away from the wall with her foot.

“Sit,” she told him.

He looked at the Prince.

“By all means,” the Prince said. “We have a guest who cares for your comfort.”

The man sat, holding the injured hand in his lap. Lyra knew she had provided the Prince with a small amusement. She preferred it to leaving the man standing.

“My lady,” the Prince said.

She put the notices on the table. “Your boats appear to have survived the maintenance review.”

“Some of them.”

“The one assigned to our meal hasn’t.”

“It remains perfectly sound.”

“Then you can send it.”

“I can.”

Lyra sat. The chair was placed so that the light fell directly in her eyes if she looked toward the water. She moved it a quarter turn. The Prince watched her do it, then sat opposite her.

“I had hoped we might begin with the terms,” he said.

“You began with the boats.”

“They make the terms less abstract.”

He had brought no clerk. The paper lay ready beneath a smooth stone, its corners uncurled, as though it had not spent a moment waiting. Lyra took it without asking. The first page was a schedule of services: towage from the outer stores, access to two loading places, pump hire at a fixed rate, emergency machinery assistance from his maintenance crews. Several rates were lower than the coalition had obtained before the dispute. The fixed hire would spare her having to find a new boat every time a master learned there was an argument at the Opera House.

She read the quantities. They were plausible. Too much would have made the offer easier to discard.

“The fourth entry,” she said. “That pump is too small for the west store if the drain backs.”

“Then substitute the next size.”

“At the same hire?”

“For the first season.”

“You have the larger machine available?”

“Yes.”

She thought of the excellent pumps she had seen at the poor landing, of their polished fittings and the white stone carried away with dredging waste. He could supply what he offered. That was part of the difficulty.

“The second loading place needs a wider turn for grain carts.”

“It will be widened.”

“Before the agreement begins.”

He inclined his head. “You are welcome to inspect it.”

He made these concessions without the theatrical patience of a person giving away nothing. They would cost him work, perhaps an afternoon and two men’s wages. In return he expected something worth more.

Lyra turned the page.

The berths came first. For the term of the agreement, incoming cargo for the Opera House would be assigned a place by his harbor officers. Those officers would have priority use of the quay during high demand and authority to direct unloading to another place if maintenance required it. The agreement renewed unless both parties released it. The wording made her permission necessary to end her own participation and his permission necessary to end his control.

“How long?” she asked.

“Long enough to justify maintaining the works.”

“You have written no last day.”

“Days arrive without our assistance.”

She did not look up. “The cargo would wait wherever your officer put it.”

“Where a boat could reach it.”

“And if the officer needed my berth for your cargo?”

“Your cargo would be handled at another.”

“At my cost.”

“Somebody’s.”

She turned to the next clause. Labor engaged through the Prince’s companies remained subject to those companies’ recall. No signatory could receive, conceal, reassign, or assist the removal of a worker whose term had not been certified complete. Disputes went to a named officer appointed from the companies. The officer’s finding was binding while a further petition was heard.

“Here,” Lyra said.

The Prince folded his hands.

“You mean people who leave.”

“I mean people who have not completed their work.”

“Who decides that they have completed it?”

“The record.”

“Your record.”

“Usually the person supplying the work keeps it. You do the same.”

The foreman shifted on the stool. A drop from his wrapping struck the floor. Lyra glanced down and saw a little pink water spreading through the swept dust.

“What about a man taken from a damaged lighter?” she asked.

“If he belongs to one of the companies, he should be returned to it for treatment.”

“The treatment appears to have left him unloading an empty boat.”

“People persevere in habits. It can be a desirable quality.”

“And a man who cannot tell your officer that he wishes to leave?”

“The officer can read his engagement.”

Lyra set the second page beside the first. She did not say Jorin’s name. The Prince had not offered to identify anyone, and the paper contained no hidden route to a missing man. It was a way of making every future rescue a breach of an agreement she had signed.

“Remove the labor clause.”

“No.”

“Limit it to ordinary paid engagements, terminable by either party with notice.”

“No.”

“The berth assignments require the agreement of our keeper.”

“They require knowledge of the harbor.”

“Our keeper can have both.”

The Prince’s lower jaw moved slightly, a correction to the shape of his face. Salt broke from the seam beside one ear and fell against the cloak. He brushed it away with the back of his finger. The rasp continued.

“You have dismissed a very capable clerk,” he said.

Lyra kept her hands flat on the paper. “You should employ better informants. She was capable before you learned her name.”

“I have no need of her. My clerks generally remain where they are put.”

“You are welcome to the arrangement.”

“I am offering to extend its advantages.”

She could see the usefulness of the first page so clearly that for a moment she wanted to tear off the others and make the world accept the separation. A reliable pump, a boat when grain waited, a place from which cargo could move without Odel’s men charging at every turn. She knew exactly how many hours of labor those promises would save. She had dismissed the person who would usually turn those saved hours into paid days and meals.

“The services are worth buying,” she said. “I’ll pay for them.”

“You would like to purchase the parts that require my continuing effort while retaining the right to obstruct my people.”

“I would like a towboat.”

“You have had towboats. You have also taken rooms, workers, records, and a valuable source of income from me. I think we should now have an arrangement that lasts.”

There was no accusation in his voice. She had heard him sound more displeased when a man brought the wrong lamp. He considered the list a description of property moved without settlement.

“The crystal is gone,” she said.

“I noticed.”

“You won’t obtain anything by threatening the receiving house.”

He looked toward the canal, almost bored. “I have offered you boats. You keep proposing less profitable uses for my time.”

Lyra watched him. If he had intended an attack there, he would not have told her. But nothing in the terms led to the quarry. His pressure was laid along the working quays, around machinery and cargo and the places people needed to enter every morning. She had spent months making Aria the center of a protection the Prince had been going around.

She pushed the papers back.

“No.”

“To which amendment?”

“To the agreement.”

He did not take the papers. “You will find the present interruptions expensive.”

“I have begun.”

“And the other providers will discover reasons to be cautious. Their machinery is costly. Their families prefer them uninjured.”

She stood. Seldo shifted at the doorway; she could see only the edge of his coat. The foreman remained on the stool with his hand clamped against his stomach.

“Send that man to someone who can dress the wound,” Lyra said.

The Prince turned his head toward him. “You see? You have made an impression.”

Lyra left before she gave him another.

Sera met her in the upper receiving court, where three carts waited for a cargo that had not arrived. The carters had found chairs and were sitting together beneath an awning. They would be paid for the hours. Lyra knew this before the nearest man raised his fingers to show how long they had been there.

“I refused,” she told Sera.

“The entire agreement?”

“He would remove nothing that mattered.”

She gave her a copy she had asked the Prince’s clerk for at the outer gate. The clerk had been waiting there after all, out of sight of the negotiation, with the duplicate already prepared. Sera spread it on the broad back of a barrel, read through the labor clause twice, then turned back to the pump schedule.

“That rate is going to make refusal difficult to explain.”

“You could send them to the man with the bandaged hand. He was there for the discussion.”

Sera lowered the pages. For the first time since the departure, they looked at one another without immediately finding Aria between them. Lyra was too tired to welcome the change properly.

“What can you pay for?” she asked.

“Some alternate towage. Not a permanent second operation.”

“What can the council provide?”

“An order directing people who don’t have boats to assist us.”

Lyra laughed. Sera smiled, briefly, then tucked the agreement into her case.

“Serafine has boats,” Sera said.

“Serafine would like the files.”

“She has asked for the agreed custody.”

“The files are useful.”

“I know you find them useful.”

Lyra looked away. They had nearly managed a conversation. She went to the nearest cart and asked the driver whether he could take a half load over the lower harbor road. He said he could, provided she wanted the cart as well as the cargo to arrive in separate pieces. She asked which part of the road was worst. He described it with enough detail to establish he had driven it recently and disliked having survived.

“There is a stone footway by Cinder Basin,” Sera said when Lyra returned. “Too narrow for these carts. Small barrows use it.”

“And a landing?”

“The repair slip below it.”

“Harrow?”

“Yes. It has been let for storage since the outer timbers failed.”

Lyra knew the name. She had passed it on the water, a low broken place with weeds growing from a roofless shed. It faced the lower harbor side of the basin, beyond the easiest reach of the Prince’s berthing officers. Getting to it by water would mean dealing with another person’s passage. Getting cargo away from it would mean smaller loads and more hands.

“Who owns it?”

“A widow called Sova and her husband’s brother. They disagree about almost everything except the need to be paid.”

“Can either let it?”

“Together they can. I don’t know what they’ve agreed between themselves.”

“Then we should look before deciding what we can afford.”

Sera glanced at the waiting carts. “We should release these first.”

Lyra paid the hours already used and sent them away with a date for an answer, not a promise of cargo. The nearest driver counted the coins twice. She bore it more patiently than she would have borne sympathy.

The lower harbor road was as bad as the carter had claimed. A wheel had bitten through the edge of a broken drain cover, leaving one board tilted across the rut. Lyra and Sera had to step onto the footway, where a woman with a basket of mended shoes made them turn sideways to pass. Below it, the water was stippled with wind.

Harrow Slip lay beyond a low store with three doors of different widths. The larger gate was closed with a chain. Through it Lyra could see the old cradle, one rail intact and the other lifted out of line where a support had failed. The shed roof had collapsed at the far end, leaving a dry patch nearer the road where someone stacked firewood under cloth. There was a landing face that could take a small boat at the right water, and a stone sill broad enough to build out from.

It was worse than she remembered from the canal. It was also there.

She tested the gate with her left hand. The chain held. A woman came out of the store carrying a ring of keys, saw Sera, and stopped short of opening anything.

“It’s not let,” she said.

“We came to see whether it could be,” Sera answered.

“Pellan will tell you it can. He doesn’t get to say by himself.”

“Neither do we,” Lyra said. “Can we look from here?”

The woman considered that, then nodded. “From there costs nothing.”

Lyra crouched to see beneath the lifted rail. The stone supporting it had settled into soft mud. They could leave the damaged cradle unused if they found a safe place for feet, a place to fix a hauling line, and a route to the footway. The work would have to stay clear of the damaged roof.

“Does the lower landing hold at ordinary water?” she asked.

“The stones do. The timber doesn’t.”

“And the wall?”

“It’s older than me.”

“That doesn’t make it sound.”

“No,” the woman said. “You’ll have to bring someone who knows walls.”

Lyra liked her for that, which was no reason to trust her about the rest. She walked along the public side of the gate, measuring the distance by eye. Sera waited until she returned.

“Odel would have to arrange the passage,” Sera said.

“I know.”

“The Ragman’s price may erase the saving.”

“There isn’t a saving. We are paying to have somewhere else.”

The woman with the keys watched them. “If you want a price, come when Pellan is here. I won’t hear afterward that I let him keep a share of something he didn’t agree to.”

“We will,” Sera said.

Lyra remained at the gate after the woman went inside. The wind pushed water against the broken timbers with a hollow clapping sound. Across the basin, someone had hung shirts from a window above a workshop. A boy was trying to retrieve one that had slipped from its peg, using a pole much too short. The cloth moved every time he nearly reached it.

“We can put the first load ashore there,” Lyra said, pointing beyond the failed cradle. “Small boat. Hand carriage to the stone path. No carts until someone mends the drain cover.”

Sera looked past the landing to the narrow water approach. “Odel may have a different plan for that boat.”

“Ask her to meet us tomorrow. A working rate, not a festival courtesy. I’ll bring the cargo weights.”

Sera took a page from her case and wrote the message against the gatepost. Lyra held the case for her while the ink dried. The little exchange felt unexpectedly familiar, and for an instant she looked toward the footway as though Tesset might be waiting there with the missing figures.

She would have to find them herself.

On the walk back, Lyra stopped at a smith’s stall and bought two sound ring bolts. They were not enough for a landing. She checked the shanks for flaws, paid the stated price after one unsuccessful attempt to lower it, and carried them home wrapped in a piece of sacking. At the office she put them beside the Prince’s offer, cleared a space on the desk, and began copying the weights of the cargo waiting offshore.

Part Three The Outer Landings

Chapter 13 The repair slip

Odel brought her own chair. She carried it down the stone footway with the seat against her shoulder, put it beside the broken windlass, and tested the ground with one heel before sitting. Lyra had expected a representative of the Ragman to arrive with somebody to make a demand look reasonable. The chair was more irritating.

“You can stand if you like,” Odel said.

Lyra sat on the windlass drum. A tooth of rust caught her coat. Beyond them Harrow Slip descended between two stone cheeks to a basin of brown weed, rubbish, and four feet of water. A boat could enter at the upper half of the tide. Whether a loaded boat could leave again depended on what lay under the weed.

“I need passage for two lighters,” she said. “Cargo in, empty boats out. People on foot along this way.”

“What cargo?”

“Oil. Meal. Rope. Whatever I can persuade somebody to buy after they’ve seen where I’m landing it.”

“Powder?”

“No.”

“Live animals?”

“Not this week.”

“Your men carrying weapons?”

Lyra looked at the narrow break between the sheds. It admitted a cart if the carter first got down and folded the shafts together. Someone had chalked a child’s crooked boat on a shutter. Beside it, much lower, was the Ragman’s mark.

“Some,” she said.

Odel took a folded sheet from inside her waistcoat. “They can carry them. They can’t stop anybody else walking through. No examinations of bundles, no orders to the houses, no putting your sign above his. You miss the agreed water and you wait. You don’t send men ahead to clear it for you.”

“And if somebody stops us?”

“You bring me a name and a door. I won’t have somebody’s house pulled apart because a boatman was slow with a rope.”

Lyra laughed once, without pleasure. “I’m paying for passage I can ask you to discuss.”

“You’re paying for a passage that has people living on either side of it.” She opened the sheet on her knee. “You could have asked them separately.”

Lyra could. The first household would have wanted a roof patch, the second a cousin released from a different employer, the third enough money to frighten every house after it. Odel’s price was more than the passage was worth on a good week. This would be a bad one.

Odel listened while Lyra worked the figure down. She moved on the charge for the footway, held to the charge for boats, and gave Lyra nothing when she asked for permission to carry at night. Lyra wanted the hours because the Prince’s men would be watching daylight water. Odel wanted the people in the houses to be able to sleep without a strange crew knocking at their doors.

“Two nights to clear our first delivery,” Lyra said at last. “You choose the watch. I pay them. No right after that.”

Odel scratched the side of her nose. “No carts.”

“Hand barrows.”

“Wheels wrapped.”

“Have you ever pushed a loaded barrow with its wheels wrapped?”

“Often enough to know why you’d rather not.”

Lyra agreed. Odel wrote it down, then made her read the part about the second night. The permission ended at dawn whether or not the cargo was clear.

While Lyra signed, a woman emerged from the shed behind them carrying a kettle. She poured into a chipped cup for Odel and held out a second to Lyra.

“Sova,” she said. “You can talk to me now.”

“I thought you were waiting for your brother.”

“My husband’s brother. He’s here.”

From inside the shed came a man’s unhappy cough.

The room had once held sailcloth. Its long table was scarred with knife work and stood on blocks above the damp. Pellan sat at its far end with his boots carefully removed from a puddle beneath his chair. He had a soft, well-kept beard and the expression of a man preparing to be accused. Lyra had seen better beginnings to an agreement.

Sova set down the kettle. “He says I’ll let the place fall into the water if he doesn’t watch me.”

“I said the north cheek was moving.”

“It’s been moving since your brother was alive.”

“It won’t stop out of respect.”

Lyra put her cup between them. “Who can rent me the slip?”

Both said, “We can.”

Odel came in with her chair. She did not smile, which improved Lyra’s opinion of her.

They had the original division of ownership and three later papers. Sova owned the shed and her husband’s share of the slip; Pellan had inherited the remainder from their mother. Neither could sell the other person’s ground. Both could make a nuisance of its use. Lyra read their boundaries, asked where the drain ran, then went outside and made them show her.

The north cheek had moved. It had opened a crack wide enough to accept the edge of a finger. A clump of grass grew from its lower end. She would not bring a laden cart near it.

“You want the right to repair?” Pellan asked.

“I want the right to stop a boat breaking itself while I unload it.”

“Repairs can become an ownership claim.”

“Then write that they don’t.”

He considered that until Sova said, “She hasn’t asked you to marry her.”

Pellan flushed. Lyra bent to examine the old anchoring iron, allowing him time to recover without looking at her. Its head had rusted into the stone. The eye was still sound. She put her left hand through and pulled, feeling the force settle into the buried shank.

She wanted it. She wanted the narrow shed with a floor that could be lifted, the slope that would let a small hull come clear of the water, the poor patch of ground where she could put something heavy without waiting for three people to approve. She straightened before that want reached her face.

They agreed a short rent, renewable at the same figure once. Further renewals could cost whatever Sova and Pellan chose to ask. No part of the rent counted toward purchase. Improvements remained when she left unless they could be carried away without damage. She made Pellan sign beside Sova and asked Odel to witness it. Pellan wanted a copy before anybody touched the windlass.

“You can stand here and watch me write one,” Lyra said.

“I would rather sit.”

Odel rose and gave him the chair.

Esten arrived next morning with a plumb line, a flat iron bar, and Dannet, who carried most of the tools and a paper packet of something he would not put down in the wet.

“Breakfast?” Lyra asked.

“Quinces. For my sister.”

“Does she live in the slip?”

“Other end of the footway. If I give them to her first she’ll tell me what’s wrong with her stove, and I’ll be there when you sail.”

He tucked the packet high on a shelf and took off his coat. Esten had already lowered himself into the old winch pit. He was a broad man with a pale strip of scalp through his dark hair and spectacles that he removed whenever he needed to see something close. He held them by one earpiece while he stared at the axle.

“This is dead,” he said.

Lyra crouched beside the pit. “Can it be made less dead by afternoon?”

“Yes. By replacing it.”

“I paid you to inspect it.”

“And now you know.”

Dannet lowered him the iron bar. Esten put it under the axle housing and leaned. Rust cracked along the underside. The movement exposed a split through the bearing block that Lyra had missed.

“You pull on that,” he said, “and it will take the cheek of the housing with it. No lifting. You could hold a boat against a soft fender with it, if you were aboard the other boat.”

Lyra stood. The first lighter would come on the afternoon water. She had paid for a hoist at the Opera House and now had nothing to hoist with.

“We carry,” she said.

Esten looked at the slope, the quay, the distance to the shed. “You carry a great many times.”

“I’ll pay a great many people.”

“You need a gangway they can pass on, and a rail on the water side. Dannet, fetch the long level.”

They spent the morning making the path wider. Berris sent timber, two men, and a note specifying which pieces she expected back. Esten marked out a temporary timber platform spanning the broken stones, kept well away from the moving cheek. Dannet knelt in the mud to thread rope underneath it. Lyra held the boards while the others secured them, and when Esten ordered a board moved she moved it without arguing, enjoying the bluntness of a task that changed when she put her hand to it.

By noon the platform would bear two people abreast. The rail consisted of rope through uprights. Dannet pushed against it with his hip until an upright loosened.

“That one’s mine,” he said, before Lyra could ask.

He pulled out his own work and did it again. Esten watched once, nodded, and went to look at the drain. He inspected everything with the same attention, whether it would earn him more money or merely delay his supper.

Sova brought bread and a pot of soft cheese. The men sat on the new platform to eat. Lyra remained standing until she found herself holding the bread too tightly in her right hand. The scarred forearm had begun to ache. She changed hands, sat with her boots over the edge, and let the water move below her.

Dannet had saved the unbruised quince from his packet. He rubbed it on his sleeve and cut it into thin pieces with his work knife.

“You can’t eat those raw,” Esten said.

“I can.”

“You can eat nails. It doesn’t make them lunch.”

Dannet offered a slice to Lyra. The fruit was hard and sour enough to make her jaw hurt. She ate it anyway. He grinned, pleased to have found somebody who would.

“What does your sister do with them?” she asked.

“Stews them until they forget what they were. Her husband likes soft things.”

“That’s not what you said when he fixed your boots.”

“Esten remembers everything except where he puts his spectacles.”

Esten touched his breast pocket, found them there, and looked relieved. Lyra laughed. For a few moments she could hear the different conversations along the platform without having to answer any of them.

Then Daven called from the water that they had a problem.

The lighter behind Brindle rode lower than it should. Its master had accepted six additional casks of lamp oil after Lyra’s cargo was loaded and put them where they blocked the after hatch. The sacks could not be reached without moving the casks. The casks could not be landed on the temporary platform at the weight they carried.

“Take them back,” Lyra said.

The lighter master held up both hands. “I’ll miss the water.”

“You should have considered that before you took them.”

Daven rested his elbow on Brindle’s rail. “He can come alongside me. We can shift two to my hold, take the meal through the space, then put the oil back.”

“And charge me.”

“You can have the argument while we’re shifting it.”

She wanted to make the lighter master suffer the whole inconvenience. The tide was still rising; every moment of pleasure she took in his embarrassment reduced their time to unload. She sent him aboard Brindle with Daven and a warning that if he dropped anything she would take its value from his hire.

The first sack came ashore with a wet corner. The second split. Fine meal spilled down Dannet’s trousers into his boot. He stood motionless while two men caught the sack between them, and for some reason that made Lyra angrier than the casks had.

“Wrap it,” she said, sharply enough that he flinched.

“I’m trying to keep the rest inside.”

She fetched the sailcloth herself. They rolled the damaged sack into it, carried it up, and set it in the shed apart from the dry goods. When she returned, Dannet had emptied his boot and was rubbing the meal from between his toes.

“I wasn’t blaming you.”

He looked up. “It sounded very similar.”

She crouched to pick up a scrap of sacking. “Yes.”

He put his sock on. It took a while because the damp fabric clung to his heel. Then he stood and went back to the gangway.

All afternoon they carried. Once the lighter had risen against its remaining cargo, they adjusted the gangway. Later, as the tide turned, they adjusted it again. Lyra took sacks until Esten caught her trying to hide the way her right hand had begun to open under a load.

“Go to the shed.”

“I can carry with the other side.”

“And twist your back so we have two pieces of you to work around. Go count something.”

She almost told him who had hired him. He had turned away already to call for another rope.

In the shed, Sova was chalking the loads on the wall. She handed Lyra the chalk without comment. The little dry room had grown warm with people going in and out. Meal dust settled on everything. Lyra counted sacks, stopped a porter from setting a wet one against a dry stack, and found that her hand hurt more now she had nothing heavy to distract it.

Sera arrived shortly before dusk. She stood outside until a pair of men carrying a cask had passed, then came in with mud to the tops of her shoes.

“It works,” she said.

“It takes twice the hands and three times the time.”

“Will it work tomorrow?”

“If you send the same hands.”

Sera looked at the chalk wall. “I can pay a proportion of the landing cost from the houses receiving these goods. They agreed to the ordinary charge. I need something exact before I ask them for more.”

Lyra gave her the sheet on which she had begun writing the day’s costs. Sera read it beside a stack of oilcloth, turned it over in case there was more, then looked at the wall again.

“Who is doing the work here?”

“We all are. You can carry a sack if you want.”

“This work.” Sera touched the sheet.

“I am.”

“Today.”

The thought of Tesset arrived between them so plainly that Lyra could have slapped it. She put the chalk down.

“Hire whomever you like. They answer to you.”

“For the independent traffic, yes.”

“Then there’s no difficulty.”

Sera folded the paper. Outside, Dannet was asking whether he could leave in time to take the quinces to his sister. Esten asked him if he planned to leave the large auger in the boat all night. Dannet swore softly and turned back.

“There will be difficulties,” Sera said. “I won’t pretend otherwise.”

“You could wait to tell me about them until tomorrow.”

Sera watched her a moment, then put down the wrapped food she had been carrying. “I brought these before I saw your figures. You needn’t look for a condition.”

There were hot onion cakes inside the paper. Lyra could smell the pepper. She had not known she was hungry until she picked one up.

“Thank you,” she said, and Sera took one too.

They ate standing at the door. The final sack came up the platform under a lantern, its shadow moving far ahead of the men carrying it. Daven waited while the lighter master inspected the empty hold and searched for some last objection to leaving. The man’s voice drifted over the water, tired and indignant.

Lyra watched the boat back away. Its stern cleared the broken stone by the width of a hand. When it had turned into the channel, Dannet sat where the sack had rested and pulled off his boot once more.

She stayed after the others had gone. Sova locked the shed and left her the spare key attached to a piece of yellowed bone. The rent allowed night watch, and Lyra intended to have one. For tonight she could do it herself.

The sound of the slip changed when nobody was carrying. Water touched the lowest boards in small irregular blows. Somewhere under the platform a loose fastening clicked. She walked down with the lantern and found it, tightened its lashing, then put her left palm against the sound stone of the south cheek.

She had brought brine in a stoppered bottle. She poured a little into the hollow of her hand and touched her throat before wetting the narrow practice frame she kept wrapped in her bag. It was a little larger than her two hands together, with a hinged wooden face and a peg that could be removed. Maeron had laughed the first time he saw how carefully she had joined it. He had said she would make a door fit to keep out the sea and then forget to leave herself somewhere to sit.

She remembered his hands taking it from her. She remembered wanting him to approve the joints.

The face opened toward her. She braced the frame between two boards, set a measured stone against the other side, and held frame and face with her fingers spread. She knew the weight, the direction, the soundness of the hinge. She worked until the pressure came into her palms without movement. Her throat tightened around the words. Beneath the platform the water darkened in long curved folds. She knew where the weed was caught on the piles. This seemed farther down. One fold drew under another, and she had to make herself look back at the hinge.

She held for less than a minute. Then the strain in her right forearm deepened, and she let the face open before she lost the shape of the working. The stone dropped onto the folded cloth she had put beneath it.

Again, after a rest. This time with less weight.

She wanted the next breath with an eagerness that made her smile despite the pain. The cold passed down through her breast, familiar and entirely hers to seek. When she finished she cleaned the hinge, dried the frame, and set both beside the lantern. She ate the last onion cake sitting on the platform with her boots off.

It was cold by then, and very good.

Chapter 14 The washing

Leda had pinned a sheet of spoiled printing over the crack in Tesset’s window. In the morning the light came through a list of harbor penalties, backwards and pale. Tesset lay looking at the enormous fine for keeping swine in an unlicensed yard while Leda slept with one arm across her waist.

Upstairs, Nella was asking somebody where the other shoe had gone. A child answered too quietly for Tesset to hear. Perron crossed the room above them, his step unmistakable where a board gave under his weight. Then came the sound of a chair being dragged and the shoe apparently found, because the argument became about breakfast.

Leda opened her eyes.

“They’ll come down,” she said.

“Only if they want something.”

“They always want something.”

Tesset turned onto her side, carefully untangling the arm. Leda’s hand followed her hip and settled there.

“You’re going to be late.”

“It isn’t until the second bell.”

“You’ve been lying awake planning to be early.”

Tesset kissed her. Leda tasted faintly of the mint they had bought after supper, the last of which lay on the table wrapped in paper. It had been an expensive supper for somebody who no longer had regular wages. Tesset had ordered it anyway and spent part of the walk home wanting to apologize. Leda had not let her. Now she drew Tesset closer without offering reassurance about anything.

They were late enough that Nella came down herself and knocked with the side of a cup.

“I can hear you being awake.”

“We’ll come up,” Tesset said.

“The stove’s going out. If you want tea, come up before it does.”

They sat a little longer after her footsteps had receded. Leda rubbed a thumb across the rough skin at the base of Tesset’s forefinger.

“What do they want you to do today?”

“Find out how much everybody worked yesterday.”

“Did they not write it down?”

“They wrote down what came off the boat. The people were supposed to be obvious.”

Leda laughed. “And are they?”

“Some of them. If I can catch them before they go to a different employer.”

She got up. The floor was cold. Leda watched while she found her stockings, then reached under the blanket and produced one of them.

“You could stay this evening,” Tesset said.

“I thought I had.”

“I mean again.”

“All right.”

Tesset stood with the stocking in her hand, absurdly pleased, until Leda told her to put it on before Nella brought the kettle downstairs.

At the landing, Sera had provided a table, a locked box, and a man who believed his brother’s hours should be paid to him. Tesset put the table on the side of the shed that stayed dry. The man followed every movement, arguing before she had taken off her coat.

“He’s working across town.”

“Then I can keep his money until he comes.”

“He told me to collect it.”

“Did he give you a message?”

“I’m giving you the message.”

She wrote the brother’s name on a piece of paper and put it beneath the unpaid weights in the box. The man’s expression made her feel needlessly grand. She had spent half her working life accepting a known face in place of a proper instruction, and she knew the need for wages before dark.

“Bring someone who heard him ask,” she said. “Or have him send me a word with somebody going this way. I can’t give it to you because you’re annoyed.”

He went out muttering. When he returned an hour later with his brother, the brother took the money himself and gave half of it to him. Tesset pretended to examine a damaged sack while they divided it.

She could see Lyra beyond the door, talking to Daven. Lyra wore the coat Tesset had once mended, badly, along the pocket seam. The old stitch showed whenever she put her hand inside. Tesset had expected to feel relief at having work that did not require her to wait for that hand to emerge with an instruction. Instead she wanted Lyra to come in and ask where something was. She wanted to answer before finishing the question, then resume her work while Lyra made the room feel occupied.

Lyra glanced toward the shed. Tesset bent over the pay sheet too quickly.

A little later a pair of copper weights appeared on the table.

“The wind takes everything through here,” Lyra said. “Sova uses stones. They mark the paper.”

“Thank you.”

“Sera said you’d taken the work.”

“Yes.”

Lyra looked at the locked box. Tesset waited for some remark about its key. None came.

“The independent cargo goes on these sheets,” Tesset said. “Your own deliveries need their own count. I’ve put out another book.”

“I’m capable of recognizing my goods.”

“I know. Other people have to recognize them when you’re elsewhere.”

Lyra picked up one of the weights, turned it, and put it down with its flat side uppermost. “I’ll tell Daven.”

After she left, Tesset found she had written the same porter twice. She drew a line through the second entry so hard that she tore the paper.

Ada arrived carrying a washing basket with nothing in it. She looked at Tesset’s table, at the people waiting, and began to back out.

“I can come later.”

“No. I’m nearly finished.”

Sova, who was peeling roots by the door, made a noise. Tesset accepted the correction and asked for half an hour. Ada sat beside Sova and took a knife from her basket. By the time Tesset locked the box they had peeled enough for a household twice the size of the one Sova lived in.

“I’ve been back to Isen,” Ada said as they walked. “She kept the work separate like I asked. She has somebody I should see.”

“Did you ask Lyra?”

“I asked you.”

Tesset looked at her. Ada had put the knife away carefully, wrapping the edge before dropping it into the basket. Her face showed none of the resentment Tesset had expected. She was already watching the turn toward the Shingle Steps.

“I can read the collection marks,” Tesset said. “If that’s useful.”

“I think it is.”

Isen’s two tubs stood beneath an awning that had been shortened by repeated repairs. She was pulling a wet cloth between the wringer bars while an adult man emptied gray water into a runnel. The cloth stuck. Isen swore, released the screw, and waved them back from the splash.

“That’s the last,” she said. “Except it isn’t, because I’ve got this to do again.”

Ada put down her basket and helped lift the bundle. Tesset held the loose end off the ground. They worked without discussion until the wringer moved, then Isen wiped her face on the dry part of her sleeve.

“Bora’s got the boat work now. At the red shed. She says she knows your patch.”

Ada’s hands tightened on the basket handle.

“She knows the sleeve,” Isen said. “I told her not to make it more than that.”

They found Bora laying cloths on the roof of a low drying frame. She wore a leather apron slit at the knees and had pushed a pair of wooden pegs through her hair to keep it out of her face. The red shed had faded to the color of raw meat. Its door was propped with a broken tub.

“I’m not doing the boats,” she said before they spoke. “I do the boat washing. They bring it to me.”

“Isen sent us,” Ada said.

“I know who you are.”

That took something from Ada’s expression. Tesset stepped nearer, then stopped herself. Bora wiped both hands on her apron and led them inside.

Rows of garments hung from poles overhead. Most were plain work coats whose color had become uncertain. Some had been patched with cloth too stiff to bend well at the elbow. Tesset knew the labor of washing something when its owner would wear it wet if it was not returned in time. Her mother had done that for a dyer one winter, and for weeks the room had smelled of cloth that would never dry.

Bora took a folded sleeve from a shelf.

“It came loose,” she said. “I sewed it on. They brought it back loose again. I kept this piece when I replaced it, because the man said I’d lost him a whole coat and should pay.”

Ada touched the narrow cream strips. There were several generations of them, some hand-cut straight, others curved around damage. At the wrist a dark triangle had been sewn over the original cuff. Its smallest corner was turned under twice.

“That’s mine.” Her voice was low. “That piece. I put that there.”

“Isen said.”

“May I?”

Bora nodded. Ada held the sleeve across both palms. She did not raise it to her face or try it against her own arm. She looked at a line of stitching near the elbow, then rubbed one thread between finger and thumb.

“He used to wear through here carrying wet cloth. He wouldn’t use the padded strap. Said he couldn’t feel the load.”

Tesset waited until Ada lowered it. “Which boat?”

Bora took a slate from a nail. Three boat names had been written down its side, with cuts in the wood beside them marking the bundles. She pointed to the middle one.

“Silt.”

“When?”

“Last time? Six days. Before that, eight, I think. I don’t put a date until somebody pays. You can tell them that if you’re asking for me.”

“Where do they collect?”

“Here.”

“Who?”

“A man with two fingers that won’t bend on his left hand. Sometimes a boy. Grown enough to know what he’s doing, if that’s what you’re thinking. They come with a handcart.”

“Do the same clothes always go back to the same boat?”

Bora took the sleeve gently out of Ada’s hands and laid it on the counter.

“That’s a sensible question,” she said. “No. They don’t. But this one did. The sleeve kept coming back because somebody kept putting his elbow through it. See the rubbed place? He’s catching it against something every time he lifts. I asked for a leather patch. They said the cost would come out of my next payment. So I used what I had.”

Ada stared at the small worn oval. Tesset could hear a cart outside, the wheel knocking on one loose stone each turn.

“Have you seen him?” she asked.

Bora shook her head. “I’ve seen his coat. Sometimes they bring it with the seams white, and sometimes with black grit, and sometimes it’s only sweat. I shake it before I wash. I’m not putting half the canal in my water.”

Tesset asked to see the loose dirt if there was any left. Bora looked at her with new suspicion.

“You’re not setting up a rival laundry, are you?”

Tesset laughed, surprising herself. “I can’t even wring a blanket without making a pool.”

“Then you’re better qualified than my last girl. She could make a pool with dry cloth.”

There was no useful dirt. Bora swept every day and threw the sweepings into the yard. She did have the last collection instruction, written on a scrap folded around a peg. Tesset read it twice. It required the returned bundle before the afternoon water on the sixth day, to be delivered to a lighter waiting at the outer transfer stage. The boat name was Silt, written in the same careful hand as the other two boats on the scrap.

“May I copy this?”

“Don’t take it. He’ll say he never told me.”

Tesset copied the words and the crooked identifying stroke beneath the name. She asked about the transfer stage, where it faced, whether a person on the footway could see the stern of a boat there. Bora answered what she knew and grew irritated when Tesset returned to a question she had already answered.

“I don’t live aboard it. I don’t know where it sleeps.”

“No. I’m sorry.”

Ada had not spoken again. When they went to leave, Bora wrapped the sleeve in a clean rag and gave it to her.

“I’ve shown it to him. If he still wants to charge me, a piece of cloth won’t stop him.”

Ada searched her purse.

“Put that away. I didn’t ask you to pay for his meanness.”

Outside, Ada stopped against the shed wall. Tesset stood with her, the copied instruction held flat between the boards of her work book.

“If he’s on another boat now,” Ada said, “we still know one he was on.”

“Yes.”

“And if she is wrong about the last washing, the patch is mine.”

“Yes.”

Ada opened the basket and put the sleeve inside. “Then we’ll go to Katerina.”

Katerina was in the city for Callista’s medicine. They found her in the quiet house with a bowl of water on her knee, washing a stain from the edge of Callista’s blanket. Callista sat in a chair by the open window, her shoes on a folded towel. She was pale, with the concentrated expression of a person trying to do a small thing without help.

“Let me hold it,” Katerina said.

“I can hold a cup.”

“You can. I’m offering to hold it while you move.”

Callista looked at Tesset and Ada, then set the cup down too firmly. Tea ran into the saucer.

“Everybody can watch me stand, if they have nothing better.”

“We’ve come to see Katerina,” Tesset said.

“Then help her with her washing. She’ll be at it until dark.”

Ada took the bowl. Katerina rose, shaking water from her fingers. Her face changed when Ada unwrapped the sleeve. She did not touch it until Ada invited her.

“Silt,” Tesset said, and gave her the copied instruction.

Katerina read it by the window. Behind her, Callista got up and stood holding the back of the chair. No one looked directly at her. Tesset could feel the effort of that courtesy in the room.

“The outer stage takes stone from the service boats,” Katerina said. “They exchange full baskets for empty ones when the receiving cranes are occupied. Silt used to carry cloth and small stores too. She could go under the low chain without unloading.”

“Used to?”

“I haven’t worked there for some time.”

“Can you tell us when she’ll come?” Ada asked.

Katerina looked at the date copied from Bora’s paper. “I can tell you when somebody expects her. I can go and see if they still work the same way.”

“You’ll be recognized.”

“Yes.”

Tesset waited. She remembered Katerina at the cutting house with both hands flat against her skirt, asking permission to visit with Callista on the morning after the transfer. Aria had given one held note. The permission was for that visit. Katerina had left the room with Callista when Aria ended it.

“Tell me where to look,” Tesset said. “They don’t know me.”

“They may know you from the Opera House.”

“Then they know a clerk who isn’t employed there now.”

Katerina sat at the table and pulled a loose sheet toward her. She drew the stage, the chain above it, the steps at the landward end. Her drawing was poor. She began again with a larger piece of paper, putting the boats where they would actually lie. Tesset made her mark the part that could be seen from the street.

Ada asked about Jorin. Katerina could tell her nothing new. The recovered new-assignments list still offered no destination, and the missing identifying pages had not appeared because they needed them. After a while Ada stopped asking and watched the drawing.

Callista came slowly to the table. She set her empty cup beside the paper.

“Will you be back?” she asked Katerina.

“Yes. Before evening.”

“If you aren’t, send somebody. Don’t make me listen for your step all night.”

Katerina’s hand moved toward hers, stopped, then settled over it. Callista left the hand there while they finished.

Tesset went alone to the stage. Ada wanted to come, and Tesset very nearly agreed because refusing her was harder than taking her. In the end she asked Ada to wait with the sleeve, where the people they were watching could not see what she carried.

A woman selling fried fish occupied the best place above the steps. Tesset bought a small portion and stood eating beside the stall. Below, the stage rose and fell against piles worn pale where ropes had rubbed them. Two cargo boats lay alongside. Neither bore the name they wanted.

She watched one man scratch his chest through his shirt. Another changed a basket’s rope by cutting it in the middle and throwing the old length into the water. She wanted to write down every movement, afraid that the useful one would pass while she was licking grease from her fingers. The fish seller told her to move because she was blocking a customer. She moved too far and lost the view.

Silt arrived while she was returning to it. The name was painted on the stern in black, one letter fresher than the others. The boat had a narrow green band below its rail and an iron patch on the outer face of the after post. It came stern first beneath the hanging chain, its towline slackening as the boat ahead drew away.

Three people stood in the low hold. The one nearest her raised a basket. His sleeve was patched in a broad square, not the narrow strips Ada had shown her. He turned his head and Tesset saw the dullness of his gaze, the skin cracked near his ear. She did not know him.

Somebody behind the deckhouse lifted an arm. The cuff caught against a protruding ring, exactly at the height of the worn place Bora had shown them. Tesset leaned forward. A sailor crossed between them with a coil of rope, and when he passed the arm had disappeared.
