She stayed until Silt left. The crew exchanged stone baskets, fresh water, and two bundles of folded clothing. A man with stiff fingers signed for them. When the boat moved away, Tesset saw pale grit left on the stage, bright against its old wet boards.

She went back to Ada without buying more fish. Her mouth had gone dry.

“I saw the boat,” she said. “I saw the clothing come aboard. There was a man behind the deckhouse, but I couldn’t see his face.”

Ada closed her eyes. Tesset wanted to apologize for the portion of the news she had been unable to bring, and knew Ada would have to comfort her if she did.

“Tell me the rest,” Ada said.

Tesset sat on the step beside her and opened the book. She drew Silt’s stern with its iron patch, the transfer stage, and the ring that caught a man’s elbow when he lifted a basket. Ada held down the page while she worked.

Chapter 15 The cargo change

Corven asked to see the boat before he named his price.

Lyra took him to the loft above a net store, from which Silt could be watched without leaning out. He stood well back from the opening. His chain lay inside a leather bag at his feet; the jar with the eyeless eel was wrapped in damp cloth. He had brought his own supper and ate it while they waited.

“She may have gone through already,” Lyra said.

“Then we’ll know what we don’t know.”

Below them a boy rolled a hoop into a pile of nets, retrieved it, and tried again. The hoop was warped. Every time he gave it enough force to clear the same loose plank, it turned toward the nets. Lyra watched three attempts before she wanted to go down and take the thing from him.

Corven offered her half a hard-boiled egg. She accepted it.

When Silt came through, he watched the distance between the stern and the following boat. He watched how the crew took the line, where the unaltered handlers stood, who carried a knife. Only after the boat was fast did he look at the people in the hold.

“There are six under that forward cover,” he said.

“Can you see six?”

“Four. Two more sets of feet.”

They remained until the first baskets had been lifted. The people in the hold moved as though some portion of each task had to be rediscovered between one motion and the next. One man finished setting down his basket and continued to press his palms against it. A handler struck the rail with a stick. He lifted it again.

Lyra looked for the cream sleeve. There was no sign of it.

“What do you want me to do?” Corven asked.

“Keep the handlers from closing the passage between the after hold and the stage. I go down. We bring the people we can reach onto Brindle.”

“How long?”

“As long as it takes.”

“No.”

She turned toward him.

“Daven gives us until the towboat returns,” Corven said. “After that we’re a stationary boat under a man who can put six armed people aboard. Give me a limit I can work with.”

She wanted to say she would know when it became impossible. She had been in enough passages with him to know what he thought of that assurance.

“Until the return whistle. One long call, then the towboat turns the corner. If we’re aboard Brindle, we cast off. If I haven’t come up, you come for me.”

“And the others?”

She looked down at the boat.

“They have the same time.”

Corven finished his egg and folded the cloth around its shell. “I hold the after way. I don’t pursue anyone ashore, and I don’t go beneath the forward cover unless the way out is clear. When the boat is secured at your receiving place, my job ends. If someone needs me to stand guard after, they can ask and I’ll decide then.”

He named a sum. Lyra disliked it on principle and accepted without bargaining. He made her say the terms again. Then he picked up the jar, checked the water through its glass, and asked whether they had another tide to watch the unloading.

They had four days before Silt was due back with the same class of cargo. Lyra made herself use three of them.

Ada tapped twice on the edge of the table. The second tap came almost on the first, impatient and familiar. She looked at Lyra’s hand until Lyra copied it.

“No. Closer.”

Lyra tried again.

“That.”

“What did it mean?”

“I was behind him. If he had a load and couldn’t look round, I’d do that on whatever he was holding. Then he’d know he could pass it back.”

“Does it still?”

“I don’t know.”

Lyra kept her hand on the table. Katerina stood by the window with Tesset’s drawing. She had added the times at which the stage crew went to the cookshop, where the replacement line was kept, and a mark where the handler with the stiff fingers checked the washing bundles. None of it told them that Jorin would be aboard.

“I should come,” Ada said.

“No.”

“You don’t know if he’ll hear you.”

“I know you’re the person he may need afterward.”

Ada pushed back her chair. “Don’t tell me what he needs so you don’t have to take me.”

Lyra swallowed the reply that would have made her obedience sound inevitable. Katerina looked away from the window.

“You can be at the receiving house,” Lyra said. “We have people there who haven’t met him. You can tell them which movements are new and which aren’t. If he’s hurt, you can help them keep him still.”

“If he knows me.”

“Yes.”

“And if you don’t find him?”

“I’ll come and tell you.”

Ada stood. She took the sleeve from the table and folded it along its old creases. Lyra did not know whether she had agreed until she asked what time the receiving house would be open.

It was a disused rope loft above an old curing room on high ground, rented through Sera for the rescued workers’ first care. It was nowhere near the cutting house. Avenna had found a healer who would come without demanding that the people be made presentable before she examined them. Ada knew the street. She had passed it while looking for Jorin, one of the hundreds of doors she had not entered.

Katerina put down the drawing when Ada left.

“I can make them move the bundle table,” she said. “The man who receives the cloth knows me.”

“That seems a reason for you to stay away.”

“He knows what work I used to do. He’ll listen while I complain about a return. He won’t listen to a stranger.”

“What will you tell him?”

“That a marked bundle came back short. I still have one of the old covers. The mark is real.”

Lyra looked at her. “Have you been saving it for this?”

“No. I’ve been using it under Callista’s basin.”

Katerina folded the drawing. Her fingers were steady, but she had rubbed the skin beside one nail until it bled.

“I can’t give back what I took from Aria,” she said. “I can move a table.”

“Don’t make this a payment.”

Katerina’s expression sharpened. “Then don’t count it as one.”

On the day of the transfer, Brindle carried six casks of cheap oil and three coils of rope. Daven had bought the oil from a merchant who supplied the outer stage. The delivery was real. The merchant knew nothing about Jorin and had not been promised safe passage for any of the people aboard. Daven had made Lyra pay for the cargo before he agreed to risk it.

They lay outside the chain while the towboat brought Silt in. Lyra wore a porter’s loose coat with the sleeves rolled above her wrists. The air underneath it was too warm. Corven crouched behind the casks preparing two links of his chain. He passed a short cord through the hole in his cracked shell and bound it against one marked link, keeping its pale inner face clear. He had set the eel jar in a box packed with wet rope, where it could not roll.

“I don’t want oil on this deck,” Daven said for the third time.

“Then tell your crew not to break the casks.”

“I am telling you not to cut through one because somebody stands behind it.”

She gave him a look. He returned it, entirely unimpressed.

Silt settled against the stage. The towboat’s master called across to say he would be back after taking another lighter beneath the chain. The handler waved him away. Lyra felt Corven’s hand touch her ankle once. He had heard.

Katerina appeared at the top of the steps carrying the old laundry cover. She had dressed as she once dressed for the Prince’s offices, with her hair secured and no loose edge to catch. For a moment Lyra could imagine how reassuring she had looked to people who did not know where she was taking them.

The handler with the stiff fingers saw her. He came up the steps slowly, his mouth moving before he reached her. Katerina laid the cover on the table and pointed to its corner. Whatever she said made him unfold it. He called for someone to move the oil delivery out of his way.

Brindle went alongside Silt’s stern.

The gap was less than Lyra’s stride, but it moved. She waited until the rails approached, stepped across with a coil of rope, and set it down where the deckhand indicated. Corven followed with the second coil. The deckhand had a knife in his belt and a sore on his lip. He looked bored enough to be dangerous.

“Put them by the forward hatch.”

Lyra bent to lift the coil again. In the after hold a man was trying to move an empty basket past a woman who stood against the ladder. His right sleeve had a new broad patch. The cuff below it was dark, triangular at the edge.

Jorin.

She knew him before she saw his face fully. The way his shoulder rose as he drew the basket toward him belonged to the man who had stood beside Ada carrying bundles at the wash yard. Then he turned, and the recognition became difficult. Salt had gathered in the skin beside his mouth. One eyebrow had thinned to almost nothing. He looked through the space where Lyra stood as though she were another upright he needed to pass.

She carried the coil to the ladder. The deckhand opened his mouth to object. Corven spoke to him quietly, and the man leaned closer.

Lyra went down.

“Jorin.”

He lifted the basket. Its rim caught against her coat. She moved aside before it trapped her against the ladder.

“Jorin. Ada sent me.”

The woman beside him turned her head. Her lips had cracked deeply at one corner. She held an empty basket so tightly that the wicker had broken beneath her thumb.

“Load,” Jorin said.

Lyra put both fingertips on the basket’s rim and tapped twice.

He stopped.

Above them came a small hard sound, followed by a man’s breath cut short. She did not look up. She tapped again, the second beat crowded close against the first.

Jorin’s hands moved along the basket. He offered it toward her.

She took the weight. “This way.”

His arms remained extended after she turned. Then his foot lifted and came down beside hers.

The woman moved too. Lyra could not tell whether she had followed the basket or the words. She took the edge of the woman’s basket and felt the grip tighten against her own.

“You can bring it,” she said. “Bring the whole thing.”

At the foot of the ladder lay a man with one leg caught beneath the corner of a stone hamper. His eyes followed the baskets. Lyra had missed him in the shadow. Pale grit covered his chest and had worked into the seams of his coat.

She crouched. “Can you move?”

He dragged his free heel along the boards.

“Jast,” the woman said.

It was so sudden that Lyra looked at her. “His name?”

The woman opened her mouth again, but only air came out.

Lyra tried the hamper. Its weight settled into her injured arm and sent a hot line through her elbow. She let it down slowly. The man under it made no sound.

“Corven.”

His face appeared above the ladder. Behind him the deckhand knelt with the cracked shell held at his throat, its cord drawn tight through the marked link. His mouth was wide open. No call came from it.

“One,” Corven said. “That’s all it will hold.”

“I need this corner lifted.”

“I can’t leave him.”

Lyra looked toward Brindle. Daven was watching through the gap. She held up two fingers, then pointed down. He swore at a member of his crew, shoved a boat hook into the man’s hands, and came across himself.

The next moments went badly. Daven came down too quickly and struck his shin against the ladder. Jorin started up before there was room. The woman tried to follow and jammed her basket across both rails. Lyra had to put her back against Jorin to keep him from falling, and he responded to the obstruction by trying to lift her.

“Wait,” she said. “Wait.”

His hands closed on her waist.

Daven got the boat hook beneath the hamper. Lyra braced its shaft on a folded coil and pushed with him. The corner rose. Jast pulled his knee free, then stopped with his foot still trapped.

“Keep pulling,” she told him.

He looked at her. Daven’s face darkened with strain.

Lyra seized the back of Jast’s coat and dragged him the last distance. The hamper fell with a noise that everyone on the stage must have heard.

Above, somebody shouted Katerina’s name.

Corven said, “Now.”

They got Jorin up first by passing his basket to Brindle. He followed it, stepping across the moving gap without looking down. The woman would not let hers leave her hands. Daven helped her onto the rail and took her elbow while she crossed with it held against her chest.

“Hessa,” she said to him, or to the boat, or because the movement had brought the word up from somewhere. Daven repeated it and told her to sit.

Jast could not climb. Lyra got under his left arm while Daven lifted his hips. His damaged leg dangled, the foot turned at an angle that made Lyra stop thinking about anything beyond placing it safely. Halfway up, a guard appeared from the forward cover.

He had seen enough. He came along the side deck with a hooked pole, its head low.

Corven let go of the silent deckhand and met him. Chain struck the pole, wrapped, and slid. The hook went through the loose cloth of Corven’s coat and caught his side. He stepped into it instead of trying to pull away. His shoulder hit the guard’s chest. A plank cracked sharply beneath them. Both men vanished from Lyra’s view.

She pushed Jast upward. Daven caught his belt. Together they laid him across the rail, then rolled him into Brindle’s waiting hands.

The deckhand’s voice returned in a hoarse shriek.

Lyra turned toward the forward hold.

Two faces looked out from beneath the cover. A woman had caught the edge with her fingers. A man behind her was lifting something too large for the opening. Between them and Lyra, the narrow side deck had broken under Corven and the guard. One of the planks lay across the hold. The other stood upright with its loose end striking the rail whenever the boats moved.

She could jump it. She could get across alone. Bringing anyone back would require a line, and someone below to keep them from stepping into the gap.

The towboat whistled.

Katerina came down the stage steps with the stiff-fingered handler behind her. He held her arm. She struck his wrist with the wooden peg from the laundry bundle, hard enough that he let go. Daven’s man reached her with the boat hook, turned it so she could take the shaft, and pulled her toward Brindle’s bow.

Lyra put a foot on the remaining rail.

“There’s no crossing,” Corven said behind her.

The guard lay on the deck, breathing in wet little grunts. Corven had blood on his coat and his chain in one hand. He took Lyra’s sleeve with the other.

“I can get there.”

“And then?”

The woman beneath the cover was watching her. Lyra raised her hand as though she could explain the distance with it. The woman did not move.

Daven shouted to cast off.

Corven pulled once. Lyra let him. They crossed to Brindle while the boats ground together, and Daven cut the forward line because nobody had time to untie it. The handler on the stage threw something. It struck Lyra’s cheek and fell into the water. She felt blood reach the corner of her mouth.

Then the gap widened. Silt swung against her remaining line. The woman at the forward cover still held its edge. Lyra kept looking until Daven’s shoulder came between them and he forced her down behind the casks.

Jorin tried to unload Brindle halfway through the passage. He lifted his basket, discovered it was empty, and began reaching for a rope coil. Daven’s crewman caught his wrist. Jorin pushed him so hard that he struck the opposite rail.

“Don’t take it,” Lyra said.

“I’m not letting him throw our line overboard.”

“Give him this.”

She put the empty basket back into Jorin’s hands. He pressed it against his stomach. She tapped its rim and held out her own hands. For a moment he looked directly at her. Then he looked past her, over her shoulder.

“Load,” he said.

“Yes. When we stop.”

She hated how quickly she had learned to use the word.

Katerina knelt beside Jast. She had torn the laundry cover into strips to secure his leg to a board. Hessa sat with the basket on her lap, drawing one cracked finger through a broken place in its weave. Corven held a cloth against his side. The cut was shallow, but the bruise beneath it would be deep. He refused Lyra’s offer to examine it until they were out of sight of the stage.

No boat pursued them immediately. Daven took a side channel where Brindle could turn and a larger towboat could not. They waited under a low bridge while a patrol passed across the mouth, then continued on the agreed water. Nobody spoke about the forward hold.

At the receiving loft Ada stood at the top of the ramp. She had put her basket down. When Jorin came off the boat she took one step toward him and stopped so abruptly that her skirt continued moving after she did.

“Jorin.”

He carried his empty basket up the ramp.

She walked beside him without touching him. Lyra followed carrying the board under Jast’s leg. The healer met them at the door and began asking questions before the board was fully down.

Ada tapped twice on Jorin’s basket. He turned toward her.

Her face broke open with relief. She reached for him, and his head jerked away so violently that she lowered both hands.

“All right,” she said. “All right. I’ll stand here.”

Lyra stopped in the doorway. She had imagined telling Ada they had found him. She had imagined the weight leaving Ada’s face, the room made easier by her own arrival. Jorin stood beside the wall, holding the basket as though the next thing required of him might come from anywhere.

Corven touched Lyra’s shoulder. “Daven needs his deck clear.”

She went back to the boat. They washed blood out of the boards with two buckets and a stiff brush. Daven held the lamp while she worked. By the time she looked up, Ada had closed the loft’s outer door against the evening wind.

Chapter 16 A room too warm

Ada had put the blue cover on the bed. Lyra recognized it from an earlier visit, folded on the top shelf where clean things were kept above the washing steam. Someone had mended a small hole near the foot with thread that almost matched. The cover lay smooth except where Jorin had caught one edge and pulled it into his fist.

He stood between the bed and the wall. The room was warm. Ada had kept the stove going since morning because the healer had said he should not be brought home cold from the boat. Sweat ran down the side of his face and gathered beneath the white crust at his jaw. When Lyra came in, his eyes moved to the door. They stayed there.

“He hasn’t sat,” Ada said.

“How long?”

“Since he came.”

They had brought him from the rope loft before noon. It was nearly dusk.

“Has he drunk?”

“Some. He’ll take the cup if I put it on the shelf. If I hand it to him, he doesn’t.”

Lyra set down the food she had brought. She could smell hot wool, the stove, the damp inside Jorin’s coat. The room would have been comfortable for someone lying still after a chill. Jorin looked as though he had been made to stand in a locked oven.

“May I open the window?”

Ada nodded. Lyra lifted the latch. The lower pane had swollen in its frame and would not move. She used her knife beneath it, aware of Ada looking at the blade, and put the knife away as soon as the wood gave.

Cool air reached the bed. Jorin let go of the cover.

Ada took a step toward him. “That’s better?”

He held out an arm. She thought it an invitation; Lyra saw the mistake too late. Ada touched his forearm and he struck outward, throwing her hand away. The movement drove her against the bedpost. She caught herself with a small surprised sound.

Lyra moved between them.

“Don’t,” Ada said.

“He hurt you.”

“I put my hand where he didn’t want it.”

Jorin had pressed back against the wall. He held his arm across his body, palm open, as though he expected somebody to take it.

Lyra stepped aside. The cut on her cheek pulled where its edges had begun to close. Ada rubbed the heel of her hand once, then put both hands down.

“I won’t touch,” she told Jorin. “I’m over here.”

They waited. Downstairs somebody called for a tub. Ada went to the door and called back that the small one was behind the water butt. Her ordinary voice sounded almost indecently loud in the room.

“Can we stop the stove?” Lyra asked.

“The pipe warms the drying room. If I put it out, tomorrow’s cloth will still be wet.”

“Another room, then.”

“This is his room.”

Ada looked at the bed. Lyra did too. There was a small depression in one pillow that might have been made by Ada’s own head or by a hand arranging it. The boots beneath the bed had been cleaned, their cracked leather dark with grease.

“The room behind the washing,” Lyra said. “You used to keep the empty hampers there.”

“It’s damp.”

“We could lift the matting. Open the high vent.”

“The door catches.”

“I can fix a door.”

Ada pressed her lips together. She went to the wardrobe and took out a folded shirt. When she turned back she was holding it against her breast with both arms.

“I thought he’d want his things.”

Lyra had no answer that would help move him. She waited while Ada put the shirt down, then went downstairs for a hammer.

The rear room had room for a narrow bed if the washing hampers were stacked elsewhere. They moved those first. Ada refused to let Lyra throw out the two with broken rims because she knew somebody who would mend them cheaply. Lyra carried them to the yard and wedged them beneath the eaves. When she returned, Jorin stood at the stairhead with the empty basket from Silt against his hip.

He had followed the movement of the hampers.

Ada came slowly down ahead of him, keeping the space clear. Lyra wanted to put a hand out in case he fell. She kept it on the stair rail instead.

In the back room, he set the basket down. He looked at the high vent, the open yard door, the corner where the wall met the floor. Then he sat on the floor beside the basket.

Ada leaned against the doorframe. Lyra found a cushion and placed it within reach. Jorin did not take it.

They spent the evening making the room usable around him. Lyra planed the door where it caught, working on two overturned tubs. Ada lifted the old matting and swept the dust from beneath it. They brought down the narrower of the two bed frames from the upper room, carrying it sideways through the washroom. Jorin stood to let them pass, then sat in the same place again.

At last Ada put the blue cover on the new bed. She left his boots beneath it, set a cup of water on a low crate, and went to make tea.

“Will you eat?” she asked Lyra.

“I brought food.”

“I know. Will you eat it here?”

Lyra followed her into the washing room. They sat on the bench with their backs against the cooling stove. Through the open door they could see Jorin’s hand resting on the basket rim. Ada ate slowly, looking toward the back room between mouthfuls. When Lyra offered her more bread she accepted it without speaking.

Jast’s injured leg had swollen. The healer cut away the binding Katerina had made, examined the places where it pressed, and had Lyra support the ankle while she remade it. The bone was broken below the knee. It had not come through the skin. Those were the only good things she would say about it.

“Hold there,” she told Lyra. “Keep your hand flat. If he moves, move with him.”

Jast watched the window. Whenever someone crossed the yard below, his head turned to follow the sound. Once he tried to rise. The healer put a hand on his chest and spoke until he settled, then told everyone else to stop crowding the bed.

Lyra knew how to ease the heat in a small wound. Gyllan had taught her long before she knew what she would do with the skill. She offered it now, and the healer asked exactly what she intended.

“The torn skin at the ankle. I may be able to bring the swelling down a little.”

“The swelling there is protecting the injury as well as hurting him. No farther than the skin. I want to know what the leg does before anyone changes it.”

Lyra nodded. Jast did not answer when she asked to touch his ankle. She waited, asked again, then rested a finger on the blanket near it. His hand came down over hers with frightening speed.

She went still. He held her without crushing, his skin dry and rough around the knuckles. His eyes moved to her face.

“Down,” he said.

“Yes. You’re lying down.”

“Down.”

The healer touched his other wrist. He looked toward her and loosened his grip. Lyra withdrew her hand.

“Another time,” she said.

She helped with the bandage instead. It was work she could do without needing him to accept more from her than a supported foot.

Across the room, Hessa was sitting on a stool with a shallow wooden bowl. She would drink from that. Cups seemed to puzzle her. A woman paid to attend the loft had tried three vessels before discovering it, and now resented anyone who moved the bowl to tidy the room. Hessa had let them clean the cracks at her mouth, but she would not let the basket go. The attendant had lined its broken edge with cloth so it would stop cutting her fingers.

When Lyra passed, Hessa lifted it toward her.

“Not today,” Lyra said.

Hessa held it out. Lyra took it before the weight pulled her from the stool. Empty, it weighed very little. She put it on the table between them.

Hessa looked at her hands. Slowly, she laid them flat on her knees. Her mouth eased. Lyra stayed beside the table, unwilling to spoil the small relief by moving too soon. Then Hessa looked from the empty basket to Lyra’s face, expectant again. Her fingers had begun to curl. The attendant drew up a stool and sat within reach.

Later, outside, she asked how long the room could be kept.

“As long as it is paid for,” the attendant said. “And as long as you stop people coming to stare.”

“Who has?”

“A man who says he writes notices. Two from a charitable house who wanted them gathered in the yard. Someone who heard they could lift stones.”

Lyra felt the cold rise under her ribs. “Give me the names.”

“I have one. The others didn’t tell me.”

“Tell them I want to see them if they come back.”

The woman looked toward the loft. “Would it be enough to tell them the door is shut?”

Lyra wanted the names. She wanted the man who had asked about lifting stones to know she had heard of him. The attendant was waiting for an answer she could use tomorrow without Lyra present.

“Yes,” she said. “Shut it. Send to Sera if they won’t go.”

The woman nodded and went upstairs carrying a basket of clean cloth.

Ten days after the extraction, Lyra came to Ada’s while a sheet was being put through the wringer. Ada’s hired helper had caught her sleeve in the handle and was trying to free it without admitting the difficulty. Lyra took the handle while Ada pulled the cloth loose. Nobody thanked anybody until the sheet was safely over the rail.

Jorin sat in the yard. A rope coil lay beside his foot. Ada had given it to him because he kept reaching for the long line on which the wet washing hung. The short coil had no load attached and no purpose anyone could require him to finish. Sometimes he laid it in a neat circle. Sometimes he held the end for an hour.

Today he was looking at a small white bird on the wall.

Ada saw Lyra look. “It comes for the seeds from next door.”

“Does he watch it often?”

“Sometimes. I don’t know whether it’s the same bird.”

The helper took the sheet away. Lyra put down the soap she had brought and began emptying the dirty water. Ada stopped her after the first bucket.

“You don’t have to find work every time.”

“I thought there was work.”

“There is. Sit down anyway.”

They sat under the little roof beside the yard door. Jorin could see both of them. His chin moved slightly when Ada spoke, but he kept watching the wall after the bird flew away.

“He was awake most of last night,” Ada said. “Or standing. I don’t always know the difference. I put a chair beside the bed and went to sleep in it. When I woke, he’d put the blanket over the chair.”

Lyra turned toward him.

“It might have been in his way,” Ada said quickly. “He moves things if they catch his foot.”

“It might.”

“I wanted you to say it meant he knew.”

Lyra looked back at Ada. There were new small scratches on her hands from the room’s broken hamper rims. She had bound one knuckle with thread instead of cloth because she needed to get it wet.

“I want it to mean that too.”

Ada rubbed her eyes with the backs of her wrists. “And then this morning I told him to pass me the soap, and he looked at me as if I’d spoken from under the floor.”

They stayed quiet. From next door came the rapid scrape of somebody sharpening a knife. It stopped, began again, then stopped for good. Jorin picked up the end of the rope and laid it over his knee.

“He never liked that blue cover,” Ada said.

Lyra looked at her.

“He said it was too heavy. I used to put it on when the weather turned, and he’d push it off in his sleep. I put it on for him like I was making everything just as he wanted.”

“You were making the bed.”

Ada laughed, and the laugh became a sob before she had finished. She covered her face. Lyra put an arm around her shoulders and let Ada lean into her. The warmth of her was startling. Lyra held her with the left arm because the right still ached when she gripped too long, and felt Ada’s breathing catch, settle, then catch again.

Jorin turned his head. His fingers tightened on the rope. Ada looked up, saw him, and wiped her face without moving away from Lyra.

“It’s me,” she said. “I’m just crying. You can sit.”

He remained where he was.

When Ada could speak again, she asked about the people they had left aboard Silt.

Lyra told her what she had seen. The forward cover, the broken deck, the woman holding its edge. She described the distance without making it sound larger than it had been.

“Could you go back?”

“I’ve had the stage watched. Silt hasn’t returned there. We don’t know which berth they’ve taken her to.”

Ada nodded. She had lived with the shape of that answer long enough to recognize it.

“Don’t stop looking because you’ve found mine.”

“I haven’t stopped.”

Ada believed her. That was almost harder. Lyra picked up the empty bucket and held it between her knees until Ada asked her to refill it.

At Harrow Slip, the new canvas roof leaked in only two places. Dannet had put a bucket beneath one and a large pot beneath the other, then forgotten which was the drinking water. Esten made him empty both and start again.

Lyra stood under the canvas with a merchant who had expected his goods at the main receiving hoist. He examined the wet stones, the hand barrows, the platform, and said he could not be held to the usual rate of delivery.

“You aren’t,” she said. “I’ve paid the difference.”

“The delay costs me.”

“So does talking.”

He did not like that. He liked it less when she asked whether he wanted the goods back aboard his own lighter. In the end he accepted the cargo and left two men to carry it away.

Dannet waited until he was gone before showing her the new brake shoe Esten had fitted to their small hand winch. It would not lift a boat, but it could draw a hand barrow up the worst of the slope. The shoe came down neatly over the drum. Lyra tried it once and felt the load settle.

“Whose work?” she asked.

“Mine. Esten made me do the face again.”

“Why?”

“Because it was wrong.”

She tested it under a heavier load. It held. Dannet tried not to look pleased and failed.

“I heard you were looking for a room,” she said.

He glanced toward Esten, accusing him of having talked. “A different one.”

“Sova has the one above the shed.”

“No yard.”

“You want a yard?”

“Enough for a few beans. I had a pot on the last sill, but the landlord said the water was coming through his ceiling.”

“Was it?”

“Yes. I asked if I could fix the sill. He said no, then charged me for the ceiling.”

Lyra could have offered to speak to the landlord. Dannet was already explaining to Esten how deep a trough needed to be to keep the roots from drying. Esten listened with the resigned attention of a man who had heard it before and would hear it again. Lyra left them to it.

Before she left, she walked down Mere Lane to the old cooper’s store. Its doors were broad enough for a cart, and the floor lay only one step above the lane. That had seemed a virtue when they began moving the Opera business’s reserve goods here. She could receive from Harrow Slip without carrying every sack and fitting back across the closed quay routes.

The storekeeper had stacked the oil away from the meal and put the more valuable lifting gear on timber bearers. Lyra walked between them with a lantern. There were bronze fittings she had waited weeks to obtain, two good blocks from the Opera stores, lengths of sound chain, sailcloth enough for three repairs. All would sell. All could keep her private watch paid while the disputed business sorted itself out.

The storekeeper wanted to bring the next load of barrels in against the north wall.

“Leave a way through,” she said.

“For what?”

“For me when I want something from the back.”

He showed her how much space the gap would waste. She made him leave it anyway, then helped roll the first barrel so its bung faced upward. A little water lay in the drain outside. The storekeeper said it came back on the largest tides, though it had never reached the floor in his time.

“Esten’s looking at the basin,” Lyra said.

“Good. Somebody should.”

She checked the lock herself before leaving. The key joined Sova’s on its piece of bone, an awkward pair that knocked against her thigh as she walked uphill to the rope loft with the attendants’ pay. Jast’s old coat lay in a shallow tray. The woman who had been mending it showed Lyra why she had stopped.

“It’s in every seam. I wash it, and more comes out when I turn the cloth.”

She opened a fold near the shoulder. Pale dust fell onto the tray, with small sharp chips mixed through it. One chip was flat enough to shine when Lyra turned it beneath the lamp. The white was familiar: the spoil taken from the Prince’s quiet channel, where pumps had been installed that would have served a much busier trade.

“Was this on him when he came?”

“Under his arm. Inside the folds. You could wash his face without touching it.”

Lyra picked up a chip between finger and thumb. She could not tell what kind of stone it was. She knew only that it had not come from Silt’s ordinary river gravel, and that someone had been willing to buy the discarded white blocks before she asked where they had been cut.

“Keep the coat,” she said. “Keep what comes out of it. I’ll bring someone who knows stone.”

Jast moved on the bed. She went to him before leaving. His eyes were open; the leg rested on its support. She told him she was taking one small chip from his coat, though she could not tell whether he understood what a chip or a coat meant to him now.

He looked at her closed hand.

“Down,” he said.

Lyra laid the chip on the table where he could see it. After a while his attention moved to the window. She wrapped the stone in paper and went out quietly.

Chapter 17 Under the quay

Mavet refused to identify the stone from the packet.

“I can tell you it’s pale,” she said. “You knew that when you paid me to come.”

Lyra had laid the chip from Jast’s coat beside the larger piece she had kept from the Prince’s spoil. Both lay on a dark cloth to make their edges easier to see. Mavet picked up the larger piece, turned it, and set it down with its broken face uppermost.

“Same material?”

“Probably. I’d want a clean break on each before saying even that. Where is the rest?”

“Some went to a filling yard by the lower harbor.”

“Then take me to the yard.”

Mavet was a broad-shouldered woman whose working apron had been repaired with thin leather along one thigh. She carried no hammer large enough to impress a customer. Her tools fitted in a roll she tucked beneath her arm. Lyra had found her through Berris, who complained that Mavet had once refused to carve a false joint on a stage monument because the joint would have made the whole thing look as though it ought to fall down. Berris had then recommended her for the job.

At the filling yard, they were told the pale blocks had been bought. Lyra asked by whom. The keeper told her to ask the buyer. Mavet was already walking toward a stack of broken paving behind him.

“They’re there,” she said.

“Those aren’t for sale.”

“She’s not asking to eat them.”

Lyra paid for an hour in the yard and the keeper’s inconvenience. He became helpful enough to find a bucket of clean water. Mavet chose a block with one dressed face left intact and asked Lyra to hold a mirror while she directed light along it.

The stone looked smooth until the light fell almost sideways. Then a pattern emerged: shallow paired cuts, each ending beneath the beginning of the next. In one corner, a later iron tool had struck across them and left a much coarser scar.

Mavet washed the face. She dried it with a cloth, waited, and examined it again. Lyra stood with the mirror until her left wrist began to ache.

“Turn it a little. Toward me.”

Lyra obeyed.

“That’s enough.”

“What is it?”

“Old dressing. Astroegri work, or work made by somebody trained in the same tradition. See how the second pass takes the end out of the first? They weren’t smoothing a wall by rubbing it. The face was cut to meet another surface closely.”

“A door?”

“Could be. Could be the bed under a fitting. Could be a block turned from a place where the old face had no use.”

“How old?”

“Older than this quay by a long way. I won’t give you a reign from one block.”

She chose another piece and made Lyra shift the mirror again. This one bore a shallow circular seat around a hole. The hole had been broken open by recent hammering. In its unbroken part the same fine paired cuts continued, too regular for Lyra to distinguish where one tool stroke ended.

“Fresh break,” Mavet said, touching the rough outer edge. “Somebody wanted what was inside that hole.”

“Bronze?”

“Or iron. Or a pin made of the same stone. You’re asking me to see what’s gone.”

Lyra liked her less by the minute and trusted her more. She asked what the woman could say in writing. Mavet dictated a careful account that identified the cut surfaces and the tradition of work, and refused to say anything about the place beneath the quay from which the stones had come.

The yard keeper listened from a distance. When Lyra had paid Mavet, he brought out the delivery instruction for the blocks. They had come as waste from the western service works at Cinder Basin, an earlier cartload than the spoil Lyra had seen on the barge. The cartman had signed with a cross. A second hand had added a request that any remaining pieces be held for collection.

“Who asked?” Lyra said.

“A clerk. Paid a little to keep them.”

“Have you?”

“The large ones. Those broken corners weren’t worth his cart.” He looked at the pieces they had just examined. “Or that’s what he told me.”

She paid him to retain the two broken pieces for Mavet. He wanted to know whether the money was for ownership or delay. She paid the difference and made him put them in the locked shed.

Esten was fitting a new spindle in a pump whose owner believed it ought to work because it had worked for his father. The pump stood in a cellar under a shop. Its owner repeated the father’s history while Esten put his ear against the housing and Dannet turned the handle slowly.

When the owner went upstairs, Esten raised his head.

“His father drowned this bearing twice and packed it with kitchen grease. I can’t make it remember a better childhood.”

Dannet laughed too loudly, then looked toward the stair. Lyra held the lamp while Esten finished the inspection. She had learned that interrupting him only made him start again.

He listened to the account of the pale stone while he wiped his hands.

“Western service works?” he said. “I’ve got something from there.”

At his workshop he searched three boxes before finding the fitting. It was a bronze collar with an inner shoulder unlike any Lyra had seen on the Opera hoist. Someone had drilled new holes around its outside, badly, and filed part of its rim to make it fit a standard housing. The alteration had cracked one edge.

“They paid for an adapter because somebody insisted this had to go into the pressure pump,” Esten said. “It didn’t suit the shaft. I told the foreman. He said they wanted to try it.”

“Did they?”

“Yes. Then they paid for the right part. I kept this because they said they wouldn’t pay to have the holes undone.”

“What is it?”

“A fitting that belongs in a machine I haven’t seen.”

He laid its packing board beside it. On the board, beneath a later service order, was a burned shipping mark and a painted word: Zakov. Lyra had seen enough imported salvage to recognize a broker’s sorting marks around the name. The small stamped shape on the bronze matched the one burned into the board. It showed where the piece had passed through trade, not who had made it.

“Was it new when it came?”

“No. Cleaned. Old wear under the clean.” Esten pointed with the end of a pencil. “Here. They took it out of something where the shaft moved differently.”

She turned the collar. Its inside carried a fine circle, doubled where it met a shallow socket. She remembered the dressed block in the yard.

“Would you come and look at the service reach?”

“I’ve been asking for entry to that reach for weeks.”

“Why?”

“Because the outflow gear needs a bearing replaced. And because the people who won’t let me inspect it keep asking me what it will take to certify it.”

He found the repair order. It carried an old public maintenance authorization, a later private payment for a pump, and no permission for him to enter the inner working chambers. The exterior inspection reach was still listed as abandoned. Its mouth opened from the lower harbor wall beyond the Sluice House.

Lyra traced the location with one finger. “Can we get in there?”

“At low water. Unless they’ve blocked it.”

“We’ll look.”

Esten put his spectacles on, looked at her over them, and took them off again. “I want somebody at the mouth. I want a line the whole way. And if I say the stone is moving, you come out.”

“Does it move?”

“I haven’t been in. That’s why I want the agreement before you find something you like.”

She agreed. He asked for it again in front of Dannet.

They went on a morning when the low water left three courses of the harbor wall exposed. Mavet came because Lyra had shown her the collar and paid for another day. She made no promises about entering water and wore heavy boots that would be useless for swimming. Dannet stayed at the mouth with the line, a hand lantern, and a small bell to call them back if the harbor began rising sooner than expected.

“Three pulls if you want us,” Esten told him.

“Two if the mouth’s filling,” Dannet said.

“Three.”

“Then use words for the other one. You changed them yesterday.”

Esten looked at Lyra. “This is why I bring him.”

The opening had been blocked with loose boards, one of which had fallen inward. Behind them lay a low passage with a channel cut down its middle. Lyra went first carrying a lantern. She placed each foot before putting weight on it. Esten followed with the line passed through his belt; Mavet came last, touching the wall with the back of a gloved hand.

The air smelled of old salt and wet iron. Twice they had to crouch under stone lintels. At the second, Mavet stopped them and pointed to a crack. It ran along the mortar of later repairs and did not continue into the original stone.

“Don’t lean your equipment there,” she said.

The passage opened into an inspection chamber where a narrow stair led upward toward the western culvert works. A locked iron grille barred that way. Through it Lyra could hear water, and farther off the regular knock of a pump. The newer pipework ran through a hole smashed in the side of the chamber, its bright couplings almost indecent against the old stone.

Esten crouched beside the pipe. He touched two connections, listened, then looked back at Lyra.

“They’ve put the good pump below the ordinary intake. This isn’t draining the service basin. It’s keeping something farther down dry.”

“Can you follow it?”

“As far as that opening.”

Beyond the broken hole a wooden partition had been put up, more to keep debris from the pump than to stop a person. The door in it was unlatched. Lyra pushed it open with the butt of her knife.

A work platform stood on timber feet above shallow water. Empty baskets lay on their sides. One had a strip of dark cloth caught in a splinter. The pale stone chips on the platform were sharp enough to cut through a soft sole. Lyra held the lantern low, seeing the line of a dragged load, the heel marks beside it, the place where someone had knelt.

She thought of Jast saying down. That was all she had. She would not build the rest of his time here from a word he might have meant for the bed.

Mavet walked ahead until the platform ended at three broad steps. She knelt beside the lowest one and washed a patch with water from her bottle. The paired tool cuts appeared under the mud.

“Same work,” she said. “In its place this time.”

Above the steps stood a sealed face of pale stone. Bronze fittings ran into it at intervals, some whole, some with their outer ends removed. A missing cover exposed three small toothed wheels on a common spindle, their teeth packed with green corrosion. In the center a dark crystal pane lay behind a bolted bronze rim. An engraved scale curved beneath it; the pointer had stopped hard against one end. Lyra moved the lantern. For a moment she could see her flame and the little mechanism together, as if the light had been put inside the machine.

The chamber around it was old. Its new injuries were unmistakable. Someone had cut through a later wall to reach it, removed two outer blocks, and stopped where the bronze disappeared into the inner structure. Beside the steps, a modern drill lay with its tip broken off.

Esten set down his bag. “That isn’t a pump fitting.”

“No,” Lyra said.

She had felt the change in her ears before she reached the steps. Now the sensation tightened behind her eyes. There could be only a few feet of harbor water above this place, perhaps less at the present tide. She had stood at greater depths without that slow pressure against the bones of her head.

She put her hand on the fixed stone beside the crystal. Cold passed into her palm. Under it, the strain seemed to curve back upon itself. She tried to follow that curve and felt another beneath it, folding across the first. Her fingers tightened against the wall. There was no direction she could confidently name.

“What is it doing?” Esten asked.

“I don’t know.”

“Can you open it?”

She looked at the bronze sockets. One might have accepted the collar they had brought. Its shallow double circle had the same relation to the inner shoulder. That was resemblance, not a method. The missing outer pieces might be locks, braces, or things broken away by somebody who had not understood them.

“I won’t try.”

Mavet set her hand against the wall well away from Lyra’s. “There’s movement?”

“No. Pressure.”

“From the harbor?”

“I don’t think so.”

Lyra took her hand away. The sensation remained for several breaths. She could hold a sound door against a force she understood for a little time. There was no sound moving door here, no known hinge, no reliable direction of opening. Whatever the frame contained had been made to stay contained by people whose tools she could scarcely recognize.

She wanted to kneel closer. She wanted an hour without Esten looking at the water marks, without the line leading back to Dannet, without her right arm reminding her how a smaller gate had pinned her. She put the lantern down and drew the chamber instead.

Mavet measured the recess with a cord. Esten compared the salvaged collar to an empty socket, holding it near without inserting it. The fit was close in diameter and wrong in one important shoulder. He showed Lyra the difference, then wrapped the collar again.

“Someone might have thought this one would suit that,” he said.

“Someone paid to find out.”

He looked at the pump line running through the broken wall. “Paid a great deal.”

On the left side of the lintel, shallow grooves crossed one another around an inset bronze strip. Lyra laid paper against them and rubbed carefully with charcoal. Mavet held the lower edge. The first sheet tore where it crossed a sharp corner. Lyra began again, slower, taking the whole face in separate measured sections.

The bell sounded from the passage.

Esten straightened. “Time.”

“I need the lower edge.”

“Then take it now.”

She finished while he packed. Mavet wrote the measurements on the back of the sheets in her small blunt hand. Lyra put them between dry boards, wrapped the boards in oiled cloth, and tied the bundle twice.

On the way out she looked back. The broken drill still lay beside the sealed face. As her lantern withdrew, its shadow passed over the bronze teeth and made them appear to turn.

The harbor had risen by the width of two fingers when they came out. Dannet helped Mavet over the exposed stones, then took Esten’s bag. He had a list of people who had passed above them, none of whom had stopped to look down.

Lyra sat on the lowest dry step and pulled off one boot. A chip had worked through its sole. It had not broken the skin, but it had made a place she would feel tomorrow.

“You’re boarding it?” Esten asked.

“The exterior mouth. With a warning for the rotten lintel and a fastening we can remove.”

“They can still come from inside.”

“I know.”

She owned neither the inner stair nor the works beyond it. The boards would keep a curious person from walking through the broken entrance and falling into a place she could not make safe. They would also tell her if someone came that way after them.

Dannet and Esten fixed the boards above the reach of ordinary high water. Lyra wrote the warning and put her mark beneath it. Mavet added a more exact warning about the cracked repairs, then waited while Lyra paid the rest of her hire.

“You’ll want more than me if you go farther,” she said.

“What kind of more?”

“Someone who can shore a chamber without loading the face you want to examine. Someone who knows old bronze under strain. And a way out that doesn’t close while you’re thinking.”

Lyra nodded. She put the oiled bundle inside her coat. It lay stiff against her chest all the way back to the Opera House.

In her room she unwrapped it on the cleared table. The charcoal grooves looked smaller in daylight. She copied the measurements onto a separate sheet, noting where they had guessed at distances across water. Then she wrapped the rubbing again and put it in the locked tool chest under her own bed, away from the Gallery papers.

The chest also held brine bottles, spare cords, her practice frame, and the hooks she had begun making for herself. She moved them to make room. There was no space left for the small repair knife Maeron had given her, so she took it out, oiled its blade, and put it in her pocket.

Esten came before supper. He carried no spectacles and had the distracted look of a man who had forgotten them while coming to say something he did not want delayed.

“They’ve refused the outflow inspection again,” he said. “And a notice went up at Cinder Basin while we were below. They say maintenance will be withheld unless the lane houses accept their labor terms.”

Lyra set down the cup she had been warming between her palms.

“What maintenance?”

“The kind you notice when the tide has nowhere to leave.”

Chapter 18 The basin

The first woman Lyra spoke to had already signed.

She lived above a shop on Mere Lane where her husband made coarse bowls for cookshops. The lower room held shelves of unfired clay and a table spread with shallow dishes. She had moved the best pieces upstairs after the notice appeared. The rest would have to stay; there was nowhere to put them without blocking the bed.

“They said the drains would be kept clear,” she told Esten.

“They should be kept clear anyway.”

“Then tell me why I had to send my husband twice last month to pull rags out of the street mouth.”

Esten took off his spectacles. Lyra could see him deciding whether to explain the difference between a street drain and the outflow works. The woman would know the difference. She had signed because nobody had come to maintain either.

“What did you agree to?” Lyra asked.

The woman showed her the paper. The household would obtain loading labor and small transport only through the Prince’s appointed stores. Any worker claimed as belonging to those stores was to be returned. Berth allocations could be changed on notice, and maintenance could be withdrawn if the household refused a change. The promises occupied the top third of the sheet. The rest described what would happen to somebody who accepted them.

“My husband said not to sign until he came back.”

“Where is he?”

“Delivering. He’ll be furious.” She looked at the dishes. “He can be furious upstairs if the room is dry.”

Lyra wanted to tear the paper in half. Instead she put it back where the woman could reach it.

“We’re arranging another way to keep the basin draining.”

“Before the spring water?”

“Yes.”

“Is that a promise?”

Lyra looked at Esten. He was putting his spectacles away with unnecessary care.

“It’s what we’re working to do,” she said.

The woman picked up a damp cloth and laid it over the clay. “Then I’ll keep this until you’ve done it.”

Outside, Esten led Lyra to the end of the lane. The ground fell more than she had noticed when carrying goods to the cooper’s store. Potters’ Row lay lower still. Rope Court made a shallow bowl around a blocked central drain, with doorsteps raised piecemeal by householders who disagreed over how high a doorstep ought to be.

He made her walk it all. At each change in level he stopped and pointed to the marks that ordinary water left. He showed her where rain came down from higher streets, where a buried branch drain entered the service basin, and the stone footway that would remain passable after the dock causeway began to disappear.

“Here,” he said, at the causeway’s lowest point. “This goes first. Then the end of Mere Lane. Once you have water against these doors, the people at the back can’t get out by cart.”

“How much of the district?”

“These three streets. The low stores along the basin. The court behind the potters if the water stays up. The higher houses get wet cellars, unless they have their drains stopped properly.”

“Farther inland?”

“No. The ground rises. The water has to go somewhere, but it can’t climb a hill because somebody’s angry.”

Lyra looked toward the roof of her rented store. Its broad doors faced the lane. She had made the storekeeper leave a way between the cargo because she wanted to reach the back. It would give water an equally convenient way through.

“Show me the gates.”

They went first to the eastern inlet. It connected the Salt Ward navigation canal to Cinder Basin through a short, wide throat. Boats with low cargo could use it when the barrier was raised. The Prince’s men occupied the small landing beside it. One watched Esten without moving; another set a cup down and put a hand on the rail.

Esten stopped on the public path.

“Usually they limit the water entering here once the basin reaches its working level. On the ebb, the western gate opens to the lower harbor and the basin drains. If they leave this inlet open on the spring rise, the water in the basin comes up with it. If they keep the western gate shut afterward, the low streets stay wet through the falling tide.”

“Can we close this one?”

“From that house.”

The house stood beyond the guarded landing with its door facing the canal. There was no way to reach it from the public path without crossing an exposed bridge. Lyra counted three men in sight and a rack holding more weapons than three men required.

“We open the outflow.”

“When the outside is lower. You open it before, and you’ll help them fill the basin from the other side.”

“I heard you.”

“I’m going to say it more than once.”

She glanced at him, then saw that he was frightened. He knew the works. He could describe the proper movement of every part. That knowledge had brought him here to stand under the attention of armed men while he explained how to take their possession away.

“All right,” she said. “Say it when you need to.”

The Sluice House stood at the western end of the basin, built into a shoulder of higher stone above the outflow culvert. Its upper door faced the high footway. Below that, a service entrance led down toward the gear chamber. The dock causeway passed beneath the house before descending to the low stores.

Esten had worked there when a council maintenance crew still held the keys. He knew the route from the upper door, the stair past the disused inspection grille, and the short service passage that brought a person to the working gear. The old buried chamber lay beyond the grille and below the neighboring stone; Lyra recognized the relation from the sketch she had made underground.

A man at the upper door refused them entry. Esten handed him the inspection order and waited while he read it.

“This has been replaced.”

“By what?”

“Ask the office.”

“I did. They sent me here.”

“Then ask them again.”

Lyra moved to the outer edge of the footway, where a ventilation grille looked into the top of the gear space. Through it she could see the main shaft, the pinion teeth below it, and a bright raw space where one bearing’s cap had been lifted clear of its seat. The cap hung crookedly from a single remaining bolt. A heavy stop had been keyed onto the shaft beside the gear. It was secured against the housing with an iron bar.

The man in the doorway noticed her looking.

“Get away from there.”

She remained long enough to see the gate rack descending into shadow. Then she stepped back.

“Why have you left the cap hanging?” Esten asked.

“Repairs.”

“Where’s the new one?”

“Coming.”

“The bearing underneath was worn before you touched it.”

“Then you should be grateful we’re repairing it.”

Esten folded his order, turned, and walked away so quickly Lyra had to catch him at the steps.

“He’s fitted a keyed stop,” she said.

“I saw.”

“And lifted the cap out of its seat.”

“I saw that too.” He stopped where the wall hid them from the door. “The gate can still be raised. But not with its weight dragging on that open bearing. If somebody gets at the handwheel and just turns it, the shaft will climb out of its seat.”

“How do we keep it down?”

“We take the load on a temporary purchase. Change the worn half-bearing, fit a cap, then remove the stop. After that the proper gear can carry it.”

“Do you have the pieces?”

“I started making the bearing when they first asked for an inspection. I haven’t drilled the cap because I wanted to check the holes.”

“Can you?”

He looked back at the house. “From my old measurements. I’ll make the bolt slots long enough to fit. I don’t like it.”

“Can it work?”

“Yes.”

They went down to the harbor side to check the tide marks. Esten showed her a fixed notch cut into the stone beside the outfall and a second higher mark stained by the largest recent water. A float on a line would let someone in the upper house compare the outside level without stepping onto the flooded causeway. He insisted that they watch actual falling water before opening the gate. The predicted time alone was not enough.

Lyra looked along the wall to the boarded mouth through which they had entered the older works. It was already partly hidden by the rising tide. Nothing there offered a shorter way to the gate. The inner stair was barred, the low passage would fill, and she would not put Esten’s crew through the unknown chamber because she wanted another entrance.

“We use the house,” she said.

Esten nodded. “With enough people to get us to the gear.”

At the workshop, Dannet made Lyra operate the brake until she understood what he would be doing below.

They had rigged a beam over a heavy stone and run a temporary purchase through it. The stone represented the load that had to be taken off the gate shaft. Esten stood beside the mock bearing with a piece of wood in place of the cap. Dannet controlled the hauling tail, letting the rope come and stopping it in small exact amounts.

“If I take my hand off to help him,” he said, “this moves. If this moves while his fingers are there, we have a different problem.”

Lyra pulled. The stone lifted.

“Stop,” Esten said.

She stopped pulling. The stone sank a finger’s width before Dannet held it.

“Don’t let your side go slack,” he told her. “I said stop, not give it back.”

They tried again. On the fourth attempt Esten could lift the wooden cap away without feeling the load shift. Dannet explained the hand signal he would give when the brake had taken the weight. Palm flat, then fist. No one moved beneath the load before the fist closed.

“Who else knows it?” Lyra asked.

“Anyone I show.”

“Show me again.”

He did. His hands were broad, the knuckles scraped from carrying stone. A little white meal remained in a seam of his boot despite all his complaints about it. When Esten went to fetch the real cap, Dannet asked whether the people in Mere Lane had started moving their things.

“Some.”

“My sister’s helping the woman at the corner. She’s got a bed upstairs and no room to turn it.”

“Your sister can go to the shore house if she needs somewhere.”

“She doesn’t live low. She just thinks everybody needs her to tell them how to move a bed.”

“Do they?”

“Usually.” He smiled and unwound the hauling tail from his hand. “I’m getting that room with the yard, if this keeps paying. The landlord says I can have it after the man in it gets married.”

“Has he set a day?”

“That’s the difficulty. He’s very fond of being engaged.”

Esten came back with the iron cap. He set it on the bench and made both of them look at the bolt slots. It was ugly, broad enough to cover uncertain holes, and heavy enough that Lyra understood why the assistant could not both hold the purchase and help fit it.

The gate’s own counterweight would ease the eventual lift. It would not hold the shaft safely while the bearing was changed. The temporary purchase needed its own operator; the cap needed two sets of hands; somebody had to keep the route clear. Once the gear was sound, Dannet would leave the tail and take the gate catch, preventing a fall if the lift had to stop.

Esten drew each position. He began with a quick plan, became dissatisfied, and took a fresh sheet. The sound hatch lay between the low service passage and the higher gear chamber. It opened inward into that chamber. Water gathering on its passage side would press it toward the crew, forcing it open unless the latch or something stronger held.

“The frame?” Lyra asked.

“Stone, faced with iron. It was sound last time I tested it. The hinges too.”

“Last time isn’t enough.”

“I’ll test it again if we get there.”

She touched the place on his drawing where the moving face met the frame. If Esten found both sound, she could hold against the inward force. Brine within reach, room for both hands, someone between her and any blade. She marked where she would stand while they worked on the gate.

Esten watched her finger move.

“Don’t put something in the plan that only works if everything else has already gone right,” he said.

“I won’t.”

She asked him to finish the drawing and bring it to her before the next afternoon. Then she sent an invitation to Corven describing a single attempt at the outflow and the withdrawal afterward. She included enough of the route for him to decide whether to come and specified that any agreement would end when the crew was out.

Sera had three people waiting when Lyra reached her rooms. One wanted carts. Another wanted to know whether removing tenants from their rooms would end their right to return. The third had brought the wrong street list and spent several minutes insisting that it was the one he had been given.

Lyra stood in the passage until Sera admitted her. The Coin scholar had ink on the side of one finger and a cup of tea gone cold near her elbow.

“How many boats?” she asked.

“Enough to take the crew to the house and bring people off the low stores if the causeway goes. Brindle can do one journey at a time. She can’t be in two channels.”

“Odel?”

“Her passage lets us move through. It doesn’t conjure boats. She has two in use and won’t strip the other houses to give them to me.”

“Nor should she.”

Lyra sat down harder than she intended. “I know what she should do. I know what every person I’ve spoken to should do. I need boats.”

Sera let the outburst pass. She opened a sheet from Madam Serafine and turned it so Lyra could read.

Three craft with crews: a shallow towboat and two punts. Access through the Court’s walled property to the high landing. The offer covered the forthcoming high water and falling tide; Lyra marked the line charging extra if they needed longer. She would have to argue that with Serafine herself.

Below those terms was the condition Lyra had expected since Sera put the paper down.

“All of them?” she said.

“All the Gallery material you retained.”

“They don’t get the private sheets to use themselves.”

“No. That is why the custody is divided. Evidence of wrongdoing stays available for proceedings. The private material goes under a different seal, with witnesses to its protection. We agree who can open which part.”

“Serafine will want to see it.”

“Serafine will want many things. She has accepted the division in principle.”

“And you trust that?”

“I trust a witnessed transfer more than I trust you keeping the whole collection beneath your own lock.”

Lyra looked up. Sera did not soften the statement.

She had used the Revet file and received the fitting. The memory still had a clean, satisfying shape: an obstruction, the right piece of information, the obstruction removed. Ansel had paid something for that shape without being in the room. Giving up the files would not make them cease to have been useful. It would mean that the next man who refused her could not be made to imagine his brother in a Court doorway while she waited.

“When?” she asked.

“Tomorrow afternoon. Before the boats move.”

“The water begins before afternoon.”

“The dangerous high is in the evening. Serafine’s crews are being prepared now. They won’t come through her gate on your assurance that you’ll bring the papers later.”

Lyra looked at the window. Beyond it the city was going about a dry afternoon. Someone was airing a carpet over a rail. A delivery man called for a door to be opened and waited, tapping his heel against the step. She could leave the files where they were, hire what boats remained at whatever price their owners named, and discover at the causeway that she had bought less than the journey required.

“Have Tesset start the room arrangements,” she said. “If she will. She knows the people who can take families without putting them all in one hall.”

“I’ve asked her.”

Of course she had. Lyra pressed her thumb against the edge of Serafine’s sheet until the paper bent.

“Send me the custody terms. Every seal, every witness. I want the boat hire written beside them, not in a letter someone can later say concerned something else.”

Sera reached for a clean sheet. “I’ll have them ready.”

Lyra rose. At the door she stopped, thinking of the woman who had signed the Prince’s terms because the clay could not be moved upstairs.

“The people who have already agreed to his maintenance. They need a way out without being made to prove they were brave enough to refuse.”

“I can put that before the signatories.”

“Today.”

“Today,” Sera said, and drew the street list toward her.

Lyra went back to the Opera House before dusk. The rooms were busy with ordinary deliveries, questions about shutters, a cook who needed another sack of grain. She answered what she could while walking. In her own room she shut the door and took out the keys to the Gallery collection.

She did not open the tool chest beneath the bed. The rubbing stayed in its oiled wrap with the things she would need for a different descent.

The Gallery papers filled two locked chests and the shallow ironbound case beneath her bed. She knelt and drew the case out, keeping it apart from the tool chest where the rubbing lay. Its iron edge had marked the floor. She set it beside the larger chests, then put the keys on the table.

There were faces in some of those descriptions that she had never seen, names she knew only because somebody had made them useful to threaten. She could remember where she had put the packets without opening the lids. Instead she began a list of what the witnesses would have to count.

By the time the evening bell sounded, she had cleared enough space for another chair.

Part Four Water through the house

Chapter 19 The Gallery leaves

The bottom of the large chest had begun to part from its sides.

Lyra discovered it when she tried to move the chest away from the wall. A sheet caught beneath the lowest drawer and came free with a gray edge. She set it on the desk, lifted the drawer out, and found that the carpenter who had made the chest had saved his worst wood for the places no one would examine.

Seldo knelt beside her.

“Shall I get Berris?”

“Get two smaller boxes. Dry ones.”

“For all of it?”

She held up the drawer. He took it, slowly enough that she knew he had another question.

“Yes,” she said.

He went to look for boxes.

The office had warmed in the afternoon sun. Lyra opened the window above the canal, then pushed the chair away from its patch of light. She could work with her back against the wall. The table stood less conveniently there, and she pulled it after her until one leg caught in a floorboard. For an instant she wanted to kick it. She put her shoulder against the edge instead.

The bundles had been tied by people with different habits. Some used a bow so tight it had to be cut. Others left enough cord to wrap the papers twice more. The Gallery’s original covers were thin blue pasteboard, with the person’s name written along the folded edge. Names had been crossed out and replaced without changing the papers inside. Sometimes a place survived a person. Sometimes a woman had been filed under three different men, and a reference in the fourth bundle explained which room she had actually occupied.

Lyra had begun to understand that arrangement. She could put her hand on things without going back to the index. She knew which of the neat entries concealed a threat, which were merely the Court boasting to itself, and which described something she would have wanted to know even if no use could be made of it.

When the large chest’s drawers were empty, she unlocked the second chest and carried its contents to the table. The shallow iron-bound case would come last. She had brought it from beneath her bed before Seldo arrived; it stood by the door with the key in its lock.

Revet’s bundle lay near the top.

She took it out and opened it to the page she had shown him. The paper was thick, expensive enough that the writer had used the reverse for an unrelated instruction concerning flowers. She had almost shown that side to him first. She remembered her annoyance, turning it over while he stood beside the desk. She had been afraid of losing the moment in which he understood that she could hurt his brother.

The bronze fitting had arrived. It had been fitted. Goods were moving through the receiving house again.

She closed the cover.

Seldo brought two fish boxes with new lids. He had lined them with a length of clean sailcloth. The cloth was finer than the boxes deserved.

“That’s from the reserve,” she said.

“There’s nothing else dry in the lower store.”

“All right.”

He set one beside her. “Do they get the names of our people too?”

“Their names are already in here.”

“I meant the ones we put in.”

She looked up. He meant the complaints, the accounts of injuries, the statements she had asked people to give because it was useful to have an account in their own words. They had come to her because the Court already possessed the other side of what had happened.

“Those go separately. Sera’s people will keep the statements. The Court doesn’t get to take them back.”

“Will they know who said what?”

“Where there’s a case to bring, they may have to.”

Seldo shifted the empty box with his boot. “I should tell Orrel before somebody else does.”

“Yes. Tell him exactly that.”

She wanted to add a reason why it was necessary. He was already lifting the full drawer onto the table, careful of the loose bottom. She helped him, and they worked without talking until the first box was full.

The contents required three boxes in the end. Seldo had to go down again. Lyra sat alone with the last small bundle on her knee. It would fit in the drawer where she kept her own letters. Nobody coming to count the blue covers would know it had existed; it had never acquired one.

She could remember the names. There was no reason to keep the statements themselves.

She turned the bundle over, looking for a mark that would tell the receiving clerk where to put it. On the reverse someone had drawn a little boat. The mast leaned so badly that another hand had given it a prop.

When Seldo returned she placed the bundle in the third box. He nailed the lids down while she wrote their destinations on the sailcloth left showing at the corners.

The Court’s boat master was sitting on a stool outside the dressmaker’s rooms, eating cherries from a paper cone. He rose when Lyra came up the stair, then tried to put the cone in a pocket too small to receive it.

“Keep them,” she told him. “We’re all waiting on someone.”

He remained standing, uncertain whether that had been permission to sit.

Inside, Serafine was having a sleeve altered. Her arm rested on a wooden form padded with scraps of wool. The dressmaker had pins between her lips and gave Lyra a look that forbade interruption more effectively than a guard.

Sera occupied a chair beside the window, with a folded schedule on her knee. She had brought no emblem of the Coin. She wore her ordinary coat, and one cuff had been turned back where the stitching needed attention. Lyra wondered whether Serafine had offered to have it repaired and whether Sera had refused.

“The boxes are below,” Lyra said.

“With whom?” Sera asked.

“Seldo and the carrier. No one has taken possession.”

Sera nodded and made a mark on her schedule.

Serafine watched the dressmaker draw a line along the sleeve. “I like this part. A person touches where it is wrong, and another person fixes it.”

“If she keeps still,” said the dressmaker through the pins.

Serafine held her arm more rigidly. Lyra found herself smiling and stopped trying to hide it. The woman who could make three warehouses cease receiving goods could still be made uncomfortable by someone measuring the back of her elbow.

The dressmaker removed the sleeve and withdrew behind a screen. Serafine lowered her bare arm. There was a red line where the cloth had pressed it.

“You’ve found water,” she said.

“I’ve found the people keeping it where it shouldn’t be.”

“A considerable part of this city’s business.”

“Not this much of it.”

Lyra laid Esten’s rough section of the basin on the table. It had been drawn on heavy wrapping paper. At its edge a thumbprint crossed the words the pumpwright had added after the walk. She smoothed the crease with the side of her hand.

“These streets first. The house fronts are higher than the rear doors. Once the yards fill, people who think they’re above it will have water coming through their kitchens.”

Serafine came over. She bent close enough that a loose piece of hair brushed the paper, and tucked it behind her ear with impatience.

“This passage belongs to the Court.”

“Which is why we need to use it.”

“It is occupied.”

“Then let its occupants use it too.”

“You are very generous with my property today.”

“I’ve brought something you asked for.”

Serafine’s gaze lifted. The amusement did not leave it, but it became less available to the room.

Sera stood and put her schedule beside the drawing.

“The boxes go to independent custody,” she said. “Your witness can identify ordinary administration. Private papers stay protected, and the statements remain available to the people who made them. No one leaves with a bundle.”

“I have listened to that explanation already.”

“Lyra has not heard you accept it.”

The two women looked at each other. Lyra could hear the dressmaker cutting behind the screen. The scissors made a patient sound through the cloth.

“I accept the custody described,” Serafine said. “I also expect an end to the use of my own papers against people who supply my house.”

“They stopped being only your papers when you made those people afraid of them,” Lyra said.

“And became yours when you found the fear useful?”

Sera placed a finger on the boat schedule before Lyra could answer.

“Three craft. The shallow towboat and two punts, with their usual crews. Available through the falling water after the next high tide. Their masters take navigation orders from the person you nominate, subject to the safety of the craft. They are not included in a transfer of service afterward.”

“Through the following low,” Lyra said. “If the outflow is damaged, we may have to wait.”

“The masters have another engagement,” Serafine said.

“Then pay them to miss it.”

Serafine laughed softly. She went to the door and called the waiting man in.

He had eaten the cherries. He stood with his hands behind his back, probably holding the stones in the paper. Lyra moved a saucer toward him. He did not use it.

“You have heard what is wanted?” Serafine asked.

“Most of it, madam.”

“And could you remain until the following low?”

“The shallow boat could. The punts would need relief men.”

“Why?”

“Because those men have been working since yesterday afternoon, and if they carry people on a dark canal after two nights without sleep, somebody will end up in it.”

Serafine turned to Lyra. “There. A cost we hadn’t yet discovered.”

“We can provide relief,” Lyra said.

The man shook his head. “You can provide men. I need people who have worked those punts. They run broad at the turn.”

Lyra asked where they could be found. He named the landing, and she wrote it on the corner of Esten’s drawing. Sera offered to pay the relief directly from the emergency subscription. Serafine gave the master permission to hire them, then held out the saucer without looking at it. He emptied the stones into it with visible relief.

The arrangements continued. One passage door had been blocked by scenery since a production abandoned two summers earlier. There was a key, but the key would not move the scenery. Serafine sent for the man who had ordered it stored. The man arrived with the air of someone summoned to explain an artistic failure, and brightened when he discovered that only the position of a painted mountain was at issue.
