Lyra wanted the hour back. Each little obstruction took an ordinary person’s time to remove, and she had already spent the morning looking at water marks on walls. She drew her chair closer to the table and made herself listen while the scenery man explained which frame would collapse if carried on its side.

The dressmaker brought the sleeve out once. Serafine tried to wave her away, and was told that if she left without it fitted she could wear something else tomorrow. She put out her arm. The scenery man went on speaking to Lyra. Sera wrote down the promised hour of clearance.

By the time they finished, light had left the upper windows.

Serafine dismissed everyone but the two women. She flexed the newly fitted arm.

“Do you know what I am buying?” she asked Lyra.

“A service basin your people need.”

“Among other things. If he can turn maintenance into permanent possession, every building with a wet foundation will eventually belong to him.”

“You didn’t mind while he supplied the lamps.”

“I minded several things while he supplied the lamps. I judged the arrangement manageable.”

“And Aria?”

Serafine looked toward Sera, who had stopped folding her paper.

“You kept her too,” she said.

Lyra felt the answer come up hot enough to surprise her. There were differences. She had stopped the harvesting. She had put people beside the crystal who would record what Aria asked. She had done work Serafine had not even thought necessary.

She could not say any of it without hearing the two notes in the measuring room.

“The boats,” she said. “Will they be at the passage before dusk?”

“They will.”

They opened the boxes in a room that belonged to neither woman.

The house had been lent by one of the merchants who had helped fund Aria’s transfer. Its old owner had used the lower room for storing citrus fruit. The walls still held the smell after the shelves were taken out. A long table stood beneath two windows, and at each end an independent witness sat with the written list of what was being received.

Lyra recognized one witness as a woman who had once come to Ropewalk to collect her husband’s pay. The woman recognized her too. They nodded without attempting a conversation.

Seldo brought the boxes in. He had carried the smallest himself. The nail heads had bent, and he struggled with the first until the carrier produced a tool from his belt.

“Give it here. You’ll split the lid.”

Seldo let him work.

One of Serafine’s clerks stood at the far side of the table. He had an ink stain under his thumbnail and a shirt much cleaner than his cuffs. Sera introduced him by name. Lyra made herself repeat it and then found she could not remember it when she wanted to ask him to move.

They counted the covers, checked the loose packets against the list, and opened the drawers that had been lifted bodily from the chest. Lyra described the additions made since the Festival. She showed where the new statements began and which cross-references led into private material. Her familiarity made the work faster. It also kept her beside the table long after she had intended to step away.

When Revet’s packet emerged, she reached out before the receiving woman had taken it.

“There’s an instruction on the back unrelated to the statement.”

The woman turned it over.

“I see it.”

“It shouldn’t go with his brother’s account.”

“We’ll separate it.”

Lyra withdrew her hand. The woman set the packet down within her own reach.

The smallest box held the papers without blue covers. Sera took particular care over those. She asked Lyra which had been given voluntarily, which copied from surviving Court books, and which sent by people who had not wanted their names entered elsewhere. Lyra answered until she did not know.

“I can find out,” she said.

“Give us the person to ask.”

She gave the name.

The Court clerk objected to one bundle being placed with the complaints. He said the person named had broken an agreement of service. The woman at the end of the table asked whether he was claiming the man’s original agreement or the account of the beating that followed. He looked at the cover again and withdrew the objection.

It took another hour. Lyra’s hand grew stiff around the pen when she signed the transfer. She loosened her fingers beneath the table, then held the page flat while Sera signed beside her. The witnesses each received a copy. One remained in the room. Lyra’s copy was folded, dried, and placed in the inner pocket of her coat.

“Is there anything else in your custody?” Sera asked.

The room quietened around the question.

“My business records. Copies of my own correspondence concerning the people in these files. Nothing taken from the Gallery.”

“And copies made from the Gallery papers?”

“In the second box, where I showed you.”

Sera waited.

“That’s all of them.”

The receiving woman put her palm on the nearest lid. It was a small gesture, but Lyra knew the moment at which the contents ceased being hers to order.

Outside, Seldo was fastening the empty handcart. He looked past her for the boxes, then said nothing. She stood beside him until he had the strap through its buckle.

“We have the boats,” she told him.

“I heard.”

“And the passage.”

He pulled the strap tight. “Good.”

They walked back by the lower arcade. Shopkeepers were carrying goods upstairs without closing their shutters. A woman had put her children’s shoes on a high shelf and then taken down the pair the youngest would need to leave in. Lyra waited while Seldo helped her move a basket that had caught across the doorway.

At the canal they met the Court’s shallow towboat coming under the bridge. The master from the dressmaker’s room stood at the bow, testing the depth with a pole. He recognized Lyra and raised the pole a little before setting it down again.

The boat was broader than she had expected. Its sides were scraped nearly white. A stack of folded blankets lay under oilcloth at the stern, held down by the master’s empty cherry cone with a stone inside it.

She went to the water’s edge and called out the landing he would need. He repeated it to the steersman. The bow turned slowly, gathering the reflected windows along its side, and passed beneath the next arch.

Lyra watched until she could see how it took the turn. Then she sent Seldo ahead to tell Esten it was here.

Chapter 20 Rooms ashore

Tesset had allowed for the beds and forgotten the people getting into them.

On the plan, the upper room took eighteen pallets. The pallets themselves fitted. She and Leda put down the first six, turned them as shown, and discovered that anyone sleeping beside the far wall would have to climb over two neighbors to reach the stair. Someone leaving in a hurry would put a foot on a face.

Leda stood with a folded blanket against her chest.

“You could put the children there.”

“Children need to get out too.”

“I know. I was trying to make you look less miserable.”

Tesset knelt and pulled the nearest pallet back. Its straw rasped over the boards. The room was wider than their own, bright under a sloping roof, with a window at each end. When she had inspected it she had imagined people grateful to be dry. She had not imagined the whole floor occupied at once.

“Fourteen,” Leda said, after they moved the last one.

“Thirteen. We need a place for the water.”

They looked at the corner beside the door. A jar there would be kicked over. A jar farther in would need someone to carry it over the sleepers. Tesset had brought two, pleased with herself for finding them without paying the price asked at the first shop.

Leda set the blanket down. “I’ll fetch a table. They can go underneath.”

“There isn’t room.”

“A little table.”

Tesset put both hands over her face. The first people were due before the closing bell of the market. She had told the boat master he could leave them here and take the next load straight to the shore house. He would have to come back. If he had gone up the narrow branch by then, he would have to back the punt for half its length before turning.

“I’m sorry,” she said.

Leda waited until Tesset took her hands away.

“I haven’t brought the table yet. You can still refuse it.”

Tesset laughed unwillingly, then reached for her bag. “Tell them thirteen. No more than thirteen. I’ll find somewhere for the others.”

“Take your bread.”

“Later.”

Leda picked up the wrapped piece from the windowsill and put it in the bag herself. Her fingers stayed briefly against Tesset’s wrist.

“Eat it on the way. I don’t want the version of you that hasn’t eaten finding beds for anyone.”

Downstairs, the proprietor was moving a cabinet across the little room he had kept for himself. He had consented to let people use the stair through it, but the sight of each new pallet passing his possessions seemed to add another condition to the agreement.

“No cooking up there,” he said as Tesset came down.

“We agreed.”

“Not even with a little lamp.”

“I’ll put it on the notice.”

“People say they understand, and then somebody wants tea.”

“There’s tea at the shore house. We’ll bring it in covered jars.”

That pleased him. He glanced toward the stair and lowered his voice.

“My sister says a warm jar will crack if the room is cold.”

“These are made for it.”

“Good. Only I have had that happen.”

He showed her the mark on the cabinet where something had been spilled years before. Tesset looked because it was his room, he had let them use it, and she needed him to keep letting them. The stain resembled a small country outlined in darker wood. She promised to keep the jars off his furniture.

Outside, a porter had gone to sleep on the cart beside the second load of bedding. She woke him, explained that five of the rolls had to go elsewhere, and gave him the shore house address. He asked who would sign for the extra journey.

“I will.”

He handed her his slate. She wrote the change, then crossed out the first version because she had put the wrong street. He watched her with the restrained patience of a person paid for carrying, not for being kind about the mistakes of clerks.

“You can read that?” she asked.

“Enough to ask you what it says.”

She read it aloud. He nodded, tucked the slate under a strap, and began turning the cart.

For the first time that morning she wished Lyra were there. Lyra would have found an additional room, frightened the person keeping it closed, and made the interruption seem like something she had planned to demonstrate. Tesset could almost hear the instruction: go inside and see it before you pay.

She had seen the room. That was the worst of it.

She ate the bread as she walked. The crust was hard enough to hurt the gum above a tooth she had been meaning to have examined. She shifted it to the other side of her mouth and kept going.

At the shore house, Marda had her sleeves rolled up over arms that no longer looked comfortable in daylight. She stood by the door because the heat of the kitchen was too much. A girl carried bowls to her, and she added bread from a basket before passing them on.

“Five more?” she said.

“Five places. It might be four people and a child. I need to check who can use the upper room.”

“Everyone says a child as if children take up less space. Give one a blanket and see how far they can spread.”

“I can bring more bedding.”

“Bring it. Then we’ll find out where it goes.”

Tesset put her bag on the table. Two men had brought a narrow bed through from the side room and were trying to angle it behind the stairs. One said it would fit. The other said the bed would fit but the man in it could not breathe there. Marda pointed them toward the end wall.

“Move my cupboard.”

“Where?”

“Anywhere I’m not standing.”

They moved the cupboard. The space it left was dark and smelled of damp plaster. Marda told Tesset which of the expected people might prefer it. Tesset wrote the names, then put the paper down and went to help carry the bed.

Its foot caught the doorframe. She and the older man lifted too soon, striking the polished wood against the wall. He swore without raising his voice. She apologized, and they set it down to get a different grip.

“It’s my mother’s,” he said.

“She’s coming?”

“She won’t come without it.”

The bed had belonged to his mother for forty years. Its woven support had been mended with three kinds of cord. The upper rail bore little scratches he said were from a dog she had loved and a cat she had not. Tesset ran her finger beneath the splinter she had made in the polished edge.

“We can smooth that.”

“She’ll find it.”

“Yes.”

He looked at her and began laughing, suddenly and with such exhaustion that she nearly did too. They lifted again. This time the bed passed through.

The first families arrived before the bedding.

There were eleven people where Tesset’s note said eight. Two were neighbors who had helped carry an old woman to the boat and then found they could not be taken home without delaying the next journey. The third was a boy who had been visiting a cousin and could not remember the name of the landing where his father would look for him.

Tesset sat him beside Marda and gave him a cup. He could describe the landing perfectly. There was a carving of a fish with a broken tail, a man who sold fried skins, and a woman who shouted at the man for putting his fire too close to her wall. None of that distinguished it from the next five landings.

“Is there a bridge?”

He looked at her with disbelief.

“Near the stair,” she amended. “One you can see without going into the street.”

“Two.”

That helped. She asked about the market day, and Marda interrupted to say that the fried-skin man was her cousin’s former husband and that the woman would have shouted regardless of where he put the fire. She sent a runner.

The old woman wanted to see her bed before agreeing to stay. Her son led her in with the attention of a man hoping she would notice everything except one small area of polished wood. Tesset went after them, prepared to explain. The woman put her bag on the cover, tested the support with the heel of her hand, and demanded to know where the window was.

“The light comes through the stair,” her son said.

“That isn’t a window.”

He looked at Tesset. She considered the upper room, its narrow stair, its thirteen places already promised. She could not put the bed there even if someone yielded a space.

“We can leave the yard door open while the weather holds,” she said. “From here you’ll see people coming and going.”

“Will they be coming past me in the night?”

“Not unless they need the kitchen.”

“Then they will. Nobody sleeps without something to eat.”

Her son began to apologize. The old woman waved him away and sat on the bed. She took a little pot from her bag and asked for somewhere cool to keep it. It contained a pudding she had refused to leave behind.

Tesset found a shelf. When she returned, the woman had put her shawl over the scratched rail.

A man at the front table was refusing a bowl from Marda.

“I know whose house this is,” he said.

“Then you’ll know where to bring the bowl back.”

“My brother went to him.”

Marda held the bowl. The girl’s hands hovered behind it, waiting for the next one.

“He said there was work. He said my brother would manage it.”

“What was his name?” Marda asked.

The man gave it.

She set the bowl down in front of him. “I remember.”

“I expect you do.”

The line outside the door had stopped moving. Tesset could see two people listening who had slept in the refuge when Maeron sang. One held her coat closed though the afternoon was warm. The other kept looking at the kitchen door.

Marda asked the girl for another bowl. The man had not touched his.

“You can eat it or leave it,” Marda said. Her voice had changed. “You can sit in the yard if you don’t want to look at me. But don’t stand between the food and the people waiting for it.”

He picked up the bowl and went outside. Marda continued serving until the girl took the basket from her without asking. Then she stepped into the side passage and stood with her forehead against the cool wall.

Tesset followed far enough to see that she was breathing.

“I’ll do the bread,” she said.

Marda nodded without turning.

Jorad arrived carrying a child on his back and another person’s bundle under each arm. The child was awake and furious. One of her shoes had been dropped between the boat and the landing. She wanted him to go back for it.

“It won’t be at the landing now,” he said.

“You don’t know.”

“I saw it go.”

“You should have caught it.”

He agreed that he should have. He lowered her onto a bench, set the bundles beside her, and waited until her father could come through the crowd. Tesset had never seen him with a child before. The child put her bare foot against the side of his leg to keep him from moving away.

“You’re cold.”

“Yes.”

“Are you ill?”

“No.”

“My aunt’s ill. She’s hot.”

“Then she would not like sitting here.”

The child considered this and removed her foot. Her father arrived with the surviving shoe in his hand. Jorad gave him the bundles and turned toward the door.

“Can you take a message to Lyra?” Tesset asked.

His expression shut before she had finished the name.

“There are runners.”

“I need her to know that the first house takes thirteen. The boat has to bring the others here. If the runner misses the turn—”

“Write it.”

“I have.”

“Then give it to a runner.”

She nearly said that he had just carried people through the same streets. He could see her about to do it.

“I’ll carry her people,” he said. “I won’t wear her instructions.”

Tesset held the folded notice. “These aren’t her people.”

“No. They aren’t.”

He went to help unload the next boat. She stayed where she was for a moment, irritated with him and ashamed that the irritation had arrived before she understood. He was going back into the wet streets. He simply would not do this particular thing because she had asked it in Lyra’s name.

She found the runner herself.

By the time the second punt came back from the overcrowded upper room, dusk had reached the canal. The master was the man with whom she had made the morning arrangement. He helped an old man step ashore, then came to her while his crew moved the luggage.

“I backed it all the way to the dye stair,” he said.

“I know.”

“You weren’t there.”

“No. I’m sorry. I made the arrangement wrong.”

He looked prepared for a longer argument. Finding none, he rubbed the side of his neck.

“The bowman needs replacing before we go out again. He’s done.”

“There’s relief coming to the Court’s landing.”

“For their boats.”

Tesset opened her bag. There was enough of the emergency money left to pay an additional man. She told him what she could offer. He named someone already in the yard, a former oarsman who had come to help his mother. They went to ask him together.

The man wanted his mother settled before he left. Tesset promised she would stay until that was done. It took longer than paying him. His mother had lost the packet containing her spectacles, and he could not bear to leave her unable to see the room in which she would wake. They emptied both bags on a blanket. The packet was inside a stocking, exactly where she had put it to keep it safe.

When the punt left, the relieved bowman remained on the steps eating from a bowl held between his knees. He was too tired to lift it. Tesset sat a little way above him and watched the stern light disappear.

Leda found her there.

“Thirteen,” she said. “With room for the jars, if nobody insists on being very tall.”

Tesset leaned against her leg. Leda put a hand into her hair and undid a pin that had worked loose.

“Did you eat the bread?”

“All of it.”

“Good. I’ve brought more.”

They went into the yard, where someone had lit a lamp beneath the lean-to. The displaced families had begun arranging their belongings around themselves. A woman was making everyone remove their shoes before approaching her blanket, although the whole yard was wet. Two men who had arrived separately were arguing over a shared memory of a marriage feast. The boy from the fish landing had been collected by his father and now wanted to stay.

Leda unwrapped a piece of cheese. It had been squashed flat in her bag. They ate it from the paper, taking turns with the knife.

“I was going to wear the blue dress,” Leda said.

“Tonight?”

“For the supper you said you’d remember.”

Tesset closed her eyes.

“I had remembered. Yesterday.”

“A day early. That’s something.”

“I’m sorry.”

“I know.”

For a while they ate. Tesset wanted to promise another evening and knew that if she named one too quickly it would become one more thing Leda had to remind her of. She wiped the knife on the paper and gave it back.

“I want to see you in it.”

“You have.”

“At supper. With nobody asking us for a bed.”

Leda put the knife away. “Then come home when you can. I’ll still have the dress.”

Tesset took her hand. The fingertips were rough from folding paper, a small split beside one nail covered with wax. She pressed her mouth against the unhurt knuckles and remained there until someone called her name.

The old woman with the pudding needed the stair cleared so her son could bring in another chair. Marda had come back to the doorway. Jorad was asleep sitting upright beside the child whose shoe he had lost. The child had fallen asleep too, her remaining shoe resting against his wet coat.

Tesset got up and helped move the chair. Then she brought Leda a bowl while there was still something hot to put in it.

Chapter 21 The service passage

Esten had tied the new bearing to a board so nobody would drop it through the slats of the landing. The board was larger than the bearing by half and had once formed the side of a child’s bed. A faded flower remained beneath the binding.

Lyra lifted it with her left hand.

“That end’s heavier,” Esten said.

“I’d found that.”

“People find it when they’re letting go.”

He took the board from her and set it across the middle of the Brindle. The repaired portion of the lighter’s bench showed pale against its older wood. Daven had covered the raw edge with a folded sack, but Lyra could still see where Tarn’s hand had been trapped. Daven noticed her looking.

“It holds,” he said. “Your carpenter wouldn’t leave until I’d stood on it.”

“Does she know how little you weigh?”

“She stood beside me.”

He went back to securing the poles. Lyra watched the water between the lighter and the landing. It had risen past the lower iron ring. A little weed that ordinarily hung from the stone was floating straight out into the canal, moving as though something breathed beneath it.

Esten was checking his equipment for the third time. He had put each tool on the same strip of cloth: the drift for the stop on the shaft, the short hammer, the bolts for the new cap, and the wedges that would keep the work steady while the weight was taken. Dannet knelt opposite, wrapping the smaller pieces so they would not rattle together.

“What have you forgotten?” Lyra asked.

“If I knew, I wouldn’t have forgotten it.”

“He thinks that counts as an answer,” Dannet said.

Esten handed him the last bolt. “Make certain it’s an answer you can find in the dark.”

Dannet put it in a cloth pocket and fastened the pocket inside his waistcoat. He had taken off the better coat he wore when negotiating a job. This one had a darn near the shoulder and sleeves he could turn well above the wrist. A little white thread clung to his collar. He rubbed at it without finding it.

Lyra reached over and took it away.

“Flour?” she asked.

“My sister’s mending. She says I can’t go anywhere without leaving part of my shirt behind.”

“She could make you a better one,” Esten said.

“That’s what she says about you.”

Esten laughed and stopped checking the tools. For a moment they were men preparing an ordinary repair, pleased to be working with someone whose mistakes they already knew.

Corven came down the steps with the chain bag over his shoulder. He had shaved badly. A narrow strip beside his ear had been missed, and Lyra had the absurd wish to put a finger against it. She had once watched him shave in the yard at Low Bell while Maeron told a story neither of them quite believed. There had been time to tell him where the razor had failed.

He looked at the board and the tied bearing.

“Who carries that?”

“Dannet,” Esten said. “Until we need him on the purchase.”

“He’ll need both hands.”

“That’s why we brought you.”

Corven smiled. He crouched by the edge and lowered the eel’s vessel into the canal, keeping the cord looped around his wrist. The little animal pressed against the glass. Its pale underside turned toward the last of the light.

Lyra stood beside him.

“You can leave before we go in.”

“I know.”

“Afterward there may not be time.”

“There generally isn’t.”

He drew the vessel up, wiped the outside with a cloth, and checked the stopper. Lyra had already paid him for the attempt. The money would go to a person he had named if he did not return; she knew the woman’s address and nothing else about her. He had not asked Lyra to hold the rest of his possessions.

“I won’t ask you to keep the place afterward,” she said.

“Let’s get to afterward.”

He stood and moved away to speak to Daven.

Lyra called him back before he reached the boat. There was one part of the agreement they had made by message, and she wanted him to hear it from her.

“The attempt and the withdrawal. If Esten says we can’t open it, we bring the crew out. You aren’t being hired to take the whole Salt Ward.”

“And if you change your mind inside?”

“You have the agreement.”

He considered her for a moment, then nodded. She disliked the examination and was relieved by it. He had come with a task he could name, and a point at which it would be finished. She could ask for help without pretending they had returned to the arrangement they once knew.

“Tell Daven the same,” she said. “He should hear it from both of us.”

Lyra went up the steps to look across the basin. Lamps had begun to show in the windows. One opened as she watched, and a woman shook a cloth out above the water, just as she might have done on any evening. Farther along, someone was lifting the shutters from a lower shop and carrying them upstairs. The empty openings made the building seem unfinished.

The Court’s punts waited beyond the bridge. Their relief men had arrived, but one of the original crew was still asleep beneath a blanket. Lyra did not wake him. She found the master of the shallow towboat and asked him to show her where he could turn if the footway went under.

He pointed to a widened place by the old loading posts. The boat would need room to bring its stern around. A small fishing skiff tied across it would have to be moved. Lyra sent Seldo to find its owner. If he could not, he was to move it without cutting the rope and leave it where the man could still reach it.

“He may charge you,” the master said.

“He can find me.”

“That’s what worries most of them.”

She looked at him. He was studying the turn with deliberate attention, and she could not tell whether he had meant it as a joke.

They did not take a boat all the way to the Sluice House.

The raised footway ran from the last shop behind the warehouses and crossed the narrow inlet that supplied the basin. Esten knew it as a route for bringing tools without hiring a lighter. The Prince’s people knew it too. A man stood where the parapet narrowed, with a lantern at his feet and a short spear held across the way.

“Closed,” he said.

Lyra stopped far enough from him that he had to look at her instead of the point of his own spear.

“We’re here to open the western outflow.”

“Not my instruction.”

“Whose?”

He looked beyond her at the men carrying equipment.

“You can wait at the receiving office.”

“By the time the office answers, the lower yards will be full.”

“Then you should have come earlier.”

He was young enough to take pleasure in having a sentence ready. He did not look Worn. His ears were red from the wind, and one glove had been patched with leather a different color from the rest. Lyra thought he might be cold rather than brave.

“Move the spear,” she said. “You can report that we came past you.”

He raised the point a little.

Corven set down his bag behind Lyra. The motion drew the guard’s gaze. Lyra stepped inside the reach of the spear and caught the shaft beneath his hand. She pulled it toward her as Corven took his other arm.

The guard tried to shout. Corven put him against the parapet with a force that emptied his breath instead. The lantern rolled, struck the stone, and stayed lit. Esten caught it before it went into the water.

“Don’t break his arm,” Lyra said.

“Then tell him to stop trying to break mine.”

The guard stopped. They bound his hands in front, where he could use them if he fell, and left him sitting at the broad part of the footway with Seldo. Lyra took the spear. It was lighter than she expected. She handed it back to Seldo after deciding she could not carry it and keep her injured arm useful.

“If he tries to run?” Seldo asked.

“Let him run away from us.”

The man looked at her as though she had made a mistake in the instruction. She continued along the footway before he could test it.

Beyond the turn, the Sluice House showed as a low building with a tiled roof pressed against the taller store behind it. The upper door had been barred from within. Esten pointed to the stair descending at its side.

“That gets us into the service passage. The gear room is at the end.”

“And the passage takes water?”

“It always takes some. There’s a drain into the basin.”

“Which will run backward.”

“Once the basin’s high enough.”

Lyra looked at the dark opening below. The water had not reached it yet. A smell of old oil came up the stair, familiar enough to make the place seem less dangerous than it was.

Esten put the lantern on the third step and took off his coat. Dannet folded it without being asked, then did the same with his own. Corven left his outer coat on the landing beside them. Lyra kept hers. She had the brine vessel in its inner pocket and did not want to spend another moment deciding where things belonged.

They went down with the bearing between Esten and Dannet. Corven followed the lantern. Lyra came last, carrying the bag of smaller tools.

The passage was high enough to stand in, but the pipes along one wall made people turn their shoulders. Moisture shone on the stone beneath them. Lyra heard the others’ breathing become careful as they adjusted to the cold.

Her own breath eased. The air close to the wall felt almost soft against her face. She had been holding herself stiffly on the open footway, disturbed by the sky without admitting it. Down here she could feel where the room ended.

Something moved at the far side of the lantern’s light.

Corven raised one hand. Esten stopped. The bearing board struck the wall with a dull knock.

“Maintenance,” Esten called.

“I know what it looks like.”

The voice came from behind a low pile of timber. Lyra could see two men now, one standing back by the iron-faced hatch, the other holding something across his chest. A third shape stood between them without moving. Its head was tilted too far toward the sound of Esten’s voice.

“We need the gear room,” Esten said.

“You’ll get it when the order comes.”

“It has.” Lyra stepped beside the lantern. “From the people whose houses you’re filling.”

The nearer man smiled. His teeth were ordinary and bad. “They should have paid to keep them dry.”

Corven took the cracked shell from its wrapping. Lyra saw the man’s eyes follow his hand.

“Down,” she said.

The crossbow made a small noise. The bolt struck the wall at the height her throat had been. Dannet dragged Esten backward by his collar; the board hit the floor and the new bearing shifted against its ties.

Corven spoke into the shell. A sound began in the crossbowman’s mouth and stopped while the mouth remained open. Corven moved past Lyra with the chain gathered in his left hand.

The still figure came forward. Its foot struck the bolt on the floor and broke it without altering its step. Lyra caught the lantern and lifted it clear as the figure reached the place where it had stood.

Esten was trying to pull the bearing out of the way. Dannet got underneath one end and dragged. The crossbowman dropped his empty weapon and drew a knife. He could not call the warning he wanted to give the man beside the hatch.

Lyra had a narrow blade in the sheath at her waist. She drew it and found that her hand had gone very steady. The steadiness frightened her less than she thought it ought to. She moved along the pipe wall while Corven caught the advancing Worn with his chain.

The weight struck him sideways. He went down on one knee. The links tightened, each wet mark briefly visible in the lantern light. The Worn’s arms kept moving. Its body had stopped, pinned with one shoulder against the timber, but its hands continued reaching for whatever work had been given them.

The knife man came at Lyra.

She put the lantern behind the pipe and used her left hand to catch his coat as he passed the point of her blade. He was stronger than she expected. He pulled her toward him, and her right shoulder struck the stone. Pain ran from the old wound to the base of her thumb.

She drove the blade low, where his coat had opened.

He made no sound. That was Corven’s working, she remembered, as if it were something she needed to account for before she could understand what her hand had done. She pulled free. The man sat down abruptly against the wall, holding himself below the ribs.

At the hatch, the third defender pushed a long iron bar toward Esten’s face. Esten ducked. The bar struck the bearing board. Dannet reached around it, seized the man’s sleeve, and tried to pull him off balance.

The man let go of the bar.

Lyra saw his other hand come out with a short, square-headed hammer. There was no room to call anything useful. He struck Dannet beside the ear. Dannet’s knees gave. The defender hit him again as he fell.

Corven released one end of the chain and came across the passage. The Worn jerked free behind him. Lyra kicked the loose bar under its feet and caught Dannet’s shirt to pull him away from the hatch.

His weight was wrong. He did not help at all.

Corven drove the hammer man through the opening into the gear room. Something heavy struck the floor inside. Esten was calling Dannet’s name. Lyra pulled him another pace and knelt beside him where the lantern reached.

The skin at his temple had broken inward.

She placed her fingers beneath his jaw. She knew where to look, how little pressure to use. She had taught people that pressing too hard could make them fail to feel the very thing they wanted to find.

There was nothing.

She moved her hand and tried again.

“Lyra,” Esten said.

“Give me a moment.”

The Worn was trying to step over the bar. Its foot caught again and again. Corven came out of the gear room, saw Lyra on the floor, and stood between them while she put both hands against Dannet’s head.

The little healing she knew asked for a wound whose edges could still answer one another. She had closed cuts, eased damaged flesh, stopped blood long enough for someone better trained to arrive. There was a terrible softness under her fingers. She did not speak the words.

“He’s gone,” Corven said.

She looked at him with such anger that he drew back half a step. Then she understood he had said it because Esten could not.

Esten crouched beside the body. He put his palm on Dannet’s chest as though preventing him from getting up too soon.

“He was on the brake,” he said.

Lyra could hear herself breathing. It sounded louder than the struggle at the timber. She wanted to remain on the floor until something in the room changed without her making it change.

A little water came over the toe of her boot.

She looked toward the passage mouth. The thin stream entered along the wall, finding every hollow between the stones.

“We take him inside,” she said.

They carried Dannet through the hatch and laid him on the dry platform beside the gear. Esten refused to leave the board behind, so Corven brought it in with the bearing still tied to it. The bindings had held. A chip was missing from the painted flower.

The hammer man lay against the far wall. He was breathing through his mouth. Corven took his hammer and knife away, then tied his wrists with a length cut from the carrying cord. He did it without looking toward Lyra for instruction.

The gear chamber had a narrow upper door that led to the barred stair. Esten climbed enough of it to lift the bar, and Seldo came down carrying the guard’s spear. He had sent the young man away from the house. The man had run.

“There’ll be others,” Seldo said.

“Hold the upper door,” Lyra told him. “Don’t go out after anyone.”

He saw Dannet as he turned. His mouth opened, then closed. He leaned the spear against the wall and helped Esten lift the tool bag onto the work platform.

Corven pushed the iron-faced hatch shut. A built-in catch held it for the moment. Through its lower seam, water began seeping into the room. Lyra crouched and examined the frame. The stonework around it was sound, the wood behind the iron face swollen but unbroken. The hinges were on their side. Pressure from the passage would push the whole thing inward.

She touched the frame with her left hand, then the moving face with her right. The old wound pulled, and she shifted until her elbow rested against her ribs. She could hold it if she had to. She could not do much else while holding it.

Esten had unwrapped the repair pieces. He stood looking from one to another.

“What changes?” Lyra asked him.

He did not answer.

“Esten. What changes without Dannet?”

The pumpwright looked at the body. “He was to take the tail while I had the cap off. Then catch it when we let the load onto the new bearing.”

“Can Seldo?”

“If he listens.”

“He will.”

“And somebody has to hold the upper door.”

Corven lifted the hammer he had taken from the defender. “I can see it from there.”

“You’re needed at the lower one,” Lyra said.

He looked at her hand on the hatch.

“Not yet.”

They moved the tools. Esten made Seldo repeat the release signal, then repeat it with his eyes shut. It had been a little gesture when Dannet made it, something almost beneath notice. Seldo had to be shown which two fingers moved and which hand held the rope. He was embarrassed by how difficult it seemed while everyone waited.

“Again,” Esten said.

Lyra went to Dannet and took the wrapped bolt pocket from inside his waistcoat. His sleeve caught beneath him. She freed it carefully, as though he might complain that she was pulling the seam. She put the bolts where Esten could reach them and covered the damaged side of Dannet’s head with the clean corner of the tool cloth.

Outside the upper door, somebody began striking the wood with the butt of a weapon.

Corven moved to the stair. Seldo repeated the signal correctly this time. Esten nodded once and put his hand against the old bearing to judge its heat.

Chapter 22 Send the boats

The remaining bolt would not turn.

Esten tried the short wrench, then the long one, then put them both down and asked for a lamp close enough to see the head. Lyra held it over his shoulder. He scraped the packed salt from the edges with the point of a small tool.

“They’ve driven it crooked.”

“Deliberately?”

“I wasn’t here to watch.”

She waited while he cleaned the last side. His hand was shaking. It stopped when he rested his wrist against the housing.

“Can it be moved?”

“Yes. I’d like to move it without breaking what it goes through.”

He took the hammer from the cloth and tapped the wrench. The sound filled the chamber. At the upper door, the men outside began striking in answer. Corven remained on the stair with his shoulder against the wood. He could not see what they were using on the other side. The bar shifted in its brackets with each blow.

Seldo stood on the work platform holding the tail of the rope purchase. His right foot was braced against a block Esten had placed for him. He watched the pumpwright’s free hand so intently that he did not notice the rope cutting into his own palm.

Lyra put the lamp in a wall recess and went to him.

“Loosen your grip. It isn’t under the load yet.”

“He said not to let it slip.”

“You won’t have a hand left to hold it when it matters.”

He eased his fingers one at a time. The rope retained a dark line where they had been. Lyra tore a strip from the unused part of the tool cloth and wound it around his palm. He looked at the cloth over Dannet’s head and quickly looked away.

“This part’s clean,” she said.

He nodded, ashamed that she had noticed what he was looking at.

From the lower passage came a dragging noise. It had continued after the hatch was closed, stopped for a while, and begun again. Something out there was moving the iron bar along the stones. The Worn they had pinned in the timber had found it, perhaps. Or the wounded crossbowman was trying to get himself up the stair.

Lyra could not distinguish which, and did not open the hatch to find out.

Esten struck the wrench again. This time the bolt turned far enough for everyone to hear it.

“There,” he said.

The relief in his voice was so naked that Lyra smiled at him. He did not see. He was already deciding how to support the old bearing when the cap came free.

A voice called through the upper wall.

At first Lyra thought it was one of the men at the door. Then she heard her own name with an impatience the attackers had not yet acquired.

“Lyra! The window!”

Corven looked up the stair. “Eda.”

Lyra climbed past him to the little inspection loft above the mechanism. The place had a louvered opening toward the canal. A bird had nested in it at some earlier season, leaving a mass of dry stalks too large for the gap through which it must have been carried.

She pushed the nest aside. A feather caught on her cuff. Through the slats she could see Eda standing on the neighboring store’s roof, one hand hooked over a chimney. The runner had taken off her shoes to climb. They hung by their laces from her belt.

“What?” Lyra called.

“The far footbridge has gone under. Rope Court can’t get out that way.”

“They were told to leave.”

“Some did. Some went back for people.”

Lyra tried to see beyond the roof. The low houses were hidden by the warehouse between them and the court. She could see the line of the water along the nearer wall, higher than it had been when they came in. A little boat was working against it, its oars catching the edges of the passage.

“How many?”

“They don’t know. There’s a sick man upstairs. Two houses with shared stairs. People are on the joining roof.”

“The punts?”

“One’s taking the last from Mere Lane. The other can’t get past the washing frames without somebody moving them. The master wants the towboat to hold at the wide place. He can bring people out in loads if there’s something to put them on.”

That meant the Brindle. The Court’s towboat could hold the channel against the crosscurrent while the punts moved in and out. Daven’s broad lighter could carry the people onward.

The Brindle was meant to be clearing Lyra’s stores.

She had moved the best of the surviving goods into the rented building beside the basin when the ordinary receiving routes closed. The place was cheap because its loading floor stood low. It had seemed sensible to keep the inventory where she could move it through Harrow Slip without paying for a second carry across the city. She had checked the old water marks before agreeing the rent. They were below the floor.

Eda shouted something else. Lyra had missed it.

“Say it again.”

“Daven says he can make one run before the turn. If the towboat takes him out.”

One run with the goods. Then back for the people, if the bridges and the upper rooms held long enough. Daven’s judgment was not ridiculous. He knew the lighter; he knew how much of the load was already aboard. Most of the goods in the store were paid for. The people in Rope Court could reach their roofs. One safe journey could preserve the means by which she paid the guards and kept the quay from passing back into other hands.

She heard a crash below. Corven swore. The upper door held, but something had come through its highest panel and struck the stair.

“Find Daven,” she told Eda. “Bring him where I can speak to him.”

Eda pointed down. The lighter’s bow was already coming into view beyond the lower roof.

Daven brought the Brindle close enough that Lyra could hear the crew cursing the narrowness of the channel. The boat had crates in its bow and a roll of heavy canvas lying over two barrels. One of the crates bore her receiving mark. She recognized the shape of the larger barrel beside it: lamp oil, expensive enough now that she had ordered the cask wrapped against casual theft as well as damage.

He stood below the loft with his head tilted back.

“The towboat could get us out,” he called. “It’s not a long run. Put these ashore and come back.”

“And the people?”

“Two punts can start bringing them to the wide place.”

“Where will they stand while you’re gone?”

He looked toward the water and did not answer immediately.

“On the boats,” he said.

“The same boats meant to bring the next ones.”

“I know what you’re asking. I’m saying I can save this.”

She leaned against the slats. The wood pressed a pattern into her forehead. Below, the water made a clean bright line along the lighter’s side. Its ordinary movement did nothing to convey how quickly the floor of a house might disappear beneath it.

“How much have you loaded?”

He told her. The canvas represented nearly all her dry reserve. There was a chest of replacement tools she had spent months assembling, items easy to buy one at a time and almost impossible to replace together during a dispute. The rest remained inside the store. If she sent him away now, the lower stock would go first, and the things carried upstairs would remain in a building with no one to keep it.

Seldo appeared at the foot of the loft ladder.

“Esten wants you.”

“Tell him a moment.”

“The weight’s coming onto the purchase.”

She looked down at him. He had left the rope secured as instructed, but Esten needed to know who would be there to take it when the bearing moved. Dannet would not have had to climb the ladder to ask.

“Go back. I’m coming.”

She turned to Daven.

“Clear the lighter.”

“Back into the store?”

“Where you can leave it without blocking the way. Don’t spend the journey finding a dry floor.”

He had heard, but he waited for her to finish.

“The towboat holds at the widened reach. The punts bring people out of Rope Court. You take them on from the punts and go when you can carry a safe load. Nobody goes back for these.”

She pointed at the crates. Her hand was shaking now. She lowered it before he could look from the hand to her face.

“The tools?” he asked.

“Take what your men need to work the boat. Leave the chest.”

For a moment she disliked him more than she disliked the Prince. He had made her say it separately, as though she had failed to understand that the chest contained tools rather than useless weight. He was not wrong to ask. She wanted him to stop being careful with her losses.

“You have the instruction,” she said.

He nodded. “All right.”

He called to the men in the bow. They protested when he told them to put the crates ashore. One said they had just carried them out. Daven repeated the instruction without mentioning Lyra, and after a little more argument they began making room.

She stayed at the slats until she saw the first crate leave the boat. It landed badly on the stone. A corner split. The man who had carried it looked up, waiting for her to object.

“Go,” she told him.

He went for the next one.

Below, Esten had tied the old bearing up with two loops of rope. The cap lay on the platform beside the hammer man’s bound feet. The defender was awake now. He followed Lyra with his eyes when she came down, then looked past her toward Dannet.

She wanted to ask him whether he had known where to strike. Whether he had hit a man there before. Neither answer would help Esten put the new half-bearing beneath the shaft.

“What do you need?” she asked.

“That end of the board. Keep it flat while I undo the ties.”

She took it. Esten cut the binding, lifted the metal, and let her support enough of its weight to turn it without striking the teeth of the gear. It was colder than she expected. She had been holding warm paper, warm wood, the things people touched. This had lain out in the weather.

The shaft rested in the rope purchase. Seldo held its tail with his wrapped hand. Esten asked him to ease it a fraction.

He eased too much.

The shaft dropped against the new bearing with a noise that made everyone turn. Esten snatched his fingers away. Seldo hauled back, face white.

“Hold,” Esten said. “Just hold.”

Seldo held. Esten looked at his fingers, flexed them, and set his hand on the shaft again.

“It’s all right. Nothing’s gone.”

Lyra watched his mouth. He had bitten the inside of his cheek; blood shone briefly on his lower lip. She did not tell Seldo that. She helped move the bearing into its seat.

The hammer man said, “You won’t keep it.”

No one answered.

“Whatever you open. They’ll take it back.”

Esten reached past his boot for the iron cap. The man drew his foot away, expecting to be struck.

“I need that space,” Esten said.

Lyra set the cap in position while he found the first bolt. She turned it until the thread caught cleanly, then left the tightening to him. Her arm ached from the shoulder to the wrist. The cold in her hand had not taken that away. When she lifted it, the cap retained a pale wet shape where her fingers had rested.

Corven called from the stair.

“They’re trying the lower way.”

The blows on the upper door had stopped. That was worse than the noise, because it meant the people outside were choosing another place from which to come at them. Lyra went to the hatch. Water stood a hand’s breadth against it on the far side; she could feel the pressure when she touched the wood.

“How long?” she asked Esten.

“The cap, then the stop. After that, we wait for the marks.”

“It looks high enough outside.”

“High isn’t falling.”

He kept tightening the bolt. Until the harbor fell below the basin, opening the outflow could let more water in.

Once the cap was secure, Esten eased the shaft’s weight onto the new bearing. He and Seldo moved the purchase to the lifting eye on the gate rack and took up its slack, leaving it ready to assist the gear when the stop came free.

She climbed to the loft once more. Through the slats she could see the towboat holding below the wide turn. The Brindle had gone. A punt came toward the towboat with people sitting so close together that at first she thought it carried sacks under blankets. Then someone lifted a child, and the shapes separated into bodies.

The master at the towboat’s bow held his pole level for a moment, pointing to the line he wanted the punt to take. Its crew altered their stroke. The boats met without striking. A woman reached from one to the other with both hands, then refused to move until someone took the bundle behind her.

Lyra watched the crossing. There were still lamps in the upper windows of Rope Court.

She could not see her store from here. She knew where it was, beyond the low roof and the turn, with the tools on the landing and the torn crate open to whatever water reached it. By morning a stranger might be using one of the little things she had chosen for a purpose. She found that thought harder to bear than the possibility that it would all be ruined.

“Look at the mark,” Esten called from below.

She forced herself to look at the stone post beside the Sluice House. He had shown her the fresh notch before they entered. The water moved over it, withdrew, and came back. She waited through several small waves.

“Still covered.”

“Watch the next one.”

She watched. Her knees hurt against the loft boards. Beneath her, the hammer struck the keyed stop, once, then again. The whole chamber seemed to listen for the sound of it coming free.

The next wave withdrew from the notch and left it shining.

“I can see it.”

“Not yet. Let it stay.”

Lyra remained at the window. Behind the lantern’s reflection on the water, the stone darkened as another wave crossed it. She said nothing. She counted breaths, lost the count when someone cried out in the passage below, and began again.

At last the notch stayed above the water between one movement and the next.

“It’s falling,” she called.

The hammer rang. The stop came out of the shaft with a sharp metal sound and struck the platform.

From below the loft Corven said her name.

She knew from the way he said it that she had no more time at the window.

Chapter 23 The closed hatch

The iron face moved inward a finger’s width before the catch stopped it.

Water came through the opening in a flat sheet. Corven put his boot against the bottom rail and leaned into it, but the next blow lifted the heel of his foot from the floor. He looked toward Lyra without speaking.

She took the brine vessel from her coat.

The stopper had swollen. Her right fingers would not close firmly around it. She caught it in her teeth, twisted the vessel with her left hand, and felt the stopper come free with a taste of old salt and wax.

“How long?” she asked Esten.

He was standing over the newly seated shaft. “We lift and see whether it runs.”

“How long?”

“Longer if you ask me again.”

She went to the hatch.

There was dirt packed along the lower edge of the frame. She scraped it away with the heel of her hand and poured brine over the place where wood met stone. Corven held the face steady while she found the contact. Her left palm lay against the fixed frame. Her right rested on the iron skin of the door.

For an instant she could feel only cold metal and the painful effort of keeping her arm straight. Then the force on the far side acquired a direction. It came toward her, through the moving face, into the side of the chamber in which she stood. The frame stood around it. She had to keep those two facts separate.

She spoke the words she had practiced when the only thing at risk was a bucket of water on the wrong side of a yard door.

The hatch ceased moving.

Corven eased his weight from it. Lyra could not turn her head far enough to watch him go without pulling the fingers of her left hand away from the frame. She stayed as she was and listened to his feet crossing the chamber.

“Now,” she said.

Esten called for Seldo to take the tail. Rope slid through the blocks. The shaft moved with a heavy, reluctant noise, then stopped.

“Again.”

A man on the other side of the hatch began shouting. Lyra heard the word open clearly. The rest came through iron, wood, and rising water.

She looked at the place where her right hand touched the door. Her skin had whitened around the nails. A little line of brine ran down her wrist and disappeared beneath the cuff.

“Lift it clean,” Esten said. “Don’t pull sideways.”

The gear moved another short distance. Something deep below the chamber answered with a scraping sound. Lyra felt a vibration in the soles of her boots.

“It’s catching.”

“Where?” Corven asked.

“Under the third tooth. Give me the drift.”

The man’s shouting changed. She could hear more of it now, or he had put his mouth against the gap by the frame.

“Water. There’s water back here.”

“We know,” Lyra said.

“Open it.”

She kept her hands in place.

Someone struck the door near her palm. The sound arrived through the iron into her wrist. She did not feel an additional push. The working held the whole face against the direction she had given it, indifferent to whether the pressure came from a shoulder or the basin behind it.

“Wait,” Esten said. “Don’t let the tail run.”

Seldo’s reply was too low for her to hear.

“Both hands, man. Both.”

The chain above the shaft rattled. Corven had gone to help with the purchase. That left the upper stair unwatched for the moment. Lyra looked toward it without moving her hands. The bar remained in its brackets. A narrow splinter of daylight showed through the broken panel, gray now instead of gold.

At the hatch, the man said, “My brother’s down.”

She closed her eyes briefly.

“Can you lift him?”

“There’s nowhere to put him.”

The door was sound. If she released it, the water and the men would come into the chamber while Esten had the gate suspended on the purchase. Corven could meet them. Seldo could perhaps hold the tail alone. She would have to leave the frame to help the man lift his brother, and she could not know what the others would do behind them.

She heard a wet cough almost beside her ear.

“Wait,” she said.

The hammer struck the drift. One sharp blow, another smaller one. Something fell against the platform.

“Take it,” Esten called.

Lyra’s injured arm began to tremble. She bent her knees to bring her shoulder nearer the contact without breaking it. Her back pressed against the cool return of the wall. Down here, with the stone so close, part of her body was comfortable. Beneath that comfort came the impossible impression of a great tentacle settling around the chamber, leaving a small enclosed space for her to breathe. She hated the comfort and used it.

The man at the hatch stopped striking.

“Please,” he said.

She could have said she was trying. She was not trying to open the hatch. She was holding it shut.

The shaft turned. A sound larger than anything in the room came up from below, water finding a space from which it had been excluded. The gear jumped under Esten’s hands. He let it move and shouted for the catch.

Seldo looked toward Dannet. The gear kept moving. He turned back and dropped the catch.

Its teeth struck, slipped once, and seated. Esten kept his hands on the lever until the load had settled into it. Then he bent over the mechanism with his head down, breathing as if he had run a long way.

The outflow opened beneath them.

The force against Lyra’s hand altered. It did not disappear; it shifted, less direct, as the water in the passage found another way to move. She could no longer hold it on the same understanding. The brine running from her wrist felt suddenly warm.

“Stand clear,” she said.

Corven turned.

“The door.”

He moved to one side of the hatch and raised the hammer. Lyra took her right hand away first, then her left. The face came inward so quickly that she had to step back to avoid being struck by it.

Water entered around a man’s knees. He fell into the chamber with both hands out and struck his forehead on the platform. Behind him another man was trying to hold someone beneath the arms. The body’s head dragged against the threshold.

“Let go of the hammer,” Corven said to the standing man.

The man stared at him. The hammer remained hooked through his belt. He seemed unable to understand why it mattered now.

Lyra pulled it free herself and threw it behind the gear. She took the body by one shoulder, and together they dragged it across the sill.

The man on the floor was coughing. The one they had carried was not.

The passage had become a narrow, rushing channel. Its water was lower than it had been against the closed hatch, but it moved with enough force to turn a loose plank sideways and hammer it against the pipework.

The Worn was still there.

It stood with one leg caught beneath the timber that had fallen in the fight. Its face remained above the current, its hands moving slowly against the wood. Whatever strength had brought it toward them was being spent on a task for which no amount of pulling would supply the right angle.

Beyond it, the wounded crossbowman lay partly under water. Lyra could see his shoulder, the back of his head, and one hand resting against the stones as if he had merely put it down to steady himself.

The man whose brother they had brought in was on his knees beside the body.

“He had hold of me,” he said.

Lyra knelt opposite. She put her fingers beneath the man’s jaw. She looked into his mouth and tipped his head enough to let water run out. There were things she could try if the body would answer. She tried them, pressing where Gyllan had shown her, working the chest, pausing to listen.

The surviving brother watched each movement. When she stopped, he waited for the next one.

“Again,” he said.

She tried once more.

Corven stood beside her. He had lowered the hammer. The man who had fallen through the hatch was sitting against the wall, coughing into his sleeve. Seldo had his spear now, but its point faced the floor.

Lyra put her hand flat on the dead man’s chest.

“I’m sorry.”

The brother looked at her hands. She did not know whether he understood that the same hands had held the hatch. She thought he must. She waited for him to say it.

He asked for something to cover his brother’s face.

There was no more clean tool cloth. Lyra took off her coat and laid it over the body. She had to remove the brine vessel and the folded copy of the Gallery transfer from the inner pocket first. The paper was damp at the corner. She put it inside her shirt, against the cold skin beneath her collar.

Esten was watching the gate.

“We can’t leave it at this lift,” he said. “It’ll vibrate loose.”

“What do you need?”

“Another notch. Then we can pin it.”

Lyra stood. Her right hand would not close all the way. She worked the fingers gently with her left until she could grip the edge of the platform.

Seldo went back to the rope. This time he watched only Esten’s hand.

They lifted the gate the additional notch and secured it. The noise below deepened. The basin was emptying into the falling harbor, its water carrying the smaller debris ahead of it. Lyra could hear things striking the inside of the culvert, some light, one heavy enough to make the floor tremble.

“Will it hold?” she asked.

“If nobody comes and takes the pin out.”

“They won’t.”

Esten glanced at her. He had heard promises made in better rooms by people who owned much more than a wounded arm and a wet shirt. He went back to checking the pin himself.

Corven was looking into the service passage.

“We need to move that timber.”

Lyra followed his gaze to the trapped Worn. The current had eased enough to expose its waist. It kept pulling at the same part of the wood.

“When the water drops a little more.”

“It’s dropping.”

He wrapped the marked chain and put it away. What he took into the passage was the ordinary iron bar. He eased its end beneath the timber and tried the weight.

Lyra went after him. The water reached above her boots and soaked the hem of her clothes. Her body scarcely registered the cold. She braced the bar while he moved the loose end of the timber. The Worn pulled at the wrong moment, trapping it again.

“Hold,” she told it.

Its hands continued moving.

She thought of Jorin, the way an instruction could remain after the person who had given it ceased to be there. She put her own hand over one of the Worn’s. The skin beneath hers felt like cracked stone. She guided it to a different purchase and held it there while Corven lifted.

The leg came free.

The Worn stepped toward her. Corven moved between them, but it went past him toward the platform and stopped beside the bound hammer man. Its arms lowered.

The hammer man drew his feet back.

“Put it outside,” he said.

Lyra looked at him. “You can sit beside it.”

She took Corven’s bar and went to see whether the crossbowman was still breathing.

He was dead. Farther up the passage, a second body had caught against the bend in the pipe. They would have to bring both out. She had no more words for that, so she showed Corven where the man lay and went back to Esten.

The upper door was unguarded when Seldo opened it.

The men who had been trying to break it had left. They had taken the young guard’s lantern from the footway, but not the folded coats. Esten’s lay in a puddle with one sleeve trailing over the step. Dannet’s was still dry beneath it.

Lyra picked both up.

From the top landing she could see the basin moving in a direction it had not moved all evening. A line of floating refuse traveled toward the western outlet. A small wooden bowl turned in a slow circle, found the stronger current, and vanished beneath the edge of the footway.

People were shouting along the houses. Some had noticed the change. Others were calling for help with a door or a rope, ordinary words made strange by where they were standing to say them.

The Brindle had gone to the landing with a load of people. One Court punt remained at the wide turn, its crew waiting to see where they were wanted next. Lyra waved Esten’s coat until the bowman looked up.

“Here,” she called. “We have injured.”

He pointed toward the footway, asking whether he could come close. She looked down. The lower steps were under water, but the wall beside them was clear of ironwork. She pointed to it and nodded.

Corven came up beside her.

“The dead first?” he asked.

She shook her head. “The ones who can still get cold.”

They brought the coughing man up with the bound defender and the Worn. The surviving brother refused to leave before the body. Lyra let him remain on the landing while the punt took the others. He stood with her coat over his arms after helping them wrap his brother in a blanket brought from the boat.

“This is yours,” he said.

She took it. He would not meet her eyes. She did not try to make him.

Below, Seldo and Esten had lifted Dannet onto the bearing board. It was too short. His feet extended beyond the faded flower, and they had to bind his knees so they would not swing against the stair. Esten objected to the rope, then could not suggest a better way of bringing him out.

Lyra went down and took the foot end.

Her right arm hurt so badly on the second turn that she had to stop. Seldo offered to change places. She accepted without argument. He took the load while she carried the lamp. It was still lit; somehow it had survived everything, although its handle had bent against the stone.

At the top they set Dannet down beside his folded coat. Esten knelt to put the coat over him and found the little packet in its pocket. He held it between finger and thumb.

“Onions,” he said.

He had meant to say something more. Lyra waited. Esten put the packet back and covered Dannet’s feet.

The water continued falling.

Across the narrow reach, the old inspection mouth was visible beneath its newer boarding. Lyra recognized the narrow gap below the boards and remembered the passage beyond it, the lintels they had stooped beneath with Mavet. The old sealed face lay farther in, out of sight. It would remain after the floor of her rented store had rotted and the people who argued about its ownership were gone.

A low vibration carried through the footway beneath her. It brought back the pressure she had felt beside the stone.

It differed from the force that had pushed the hatch. That had come from water in a basin, and she had seen it lessen when a gate opened. This waited under the stone with no visible change in the canal to account for it. Her teeth ached when she looked too long at the narrow opening.

The rubbing was at home, wrapped against damp. She had put it where an accident in the gear room could not take it from her. She knew enough now to understand one more thing she would have to bring on the next descent. She would need people who could measure the pressure from both sides of a closed face, not merely tell her that it was old.

Corven followed her gaze.

“We aren’t going in there,” he said.

“No.”

“Not tonight.”

She turned toward him. He was bleeding through the bandage at his side. The cut from the earlier extraction had opened under the strain of the chain. He had not mentioned it.

“Sit down,” she told him.

“I’ll sit in the boat.”

“Then I’ll stand here and be irritating until it comes.”

He almost smiled. He sat on the upper step, keeping his bad side clear of the stone. Lyra knelt beside him to look at the bandage. His hand caught hers before she could lift it.

“In the boat,” he said again. “With something clean.”

She let go. They sat without speaking while the punt made its turn.

When it came alongside, the crewman asked where to take Dannet. Lyra gave the shore house first, where a dry room could be found until his family came. Esten added his sister’s address for the messenger. Lyra repeated both instructions to the crewman and made him say them back.

They lowered the board together. The boat shifted under the weight, and Lyra held the rope until the man below had both ends secure. Only then did she let it pass out of her hands.

Chapter 24 The water falls

The first person to return to Mere Lane brought a broom.

He stood at the edge of the water, waiting for it to go low enough to cross. He wore his good boots, the pair he had carried upstairs when the floor began to flood. One trouser leg had been rolled higher than the other. The broom was dry.

Lyra came past with Esten’s drawing inside her coat and the smell of the gear room still in her hair. She told the man to wait until someone had checked the paving.

“I can see it.”

“You can see water above it.”

He pointed with the broom. The top of a flat stone showed at the edge of the lane. The next one remained hidden beneath brown water moving toward the outlet.

She took the pole from the punt and tested the space between them. The end went down much farther than the paving should have allowed. Something had been washed out beneath the edge.

The man lowered his broom.

“That was there yesterday.”

“Put something across this end. Tell the next person.”

“What shall I put?”

She looked around and found a broken frame from one of the washing racks. Together they dragged it across the opening. He tied his clean broom to it so people would see the obstacle before they stepped over it.

The punt crew called her back. They had another place to go. She left the man looking down the lane toward his house, one hand still closed where he had held the broom.

The city was beginning to make the noise of a place people had stopped being afraid to move through. Shutters opened. Someone called for a missing dog, and someone else answered from a roof that the dog had been there for an hour and would not come down. A door fell outward into a yard with such force that three people shouted at once. Then a man began laughing at the amount of furniture that followed it.

Lyra could hear a baby crying somewhere behind the walls. She did not know whether it had been crying all night or had only just begun.

At the Sluice House, Esten had found two men who understood enough of the mechanism to watch it while he rested. They came from businesses along the basin. One had brought a lantern with a shade; the other had brought his own stool. Neither asked whether Lyra intended to pay them before going inside.

They did ask who would stop the Prince’s men from returning.

Seldo stood on the upper landing with the spear he had taken from the first guard. It looked unfamiliar in his hands. He said he would stay until the relief came.

“Whose relief?” the man with the stool asked.

Lyra answered before Seldo could.

“The landing association’s. Sera’s sending people with the order.”

“Not yours?”

“People who can keep the gate open. That’s the work.”

The man studied her face. She was too tired to know whether he believed her. He carried his stool down the stair and began asking Esten which noises were ordinary.

Seldo waited until they had gone.

“Do I come back to the Opera House afterward?”

“Yes.”

“Same watch?”

She looked at the bandage around his palm. Blood had worked through the cloth in the shape of the rope.

“Sleep first. We’ll arrange the watch when you’ve slept.”

He accepted that because he was tired enough to need it. She would have to give him a better answer later.

Daven found her at the wide turn. The Brindle was empty except for two blankets and a little wooden horse that had been left under a seat.

“Did they all get off the roof?” she asked.

“The ones they showed us. The upper boat went back around the other side.”

“Any hurt?”

“One man’s shoulder. He thought he could jump with his mother in his arms. We told him.”

She waited.

“She’s all right. He’ll be saying so for a month while someone else puts his shirt on.”

Daven picked up the wooden horse, examined its broken wheel, and tucked it beside the steering post where a parent might see it. He had water above both knees. His beard held a pale crust.

“I can take you to the store,” he said.

“Have you been?”

“Alongside. Not inside.”

“Then take me.”

They reached the rented cooper’s building as the water dropped below its loading sill. Lyra could see her own crates from the boat. Two had broken open. The canvas had taken on enough water to sag through the space between the boards. One barrel had floated beneath the edge of the upper platform and become wedged there, holding a section of floor tilted like a loose lid.

The door was open.

Daven brought the lighter against the stone and held it while she stepped across. The inside smelled of oil, wet wood, and something sweet that should have remained sealed. She had ordered the sweet goods put high. They had not stayed there.

A narrow crate lay on its side near the entrance. She recognized the chest of tools beyond it, half hidden beneath a fallen sack. Its lid had been forced. The iron hinge had bent upward, showing fresh wood around the screws.

For a moment she simply stood and looked.

She had imagined theft while she watched the water from the loft. Imagining it had done nothing to prepare her for the small, deliberate damage of a tool placed beneath the lid and leaned upon. Someone had stood where she stood, decided which things to take, and left the rest for the water.

She knelt beside the chest. The short auger was gone. So were the better chisels and the little screw clamp she had bought from a man leaving the city. A heavier mallet remained, too awkward to carry with the useful things. Beneath it lay a small wrapped packet of replacement rivets.

She took the packet out, opened it, and found that the inner cloth was dry.

“We can still clear some,” Daven said from the doorway.

She wanted him to go away. She wanted him to bring every man he knew and empty the whole building before another person could touch it. The wishes arrived together and left her unable to give either instruction.

He waited.

“Canvas out first,” she said at last. “Spread it wherever there is room. Keep the oil away from it. Don’t try to stand on that platform.”

“I can get two men.”

“Pay them from what I owe you.”

“You owe me for this boat.”

“I know.”

He looked at the room. “Get your clerk to write it.”

“I haven’t brought a clerk.”

“Then write it yourself. I’m tired enough to agree to anything and cross enough to resent it later.”

She laughed, a small unwilling sound that hurt her throat. He smiled without seeming pleased to have made her do it.

She wrote on the back of an old packing slip, leaning it against the door. Her hand was cramped and the letters poor, but the instruction was clear. She signed it, dried it against the inside of her coat, and gave it to him.

They found the spare chain beneath the fallen platform. It had not gone far; its own weight had kept it where the current pushed it. Daven looped a rope through one end, and together they drew it clear without lifting the damaged floor. Lyra’s arm failed halfway through. He took the rope from her and finished.

“Leave that hand out of it.”

“I thought I could manage.”

“You thought wrong. Sit down.”

She sat on the loading sill with the packet of rivets in her lap. From there she could see where they had left the split crate during the evacuation. A few bolts of cloth remained inside, their bright ends darkened by the water. Someone had taken the dry upper ones and left the soaked goods. At least they had known what they were doing.

She put the rivets in her pocket. Daven came back with two men and a length of clean rope, and she showed them where the remaining stock could be handled safely.

Corven was waiting at the shore house with his shirt open and an expression that discouraged questions.

The surgeon had gone into the next room. There were three people ahead of him, one of them the man who had jumped with his mother. Marda had put a folded cloth under Corven’s arm to keep the blood off the chair. He watched the doorway with patient hostility.

Lyra washed her hands. She had to change the water twice before it stopped clouding.

“May I look?”

He moved the cloth.

The earlier cut had opened along part of its length. The deeper damage lay beneath it, bruised and strained by the work with the chain. Lyra could see where he had held his own bandage in place with a knot tied awkwardly under one arm.

“Who tied this?” she asked.

“A man who expected to be somewhere dry by now.”

She cut the knot away. He hissed when the cloth moved.

“That part isn’t my fault,” she said.

“I wasn’t giving it a name.”

She cleaned the edge of the wound and felt with care for the places the old stitching had pulled. There were things she would leave to the surgeon. The bleeding at one side could be slowed. She told Corven what she meant to do before putting her fingers against it.

He nodded.

The working asked for less of her than the hatch had. It was a movement she knew from a different part of her life, the damaged edge encouraged toward something it could still become. The skin grew pale beneath her hand. Corven’s breathing settled enough that he stopped bracing his foot against the floor.

“Hold that there,” she said, giving him the clean cloth. “It still needs looking at.”

“So does your arm.”

“I’m next.”

He looked toward the queue.

“After them,” she said.

He closed his eyes. She remained beside him until the surgeon came back.

Marda brought her a bowl. Lyra could not tell what was in it until she tasted the first mouthful. Her hunger returned so abruptly that she had to stop herself from swallowing too much at once.

“There’s more,” Marda said.

Lyra nodded with her mouth full.

From the cool back room she heard something tap against a bowl. Tarn sat within sight of the door, his injured hand resting on a folded cloth. The thick wrapping was cleaner than she remembered. Two fingers had been shortened by the surgeon; the damaged hand no longer reached for the fitting that had trapped it. It lay still while his other hand moved the spoon in a small, repeated circle.

Marda followed her glance.

“He’ll take it if it’s left where he can see it. If you try to help, he waits for you to do the whole thing.”

“Does he eat?”

“Some.”

Lyra ate her own food. Across the room, the man who had refused Marda’s bowl the evening before was helping clear the tables. She did not know what had passed between them. She was glad not to have to know.

The Prince’s boat came as the eastern sky began to pale.

It stopped at the public landing beyond the damaged lane. A narrow vessel, its sides rubbed with salt until the wood showed little grain. The men aboard moved with practiced care around the seated figure at its stern. Lyra watched from the footway while Seldo sent someone for the association’s witnesses.

The Prince did not come ashore at once. He looked at the water, then at the new guard standing above the Sluice House. His cloak had been drawn around his knees. At one edge, where it folded against his wrist, the preserved skin retained a crease too familiar to belong to ordinary leather.

He lifted his head toward Lyra.

“You have opened it.”

“You can see that.”
