“I can see a crowd around a piece of machinery. It is usually a sign that someone has misunderstood ownership.”

The independent witness arrived with two people from the basin businesses. One carried the order Sera had obtained; the paper had been protected in a tin tube. He took it out and read the parts concerning access and the outflow while the Prince remained seated.

Lyra listened. The man reading had a good speaking voice. He stumbled on only one name, corrected it, and continued. It was an unexpectedly reassuring thing, someone doing a difficult ordinary task without rushing to finish it.

When he stopped, the Prince held out a hand for the order.

“A copy,” the witness said.

He passed down the copy prepared for the purpose. The Prince accepted it between two fingers. A little whiteness formed along the fold. He read without moving his lips, then gave it to the man beside him.

“The workers?” he asked.

Lyra knew which ones he meant.

“Some are being treated. The dead are at the shore house until they can be identified and taken home.”

“They had homes when I employed them.”

“One asked for his brother.”

The Prince’s jaw shifted. For a moment his mouth lay out of line with the face around it. He adjusted it with the same care another man might use to ease a stiff collar.

“Then it is fortunate you understood the request.”

Lyra put her hand on the stone rail. She could remember exactly how long she had kept the hatch shut after understanding it. She did not give that memory to him.

“Your men won’t close the outflow again.”

He looked beyond her toward the older wall. The glance was brief enough that she might have missed it if she had not spent part of the night looking there herself.

“You have repaired the newest part,” he said.

“It was the part needed today.”

His gaze returned to her. The air around the boat seemed to become very still. Water moved against the hull, but the reflections nearest his hand lost their small broken edges, drawing into a flat brightness.

One of the witnesses stepped back. Lyra remained by the rail. She could feel her arm trembling beneath the borrowed sling the surgeon had given her. She kept the sound hand where he could see it.

The Prince smiled with the surface of his mouth.

“Take care of your repairs.”

He turned to the master. The boat moved away from the landing without any raised command. It passed the Sluice House and continued toward the Salt Ward, where his store lamps were still burning.

The man with the order let out a long breath.

“Does that mean he agrees?”

“No,” Lyra said. “Keep the watch.”

The man put his copy back into its tube. He went down to speak to Esten’s relief while Lyra stayed at the rail. On the Prince’s departing boat, a covered shape rested in the bow. One corner of the cover had lifted. Pale stone showed beneath it, cut cleanly along one face.

Dannet’s sister arrived with a bag of clothes.

She had been told before setting out. Lyra was grateful for that until she saw the way the woman held the bag, both hands under it as though it contained something that could be broken by putting it down.

Esten went to meet her. He said her brother’s name and nothing more. She looked at his coat, his wet knees, the black grease on his wrists.

“You were with him.”

“Yes.”

“All the way?”

“We brought him out together.”

She nodded and asked to go in.

The little room had been cleared of its stools. Dannet lay on a table covered with clean sailcloth. Someone had washed the undamaged side of his face and combed his hair away from the wound. The packet from his coat lay beside his hand.

His sister set the bag down and opened it. She had brought a shirt, stockings, and a coat that looked newer than the one folded at his feet. She took the shirt out first and held it against him to see whether it would fit beneath the coat.

Lyra stood near the door. She had paid people to work through injuries, funerals, missed tides, and the long delay of not knowing whether someone had died. She knew the forms of help that could be offered. None of them told her where to stand while a woman dressed her brother.

“I can come back,” she said.

The sister looked around. “Are you the one who hired him?”

“Yes.”

“Then stay. I’ll need someone to help with his arm.”

Lyra put down her sling. Esten took the other side. They worked the clean shirt over Dannet’s shoulders, lifting him carefully so the cloth would not catch on the damaged part of his head. His sister did the buttons. At one of them she stopped, undid the thread, and took a needle from the bag.

“He kept pulling this loose.”

She sewed it back while the shirt was on him. Her fingers were quick and accurate. Lyra watched the thread pass through the same small place until the button sat firmly against the cloth.

“Will there be men to carry him?” the woman asked.

“Yes.”

“I have to tell my mother before they come. She’ll hear them on the stair.”

“We’ll wait for your word.”

She finished the last stitch and bit the thread. Then she put the needle away before allowing herself to touch her brother’s face.

Lyra looked toward the door. Esten was crying without making any attempt to hide it. She had not seen it begin. She put her hand against his back and remained there until the sister asked for the other coat.

Outside, daylight had reached the yard. People were waking on the borrowed bedding. Someone had found the lost child’s shoe caught in the roots of a floating plant and brought it in, proud of having retrieved a thing that could still be returned to its owner.

Lyra sat on the step while Dannet’s sister went to tell her mother. She put her arm back in the sling. The little packet of rivets pressed against her leg through the pocket, and she moved it to the other side.

The Opera House was open when she returned.

A cleaner was sweeping the public steps. The woman worked around Lyra without looking at the blood on her sleeve. Somewhere above them a singer repeated the same phrase, stopped, and began again at a different note. The sound was irritatingly beautiful.

In the receiving court, the repaired hoist stood still. Nobody had brought anything for it to lift. A porter asked whether there would be work in the afternoon. Lyra opened her mouth to give the answer he wanted and found she did not know.

“Come to the office after midday,” she said. “You’ll be paid for the time you’re here.”

He nodded and sat on the loading edge. He had brought his food with him. He meant to wait.

Upstairs, the large chest still stood away from the wall with its loose bottom exposed. She had forgotten how she had left it. A nail from the fish-box lids lay on the desk beside a broken piece of sealing wax. The second chest was empty too. She could move it easily now.

She took the damp copy of the Gallery handover from inside her shirt and spread it on the table. It had survived. The ink had run at one corner, below the signatures, leaving a dark feathered shape where there had been nothing to read. She placed two clean weights on its edges and left it to dry.

Sera had sent a note. It named the people appointed to the temporary watch at the Sluice House and asked for the surviving store inventories before the afternoon meeting. There was a separate line saying that the transfer fund for Aria remained untouched. Lyra had not asked about it. She read the line twice before realizing that Sera was telling everyone whose money had been used for what.

She put the note beside the list of missing stock.

For a while she could do nothing more useful than take off her boots. Water had worked between the inner and outer soles. When she tipped them, a small stream ran onto the floor. She set them by the open window, where they might dry if the day remained clear.

The singer above found the phrase she wanted and continued beyond it.

Lyra lay down on the narrow bed without undressing. Beneath it stood the locked tool chest containing the rubbing from the old chamber. The separate shallow case that had held Gallery papers stood open and empty beside the wall. She had carried nothing back to replace those papers.

She meant to sleep for an hour. When someone knocked, she did not know whether a minute or half the morning had passed.

It was the porter from below.

“They’ve brought the canvas,” he said. “Where do you want it?”

She pushed herself up on her good arm. For an instant the room seemed to move around the place where her head had been. Then it settled.

“The upper gallery. Along the rail where the sun reaches.”

“Will they let us put it there?”

She looked at the empty chests.

“I’ll come and ask.”

She put on a dry pair of stockings, left the wet boots beneath the window, and went downstairs to help him find someone with the gallery key.

Part Five A place of her own

Chapter 25 Reopening

The first wages after the water fell were paid beneath a roof that had not been there the week before.

Berris had put it up with sound pieces cut from the fallen end of Harrow Slip’s shed. The roof was too low for the tallest carriers to stand under it, but the table stayed dry. Tesset moved her copper weights inward when a wind gust shook the edge of the cloth. One of the waiting men ducked, though nothing fell.

“It did that all morning,” she told him.

“Then it should have finished.”

He set his hand on the table. A bandage covered the first two fingers. She found his name, checked the hours against the receiving sheet, and counted what was owed. The bandage made picking up the smallest coins difficult. She put those in a paper twist and waited while he opened it again to make sure they were all there.

There had been eight days of drying rooms, moving things twice, and discovering that objects put high enough to escape one kind of water had been spoiled by another. A ceiling dripped onto blankets rescued from a flooded bed. A woman brought back a chair she had borrowed from the shelter because the chair, she said, was older than the person who had lent it and deserved better than her kitchen. The kitchen floor had opened down the middle. Tesset found somewhere else for the woman to sleep and added the chair to a list of things whose owners still needed finding.

Today she had expected twenty-three people at the pay table. Twenty-three had come, but only nineteen were on her sheet.

The four others had worked on the cleared footway. They said Seldo had told them they would be paid. Seldo had signed for three people. Tesset asked which three, and all four began talking.

She brought them under the roof, where she could hear them without the landing crew joining in. The fourth man was the son of one of the others. He had come to carry his father’s tools and then spent half the day hauling mud. His father said it would be an insult to put down half a day. The son said half a day would suit him, and his father told him not to settle for less before a number had been offered.

“Were you working before the noon boat?” Tesset asked.

“I arrived on it.”

“Then I’ll put the afternoon.”

“The work was worth a whole day,” his father said.

“I’m writing down when he did it.”

She went to find Seldo before counting the money. He was at the upper gate of the slip with a borrowed broom, clearing a strip broad enough for a barrow. He recognized the younger man and confirmed the work. He had forgotten to add him because the instruction arrived while he was helping a woman get a trunk out of the mud.

“Will you sign now?” Tesset asked.

He wiped his hands, took the pencil, and broke its point against the board.

“I’ll sharpen it.”

“I have another.”

“I can sharpen a pencil.”

“I know.” She handed him the spare. “I need this one signed before the boat leaves.”

He signed. Then he took her broken pencil and sharpened it with such care that she understood he needed to finish something small without being asked about the last thing he had failed to finish. She waited. When he gave it back, the point was better than the spare’s.

“Thank you.”

He nodded and returned to the mud.

At the table, the father was still arguing about the length of the day. Tesset paid the son directly. The young man put the money in his pocket before his father could count it and said he would come again if needed.

Sera arrived carrying two packets and a loaf tucked beneath her arm. She put the loaf on top of the locked box. Tesset was pleased to see it until she learned it belonged to someone else.

“Could you have it sent to the woman keeping the upstairs room? She won’t take payment for the extra night.”

“She might take payment for the bedding.”

“She might. I’ll ask.”

Sera opened the first packet. The merchants using the slip had agreed to fund a regular clerk while their cargo continued to pass through it. The pay was less than Lyra had given Tesset in the better weeks. The hours were written, including two afternoons at the counting house for disputes that could not be settled on the landing. Someone had added that the clerk must be available when required outside those hours. Sera had drawn a line through it and written that additional work required an additional agreement.

Tesset read that part twice.

“Will they accept it?”

“Two have. The third says he can’t imagine a clerk whose day ends while there is still work.”

“Can he imagine a boat master whose boat leaves while there is still cargo?”

“I asked him something similar. He accepted the rate.”

Tesset looked toward the landing, where a wheelbarrow had stopped because its load was wider than the new gap between stacks. The men were deciding which stack should move. Lyra would have decided before either finished speaking. Tesset had enjoyed being beside her when that happened, the relief of having the matter made simple. She still missed it when the day’s fourth disagreement reached the table.

“Who can end the employment?” she asked.

Sera showed her the clause. The three subscribers together, with notice. A complaint from a single cargo owner came to the appointed committee. Tesset would report the hours in public, so that people could find her without asking Lyra where she had been sent.

She signed. Then she turned the paper back to Sera.

“Leave out the word assistant.”

Sera glanced at it. “It was copied from the first arrangement.”

“I’m the clerk.”

Sera struck through the word and initialed the change. Tesset signed again beside it. She thought she would feel more important. Mostly she wanted to find out what was in the second packet.

It was payment for the shelter journeys, including the extra trip caused by her bad plan for the beds. The mistake remained in the description. She made herself leave it there.

Dannet’s sister came just before the pay table closed. Esten walked with her. He carried a canvas bag whose handle had been repaired with a piece of hauling line.

Tesset had met her at the shore house, standing beside the table in the cleared room. Today the woman wore the same dark coat, brushed clean at the hem. She had a broad, open face, unlike Dannet’s, though she tucked her chin in the same way when she prepared to disagree.

“He kept his good tools separate,” she told Esten as they came under the roof. “These weren’t all his.”

“The short driver was.”

“The handle’s wrong.”

“He put a different one on.”

“Then I want the old handle too.”

Esten set the bag down. He looked at Tesset as though she might know where a broken handle would be kept. She offered the woman a chair. The woman sat, then put the bag on her knees, leaving Esten no excuse to take it away and search again.

The final pay had been witnessed and set aside. Sera had brought the authority for its collection with the morning papers. Tesset explained each sum, including the unused advance for the return work. Dannet’s sister pushed that portion back.

“He didn’t do it.”

“It was an advance for accepting the engagement. It wasn’t conditional on finishing.”

“He wasn’t a man for money he hadn’t earned.”

Esten sat on the edge of the platform. “He earned it.”

The woman held the little pile separate from the rest. Tesset could not tell whether she wanted another explanation or simply time. She gave her time. Down at the water, somebody dropped a bucket, and everyone looked toward the noise too quickly.

“Did he speak?” the woman asked.

Esten shook his head.

“After.”

“No.”

She looked into the bag. A wooden gauge lay across the top, with Dannet’s two shallow cuts near one end so it would not be mistaken for Esten’s. She took it out and ran her thumb over the cuts.

“He made those at our table. My husband said he’d cut the table next. They quarreled over a mark that had been there before either of them was born.”

Esten smiled with his mouth closed. The smile did not last, but Tesset was glad she had seen it.

“We found his room,” the woman said. “He’d paid through the end of the interval. There was a pot under the bed with nothing in it but dirt.”

“He meant to grow beans,” Tesset said.

“I know what he meant. I told him they needed daylight.”

She put the gauge back. The coins he had supposedly not earned went into the same purse as the others. Tesset recorded the payment and turned the paper for her to make her mark. Esten witnessed it. He had to stop before finishing his name.

When they were gone, Tesset found a little curl of wood on the table from Seldo’s sharpening. She had been moving it out of her way all afternoon. She put it in the scrap basket and closed the box.

At the rope loft, the afternoon attendant wanted to know whether she would still be paid if Jast could stand.

Tesset had come to confirm the next week’s shifts. The question took her by surprise. She looked toward the bed, where the injured man’s leg lay on its support and his bare foot moved faintly whenever a cart crossed the yard below.

“Can he?”

“He tried. That’s why I ask. The healer says the bone needs more time. One of the subscribers came yesterday and said it was pleasant to see him returning to useful habits.”

“Which subscriber?”

The woman gave the name. Tesset knew him. He sold lamp oil and believed every improvement should promptly lower somebody else’s expenses.

“The shifts are funded through the agreed term,” she said. “The need after that will be judged by the healer and the people doing the care.”

“Put that where I can show him.”

She did. The attendant read it and pinned it beside the watch list. Tesset had to blot the page first because the woman would not wait until the ink dried by itself. She was tired of explaining her employment to visitors who stayed long enough to see someone quiet and concluded there was nothing left to do.

Hessa sat beside the far window with the basket on her lap. The padded edge had worked loose. She was pulling the cloth back through the broken weave, undoing the repair as carefully as another person might have made it. The attendant took a clean length from the shelf and sat beside her.

“Let me have that end.”

Hessa held it tighter.

The woman waited, her own hand open. After a while Hessa gave her the basket. Then she reached for it at once and caught the woman’s wrist hard enough to leave marks. The attendant released it. Her expression changed only after she had moved beyond reach.

“That was new yesterday,” she said.

“Giving it?”

“Letting it go for a while. Today she doesn’t want to.”

Tesset watched Hessa tuck the cloth back around the broken place. There was no use announcing the day before as a victory if it meant being disappointed with her now. The basket could be repaired later, or replaced if she would accept another. For the moment the loose cloth covered the sharp edge well enough.

Jast shifted on the bed. The attendant went to him, putting one hand on the uninjured knee so he would feel where she was before she touched the bandage. He looked toward the yard and made a rough sound. A pigeon had landed on the sill outside. It walked along the stone, peered in, and withdrew its head with offended importance.

Tesset laughed. Jast’s gaze moved to her face. He studied her mouth, then looked back at the bird. She had no idea whether her laughter had pleased or troubled him. She made herself stop searching his expression for an answer she would prefer.

The attendant asked whether Tesset could stay while she fetched warm water. Tesset pulled a stool beside the door, where she could see both people without crowding either. She could hear the woman downstairs complaining that the kettle had once again been used to soak something inedible. Another voice defended the practice. The disagreement continued while Hessa put both hands around her shallow bowl and drank.

When the attendant returned, she asked for the sixth day off. Her nephew was marrying. She had missed the earlier family meal and would like to arrive before the bride was taken away from the table to be admired by people who had eaten first.

“I can probably cover the morning,” Tesset said.

“Morning doesn’t get me to the meal.”

“I meant find cover. I can’t do the whole shift myself.”

The woman looked relieved. “Good. You’re useful with the papers, but I don’t want to spend the following day teaching you what I should have taught you before leaving.”

Tesset took the correction. They worked out which of the relief attendants knew the room, where the extra pay would come from, and who would bring the evening food. It was a small arrangement with four people in it by the time it was finished.

On her way out, Tesset moved a stool that had been left against the inside of the door. The attendant thanked her. Hessa’s basket remained in her lap. Jast watched the empty sill after the pigeon had gone.

Ada met her in the washing yard with wet sleeves and a request to hold the door.

“The top one. It closes when the wind comes round.”

Tesset propped it with a broken brick. Inside, Jorin stood beside the low crate in the old hamper room. A cup rested on it. The cup was empty, and he was looking at the place where Ada usually put the water jug.

“He wants a drink,” Ada said. “Sometimes he waits. Sometimes he throws the cup.”

She filled it without putting it into his hand. Jorin picked it up from the crate. His fingers were large around the handle, the joints pale with salt. He drank, set it down too hard, and looked toward the open yard.

Tesset stayed outside the threshold. She had come before, but not often enough to know which part of the room she could occupy without troubling him. Ada continued wringing the cloth in her hands.

“He’s used the bed twice,” she said.

“To sleep?”

“Once to sit. Once to put the basket on. I count them both.”

Tesset smiled. Ada did too, then shook out the cloth and hung it where the afternoon light could reach.

“I asked about the woman who can sit with him,” Tesset said. “She can come two mornings each week. The pay would cover those mornings, if you still want them.”

“I want them.”

“The first day she wants you here.”

“I’ll be here.”

“She said she would rather learn from you before she was alone with him.”

Ada put both hands on the rim of the tub. Water ran from her sleeves onto the stones. “Good. The last man told me he knew how to manage difficult patients before he’d seen him.”

“Did he stay?”

“No.”

Jorin moved toward the door. Ada stepped aside, leaving room between the tub and the wall. He came into the yard and stopped with one foot in a patch of sun. His shoulders rose. Tesset wanted to move the hanging cloth so it would cast a wider shadow, but Ada touched her wrist lightly.

“Wait.”

He stepped back himself. Then he followed the strip of shade to the bench. Ada had put a basket there with a loose handle. He sat beside it and began turning the handle between his hands.

“Does he mend them?” Tesset asked.

“Sometimes he works at the right place. Mostly he turns it.”

“You could give him a sound one.”

“I did. He left it.”

Tesset looked at the basket. There was no weight to lift, no repeated order drawing him toward the water. There was still a motion he could spend an hour performing. Ada did not call it an improvement while she watched him.

They took their tea in the yard. Tesset had brought the loaf Sera entrusted to her as far as the upstairs lodging, received half of it back from the woman who refused to eat so much alone, and now gave Ada that half. Ada sliced it around a hard place without wasting anything. She had made a little dish of roots in vinegar. The sharp smell brought Dannet to Tesset’s mind so suddenly that she had to put down her cup.

Ada waited.

“His sister came today,” Tesset said.

“I heard she’d been looking for his things.”

“We had most of them.”

Ada nodded toward the hamper room. “I kept every shirt. The worn ones, the ones I meant to cut up. I thought if I found him there would be a use for them.”

“Is there?”

“Not the use I thought.”

She offered Jorin a piece of bread on a small board. He did not take it. She put it beside the basket and returned to her seat. A little later he picked it up, looked at it, and set it inside the basket. Ada continued eating her own.

The yard held their ordinary noises: water from the drying cloth, a pot scraped clean in the next house, someone above practicing the same few notes on a pipe. When the piper missed the highest note for the fourth time, Ada called up a complaint. A face appeared at the window and said he was learning. She said they had all learned that much. The face vanished, and after a moment the pipe began lower.

Jorin looked toward the window. Ada followed his gaze, then took another piece of bread.

Tesset stayed until the woman from the next yard came to help with the last washing. Ada needed to get a tub emptied before the light failed. Tesset helped carry it, then took her bag and went home by Potters’ Row.

One shop had opened its lower shutters. Two rows of cups stood on a temporary shelf, with a larger bowl beneath to catch the drip from the plaster. The potter had put the unmarked cups at the front; behind them stood the ones water had stained, offered more cheaply. A man was arguing that his own flooded house entitled him to the lower price on a sound cup. The potter said he could have a sound cup for the sound price.

Tesset bought one with a crooked blue line around its rim. The handle fitted her fingers. She carried it wrapped in paper beneath her coat, pleased by a purchase that did not need another person’s approval.

At home, Leda was not there. Nella had left a bowl for her at the foot of the stair with a note saying the lamp smoked because Perron had cut the wick while discussing something else. Tesset ate sitting on her bed and read the terms of her new employment once more. Then she turned the paper over and wrote to Leda, asking her to supper on the evening Tesset had kept clear. She named the place and the hour. She did not add an explanation of how busy she had been.

Chapter 26 The upper rooms

Lyra had been given the chair nearest the window. She moved it before sitting, and Sera moved her papers to make space without looking up.

The room had belonged to an opera manager who preferred people to see him above them. Two shallow steps led to the place where his desk had stood. The desk was gone; the new table sat on the lower floor, with one leg propped on a wedge. Lyra recognized the wedge. It had been cut from the same dark timber as the stops used during Aria’s transfer. She put her foot against it and felt that it was firm.

Three people waited across the table. Sera had the papers. The merchant who had withdrawn his further advances after the refused relief sat beside her, rubbing the bridge of his nose where spectacles usually rested. On the other side was the woman who had signed for the replacement watch. She wore a plain coat and had brought a small bag of food, which she set beneath her chair as though she expected the meeting to last beyond any reasonable meal.

Seldo stood at the door. Lyra had asked him to come because the watch would need to hear the final arrangement. He had understood that the sentence might end in more than one way.

Sera turned the first page toward her.

“We have accepted the valuation of the surviving stores. The damaged cloth has no value until someone buys it.”

“It has value now. It is cloth.”

“Find a buyer, then. I’ll enter what they offer. I won’t divide money we may never receive.”

Lyra had spent the previous afternoon spreading a length of that cloth in the receiving court. Mud lay between the folds. Some could be washed, some cut away, some sold to people less particular about what touched their goods. The labor of separating these kinds would consume much of what she could obtain. She disliked seeing Sera arrive at the same conclusion on a clean piece of paper.

“And the lifting gear?”

“The recovered iron is listed separately.”

“Those blocks cost more than the entire iron list.”

“They are still missing.”

Lyra leaned back. Her right shoulder pulled. The fresh strain from the hatch had eased enough for her to dress without help, but raising the arm above her head remained an ugly task. She could carry a light box. She could not haul the lost blocks out of mud by wishing hard enough.

The merchant took his hand from his nose. “You sent the boats to the houses.”

“Yes.”

“I am glad you did.”

She looked at him, waiting.

“The stores remain lost.”

“I know where they are.”

He did not argue. That made it harder to direct her anger at him.

Sera placed the proposed settlement on top of the valuation. Lyra had read it twice before the meeting. She read it again now. The Opera House would retain its coalition administration under the original charter. Sera would handle the common receiving and payments for the first term, reporting to the signatories. The guards would answer through the appointed watch, with replacements made under the charter. Lyra’s separate business could continue to use the ordinary receiving hoist and designated storage under the published rates. No one could exclude its cargo merely because she was a party to a dispute. She could take her portable tools, her personal furniture, and the goods identified as hers after the shared claims were settled.

The last page ended her claim to command the building’s entrances by herself.

She touched that paragraph with one finger.

“You have written this as though I can issue the authority away.”

“You can withdraw the conflicting instructions,” Sera said. “You can return the keys and stop paying a private watch to refuse the appointed one.”

“I haven’t the money to pay them.”

“No.”

Lyra could have found enough for another week. She knew which small reserve was still untouched and what she could sell without waiting for a buyer to inspect it. Another week would give her six men standing in doorways while the building’s other workers drew wages elsewhere. It would leave her with fewer tools and the same paragraph waiting on a table.

“The business rates are fixed for this term?”

“Yes.”

“And there is notice before they change.”

“The notice applies to every user.”

“Then put it here too. I won’t have an argument later about whether I am a user or a disputed occupant.”

Sera added the line. The merchant read it, nodded, and waited while the woman beside him initialed it. Lyra watched their hands. Nobody had hurried to give her a pen. They had learned to let her reach for it herself.

“The rooms?” she asked.

“Yours to clear by the agreed day. You can leave sooner. Anything still there afterward will be put into the named store, with an inventory you receive.”

“And no one opens the tool chest.”

“Your personal chest is listed as personal property.”

“The chest and its contents.”

Sera made that correction too.

Lyra read the page one last time and signed. Her hand was steady until she came to the final stroke. The arm trembled, making the line longer than she intended. She left it. Sera placed her signature beside it, then the others signed in turn.

Seldo moved closer when she took the keys from beneath her collar.

The old measuring room key came off first. It had ceased opening the lock weeks ago. She put it beside the keys to the west gate and the inner stores. The iron made several different sounds against the tabletop. She kept the key to her own room until she had cleared it, as the agreement allowed.

“Your instructions,” Sera said.

Lyra looked at Seldo. “The private watch ends at the evening change. You are to make the handover with the appointed keeper. No one is to refuse it in my name.”

“Yes, my lady.”

“Anyone not taking the new work gets the remaining wages through the change. Bring the list to me before supper.”

“Yes.”

The woman with the bag stood and offered Seldo a separate paper. He took it, glanced at Lyra, then looked down at the terms. He did not need her permission to read them. She waited until he finished before getting up.

At the door, Sera asked whether she wanted a copy delivered to her room.

“I’ll take it.”

The ink was still damp. Sera held the page by its corners until it could be folded. Lyra stood beside her, watching the square of afternoon light move over the old manager’s steps.

The new keeper’s first difficulty was a kitchen door whose hinge had worked loose.

Lyra encountered the argument while carrying a box from the office. The cook wanted it repaired before the evening meal. The keeper wanted to see the repair list. Berris wanted someone to hold the door while she tightened the upper fixing.

Lyra put down her box and held it with her left shoulder. They stopped arguing long enough for Berris to work. The screw turned, caught, and turned again. Berris stepped back, tested the movement, and told Lyra she could let go.

“You’ll need a longer fixing tomorrow,” she told the keeper. “This will last tonight if nobody hangs on the handle.”

“Is that on the list?”

“It is now.”

Berris picked up her tools. Lyra lifted the box, and they walked toward the stair together.

“You’re leaving today?” Berris asked.

“Most of it.”

“Where are you taking it?”

“The room over Harrow Slip’s shed.”

“The roof at that end is sound.”

“I’ve seen it.”

“The stair’s a disgrace.”

“I’ve seen that too.”

Berris smiled. There was gray dust in the creases beside her mouth. “I can give you half a day after the theatre wall is finished.”

“At your usual rate?”

“At my usual rate for stairs people use while I’m fixing them.”

Lyra thought about arguing, then nodded. Berris took the heavier end of the box without being asked and helped her to the upper landing. She had been carrying one end of Lyra’s possessions for so long that the movement felt unchanged even now.

In the office, two bare places showed where the boxes of Gallery papers had stood. Lyra had filled one with a chair, then moved it away because it obstructed the drawer. Sera’s custody confirmation lay among her business papers. She packed that too.

She opened the desk’s smallest drawer. Her own correspondence lay there, together with a little twist of blue thread, a sealed letter she had never found a satisfactory answer to, and two buttons that belonged to coats already gone. She put the letters in a cloth wallet. The buttons went into her pocket. She threw away the thread, then retrieved it because it might still serve for a repair.

Seldo brought the pay list. Three of the old watch had accepted work under the new keeper. He was among them.

“The hours are regular,” he said.

“They always were.”

He looked at her.

“When the work allowed,” she added.

“Yes.”

She counted the remaining money into separate packets. Seldo watched the window while she did it. The job at the slip would not require six guards; it would require someone willing to get wet, carry what arrived, and remain awake when a late boat came. She had thought of asking him before she asked anyone else. Looking at the paper in his hands, she found she did not want his refusal as another object to carry downstairs.

“Do you want the watch coat back?” he asked.

“You use it.”

“It was bought from your purse.”

“It can come out of the last packet if you want to make yourself poorer.”

He smiled faintly. “I’d rather keep it.”

“Then keep it.”

He took the money and the signed list. At the door he stopped.

“If you need a hand moving the chest, I finish at dusk.”

“I’ve hired two carriers.”

“All right.”

“You could walk down with it. They don’t know which turn to take.”

He nodded, and the stiffness in his shoulders eased. She had not realized how much of their conversation had been conducted through that stiffness.

The tool chest would not pass the bedroom door with its handles extended.

Lyra knew this because she had brought it in herself, empty, with the handles removed. She had forgotten until the carriers turned it sideways and one said that perhaps the frame should come off the door. She sent for a driver, knelt beside the chest, and removed the handles again. The younger carrier tried to lift it before she had finished the second fixing. She caught his wrist with her left hand.

“When I say.”

He went still. The strength she used was more than the task required. She released him, put the last screw in a paper twist, and stood back.

“Now.”

They took it out. The chest held the rubbing wrapped between its dry boards in oiled cloth, the measurements taken with Mavet, the brine bottles padded against one another, her practice frame, and the beginning of a set of hooks she intended to finish. She had checked the wrapping before locking it. Nothing from the flooded store had been put inside. It smelled of oil and clean cord, a small space in which work still waited in the order she had chosen.

Her bed took longer. The frame came apart, but a peg had swollen. Seldo arrived while the carriers were deciding whether to strike it harder. He supported the rail while Lyra eased the peg out with the repair knife Maeron had given her. Its narrow blade was useful. She did not have to remember the giving every time she used it, though today she did: his hand over hers to show her a safer grip, his pleasure when she stopped cutting against the thumb. Later, the gate. She closed the knife before either memory could hold the whole room.

The empty bedroom contained a broom, a dead moth beneath the window, and a dark square where the chest had stood. Lyra swept. Seldo offered to do it, and she told him to take the lamp down instead. She wanted to be the person who decided when the room was empty enough.

At the stairhead she met the woman whose statement she had kept in the last unmarked Gallery bundle. The woman had come about a storage claim and was waiting for Sera. She looked at the carriers, then at Lyra’s bare floor through the open door.

“You’re going?”

“To the lower harbor.”

“Will my paper go with you?”

“No. Sera’s witnesses hold it under the arrangement. You can ask to see the custody record.”

The woman watched her for a moment. “I will.”

Lyra stepped aside to let her pass. She did not say that she had nearly kept the bundle. That thought had never become something the woman needed to carry for her.

Down in the receiving court, the new keeper was asking Seldo where the spare lamp oil stood. Seldo answered without looking to Lyra. She took her room key from the cord and put it into the keeper’s outstretched hand.

“There’s a loose board beneath the window,” she said. “Have someone fix it before they put another bed there.”

He thanked her and made a note. She went out beside the handcart.

Sova had aired the room above the shed by opening both shutters and leaving the door propped with a pot.

The room was smaller than Lyra’s at the Opera House. Its ceiling followed the roof slope at one side, so she could stand comfortably beside the bed and strike her head if she forgot where she was while putting on a coat. A narrow table stood near the window. The view was of the slip’s uneven platform and the lower harbor beyond. No one else’s window faced directly into hers.

“The rent includes the table,” Sova said. “If you don’t want it, it has to come down today. I won’t have you dragging it over the stair after the carriers are gone.”

“I want it.”

“The shutters stick in rain.”

“I’ll ease them.”

“Tell me before you cut the hinges.”

Lyra set the chest down where the bed would cover it. “I don’t generally cut hinges to make shutters work.”

“You’d be surprised what people call a repair.”

The carriers assembled the bed. Seldo brought the lamp upstairs and stood by the window while Lyra paid them. There was not enough room for all four people without somebody standing on a loose board. Once the carriers left, the place immediately seemed more livable.

Sova collected the first room payment and left the key beside the lamp. It was separate from the shed key, which still hung on its piece of yellowed bone. Ownership of the slip remained divided. The renewed working rent would end soon, and the improvements below would stay with Sova and Pellan if Lyra went. She looked through the window at the new rail, the patched platform, and the place where a permanent anchorage could be fixed above the soft ground. Her body leaned toward the work before she realized it.

Seldo followed her gaze. “You could put the rope purchase there.”

“Once the stone is sound.”

“Esten could tell you.”

“Esten will tell me to ask a stoneworker.”

He smiled. The room was quiet enough that she could hear someone folding a sheet in the yard beyond the wall.

“Will you eat?” she asked.

“I told them I’d come back.”

“To the watch.”

“Yes.”

She nodded. He touched the edge of the door as he went out, checking the latch without seeming to think about it. She listened to his steps descend the bad stair.

Supper came from Sova’s pot, for a separate charge. It was good. Lyra ate at the narrow table with the shutters open and watched a boat master back his empty vessel away from the landing. He had to make the turn twice. She almost called advice, then saw that he had recognized the problem and was already correcting it.

When she had finished, she unpacked the chest. The oiled bundle went beneath the spare cloths, clear of the glass bottles. She placed the practice frame where she could reach it. The knife stayed in her pocket. There was space on the table for correspondence and tools if she did not insist on spreading both at once.

She made the bed, washed the bowl, and took it downstairs. Sova was shutting the shed.

“Pellan is coming at the end of the week,” Lyra said.

“He usually says so.”

“Ask him to bring all the ownership papers.”

Sova stopped with the key halfway turned. “For what?”

“For a price.”

The woman looked up at the lit window. “You won’t get the rent back.”

“I know what I signed.”

“Then I’ll tell him.”

Lyra went upstairs before Sova could see how much she wanted the answer.

Chapter 27 An evening off

Leda wore the blue dress to a room in which nobody knew what it had cost to get both of them there.

The woman at the door asked whether they had reserved a table. Tesset gave her name. The woman found it without dispute, took them to a place near the window, and asked whether they wanted the shutters closed against the evening air.

“No,” Tesset said too quickly.

Leda smiled. “You can let her ask the other questions.”

Tesset sat. She had arrived early enough to walk past the building twice, afraid that going in before Leda would make the waiting seem important. Now she wanted the food brought immediately so nothing could happen between their arrival and the beginning of the meal.

A runner passed the window. She looked up.

“He’s carrying flowers,” Leda said.

“I saw.”

“And I imagine they’re desperately required.”

Tesset laughed, then put her hands flat on the table. Leda reached across and turned one over. The little wound from the splinter was long healed. A new ink stain lay beneath the thumbnail.

“You’ve scrubbed at that.”

“Yes.”

“It looks worse when you scrub.”

“I’m beginning to hear that about several things.”

Leda kept hold of her hand until the food arrived. They had ordered fish baked with green herbs and a dish of beans that came in a pot between them. The fish was small; Tesset suspected the herbs occupied more of the plate than the creature had ever occupied in the sea. It tasted better than she expected. Leda ate the crisp edge first and then described a woman at the bindery who insisted every folded sheet leaned differently depending on whether it had been cut before or after the midday meal.

“Does it?” Tesset asked.

“Yes. Because the cutter drinks at midday.”

Leda was learning to sew gatherings. Tesset knew this as something that happened after her paid work, usually while other people were still making noise around her. Tonight Leda explained how the thread passed through a fold, how a knot too large could make a whole book open badly, how the little decisions vanished inside the finished object. Her hands moved over the table while she spoke. Tesset watched them and wanted to see the actual work, not merely say that she admired it.

“Could you show me?”

“You’d get bored.”

“Then I could stop.”

Leda considered her. “I have one at home. The sewing’s ugly.”

“Does it open?”

“It opens beautifully.”

“Then show me that one.”

They stayed through a second pot of tea. Nobody came to ask Tesset where a blanket had gone. Leda spoke about the room she lodged in and the woman on the other side of the wall who sang while winding thread. Sometimes the song was pleasant. Sometimes it was the same verse for most of an afternoon. She wanted a place where she could spread a book open without having to move it every time she needed to eat.

Tesset heard the desire and did not turn it into a request to share her own room. She had made that mistake once, offering to solve Leda’s inconvenience and hoping the answer would solve something else as well.

“I’m looking for a room too,” she said.

Leda’s hand paused beside the teapot.

“Nella’s?”

“The girls need more space. She hasn’t asked, but they are old enough to want somewhere that isn’t underneath their parents’ feet. And I want a door people knock on before they open it.”

“Perron knocks.”

“Perron knocks while he’s opening it.”

Leda laughed. “He thinks the sound is what matters.”

“Will you come and look with me?”

“Yes.”

When they left, Tesset paid the bill without checking how much she could have saved by eating at home. Outside, Leda took her arm. The blue cloth shone faintly beneath the lamps, and Tesset was allowed to look at it for the whole walk.

The room they found was above a woman who repaired umbrellas and below a man who owned a very small dog with a great deal to say.

The landlord opened the window to demonstrate that it opened. A chip fell from the sill. He pretended not to notice. Leda picked it up and set it on the mantel.

“The stair is shared,” he said. “No storage on it.”

“I don’t need storage on the stair,” Tesset answered.

“Everyone says that before they have a chair to put somewhere.”

She looked around the room. There was enough space for a bed, a table, and a chest without any of them blocking the door. The little fireplace would need sweeping. The window faced a courtyard in which someone had planted herbs in broken pots. No yard belonged to the room. The landlord was precise about this, as if he had previously been cheated of a yard by a person who looked at one too freely.

Leda bent to inspect the table leg. Tesset looked at the wall beside the bed. She could put a shelf there and reach a book without getting up. The thought seemed unnecessarily luxurious. She wanted it anyway.

“Who repairs the window?” she asked.

“It works.”

“The wood is coming away.”

“I can have the sill looked at.”

“Before I pay for the second interval.”

The landlord gave her a long look. “You work with papers.”

“Yes.”

“I thought so.”

She took the room for a short first term, with the repair written plainly. She had enough saved for the payment and the moving cart if she did not buy the good blanket she had seen in the market. Nella would lend her one until the next wages came. She could ask Nella to lend it without feeling that the whole household had become a favor she could never finish returning.

After the landlord left, Leda sat on the bare bed frame and bounced lightly to test it.

“It complains.”

“So do you.”

“Only when treated badly.”

Tesset sat beside her. The room smelled of soot and old lavender, with a faint sourness from the courtyard drain. The dog above them barked, stopped, and barked once more as if correcting itself.

“You don’t have to stay here because you helped find it,” Tesset said.

“Good. I’d have to start charging landlords for showing you rooms.”

“I mean sometimes. I don’t mean—”

Leda took her hand. “I’d like to stay sometimes. I’ll bring the ugly book you wanted to see.”

Tesset let the sentence remain unfinished. Leda put her shoulder against hers. They sat without deciding where every object would go. After a while Leda asked whether there was room for a second cup, and Tesset showed her the shelf she intended to have.

Lyra’s invitation came three days later, written on the reverse of a clean wrapping sheet. She named a cookshop near the upper market and asked Tesset to choose a day when she could stay long enough to eat.

Tesset chose her free evening. She almost chose a working one, so that the hours at the landing could furnish a reason to arrive late or leave early. When she caught herself doing it, she put down the first date she had thought of.

Lyra was already there when she arrived. Her right arm rested against the table rather than beneath it. The sleeve had been cut a little wider at the shoulder, a strong, ugly alteration that was unlikely to come apart while anyone could still complain about it.

“Is it worse?” Tesset asked.

“The arm? No. The chair is.”

She stood to show that the whole table moved when she shifted her weight. Tesset put a folded piece of paper beneath the shorter leg before taking her seat.

“There.”

Lyra pressed the tabletop. It stayed still.

“I should have paid you for furniture.”

“You did, occasionally.”

For a moment they were back in the office, with the day interrupted by some small irritation they could solve between them. Tesset felt the ease arrive in her shoulders. She had missed it more than she admitted when Leda asked whether she wanted to go.

Lyra ordered a dish of braised meat, roots, and fresh bread. She remembered that Tesset liked the edge of the loaf where the crust was thickest and put it on her plate without comment. Tesset took it. Refusing the bread would have made the meal into a performance neither of them had asked for.

“The new room?” Lyra said.

“Above an umbrella repairer.”

“Does she repair the roof too?”

“I asked about that.”

“Of course you did.”

Tesset told her about the dog, the landlord’s view of chairs, and the window repair she had put in writing. Lyra listened with the concentration she usually brought to a route someone else had inspected. She asked which side the drain ran on and then stopped herself.

“You have seen it,” she said.

“Yes.”

“Then you don’t need me telling you where to stand.”

Tesset broke the bread. “You would anyway, if you came.”

“I would.”

They laughed. The woman serving them brought another spoon and asked which of them had complained about the bent one. Neither had. She said somebody always did and put it down regardless.

Lyra described the room over Harrow Slip. Tesset had seen it from the stair but never been inside. She could imagine Lyra at the narrow table, impatient with the roof slope every time it interfered with a task she had already decided to do standing. There was something pleasing in that image, and something lonely. Tesset did not know whether the loneliness belonged to Lyra or to her own absence from the room.

“I’ve asked about buying it,” Lyra said.

“The room?”

“The slip. The shed. The ground that comes with them.”

“Both owners?”

“Both. Sova reminds me every time I climb the stair that the rent won’t count toward the price.”

“It won’t.”

“I know.”

Tesset smiled at the old impatience. Lyra saw it and smiled too, briefly.

“What will you use to pay?” Tesset asked.

“The reserve, what I get for the damaged goods, and the small claims I’ve spent too long letting people forget. There will be very little left.”

“You’ll need working money.”

“I have allowed for it.”

“How much?”

Lyra named the sum. Tesset thought of boat hire, rope, ordinary wages through a week in which nothing could be landed. The sum was possible. It was also small enough to become impossible through one poorly chosen promise.

“You’ll need more than one customer’s cargo.”

“I know.” Lyra reached for her cup. “That’s something I wanted to ask you.”

Tesset felt her shoulders change before the request was spoken.

“The merchants taking the lower berths have been submitting their expected loads. Sera has the private figures before the allocations. I need to know which ones will be looking for a second landing.”

“The general quantities will be posted.”

“After they’ve chosen. I’d like to approach them before someone else does.”

Tesset set down her bread.

“I can’t give you the sheets.”

“I don’t need the sheets. The names and the likely dates would do.”

“They haven’t made those public.”

“Nor have they made a vow to keep buying the Prince’s services. Some of them would be grateful for an offer.”

“Then you can send an offer to all of them.”

Lyra’s cup stopped halfway to her mouth. “That costs more.”

“Yes.”

“I have just told you how much I have left.”

“I heard you.”

The cookshop continued around them. A man at the next table was describing a cousin’s wedding with a great deal of attention to the food and none to the cousin. A child under a chair was trying to retrieve a bead without admitting to having dropped it. Tesset could hear the soft scrape of fingers against the floor.

Lyra put the cup down. “You used to understand which things could be made easier.”

“I still do.”

“And this one cannot.”

“This one I won’t.”

Tesset had imagined saying it firmly. In fact her voice was low, almost lost beneath the wedding account. Lyra heard. She looked toward the window, and Tesset waited for the meal to end.

It did not. Lyra pulled the dish nearer and served herself more roots. She offered Tesset the spoon. After an awkward pause, Tesset took it.

“Will you at least tell me when the public notice goes up?” Lyra asked.

“Yes. I can tell everyone who asks.”

“How reassuring.”

Tesset looked at her. Lyra’s mouth tightened; then she said, “Yes. Tell me.”

They ate. The meat had cooled. Tesset wanted to ask whether Lyra had invited her only for the figures, but asking would make whichever answer came difficult to believe. She chose to talk about the room again, the promise Nella had made to lend her a blanket and then expanded into an argument about which blanket she was capable of keeping clean.

Lyra listened. When Tesset said that Perron wanted to move the table by himself, she put down her spoon.

“The long one?”

“Yes.”

“He’ll break the turn.”

“I told him that.”

“Take the legs off.”

“They don’t come off.”

“Then he shouldn’t move it by himself.”

The familiar ease returned in a smaller form. Tesset let it stay small. They did not settle what the request had done to the meal. Lyra paid, with Tesset contributing her share after a brief argument in which neither used the word wages. Outside, the market lamps had been lit.

“Come and see the slip when the first repairs are finished,” Lyra said.

“I work there.”

“I know. I mean come when you aren’t working.”

Tesset considered her. “Ask me when you know the day.”

“I will.”

They separated at the turn. Tesset looked back once and found Lyra already crossing toward the lower road, her left hand resting against the coat pocket where she kept the little repair knife.

Nella had packed the wrong blanket.

Tesset discovered it when she opened the bundle in the new room. The better one lay on top, with a mended border and enough weight to make winter seem farther away. Under it were two clean sheets, a pot with its lid tied on, and a note telling her not to return the pot until she had learned to cook something that required one.

Perron arrived carrying the table with another man from the dyehouse. They had not tried to move it alone, and they made sure Tesset heard this before they began maneuvering it through the door. Leda stood at the top of the stair to guide the higher end. The umbrella repairer came out to say they were touching her wall; when the table was finally in, she offered them a cloth to wipe the mark off.

Tesset put the crooked blue cup on the mantel. Leda brought another cup from her bag, plain white and thick at the lip. She had not brought a chest or a bundle of clothes. Tesset found she could enjoy the cup without counting what was absent.

The girls came with Nella after supper, each carrying something unsuitable for a visit. One brought a wooden hoop that would not pass between the bed and the table. The other brought a picture she had drawn of the household, with Tesset added in a little square beneath the rest. She asked whether she should now draw another square across the street.

“It’s farther than across the street,” Nella said.

“I haven’t room for farther.”

Tesset put the picture against the mantel beside the cups. “This one will do.”

When the visitors left, the room seemed to have moved from somebody else’s property into her own evening. The dog barked overhead. Leda opened the little book she had sewn and showed Tesset how flat it lay. The pages were blank except for one place where ink from an older sheet showed through the paper.

“What will you put in it?” Tesset asked.

“I haven’t decided.”

“Could I have one?”

“If I make another.”

“I’ll pay you.”

“You can. For the paper. You’d better wait until I can sew more neatly before you pay for that.”

Tesset ran her finger near the fold without touching the thread. Leda watched her, then closed the book and put it aside.

“Shall I stay tonight?”

“Yes,” Tesset said.

She did not hide how much she wanted it. They made the bed together, turning the blanket so Nella’s mended edge lay nearest the wall, and put the two cups where they could reach them in the morning.

Chapter 28 The visiting hour

Lyra wrote the request twice. The first version said she would come to the cutting house on the fifth day. The second asked whether Aria wished to see her.

She sent the second. The first went into the stove beneath a handful of shavings. It burned badly because the morning fire was nearly out, and she had to open the little door again to make certain no part remained legible.

Avenna’s answer came two days later. Aria had not completed the prepared questions that morning. They would ask again after a rest. Lyra could come to the house on the proposed afternoon if she wished, but a place in the waiting room was the only arrangement Avenna could promise beforehand.

Lyra read the letter beside the open window. Below her, Daven was telling a porter that the boat could remain still or the man could keep jumping between its side and the landing; he would have to choose which result he preferred. The porter stopped jumping. The boat steadied.

She could send another letter and wait for a definite answer. That would be reasonable. It would also leave her arranging her work around a door she had not yet approached.

She hired a place on a carrier’s cart going toward the quarry and told Sova she would be away for the afternoon.

“Until when?”

“Evening.”

“I’ll keep your bowl.”

Lyra was touched by the offer and irritated that it touched her. “I’ll pay whether I’m back or not.”

“I assumed so.”

That made it easier to leave.

Callista had a parcel to send. Katerina had left it at the quiet house where Callista lived: a clean shift, a little jar, and a book with two pages marked by folded corners. Lyra stopped there on her way to the cart. Callista sat by a window with a shawl across her knees, arguing with the woman who had brought her breakfast.

“You don’t have to finish it,” the woman said. “You have to stop telling me you have.”

“I have finished the part I mean to eat.”

“That part keeps getting smaller.”

Callista looked up and saw Lyra. Her expression changed, not into pleasure exactly, but into the effort required for a visitor she wished to be capable of receiving.

“Come in. I’m being unreasonable.”

“The woman seems to have noticed.”

The attendant lifted the bowl and left them. Callista watched it go with an expression almost of regret, then pulled the shawl closer.

“I wanted it yesterday.”

“What?”

“What she made. By this morning I couldn’t bear the smell.”

Lyra sat on the plain chair beside her. She had come prepared to collect a parcel and ask after symptoms. Callista was looking out at the street, where a man was selling lamp wicks from a tray hung around his neck. He sang the price on a descending note, with a pause at the lowest part to draw attention. Lyra had heard him do it at the Opera gates.

“He changes the price when he thinks nobody remembers yesterday,” Callista said.

“Does it work?”

“Sometimes I buy one because I want him to stop outside.”

She smiled without hiding the small foolishness of it. Lyra stayed long enough for him to make another circuit. Callista bought two wicks and asked him whether his wife had recovered from her cough. The man told her more than the price of two wicks justified. Lyra listened, enjoying the warmth of the room even though her body would have preferred the shaded passage.

“Katerina left the blue ribbon too,” Callista said after he went. “It’s in the jar. Tell her I haven’t stolen it.”

“Will she think you have?”

“She thinks I’ve forgotten what I borrowed. Usually she’s right.”

Lyra put the parcel in her bag. Callista noticed Avenna’s letter when Lyra moved it aside.

“You’re going to ask for a visit?”

“I’ve asked.”

Callista nodded. There was no attempt to tell her what Aria would decide. Lyra liked her for that.

“Would you like anything brought back?”

“Something that doesn’t smell like breakfast.”

“I’ll see what the quarry offers.”

“Stone, mostly.”

Lyra laughed. Callista laughed too, then had to wait for the coughing to stop. Lyra poured water and set the cup within reach.

The cart carried three lengths of timber, two covered baskets, and a man who slept with his head against the driver’s spare coat. Lyra sat beside the baskets. She had paid for the place, which did not prevent the driver from asking her to keep one basket upright when the road sloped.

The basket smelled of young onions. She put a foot against it and watched the city thin into yards, sheds, and gardens behind low walls. It was strange to travel toward Aria at the speed of a cart. Lyra had spent so much time imagining the woman’s position beneath the Opera House that distance felt like a disagreement with what she knew.

The road followed the broad channel for part of the way. The water was high enough to hide the lower stones, but its movement was gentle. Nothing connected this sheltered reach to the drowned rooms of Mere Lane except the commerce of the city and the people who traveled between them. At a narrow turn the driver asked Lyra to get down until the cart had passed a broken edge. She walked beside the wheel, holding the basket from below. Her right arm tired before they reached level ground, and she changed hands.

“You’ve hurt it,” the driver said.

“Yes.”

“Then don’t carry the whole basket.”

“I’m keeping it upright.”

“So’s the side of the cart.”

She let go enough to discover he was right. The basket settled against its neighbor. He offered her a hand up and did not comment on the ease with which she climbed without it.

They reached the quarry road while the sun was still above the ridge. Lyra could see the cutting house’s windows from the turn. The old crane stood beyond them, unused. A shirt hung on a line beside the former tool shed. Someone had made a seat from two short blocks and a plank. The place looked less like an arrangement on a page than it had when she first read the inspection.

She paid the driver and asked when he returned. He would come back before dark, with a load that left no room for a seated passenger. She could walk to the lower junction and catch a different cart. He explained where the junction was, then pointed it out because she had looked at the wrong ridge.

Lyra thanked him. She put Callista’s parcel under her left arm and went to the waiting door.

Senn answered. He had a cloth in his hand and an open ink stain on his cuff. He looked pleased to see her before his expression grew more careful.

“You received the letter?”

“Yes.”

“Avenna is finishing the contact. You can wait here.”

He showed her into a small room with a table, three chairs, and a basin for washing. None of the chairs faced the receiving room directly. A shelf held clean cloths and a little box of chalk. The wall above it had hooks for visitors’ coats, spaced widely enough that wet garments would not touch.

Lyra hung her coat and put the parcel on the table. A chart lay there, with letters in separate groups and common choices on a second sheet. She saw LIGHT, WINDOW, QUIET, STOP. The writing was large and unornamented. At the bottom, a note said that a selection had to be confirmed separately. Beneath that someone had written that a failed confirmation ended the attempt.

The first few sheets attached to the board were dated. Avenna had written the offered choices and the sounds heard. On one day the selection appeared to be a letter, then failed when they asked it alone. It had been crossed through. On another, Aria had chosen the window, then given no answer when asked whether to open it. That line ended without an instruction.

Lyra moved her hand away from the page. These were not papers she had been given to inspect. Senn had left them on the table while he fetched Avenna. She took a chair by the basin and looked at the seam in her glove instead.

Through the closed inner door, a note sounded. It was too faint to tell whether it held to the end or broke. She could not hear the question. She sat still, her fingers pressed around the glove.

Avenna came in a little later carrying the day’s page. She closed the inner door behind her. Lyra stood before she had been asked to.

“Not today,” Avenna said.

The words were plain enough that Lyra disliked her for not softening them.

“Aria answered?”

“Yes. The checks were clear. I asked whether she wished you to come into the receiving room this afternoon. Two notes. I asked whether she wanted us to offer another day after a rest. One held note.”

“Which day?”

“We didn’t obtain one. She stopped answering after that.”

“Then how do you know she wants another?”

“Because she answered that question.”

Lyra looked at the page. Avenna placed it on the table where she could read it. Dorel and Senn had witnessed the sounds. The phrasing was as clear as any Lyra had insisted on when she wanted an answer to be undeniable.

“May I speak from here?”

“Do you mean send a message for us to read later?”

“I mean she might hear me.”

Avenna waited. The waiting made the distinction difficult to escape.

“A message, then,” Lyra said.

“I’ll ask whether she wants it.”

Lyra sat again. She had thought of several things to say on the cart. The new room. The work at the slip. That the harvesting records had been removed from her custody with everything else. Each seemed suddenly like an object she wanted to place inside Aria’s day because she had carried it all this way.

“Tell her I received the answer,” she said.

Avenna wrote it without making it larger.

Lyra glanced at the letter board. “Is that working?”

“Sometimes. Short choices work better than letters. We have a few agreed things she wants asked each morning, and a way for her to stop before we finish. A long answer can take more than one day, and we have to begin again if the checks fail.”

“What has she asked?”

“Mostly changes to the room. Light from the water bothers her at one hour and pleases her at another. She wants a particular song sometimes, but we’ve been wrong about which one.”

Avenna smiled. “She told me no four times before I admitted I was offering the same song in different words.”

Lyra almost smiled with her. Then she looked toward the closed door and the expression failed.

“Is she better?”

“The fracture is unchanged. She is able to end the contact sooner. That seems to leave her less tired afterward.”

It was an answer about a woman arranging her day, not a promise of the hand inside the crystal moving freely. Lyra knew the difference. She had no wish to be taught it in this room.

She stood and took her coat from the hook.

“Callista sent that parcel for Katerina.”

“I’ll fetch her.”

“I can leave it.”

“She is just outside.”

Avenna went to the yard door. Lyra remained beside the table until Katerina came in with a basket of washed cloths against her hip.

Katerina looked at the parcel and laughed softly when she found the ribbon in the jar.

“She said she hadn’t taken it.”

“She’s sent evidence to the contrary.”

“She’ll enjoy that sentence.”

Lyra followed her into the yard. She could leave from there without passing the receiving door. Katerina set the basket on the bench and began folding. The cloths were for the attendants and the household; some were coarse, others soft enough to lie beneath a support without scratching. She separated them by touch while she spoke.

“Will you eat before the road?”

“I can eat in the city.”

“That’s several hours.”

“Not if a cart comes.”

Katerina held up two cloths of different sizes, considered them, and put them in separate piles. “There is bread.”

Lyra accepted the bread. It came with a piece of cheese and no demand that she sit where she could hear Aria. They ate on the plank seat facing the basin. Across the water, two quarry workers were clearing reeds from a small boat. One stood in it while the other warned him that the boat was moving because he stood in it. The argument reminded Lyra of Daven that morning and made the day feel uncomfortably circular.

“Do you live here now?” she asked.

“Some days. Some with Callista. The attendants don’t need me on every watch, and Aria doesn’t always want me in the room.”

“Does she want you more often than she wants me?”

Katerina looked at her. Lyra regretted the question as soon as she heard its smallness. She did not withdraw it.

“I don’t know,” Katerina said. “We don’t count the visits against one another.”

Lyra bit into the bread. It was dry enough to require concentration.

Katerina turned the ribbon around one finger, then unwound it. “I used to think about what I could do that would make seeing me easier for her. Now Dorel tells me when the pads need cleaning, and I clean them. It doesn’t tell me what she’ll answer at the door.”

“I don’t need an account of your improvement.”

“No.” Katerina’s face had gone pale. “You don’t.”

They sat without speaking. Lyra could have apologized. She could also have named the things Katerina had done and made the cruelty seem like a reasonable contribution to memory. She did neither. She finished the bread and wiped her fingers on the paper.

“Callista wanted something that didn’t smell like breakfast,” she said.

“There are plums in the back room. I’ll send some.”

“I can take them.”

Katerina went to fetch a small basket. Lyra stood by the outer gate while she waited. From here she could see one receiving window, covered halfway by a plain curtain. The lower cloth moved slightly in the air. Someone adjusted it from inside, drawing it farther across the bright part of the water. Lyra could not see who.

Katerina brought the plums wrapped so the basket would not bruise them. Lyra took it, thanked her for the meal, and left by the road. She did not go back through the waiting room for another look at the page.

Corven was repairing a strap when she found him two days later. He had rented a workroom behind a cooper’s yard, with a shelf deep enough for the eel’s jar and a chair whose back had been worn smooth by somebody larger than he was. The window opened onto a wall close enough to keep the room cool. He appeared satisfied with it.

“Your bandage?” Lyra asked.

“Changed.”

“The wound?”

“Closing.”

“By whom?”

“Mostly by itself.”

She gave him the small packet of clean cloth she had brought. He accepted it, which was as close as he usually came to admitting that he needed something once the urgent part was over.

The chain lay along the table. Several marked links had been wrapped separately, and the cracked shell rested in a shallow box. Corven was replacing the strap because it had worn where it crossed his side, pressing against the wound each time he lifted the chain. He showed Lyra the rubbed place and asked her to hold the new leather flat while he pierced it.

She did. The tool went through with a dry little sound. They moved down the strap, making holes the same distance apart. Lyra found pleasure in the steadiness of it, in a task that required neither of them to ask who had been right.

“Have they offered you the Sluice House watch?” she asked.

“Yes.”

“You refused.”

“I told them how much they would have to pay me to keep those hours. They found another man.”

She laughed. He pulled the thread through the final hole and held the strap against his coat to judge the length.

“Will you work at the slip?”

“What work?”

“I haven’t bought it yet.”

“Then you don’t know.”

“Repairs first. Boat work later. Some of it may be below water.”

He set down the strap. “Ask when you have a job and an end to it.”

“You could say you don’t want to.”

“I might want the job.”

She nodded. It was harder to be offended by the answer when she had just helped make the equipment with which he would do the work. She asked whether he had eaten, and this time he said no without making her discover it. They bought a pot of beans from the yard’s cook and ate in his doorway while a cooper planed a stave on the far side of the open space.

Lyra told him about the room at Harrow, the gate price Pellan had not yet named, and the place she intended to set the first anchorage. She did not tell him that Aria had postponed the visit. He did not ask. When she left, he went back to the strap, pulling each seam hard enough to find the weak part before his next work could find it for him.

Chapter 29 The purchase

Mavet would not let her buy the wall without seeing its foot.

“It was here yesterday,” Lyra said.

“And we couldn’t see it yesterday.”

The tide had fallen far enough to expose the lower stones of Harrow Slip’s south cheek. Mavet stood on a temporary plank with a hammer at her belt, scraping weed from the face with a blunt iron. Above them, the north cheek showed the crack Pellan had pointed out when they first rented the place. Rain had washed the exposed edge clean. It had not narrowed out of courtesy to the approaching sale.

Lyra held the lamp, though the morning was bright. The light reached beneath the overhang where Mavet wanted to look. Sova watched from the platform. She had brought a chair and refused to call her presence an inspection.

“The deed describes ground,” Mavet said. “It doesn’t describe how long the ground will stay in its place.”

“I’ve paid you to tell me that.”

“Then hold the lamp lower.”

Lyra did. Her arm had improved through the weeks of smaller work. She could keep the light steady for several minutes before changing hands, and she no longer woke whenever she turned onto that side. The scar still pulled in cold weather. She had stopped expecting the body to mark the end of an argument by becoming convenient again.

Mavet sounded the stones in sequence. She put her ear close to one face, struck, moved to the next, and struck again. Lyra had learned enough to hear that the sounds differed. She had not learned enough to pretend she could name what the difference meant.

“The south bed is sound,” Mavet said at last. “The north needs taking back before anything heavy draws against it. If you put a fixed ring there because it’s nearer the shed, you’ll pull the repair out with the first awkward hull.”

“Here, then.” Lyra indicated a place on the sound side, above the ordinary wet line.

“Perhaps. We’d have to open the old socket and see what they left.”

“And a lighter could lie below it?”

“A lighter could. A master with ambitions could bring you something larger and tell you it was only larger at one end.”

Lyra smiled. She knew several such masters.

Mavet climbed onto the platform and cleaned her tool on a piece of sacking. “You can use the place. You’ll spend money before it looks as though you’ve spent any.”

Sova nodded from her chair. “That’s what I’ve been telling Pellan.”

“Pellan thinks I should spend it before he names a price,” Lyra said.

“He thinks many things. He doesn’t do all of them.”

They went inside to wait for him. The shed smelled of rope and the new timber Berris had used on the stair. Lyra had paid for that repair under the rent. It would stay when she left unless she bought the building, and Sova had reminded her of that fact before the first nail was driven. Now Lyra could climb to her room without turning her foot sideways on the broken tread. It had been worth doing even before it could belong to her.

Pellan arrived with the ownership papers in a flat leather case. A paid clerk came with him to compare them against the registered copies. Lyra had asked for the clerk herself. She did not intend to discover a fourth claimant after making the roof useful.

The boundaries took most of the morning. The shed and its upper room went with Sova’s share. The slip’s two inherited portions met beneath a line no one could see without measuring from the old wall. The drain crossing the corner remained available to the neighboring houses. The public footway was outside the sale. Passage by the Ragman’s ways would still require its own agreement. Neither Sova nor Pellan could give her the basin mechanism, the outer culvert, or the right to stop people using water they had used before she arrived.

Lyra walked the boundary with the clerk while Pellan held the drawing out of the wind. She asked him to show each mark. One was hidden behind a stack of firewood; Sova moved it piece by piece until the cut in the stone appeared. Another lay under a layer of lime. The clerk scraped it clean and checked the distance twice.

“Any charge against either share?” Lyra asked.

The clerk showed the entries he had checked. There was a paid claim whose release had been entered late, and the release now lay with the rest. Nothing remained due from the property. Pellan looked offended by the question despite having asked whether Lyra’s own funds were clear three times before breakfast.

“All rights described,” she said, reading the transfer again. “No retained use of the landing, no renewal required, no permission to repair sought from either seller after completion.”

“Except the drain,” Sova said.

“The drain belongs to the neighboring houses’ access. It isn’t yours to retain.”

Sova considered her, then nodded. Pellan opened his mouth and closed it when the clerk pointed to the line already covering it.

The price was worse than Lyra expected.

They had named a figure earlier, before Mavet’s inspection. She had assumed the failing north cheek would reduce it. Pellan said the recent work had made the place more valuable.

“My work,” Lyra said.

“Work that remains with the property.”

“Under a rent you collected.”

“Under a rent you agreed.”

She looked at him until he reached for his cup. Sova did not defend him. She also did not lower her own share.
