Lyra named what she would pay. Pellan shook his head. She named the necessary stonework and the cost of taking down the dangerous end of the old roof. He said she did not have to take it down at once.

“You won’t be beneath it,” she answered.

Sova’s eyes moved toward the cracked beam. “That part comes down before winter.”

“Then account for it,” Lyra said.

They argued until the tea cooled. The clerk went outside to eat the food he had brought, leaving them to discover that his time cost money whether or not he listened. Lyra stood at the window and watched a boat pass the slip because there was no cargo for it today. The boat’s master raised a hand. She raised hers back.

She could keep renting until the allowed renewal ended. After that the price could rise. She could carry away her loose equipment and begin at a different damaged place, paying once more for the work that would make it usable. Or she could accept a smaller bargain somewhere owned by people pleased to keep her as long as she remained useful to them.

She wanted this poor strip of ground with an intensity that made bargaining dangerous. Sova knew it. Pellan knew it. They did not have to be clever enough to deserve the advantage.

Lyra turned from the window.

“The upper figure includes the stone steps and the room’s fittings as listed. The cleared payment closes both shares today. You take your personal goods by the named day. Any payment still disputed between you is settled between you. It does not follow the ground.”

Pellan looked at Sova. She looked at him for longer than courtesy required.

“Yes,” she said.

The clerk came back in, brushing crumbs from his sleeve. They had found a sum he could write down.

Lyra had to leave the slip to release the funds. She had arranged enough in clear money, although having arranged it did not make the walk pleasant.

The reserve held by the trading house had been saved through years when she lived in rooms smaller than the one she was buying. Some came from salvage, some from correspondence handled for people who believed an urgent letter required a fee to make it more urgent. The sale of the recovered iron had added less than she hoped. The damaged cloth had sold in three lots, each uglier than the last. Two outstanding business claims had finally been paid after she went in person and sat where the debtor could not easily pretend to be occupied elsewhere.

She kept the working purse separate. There was enough for the first repairs, ordinary wages, and a bad week if the week confined itself to the kind of bad she had allowed for. She counted that purse again in the trading house’s little room. The clerk waited without appearing curious.

“You want the whole remaining deposit released?” he asked.

“For the purchase. The described sum, to the two named sellers.”

He read the instruction back. Lyra corrected a copied initial and made him write the name again on a clean line. No part of the property would remain in pledge to the sellers. She did not want her occupation to depend on Pellan’s patience during the next interrupted season.

The clerk prepared the instruments. She could carry them without a guard; they would pay only the people named after the transfer was acknowledged. She nevertheless put them inside her coat and kept her hand against that pocket through the market.

Revet’s stall stood on the way back. He was examining a length of bronze for a customer, turning it so the man could see a flaw close to the end. Lyra waited until the customer had gone.

“I need a fixed ring and shank made to a drawing,” she said.

Revet put down the bronze. “How large?”

She showed him Mavet’s measurements. He read them, asked what load she intended, and gave a price that included the time required to make the part properly.

“That is more than the last one,” Lyra said.

“The last one was not this one.”

Ansel was in the back of the shop, wrapping small pieces in cloth. He had looked up when she came and then returned to his work. Lyra could feel the effort with which he kept his face turned away. She had no file in her room to make his brother’s price more reasonable. The custody notice had gone to the people named under the agreed protections; whatever reassurance they obtained from it had not made her welcome.

“When?” she asked.

Revet named the day.

“I need it inspected before it goes into the stone.”

“Then send your stoneworker before it leaves the shop.”

She paid the deposit. He wrote the description and the date, and both kept a copy. There was nothing remarkable about the exchange except how carefully each of them kept it ordinary.

At the door Lyra looked toward Ansel. She could have said the private papers were no longer hers. He knew. She could have said she would not use them again. He had no reason to want another promise from her. She left with the agreed drawing in her hand.

The sale was completed in the room above the landing, where the afternoon light was good enough for every person to read without taking the paper to the window.

Sova signed first. Her name was slow and square, each letter receiving the same pressure. Pellan signed with a flourish that nearly crossed the boundary description. The clerk made two clean copies of the acknowledgment and directed the instruments to their separate names. Odel witnessed the transfer. She had brought her chair again and placed it where it would not rock.

Lyra signed last. The property passed as the document described, with its shed, room, stonework, failed timber, and continuing drain. The clerk would enter the completed transfer that afternoon and return the confirmed copy. He took the originals in his case. Lyra kept the signed counterpart and the marked boundary drawing until the final record came back.

Pellan counted the value on his instrument as if he expected it to change when he looked away. Sova opened her ring of keys and removed the duplicate to the shed. The room key was smaller. She put both on the table.

“You already have the others,” she said.

“I do.”

“The little cupboard under the stair has no lock. It never did. If you find a key for it, it’s to something else.”

Lyra picked up the keys. Sova watched them disappear into her hand, then stood and began gathering the food things she had brought into the shed over the months of letting it. Her cup had a chip on the side where her thumb usually rested. She wrapped it more carefully than the iron pot.

“Will you keep cooking here until the named day?” Lyra asked.

“If you’ll pay for the meals.”

“I will.”

“Then yes. After that, the pot goes.”

Pellan helped her lift the basket of packed belongings. They quarreled about which handle he should take and carried it outside together, leaving Lyra with Odel and a table covered in papers that no longer described a possible arrangement.

“You’ll need a new passage agreement,” Odel said.

“The old one has time left.”

“You’ll still need one after it ends.”

Lyra sat opposite Odel. “The same daylight boat rate, a fixed allowance for our foot traffic, and notice before any change.”

Odel looked toward the landing. “The amount of foot traffic hasn’t stayed fixed.”

“The amount of money passing your households hasn’t either.”

They began the next negotiation before the first afternoon had ended. Lyra did not mistake buying a gate for buying every way that led to it. She wanted a rate that could be put into a cargo price before the boat arrived. Odel wanted the right to refuse dangerous loads, night disturbance, and men who treated a paid passage as permission to search the houses.

They retained those limits. She obtained a longer term and a clear notice period. The night work would still require a separate request. Odel would bring the final writing after the Ragman’s approval. There was no secret assent in her willingness to sit at the table.

When Odel left, Lyra opened the shed doors. The light reached a place on the back wall that had spent the morning in shadow. She could see a shelf had been mounted crookedly. She had seen it before. This time she fetched a level and found out how far.

She asked the first workers individually.

Orrel came to examine the hand winch and stayed to discuss the regular work. He had other employment and did not want to give it up for a landing that might have more promises than boats. Lyra offered two fixed days and additional work by agreement. He asked who would pay when the weather prevented unloading. She showed him the reserve she had kept for wages, without opening it on the table for everyone to admire.

“The fixed days are paid,” she said. “If there’s no cargo, there are repairs.”

“And when the repairs are finished?”

“Have you looked at the place?”

He smiled. They agreed a first term. He took a copy of it home before giving his final answer the next morning.

A porter from the Opera House wanted all the work he could obtain but no night watch. His partner worked mornings, and he meant to be in their room when she returned. Lyra had thought him available precisely because he had said little about his private arrangements. She crossed the night line out and asked which other porter knew the water well enough to take it.

The woman he named came to see the landing before discussing wages. She tested the stair, asked about the door at the back, and wanted a lamp that could be carried down without taking the only light out of the shed. Lyra added a second lamp to the supplies. The woman agreed to three nights, then said she would answer about the rest after living through those.

Daven would keep ownership and command of the Brindle. Lyra had not asked for anything else, but he wanted it said before they spoke of regular cargo. She gave him first offer on the loads suited to his boat at a defined rate, with no penalty for work he refused before she confirmed a customer. He complained about the rate. She complained about his appetite for complaining. They settled between the two sums and shared a pear while he inspected the landing.

Berris took the remaining roof work as a separate job. Mavet agreed to open the south socket and set the new anchorage once Revet’s iron passed inspection. Esten would fit the purchase and brake. His assistant was a quiet woman who had worked on pumps farther up the canal. Lyra watched him begin to give her Dannet’s old signal, stop, and explain it again from the beginning. She did not hurry them.

By the end of the week there were enough people to do the first work, each with something specific to come back for. Nobody had promised to follow her wherever she went. She had not asked them to.

The confirmed transfer arrived in a flat case. Lyra read it alone in her room, checked the names and the boundary marks against the copy, and put it in the drawer beside her own correspondence. She laid Revet’s final drawing with the ordinary repairs. The oiled rubbing remained locked beneath the bed, beside the hooks she still needed to finish.

Below, Orrel called for a second pair of hands. Lyra put away the key, went down the repaired stair, and helped him lift the winch clear of the old rotten platform.

Chapter 30 The anchorage

The new iron arrived wrapped in a piece of Revet’s old sailcloth. Mavet made them leave it wrapped until she had cleared a place where it could be laid without picking up grit. Lyra had already chosen a place. It was the first of several decisions that morning which she found herself making twice.

“Over there is in the carrying line,” Mavet said.

“There is no carrying line yet.”

“There will be when we carry it.”

Lyra moved the two blocks. The men brought the iron in between them and set it down where Mavet pointed. It was larger than it had seemed on the drawing, a ring deep enough to take the heavy hauling line without pinching it against the shank. Revet had left the inspection marks visible. Mavet ran a finger along the place where one end had been worked and nodded.

The south socket had been opened during the previous low water. Its inner stone was sound, as she had expected, but a piece of old iron remained buried across the back. Removing it had taken an afternoon. Lyra had paid for that afternoon and found herself unreasonably pleased when the rusted remnant came out whole. She kept it under the stair. Mavet said it was worth only what the smith would give for scrap.

“I know,” Lyra said.

She kept it anyway. She liked seeing what had been holding the old ring while generations of boatmen cursed it for being in the wrong place.

The permanent fixing would go higher, in sound stone opened to receive it, with the hauling line clear of the damaged north cheek. The footway above had room for people to pass while the winch was worked. Lyra had stood there at three different tides before agreeing to the position. At the largest ordinary water she could expect the lower steps to disappear. The anchorage itself would remain within reach.

Esten came to inspect the purchase, followed by his new assistant with the blocks on a handcart. Orrel was already at the brake. Neither assumed the other had checked the same thing. Lyra watched them go through it and resisted the impulse to say she had watched it checked yesterday. Yesterday did not include the new rope or the pin Esten had replaced after finding wear beneath its collar.

“First lift is empty,” Esten said.

“We know,” Orrel answered.

“Then it will be easy to do it that way.”

Lyra went upstairs for the gloves she had forgotten. From the window she could see the first of Daven’s promised cargo boats approaching the lower turn. It would have to use the temporary fastening while the new iron was set. She had allowed for that. Knowing she had allowed for it gave her a pleasure so sharp that she stood a moment longer than the gloves required.

On the table lay a letter to Neris. Lyra had begun it the previous evening and stopped when Sova called that the food would not wait for good handwriting. She sat down and finished the last page.

She wrote that Aria’s room had two windows, one facing the water, and that the curtains were moved when she asked. The new receiving house had held through ordinary bad weather. The rope-eye drawing had been used. Lyra added a description of the slip, including the stair Berris had repaired, and told Neris that the hauling rope had dried cleanly where she recommended. She did not ask her to come back.

For a moment she considered writing about Maeron. There was something she wanted to say that was neither an apology for missing him nor a demand that Neris miss him too. She could not find it in time for the carrier. She left the space unused and closed the letter with news of the work.

Beneath the table, the tool chest stood unlocked. She had been sorting its contents before dawn. The rubbing lay between dry boards in its oiled wrap, with Mavet’s measurements and the uncertain distances marked separately. Beside it were two unfinished hooks, a spare cord whose inner eye she had inspected, and a narrow board on which she meant to record changes in pressure before any later descent. She had asked for advice about measuring a sealed face without trusting a single reading. The answers had given her more equipment to obtain, not a reason to open anything now.

She checked the stopper of the brine vessel and put it away. The little practice frame remained on the table. Its fixed wood fitted beneath her left palm; the moving face gave a familiar resistance when she tested the hinge with her right. She poured a few drops of brine into her hand and touched her throat. Then she set the short test cord, applied the known pull, and held the face closed for the interval she intended.

The working held. When she let it go, the weight came gently onto its folded pad. Her hand ached. She rubbed the base of her thumb and waited for the trembling to stop before putting away the brine.

She cleaned the frame, locked the chest, and went downstairs with the gloves and Neris’s letter.

The evening before, she had taken the shore house its share of the last recovered cooking pots.

Marda examined the least dented one, turned it to look at the base, and asked who had been using it to melt something.

“People who were careful enough not to leave their names,” Lyra said.

“It smells of wax.”

“Then don’t use it for soup.”

“I hadn’t reached that decision without your advice.”

Lyra smiled and put the other pot on the table. The shore house had become less crowded as the lower rooms reopened, though three households still slept there. Someone had repaired the cupboard displaced for the old woman’s bed. Its doors now met badly in a new way. Marda said she preferred the old failure, but the man who had repaired it was proud and she had not told him.

Tarn sat in the cooler room behind the kitchen. His damaged hand rested around a length of loose rope. His other hand kept finding the shortened ends of the two injured fingers. Lyra sat beside him while Marda brought clean water.

“He pulled the cloth off again,” Marda said. “The skin underneath is closed. He doesn’t like the feeling of anything over it.”

“Then leave it off while he’s here. Cover it if he goes into the dirty yard.”

“He’ll put the rope there.”

“Wash the rope.”

Marda looked at her. “Every day?”

Lyra looked at the length across Tarn’s knees. It was an old clean end, with no load and nothing for him to drag against the damaged hand. Washing it every day would wear it out. They could keep several and change them while one dried. She offered to send the offcuts from the new hauling line, provided they were soft enough after dressing.

“Bring them when you come,” Marda said. “Don’t make an errand for someone else out of three bits of rope.”

Lyra nodded. Tarn turned his head at the sound of her voice, then looked past her toward the doorway. She asked before touching his hand. He did not give an answer she could use. She left her own hand on the bench between them, near enough for him to see it.

A little later he laid the rope against her knuckles. It might have been an offer. It might have been where the loose end happened to fall. She held it lightly until he drew it back. She did not ask Marda to record it.

Jorad came through carrying a bucket from the yard. He nodded to Lyra and continued toward the kitchen. His changed skin caught the light differently along the side of his neck. He was still alive inside a body Maeron had helped make, and Lyra had learned very little about what he did with that fact when she was not there.

“There will be repair work at Harrow for a while,” she told him when he returned. “If you want days.”

“I have days here.”

“Paid?”

“Some of them.”

“I can pay.”

He set down the bucket. “I know.”

She waited, and he did not add an explanation. Marda called him to help move a cupboard shelf. He picked up the bucket and went. Lyra watched the door until Marda returned with a bowl for her.

“He might come another time,” Marda said.

“I didn’t ask you to persuade him.”

“I wasn’t offering.”

They ate beside Tarn, where the room was cool enough for Marda to stay comfortable. The kitchen’s noise came through the wall. Lyra could hear someone teaching a child to count spoons by putting each one on a different part of the table. The child kept beginning again because there were always more spoons in the drawer.

Marda asked about the new room. Lyra described it, including the low beam she had hit twice. Marda laughed openly and said Maeron had once walked into the same hanging lamp three times in a week because he refused to admit it needed moving.

Lyra laughed too. Then the image of him came too clearly: the backward step, the offended look at an object that had not acted, his hand reaching up to set the lamp straight. She put down her spoon. Marda’s laughter ended without an apology.

“He would have liked your place,” Marda said.

Lyra looked at the bowl. “He would have wanted something from it.”

“Yes.”

They sat with that. Tarn slid the rope through his sound hand. The kitchen door opened, bringing heat into the room, and Marda asked someone to close it. After a while Lyra took up the spoon again.

Daven’s boat reached Harrow before the fixing was ready to lift.

Lyra went to the lower steps to take the line. The boat carried sacks of meal and a crate of ordinary lamps for a merchant who had previously declined to trade through the slip because the second carry cost too much. His regular landing was open again, but its new charges had made him reconsider. Tesset had told Lyra when the public notice went up. The merchant’s offer had come after Lyra sent the same rate to all the houses listed on it.

She had paid for every copy. The merchant had returned his with the quantities written beneath her offered rate, and she kept it pinned above the receiving table.

Daven held the boat off until the temporary mooring took the strain. “Your new iron’s still on the ground.”

“It isn’t holding you.”

“I noticed.”

“Use the old line until Mavet says otherwise.”

He looked at the crew waiting on the platform. “They’ve been told?”

“They’ve been told. Tell them again if it makes you happier.”

“It does.”

He told them. The cargo came ashore in small loads, each set clear of the work on the south side. Orrel remained at the hand winch. The night worker, off duty after her third agreed night, had stayed for breakfast but declined a paid unloading turn. She wanted to sleep before deciding whether to take the next three. Lyra sent a runner for the extra porter she had kept available and paid the woman for the time already completed.

There was no reserve person hidden in the shed when the runner returned alone. The porter had accepted another job. Lyra took off her coat and joined the carrying line.

She could lift a sack with both arms if she kept it close. She could not carry it far without the right shoulder beginning to shake. She chose the smaller loads, carrying lamps in their padded frame and loose ropes that needed to be moved out of the receiving space. One of the men offered to take them from her. She told him to carry the sack he was paid to carry.

The work warmed everyone except her in the usual ways. Sweat ran into Orrel’s eyes. Daven’s collar darkened. Lyra felt the old altered steadiness beneath her own fatigue, the cold reserve that let her continue after the ordinary ache should have sent her to a chair. She enjoyed it. The arm still required her to put the frame down and rest before the last carry. Both facts remained true while she watched the men pass.

Ada arrived with a bundle of washed cloths ordered for the workroom. She waited until the landing was clear before bringing them inside. Lyra signed for the delivery and paid the agreed amount.

“Is he well enough for you to be away?” she asked.

“The woman came this morning. She knows where he puts the cup.”

“How was he?”

“Annoyed that I moved the basket.”

“Why did you?”

“Because I needed the floor beneath it swept.”

Ada set an extra cloth beside the bundle. “That’s for you. The old one you lent us has been boiled thin.”

Lyra took it. It was plain, clean, and neatly mended at one corner. She asked if she could come on the next free afternoon. Ada said yes, after the washing was off the line. Before that she would be put to work.

“I have work here.”

“Then come after.”

Lyra smiled. Ada went out by the footway, stepping aside for a boy carrying an empty crate. She had a household to return to. Lyra watched until the wall hid her, then used the new cloth to wipe meal dust from the table.

By noon the cargo was under cover. The customer’s carrier counted the lamps and found one wick missing. Lyra opened the second packing and found it caught beneath the straw. The man took it without admitting that he had begun the accusation too early. She entered the completed load on her own business sheet. The wages and shared passage charges would be handled at the independent table as agreed. She put the signed weights beside the completed entry and closed the book before the wind could turn its pages.

They ate before the tide turned.

Sova brought the last meal she had agreed to cook in the shed: a thick pot of beans, bread, and onions cut small enough that Dannet would once have had an opinion about them. Esten looked at the bowl, then sat where there was room. His assistant asked him to pass the bread. He did. The meal continued.

Daven had found two pears in his bag and cut them into pieces with the knife he used for everything. Mavet refused a piece until he washed the blade. He protested that it had touched nothing objectionable since morning. She asked what it had touched before morning. He got up to wash it, grumbling, and came back with the pear already bitten.

Lyra laughed with the others. The sound of her own altered voice no longer surprised her each time, though she could still hear its difference when a room went quiet. Today nobody made the laughter into a question. Orrel held out his bowl for more beans. Berris, arriving after finishing a repair elsewhere, ate standing until Sova pushed a chair against the backs of her knees.

“Sit while there is a chair,” Sova told her.

“I’m still looking at your roof.”

“It isn’t mine.”

The woman said it with relief and something less simple beneath the relief. Lyra looked up at the beam. Berris followed her gaze and told her which part would come down first. They argued over whether the saved timber could make the workroom door wider. Sova listened while cleaning the edge of the pot.

After the meal she packed the pot and the remaining food in a handcart. Pellan had sent a man to help her move the heavier pieces. Her property was going to a room beyond the upper market, where she intended to let a table to a woman who sold cooked food and perhaps make the food herself when the woman proved less capable than she hoped.

“You’ll need a cup,” she told Lyra.

“I have cups.”

“The one you’ve been using is mine.”

Lyra brought it down from her room, washed it, and put it in the basket. Sova inspected the chip, found it unchanged, and tucked it among the cloths. Then she took the handcart handles and waited for the man to open the gate.

“Send the first cargo customer to me if he wants feeding,” she said.

“Give me the address.”

Sova did. Lyra wrote it on the back of the meal account and put it beside the outgoing letters. The cart went along the footway with a rattle that diminished as it passed the walls. The shed smelled of the meal for some time afterward.

At the low water, Mavet called them to the south face.

The opening was ready. She had fitted the iron’s buried end to the socket and prepared the crosspiece that would keep the shank from drawing out. Nothing depended on the broken north wall. The wedges and packing stones lay within reach on a cloth. Esten had rigged the purchase to lower the iron under control instead of letting six people discover its weight at the same moment.

Lyra stood where the ring would hang and looked toward the channel. A hauling rope led from it in her imagination, clear of the steps, past the place where an unloaded boat could wait without blocking another. She shifted her boot a little to test the width of the working space. There was room for another person beside her. There had to be.

“Here,” she said.

Mavet checked the mark against her drawing. “Here.”

Orrel took the brake. Esten’s assistant stood at the guide line. Berris held a timber pad where the iron might otherwise strike the edge. Lyra took the second guide with her left hand and let the loose part lie clear of her feet. A loose turn lay near her boot. She moved her foot farther away before the line could tighten.

The iron lifted from its blocks. It turned a little, heavy end seeking its own direction. Lyra drew the guide until the shank lay true. Her right hand helped without taking the sustained load. Esten called for a pause. Mavet crouched to see beneath the end, moved one packing stone, and raised her hand.

They lowered it by the breadth of a finger.

For several breaths it seemed to hang against something invisible. Then the buried end entered the prepared space. The sound changed as iron met stone deep in the socket. Mavet touched the outer face, checked the line, and told them to hold while she set the crosspiece.

Lyra could hear water draining from the lowest step. Farther out, a boat passed through the turn with two people sitting on its cargo. Somewhere behind her a door closed. The passing boat’s wake reached the lower stones, lifting a length of weed and laying it down again.

The crosspiece seated. Mavet drove the first wedge, then the second, alternating the blows so the shank did not turn. The ring moved less with each strike. She stopped, inspected the stone, and handed the hammer to Lyra.

“Those two,” she said. “Lightly until I tell you.”

Lyra knelt. The stone was cold through her trousers. She set the hammer in her left hand, tested the reach, and brought it down. The first blow was clumsy. She adjusted her grip and struck again. Mavet watched the narrow space beside the shank and told her when to change sides.

They worked until the fixing held without the purchase. Esten eased the line a little at a time. The iron remained where they had put it. Orrel kept his hand on the brake until Mavet said the load was off.

“No hard pull until I’ve checked the packing tomorrow,” she said. “Tonight it can hold the spare line out of the water. Tomorrow we test it properly.”

Lyra nodded and asked what load they would use for the test. Mavet named it. Orrel said he could have it ready before the morning boat.

The others began taking away the tackle. Berris carried the timber pad up first. Esten’s assistant wound the guide line without crossing the turns. Orrel offered Lyra a hand to stand, and she accepted it. Her knees hurt. Her shoulder would remind her of the day when she woke. She could already see where the next fixing should go, far enough away that one working line would not foul another.

She stayed by the new ring while Mavet gathered the last small tools. A gap beneath the lower edge still needed its packing stone. Mavet tried two pieces, rejected the first, and passed the second to Lyra.

“Hold that there.”

Lyra set it in place with both hands. Mavet fitted the iron driver against its face. Above them, someone called that the kettle was on. Neither answered yet.

The hammer fell. The stone moved inward. Lyra steadied it while Mavet reset the driver, then held it through the next blow.
